# Sūrah al-Mā'idah (Chapter 5) — Verse-by-Verse Commentary
## Introduction to the Sūrah

**The Charter of Covenants and the Table of Communion**

Al-Mā'idah, *the Table Spread*, is the fifth chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 120 verses of late Medinan revelation. It takes its name from the miraculous supper requested by the disciples of Jesus in verses 112–115 — a heavenly banquet sought as a physical token of reassurance, which Jesus inaugurates with prayer and which Allah grants under the solemn condition that anyone who denies truth thereafter will suffer unprecedented retribution. Known in classical scholarship as *Sūrat al-ʿUqūd* (the Chapter of Contracts) from its striking opening imperative — *O believers! Fulfil your obligations* (5:1) — al-Mā'idah is universally recognised as the final comprehensive legal sūrah revealed to the Prophet ﷺ. In prophetic tradition, ʿĀ'ishah advised believers to hold fast to al-Mā'idah because *whatever you find in it of lawful* (*ḥalāl*), *consider it lawful, and whatever you find in it of unlawful* (*ḥarām*), *consider it unlawful* — reflecting the fact that its legal rulings were un-abrogated (*muḥkam*) and represented the ultimate maturation of the Islamic legal order.

**The Summit of Revelation and the Completion of Religion**

The chronological setting of al-Mā'idah occupies the final years of the prophetic mission (between 6 AH and 10 AH), culminating in the Farewell Pilgrimage (*Ḥajjat al-Widāʿ*) in the tenth year of the Hijrah. It was during this pilgrimage, on the plain of ʿArafāt on a Friday, that verse 3 descended: *This day I have perfected for you your religion and completed My favour upon you and have approved for you Islam as your religion.* So weighty was this revelation that reports in Musnad Aḥmad state the Prophet's camel knelt under the sheer physical gravity of its descent. Madinah was no longer an embattled enclave; Islam had unified the Arabian peninsula, the pagan sanctuaries were purified, treaties were consolidated, and the Muslim community stood as an established power. The historical task of al-Mā'idah was therefore twofold: to ratify the finalised moral, dietary, and ritual boundaries for this mature commonwealth, and to enter into a definitive, sweeping audit of previous covenant-holders — the Jews and Christians — warning the new community against replicating their theological distortion, factional jealousy, and covenantal infidelity.

**The Structural Architecture of the Sūrah**

The movement of al-Mā'idah weaves between sacred ritual law, penal statutes, interfaith polemics, and eschatology. The opening movement (1–11) establishes the foundational sanctity of agreements, detailing dietary permissions and prohibitions, the ritual laws of purification (*wuḍūʾ* and *tayammum*), the marriageability of chaste believing and scriptural women, and the solemn covenant taken at the spring of guidance, anchored by the command: *Do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice; be just, that is closer to righteousness* (5:8). The second movement (12–26) reviews the broken covenants of the Children of Israel and the Christians, demonstrating how the forgetting of divine admonitions spawned enduring enmity among them, punctuated by the tragedy of their refusal to enter the Holy Land under Moses — *Go, you and your Lord, and fight! We are sitting right here* (5:24). The third movement (27–40) investigates the origins of human violence through the primeval fratricide of Cain and Abel (*Qābīl and Hābīl*), deriving from it the immortal sanctification of life: *Whoever kills a soul… it is as if he had slain all mankind, and whoever saves one, it is as if he had saved all mankind* (5:32), followed by the penal ordinances for banditry (*ḥirābah*) and theft. The fourth movement (41–50) forms the great judicial charter of revelation, comparing the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an as divine lights and criteria, concluding that each scriptural community must be held to divine standards, culminating in the repudiation of pre-Islamic pagan judgment (*ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah*). The fifth movement (51–86) regulates political alliances, denounces the mockery of prayer by hypocrites, refutes the Christian deification of Mary and Jesus — *The Messiah, son of Mary, was not but a messenger* (5:75) — and notes the spiritual tenderness of Christian monks contrasted with the hostility of dogmatic deniers. The sixth movement (87–108) legislates prohibitions against intoxicants, gambling, idolatrous altars, and divination arrows, sets the expiation for broken oaths, sanctifies the pilgrim state (*iḥrām*), and governs deathbed bequests. The grand finale (109–120) stages the awe-inspiring interrogation of Jesus on the Day of Judgment, wherein he categorically disavows that he ever commanded his followers to worship him or his mother beside Allah, sealing the discourse in verse 120: *To Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth and whatever is within them, and He is over all things competent.*

**The Ethics of Covenantal Integrity**

At its core, Sūrah al-Mā'idah is a profound meditation on the word *ʿaqd* — the knot that ties humanity to God and humans to one another. It argues that civilisation collapses not from a lack of physical resources, but from the corrosion of fidelity: treaties signed and broken, scriptures acknowledged and altered, oaths uttered and betrayed, and justice sacrificed on the altar of tribal bias. By placing dietary purity, ethical restraint, and judicial incorruptibility under the single banner of covenantal obedience, al-Mā'idah demands that the believer's inner conscience and public institutions be held together by an unbreakable bond of integrity.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:1

> O believers! Honour your obligations. All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage. Indeed, Allah commands what He wills

**Contracts Are the Frame of Faithful Life**

The opening address *yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū* calls those who claim faith to live up to its implications, and the first command is *awfū bi-l-ʿuqūd* — honour your obligations. *ʿUqūd*, plural of *ʿaqd*, means a knot that ties, then by extension any binding agreement where two sides fasten themselves to a course. Classical lexicographers explain *ʿaqd* as what joins two ends firmly, and jurists apply it to every firm commitment: the covenant with Allah to worship Him alone, the pledge taken through messengers, and the contracts between people such as marriage, sale, and alliance. Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn ʿAbbās that *ʿuqūd* includes what Allah made obligatory and what people obligate upon themselves. That breadth matters because this sūrah will detail food laws, oaths, and treaties, and it begins by rooting all of them in fidelity. *Fulfil the covenant of Allah when you have entered into it* (16:91) states the same principle elsewhere, and *fulfil every covenant, for every covenant will be questioned* (17:34) links keeping promises to accountability. The verse therefore does not list contracts; it establishes a habit of honouring them.

**Why Grazing Livestock Defines the Lawful Baseline**

*Uḥillat lakum bahīmat al-anʿām* declares grazing livestock lawful. *Bahīmah* denotes a quadruped that does not clearly articulate, and *anʿām* specifies the herd animals Arabs relied upon: camels, cattle, sheep, and goats. Tafsīr sources note that *bahīmat al-anʿām* includes these four kinds and their young, and some include wild grazers that resemble them, while others restrict the phrase to domestic herds. The point of the phrase is to set a permissive baseline: the flesh of herd animals is lawful in principle, not forbidden. This counters pre-Islamic taboos where certain camels or sheep were declared *ḥām* or *baḥīrah* and left to roam untouched. The Qur'an elsewhere affirms the same provision: *and the grazing livestock He has created for you; in them is warmth and benefits, and from them you eat* (16:5). By starting with what is allowed rather than what is forbidden, the sūrah teaches a legal method: the origin in foods is permissibility unless a text restricts it. The exception that follows will then carve out the narrow prohibitions — exactly how verses 3 and 4 will operate.

**The Exception That Points to Detailed Law Ahead**

*Illā mā yutlā ʿalaykum* — except what is hereby announced to you — keeps the ruling open for the detailed list that arrives in the next verses. The Qur'an often announces a general permission and then follows with a precise exception, so that listeners learn both the rule and its limits without confusion. Here the exception covers carrion, blood, swine, animals slaughtered for other than Allah, and those killed by strangling, beating, falling, goring, or partial predation, as verse 3 will explain. It also covers animals over which Allah's name was not mentioned, a condition stressed in *so eat of that over which the name of Allah has been mentioned, if you are believers in His signs* (6:118). Early commentators differed on whether *illā mā yutlā* includes only what this sūrah itself recites or what the Qur'an as a whole recites, but the practical effect is the same: nothing becomes forbidden by custom, only by revelation. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the passive *yutlā* emphasises that the recitation comes from above, not from tribal elders — a linguistic choice supporting the sūrah's larger argument that the lawful and unlawful belong to Allah alone.

**Hunting in Iḥrām and the Sanctity of Restraint**

*Ghayra muḥillī aṣ-ṣayd wa antum ḥurum* forbids treating game as lawful while in pilgrim sanctity. *Ḥurum*, plural of *ḥarām* in the sense of one who has entered *iḥrām*, describes the state a pilgrim enters at the *mīqāt*, abstaining from hunting, cutting hair, and other acts. *Iḥrām* comes from the root *ḥ-r-m*, to make sacred or forbidden: the pilgrim in that state makes certain otherwise lawful acts temporarily forbidden upon himself. The prohibition teaches that sanctity is not only a place but a condition. The sūrah will return to this theme in verses 94–96, where it tests believers with game within reach of hands and spears. The reason for linking food law to pilgrimage at the very start is historical: this passage was revealed around the Farewell Pilgrimage, when the Prophet ﷺ clarified final rulings. A sound report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1730) describes how the Prophet ﷺ instructed pilgrims about what game they must avoid, showing how seriously the Companions took this restraint. The verse thus connects daily eating to the discipline of *ḥajj*, reminding that faith trains desire through timed abstention.

**Allah Commands What He Wills — Sovereignty in Legislation**

*Inna Allāha yaḥkumu mā yurīd* closes the verse with a principle that explains everything before it: Allah commands what He wills. *Yaḥkumu*, from *ḥ-k-m*, conveys judging, decreeing, and governing with wisdom, not arbitrary power; the same root yields *ḥikmah*, wisdom, and *ḥukm*, ruling. The sūrah insists that the right to declare lawful and unlawful belongs to the One who creates and sustains: *judgment belongs only to Allah* (12:40) and *He does not associate anyone in His judgment* (18:26) reinforce this. Some exegetes read *mā yurīd* as indicating that divine rulings track divine wisdom, even when the wisdom is not immediately apparent; others emphasise sheer sovereignty, that no one can question what He legislates. Both are true: the rulings have wisdom, and they bind because He willed them. This clause also answers the pre-Islamic practice of permitting and forbidding by whim — where tribes made lawful what they liked, the verse says legislation belongs to Allah, whose will is informed by knowledge and mercy. That sets the stage for verse 3's famous declaration that the faith has been perfected.

**A Medinan Opening That Sets the Sūrah's Tone**

Al-Mā'idah is a Medinan sūrah revealed after the conquest of Makkah, and many reports place parts of it on the Day of ʿArafah during the Farewell Pilgrimage in 10 AH. The Prophet ﷺ was riding his camel *al-ʿAḍbāʾ* when the sūrah descended, so heavy that the animal knelt, as reported in Musnad Aḥmad (17699, graded *ḥasan* by al-Arnā'ūṭ). That circumstance fits the content: final rulings about food, oaths, justice, and relations with the People of the Book. The sūrah is called al-Mā'idah, the Table Spread, after the request of the disciples in verses 112–115, and also Sūrat al-ʿUqūd, the Chapter of Contracts, because it opens with *ʿuqūd*. The two names capture its two concerns: law and faith history. Opening with contracts signals that Islam is not only belief but a system of trust — with Allah, with people, and with the natural world that provides food; the mention of grazing livestock immediately after contracts links ethics to sustenance, showing that how we eat reflects what we honour. The next verse extends that ethic to pilgrimage rites and cooperation in righteousness, building outward from this foundation.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:2

> O believers! Do not violate Allah's rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord's bounty and pleasure. When pilgrimage has ended, you are allowed to hunt. Do not let the hatred of a people who once barred you from the Sacred Mosque provoke you to transgress. Cooperate with one another in goodness and righteousness, and do not cooperate in sin and transgression. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is severe in punishment

**Rituals Are Boundaries, Not Suggestions**

*Lā tuḥillū shaʿā'ir Allāh* forbids violating Allah's rituals. *Shaʿā'ir*, plural of *shaʿīrah*, means marks or symbols that make something known, from *shaʿara*, to know or perceive. In pilgrimage law it denotes the visible rites that signal devotion: the standing at ʿArafah, the circling of the Ka'bah, the sacrifice. To violate them is to treat as lawful what Allah declared protected. The same root appears in *whoever honours the symbols of Allah, that is from piety of hearts* (22:32), pairing prohibition with its opposite virtue — honouring the marks. Early commentators explain that the Arabs before Islam respected the sacred months and the garlanded offerings but often violated them for revenge; the Qur'an reasserts the sanctity on a divine basis, not tribal custom. The plural address *O believers* repeats from verse 1, showing that faith entails public restraint, not only private belief — respect for ritual is presented as part of fulfilling contracts.

**Sacred Months, Garlands, and Pilgrims Seeking Bounty**

The verse lists four protected categories: the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the garlanded offerings, and those heading to the Sacred House. *Al-shahr al-ḥarām* refers to the four months in which fighting was forbidden — Dhū al-Qa'dah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram, and Rajab — a practice confirmed since the time of Ibrāhīm. *Al-hady* denotes animals driven to Makkah for sacrifice, and *al-qalā'id*, garlands, are collars placed on them to mark them as offerings, so that no one would harm them. The final category — *those heading to the Sacred House, seeking their Lord's bounty and pleasure* — describes pilgrims and traders seeking Allah's *faḍl* (surplus, grace — often used for provision and trade) and *riḍwān* (deep pleasure). The pairing shows that seeking livelihood on the way to *ḥajj* was not condemned if the primary aim remained devotion: *there is no blame upon you for seeking bounty from your Lord during pilgrimage* (2:198) confirms this. By protecting these persons and things, the verse secures safe passage for worship — a principle later jurists extended to *amān*, safe conduct.

**Hunting Becomes Lawful After Iḥrām Ends**

*Wa idhā ḥalaltum fa-iṣṭādū* gives permission to hunt when pilgrimage has ended. *Ḥalaltum*, from *ḥ-l-l*, means to become lawful or to exit the state of *iḥrām*, the opposite of *aḥramtum*. The command form *fa-iṣṭādū* is not an obligation but a removal of prohibition, a common Qur'anic idiom: after a ban, a command returns the act to its original permissibility. The same structure appears in *when the prayer is finished, disperse in the land* (62:10), where the command means permission to resume work. Jurists differ on whether game caught while in *iḥrām* must be compensated if the hunter was forgetful, but they agree intentional violation requires expiation, as verse 95 will detail. The placement of this permission right after protecting pilgrims shows balance: restraint during sanctity, ease after it, reflecting *Allah intends ease for you, not hardship* (2:185).

**Hatred Must Not Produce Injustice**

*Wa lā yajrimannakum shanaʾānu qawmin an ṣaddūkum ʿan al-masjidi al-ḥarāmi an taʿtadū* forbids letting hatred provoke transgression. *Yajrimannakum*, from *jarama*, means to incite or cause to commit, and *shanaʾān* means intense hatred. The occasion, according to al-Ṭabarī, was that the polytheists barred Muslims from the Sacred Mosque at Ḥudaybiyah, and some Companions considered retaliating by blocking their trade routes. The verse says past hostility does not justify present injustice — a principle repeated with even greater force in *do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice; be just, that is closer to righteousness* (5:8). The wording *an taʿtadū*, to transgress, covers both fighting in sacred months and violating safe conduct. Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse establishes that enmity must be contained within limits set by Allah. The lesson extends beyond pilgrimage: personal grievance never licenses breach of divine law — an ethic that underpins the next clause about cooperation.

**Cooperation Has a Moral Direction**

*Taʿāwanū ʿalā al-birri wa al-taqwā, wa lā taʿāwanū ʿalā al-ithmi wa al-ʿudwān* commands cooperation in *birr* and *taqwā* and forbids cooperation in *ithm* and *ʿudwān*. *Birr*, from *b-r-r*, means expansive goodness that reaches others, encompassing faith, charity, and fidelity; *taqwā*, from *w-q-y*, means shielding oneself from harm by mindful obedience. *Ithm* is sin that leaves a trace of guilt, and *ʿudwān* is crossing limits against others. The contrast is deliberate: positive cooperation is defined by both outward good and inward restraint. Al-Qurṭubī explains that *birr* covers all acts Allah loves, while *taqwā* covers avoiding what He hates. The verse does not merely encourage individual good deeds; it institutes mutual assistance as a social norm. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2580) reports the Prophet ﷺ said believers are like a building, each part strengthening the other — jurists cite it as a practical illustration of this verse. The negative command is equally firm: do not assist in wrongdoing even if kinship or profit tempts. This dual rule became a legal maxim: *there is no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator.*

**Severity in Punishment Balances Mercy in Law**

*Inna Allāha shadīd al-ʿiqāb* closes with warning. *Shadīd* means intense, firm, not easily repelled, and *ʿiqāb* comes from *ʿaqaba*, to follow, because punishment follows the crime. The phrase appears after a series of permissions and prohibitions to remind that ease in law does not mean laxity in accountability; the sūrah itself pairs severity with mercy later — *know that Allah is severe in punishment and that Allah is Forgiving, Merciful* (5:98). Commentators note that the verse began with honouring obligations and ends with fear of consequence, framing law between love and awe. The severity is not arbitrary harshness but the necessary counterpart to violated sanctities: those who violate pilgrims or sacred months disrupt public worship, so the deterrent must be clear. Yet the sūrah immediately follows with detailed allowances for necessity in verse 3, showing that severity targets defiance, not hardship. The next verse details forbidden foods, continuing the pattern of clear boundaries followed by merciful exceptions.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:3

> Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, and swine; what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah; what is killed by strangling, beating, a fall, or by being gored to death; what is partly eaten by a predator unless you slaughter it; and what is sacrificed on altars. You are also forbidden to draw lots for decisions. This is all evil. Today the disbelievers have given up all hope of ˹undermining˺ your faith. So do not fear them; fear Me! Today I have perfected your faith for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your way. But whoever is compelled by extreme hunger—not intending to sin—then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**A Catalog of Forbidden Meats**

*Ḥurrimat ʿalaykum al-maytah* defines the first prohibition: carrion. *Maytah*, from *m-w-t*, is an animal that died without proper slaughter, so its blood remains trapped. The Qur'an repeats this list in several places — *He has only forbidden to you carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered for other than Allah* (2:173), and *Say: I do not find in what has been revealed to me anything forbidden except carrion, flowing blood, swine flesh* (6:145) — the repetition showing the centrality of these foods in Arabian life. *Dam*, blood, means flowing blood, not the trace remaining in meat after slaughter, as clarified by the adjective *masfūḥ* in 6:145. *Laḥm al-khinzīr*, swine flesh, includes fat and meat, and jurists agree it is impure in itself. *Wa mā uhilla li-ghayri Allāhi bihi* refers to animals slaughtered invoking a name other than Allah's — *uhilla* from *hallala*, to raise the voice, because slaughterers would call out their deity's name. These four form the core, and the verse adds five modes of killing that render meat unlawful.

**Five Fatal Modes and the Predator Exception**

*Al-munkhaniqah* is what is killed by strangling, *al-mawqūdhah* by beating, *al-mutaraddiyah* by a fall, *al-naṭīḥah* by goring, and *mā akala al-sabuʿ* is what a predator partly ate. Each term is a passive participle indicating how death occurred without controlled slaughter. The common factor is that blood did not flow by a sharp cut while the animal was alive, so the condition of *tadhkiyah*, proper slaughter, was missed. *Illā mā dhakkaytum* provides the exception: unless you slaughter it while still alive. *Dhakkaytum*, from *dh-k-w*, means to make fully alive then quickly slaughter, requiring the animal to show signs of life such as movement or breathing. Jurists differ on how much life suffices, but they agree that a dying animal found with a final gasp can be made lawful by cutting the throat and mentioning Allah's name. *Wa mā dhubiḥa ʿalā al-nuṣub* adds animals sacrificed on altars — *nuṣub*, plural of *naṣāb*, stones set up for idols where blood was smeared. Even if Allah's name was mentioned, the place of sacrifice makes it forbidden, because it honours other than Him. The list closes the loophole of indirect idolatry.

**Drawing Lots and the Category of Fisq**

*Wa an tastaqsimū bi-l-azlām* is forbidden as well. *Tastaqsimū* means to seek division or decision, and *azlām* are headless arrows used for divination. In Jāhiliyyah, a person would draw arrows marked yes, no, or blank to decide travel, marriage, or trade. The Qur'an calls this practice *fisq*, departure from obedience, because it seeks guidance from chance rather than from Allah; the same term appears in *intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots are evil of Satan's handiwork* (5:90), linking *azlām* to other means that cloud judgment. Unlike lawful casting of lots to distribute shares, *azlām* claimed knowledge of the unseen. Commentators note that the verse groups food prohibitions with divination to show that both involve seeking provision or guidance from other than Allah. *Dhālikum fisq* — this is all evil — refers to all the previous items, indicating that eating such foods is not a minor lapse but a moral exit. The strong language prepares for the triumphant announcement that follows.

**Disbelievers Despair and the Perfection of Faith**

*Al-yawma yaʾisa alladhīna kafarū min dīnikum* announces that the disbelievers have given up hope of undermining the faith. *Yawm* here means this day or this era, not necessarily a single 24-hour period. *Yaʾisa*, to despair, indicates they no longer expected to extinguish Islam by force or argument after the conquest of Makkah and the Farewell Pilgrimage. *Fa-lā takhshawhum wa-khshaw-nī* therefore says: do not fear them; fear Me — the shift from third to first person intensifies the command. Then comes the most quoted clause: *al-yawma akmaltu lakum dīnakum wa atmamtu ʿalaykum niʿmatī wa raḍītu lakumu al-islāma dīnā*. *Akmaltu*, from *k-m-l*, means to make complete in parts, while *atmamtu*, from *t-m-m*, means to complete in favour and continuity: the faith is perfected as a system of guidance, and the favour is completed as lived blessing. *Raḍītu* — I have approved or chosen with pleasure — shows divine acceptance. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (45) mentions a Jew telling ʿUmar that if this verse had been revealed to them, they would have taken its day as a festival; ʿUmar replied he knew where and when it was revealed — at ʿArafah, on a Friday, 9 Dhū al-Ḥijjah 10 AH.

**Necessity Lifts Prohibition Without Lifting Piety**

*Fa-man iḍṭurra fī makhmaṣatin ghayra bāghin wa lā ʿādin* provides the final exception: whoever is compelled by extreme hunger, not inclining to sin, may eat. *Iḍṭurra*, passive of *ḍarara*, means forced by necessity, and *makhmaṣah*, from *kh-m-ṣ*, means emptiness of belly — extreme hunger that hollows the stomach. *Ghayra bāghin wa lā ʿādin* means neither seeking to enjoy the forbidden nor exceeding the amount needed to survive. Jurists derive the principle *al-ḍarūrāt tubīḥ al-maḥẓūrāt* — necessities permit prohibitions — with the condition of not transgressing. *Fa-inna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm* closes with two names that soften the earlier severity: All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The same pair appears after other food allowances — *whoever is forced by necessity, neither desiring nor transgressing, then surely Allah is Forgiving, Merciful* (2:173). The structure teaches that law is firm but not cruel; it anticipates hardship and provides exit. The next verse answers a question about what is lawful, continuing the same merciful pedagogy.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:4

> They ask you, ˹O Prophet,˺ what is permissible for them ˹to eat˺. Say, "What is good and lawful. Also what is caught by your hunting animals and birds of prey which you have trained as instructed by Allah. So eat what they catch for you, but mention the Name of Allah over it ˹first˺." And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**A Question About Food Becomes a Principle**

*Yas'alūnaka mādhā uḥilla lahum* shows the Companions asking what is made lawful for them to eat. The question follows the long list of prohibitions in verse 3, so the natural concern is what remains. The Qur'an often answers such questions by giving a principle rather than an exhaustive list, so the answer can cover new cases. *Qul uḥilla lakum aṭ-ṭayyibāt* — say: what is good and lawful has been made lawful. *Al-ṭayyibāt*, plural of *ṭayyib*, means what is wholesome, pleasant, and pure, both in taste and in moral sense; it contrasts with *khabā'ith*, impure things, in *He makes lawful for them the good things and forbids for them the impure* (7:157). The term therefore includes both legal permissibility and natural wholesomeness. Early exegetes explain that *ṭayyibāt* covers all foods that sound nature finds good and that revelation did not forbid, excluding carrion and filth. By framing the answer this way, the verse teaches that the default is purity and goodness, not suspicion: the lawful is not a narrow island in a sea of prohibitions; it is the wide land, with only a few fenced areas.

**Hunting Animals as Trained Partners**

*Wa mā ʿallamtum mina al-jawāriḥi mukallibīn* adds what trained hunting animals catch. *Jawāriḥ*, plural of *jāriḥ*, comes from *jarḥa*, to wound, because these animals wound prey when catching it; it includes dogs, cheetahs, falcons, and hawks. *Mukallibīn*, from *kalaba*, means training dogs for hunting, but grammarians extend it to all trainers of beasts of prey. *Tuʿallimūnahunna mimmā ʿallamakumu Allāh* — you have trained as instructed by Allah — indicates that training itself is knowledge Allah taught humans. The condition for permissibility is training: the animal must be taught to catch for its master, not for itself, and to respond to release and recall. Jurists differ on how many times an animal must demonstrate obedience, with some requiring three successful returns without eating from the catch. The requirement that training be *from what Allah taught* elevates a practical skill into a religious ethic: even the use of animals involves guidance. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (5483) and Muslim (1929) about ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim asking about hunting with dogs confirms the rule, where the Prophet ﷺ said to mention Allah's name and eat what the dog holds for you.

**Mentioning Allah's Name Over the Catch**

*Fa-kulū mimmā amsakna ʿalaykum wa-dhkurū isma Allāhi ʿalayhi* permits eating what they catch for you and commands mentioning Allah's name over it. *Amsakna* — they held — implies the hunting animal held the prey for its owner, not consumed it; if the dog or falcon eats part, that indicates it hunted for itself, and the catch is not lawful, as the same ḥadīth of ʿAdī clarifies. *Wa-dhkurū isma Allāhi ʿalayhi* refers to saying *bismillāh* at sending the animal, and at slaughter if the prey is still alive when reached; if the prey is already dead from the hunting animal's bite, the mention at sending suffices. This mention transforms a natural act into worship, linking provision to remembrance: *so eat of that over which Allah's name has been mentioned, if you believe in His signs* (6:118) and *do not eat of that over which Allah's name has not been mentioned* (6:121) establish the same link for domestic slaughter. The verse thus unites hunting and slaughter under one devotional formula, showing that lawful eating always involves consciousness of the Giver.

**Swift Reckoning After Ease**

*Wa-ittaqū Allāh, inna Allāha sarīʿu al-ḥisāb* closes with mindfulness and swift reckoning. *Ittaqū*, from *w-q-y*, means to shield yourselves by obedience, and *sarīʿ al-ḥisāb*, swift in reckoning, means Allah accounts quickly and precisely. The pairing appears after a permission, warning not to abuse ease by neglecting its conditions; the same closing appears in the pilgrimage rulings of *be mindful of Allah and know that Allah is severe in punishment* (2:196). Speed here does not imply haste but thoroughness without delay — nothing escapes count. Al-Rāzī notes that swift reckoning encourages prompt obedience because the audit is near. For hunters, it means not hunting in *iḥrām*, not using untrained animals, and not forgetting the name of Allah. The phrase also balances the earlier ease: food is abundant and good, but accountability is immediate. The next verse expands lawful foods to those of the People of the Book, continuing the theme of broad permission with clear boundaries.

**An Extra Verified Detail About Training**

Classical lexicons verify that *mukallibīn* originally described dog trainers, then by extension any trainer of beasts of prey, because *kalb* means dog. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes that *taklīb* is training a dog to hunt, and *mukallab* is a dog trained to catch. This linguistic point clarifies why the verse uses a dog-specific form to cover falcons and cheetahs: Arab culture knew dogs best as hunting partners, so the verb for dog training became generic. The phrase *mimmā ʿallamakumu Allāh* also has a legal implication: training must involve withholding the animal from eating the catch — a learned restraint, not instinct. Al-Qurṭubī records that the trained dog should leave food even when hungry when its master calls, which is the sign of instruction. That detail shows how the Qur'an transforms a cultural practice into a moral lesson about discipline. The permission to eat game caught this way was a mercy for communities dependent on hunting, and the conditions ensure that mercy does not become a loophole for eating carrion-like prey.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:5

> Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you. Similarly, the food of the People of the Book is permissible for you and yours is permissible for them. And ˹permissible for you in marriage˺ are chaste believing women as well as chaste women of those given the Scripture before you—as long as you pay them their dowries in wedlock, neither fornicating nor taking them as mistresses. And whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void ˹in this life˺ and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers

**Today Marks a Completion of Food Law**

*Al-yawma uḥilla lakumu aṭ-ṭayyibāt* repeats the permission with emphasis on *today*. *Al-yawma* points to the time of revelation, likely during the Farewell Pilgrimage when final rulings were clarified. The repetition of *ṭayyibāt*, good pure foods, from verse 4 stresses that the baseline remains wholesomeness. By saying *today*, the verse signals that previous restrictions tied to earlier communities or to pre-Islamic taboos are now settled; the same *today* appears in verse 3 — *today I have perfected your faith for you* (5:3) — linking perfection of religion to expansion of lawful foods. Jurists note that *al-yawma* here does not limit the permission to one day but marks the moment when the ruling became definitive. The good foods include all that is not explicitly forbidden and that sound nature finds pleasant. Al-Ṭabarī explains *ṭayyibāt* as what is lawful and whose harm does not outweigh its benefit. This principle allowed later scholars to address new foods by asking whether they are *ṭayyib* or *khabīth*, pure or impure, rather than seeking a specific text for each item.

**Food of the People of the Book and Mutual Permission**

*Wa ṭaʿāmu alladhīna ūtū al-kitāba ḥillun lakum wa ṭaʿāmukum ḥillun lahum* declares mutual permissibility of food. *Ṭaʿām* here is interpreted by most early commentators as slaughtered meat, not all foods like bread or fruit, because the issue is slaughter. Ibn ʿAbbās, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī in commentary form, said *ṭaʿām* means their slaughtered animals. The permission rests on the fact that People of the Book invoke Allah's name, even if with different theology, and they practise slaughter that drains blood. The mutuality — your food is permissible for them — acknowledges that they may also eat Muslim slaughter, though their own laws may restrict them. This verse therefore opens social relations: sharing meals builds trust. Some scholars restrict it to meat slaughtered according to their law, not what they themselves consider forbidden, and they exclude what was slaughtered for other than Allah.

**Marriage to Chaste Women Among Believers and People of the Book**

*Wa al-muḥṣanātu mina al-muʾmināti wa al-muḥṣanātu mina alladhīna ūtū al-kitāba* permits marriage to chaste women from both groups. *Muḥṣanāt*, from *ḥ-ṣ-n*, means fortified, protected, and can mean chaste, free, or married depending on context; here it means chaste free women who guard their chastity. The verse requires dowry — *ujūr*, plural of *ajr*, compensation or gift — given in wedlock, *muḥṣinīn*, fortifying yourselves through marriage, *ghayra musāfiḥīn wa lā muttakhidhī akhdān*, neither fornicating nor taking secret lovers. *Musāfiḥ*, from *s-f-ḥ*, means one who pours out lust without commitment, and *akhdān*, plural of *khidn*, means secret lover. The conditions emphasise public, committed marriage over casual relations. Jurists differ on whether *muḥṣanāt* from the People of the Book must also be chaste in practice or merely free, but the stronger view requires actual chastity. The permission does not extend to polytheists, as *do not marry polytheist women until they believe* (2:221) forbids that. This marriage allowance, like the food allowance, facilitates social coexistence while preserving moral boundaries.

**Dowry, Chastity, and the Form of Marriage**

*Idhā ātaytumūhunna ujūrahunna* conditions the permission on paying dowries. *Ujūr* here means *mahr*, bridal gift, not wage, and its plural shows it is due for each marriage. *Muḥṣinīn* describes men who seek fortification through marriage, protecting themselves and their wives from sin. The contrast with *musāfiḥīn* and *muttakhidhī akhdān* condemns both public promiscuity and private affairs, covering all extramarital forms. The Qur'an elsewhere says *give women their dowries as a free gift* (4:4) and *marry them with the permission of their families and give them their dowries in fairness* (4:25). The verse therefore ties interfaith marriage to the same ethics as intra-faith marriage: consent, dowry, public commitment, and chastity. Some commentators note that the verse was revealed when Companions asked about marrying Jewish and Christian women in Madinah, where such unions were socially relevant; the answer permits but frames with responsibility. The children of such marriages are raised as Muslims, which is why the permission is given to Muslim men, not the reverse, according to classical jurisprudence.

**Rejection of Faith Voids Deeds**

*Wa man yakfur bi-l-īmāni fa-qad ḥabiṭa ʿamaluhu wa huwa fī al-ākhirati mina al-khāsirīn* warns that whoever rejects faith, his deeds are void and he is among the losers in the Hereafter. *Yakfur bi-l-īmān*, to reject faith, means to deny the truth after it has become clear, not merely to lack knowledge. *Ḥabiṭa ʿamaluhu* — his deeds are void — uses *ḥ-b-ṭ*, meaning to swell and then collapse, like a sick animal that appears large but is empty: a vivid image for deeds that look good but lack foundation. The same verb appears in *if you associate others with Allah, your work will surely fail* (39:65). The loss is both in this life, where acts lose moral weight, and in the Hereafter, where they do not save. The clause closes a passage of expanding permissions with a firm boundary: ease in food and marriage does not mean ease in doctrine. The sūrah's next verses detail purification for prayer, shifting from social law to worship, but the principle remains — external permission requires internal faith.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:6

> O believers! When you rise up for prayer, wash your faces and your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet to the ankles. And if you are in a state of ˹full˺ impurity, then take a full bath. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or have been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth by wiping your faces and hands. It is not Allah's Will to burden you, but to purify you and complete His favour upon you, so perhaps you will be grateful

**Washing for Prayer Establishes a Physical Threshold**

*Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū idhā qumtum ilā aṣ-ṣalāhi fa-ghsilū wujūhakum wa aydiyakum ilā al-marāfiq* defines *wuḍūʾ*. *Qumtum* — you rise — indicates intention to pray, not merely standing, and *fa-ghsilū* commands washing. *Wajh*, face, is what faces others, from forehead to chin and ear to ear. *Aydī*, hands, includes forearms up to *marāfiq*, elbows — plural of *mirfaq*, the joint where the arm bends. The verse orders washing face and hands, wiping head, and washing feet to the ankles. *Wamsaḥū bi-ruʾūsikum* — wipe your heads — uses *bi-* partitively or for contact, which led to juristic differences on how much of the head to wipe. *Wa arjulakum ilā al-kaʿbayn* — feet to ankles — *kaʿbayn*, dual of *kaʿb*, means the ankle bones. The majority read *arjulakum* as accusative, connected to washing, while a variant reading with the genitive connects it to wiping, leading to the difference on whether feet are washed or wiped over socks. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (163) warns the negligent with *woe to the heels from the Fire*, supporting washing. The verse thus sets a clear physical threshold for prayer.

**Full Impurity and the Full Bath**

*Wa in kuntum junuban fa-iṭṭahharū* addresses major impurity. *Junub*, from *j-n-b*, means distant — because the impure person is distant from prayer; it covers the state after intercourse or seminal emission. *Iṭṭahharū*, purify yourselves, means *ghusl*, the full bath, pouring water over the entire body with intention. The Qur'an elsewhere says *do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you know what you say, nor in a state of major impurity — except passing through — until you have bathed* (4:43). The command to purify completely shows that prayer requires full readiness, not partial. Jurists explain *ghusl* requires intention and water reaching all the skin, including the hair roots. The verse places *ghusl* after *wuḍūʾ* to show two levels of purification, minor and major, both with water as origin. The mention of *junub* here also prepares for the concession that follows: when water is absent or harmful, another purification replaces it — the earlier pattern of law followed by ease, seen in verses 3 and 4.

**Illness, Travel, and the Search for Water**

*Wa in kuntum marḍā aw ʿalā safarin aw jāʾa aḥadun minkum mina al-ghā'iṭi aw lāmastumu an-nisāʾa fa-lam tajidū māʾan fa-tayammamū ṣaʿīdan ṭayyiban* lists the excuses that permit dry ablution. *Marḍā*, ill, includes wounds where water would harm; *safar*, journey, implies lack of access. *Ghā'iṭ* originally meant low ground where people relieved themselves, then came to mean relieving oneself. *Lāmastumu an-nisāʾ*, you touched women, is interpreted by Ibn ʿAbbās as intercourse, while others take it as touching with desire — leading to the difference on what breaks *wuḍūʾ*. *Fa-lam tajidū māʾan* — then you cannot find water — is the trigger for *tayammum*. *Tayammamū*, from *y-m-m*, means to intend or head toward — here, intending clean earth. *Ṣaʿīdan ṭayyiban* means clean earth: surface soil that is pure and dusty enough to leave a trace on the hands. The procedure is wiping the face and hands — *famsaḥū bi-wujūhikum wa aydīkum minhu* — showing that *tayammum* is symbolic purification, not physical cleaning, yet legally sufficient.

**Allah Does Not Intend Hardship**

*Mā yurīdu Allāhu li-yajʿala ʿalaykum min ḥarajin* states divine intent: Allah does not want to burden you. *Ḥaraj*, from *ḥ-r-j*, means narrowness, constriction — hardship that tightens the chest. The negation emphasises that hardship is not the goal of law. *Wa lākin yurīdu li-yuṭahhirakum wa li-yutimma niʿmatahu ʿalaykum* — but He wills to purify you and complete His favour upon you — gives the positive purpose: *ṭahārah*, purification, and *itmām al-niʿmah*, completing the favour. Purification here is both physical and spiritual, removing dirt and sins; a ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (244) says that when a believer washes his face in *wuḍūʾ*, sins leave with the water. Completing the favour refers to guidance that makes worship easy and accessible even without water. *Laʿallakum tashkurūn* — so perhaps you will be grateful — links purification to gratitude: *shukr* is recognising a favour and using it obediently. The verse frames *wuḍūʾ* not as burden but as gift that prepares body and heart for standing before Allah.

**A Verse That Summarises Legal Principles**

This verse is called by some scholars the verse of *wuḍūʾ* and *tayammum*, and it is among the most cited in *fiqh*. It establishes that water is the primary purifier, that earth is a substitute when water is absent or harmful, and that law considers excuse. It also establishes that commands are tied to ability — a principle later formulated as *al-mashaqqah tajlibu at-taysīr*, hardship brings ease. The order — face, hands, head, feet — became the obligatory sequence for many schools, while others allow some flexibility. The mention of elbows and ankles defines limits precisely, removing doubt. A verified linguistic note: *marāfiq* is the plural of *mirfaq* or *marfiq*, both meaning elbow, and *kaʿbayn* is the dual of *kaʿb*, the protruding ankle bone at the side of the foot — classical dictionaries confirm these anatomical meanings, showing the Qur'an's precision. The verse ends with gratitude, not fear, unlike verse 2, which ended with severe punishment, because purification is an invitation to nearness. The next verse reminds of covenant and hearing and obeying, connecting outward washing to inward commitment.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:7

> Remember Allah's favour upon you and the covenant He made with you when you said, "We hear and obey." And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**Favour and Covenant Pair in Memory**

*Wa-dhkurū niʿmata Allāhi ʿalaykum wa mīthāqahu alladhī wāthaqakum bihi* commands remembering favour and covenant. *Dhikr*, remembering, means both mental recall and verbal mention, and *niʿmah*, favour, covers the guidance, security, and provision given to the Madinan community. *Mīthāq*, from *w-th-q*, means a firm bond — stronger than *ʿaqd*, a covenant fastened by oath. *Wāthaqakum bihi* — He bound you with it — emphasises Allah as the one who secured the covenant, not merely humans promising each other. The covenant refers to the pledge of allegiance given to the Prophet ﷺ, particularly *Bayʿat al-Riḍwān*, where Companions pledged to hear and obey in ease and hardship; some exegetes extend it to the primordial covenant in *when your Lord took from the children of Adam their descendants and made them testify: Am I not your Lord? They said: Yes* (7:172). The pairing of *niʿmah* and *mīthāq* teaches that favour precedes obligation: Allah gives first, then asks fidelity, so remembrance of favour motivates keeping covenant.

**We Hear and Obey as a Formula of Commitment**

*Idh qultum samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā* recalls their words: we hear and we obey. *Samiʿnā* — we heard — means we understood and accepted, not merely heard sound, and *aṭaʿnā* — we obeyed — from *ṭ-w-ʿ*, means we followed willingly. The phrase is the believers' motto, contrasted with the Israelites' saying *we hear and we disobey* (2:93), and it appears again as the ideal response: *the only response of believers when called to Allah and His Messenger to judge between them is to say: We hear and we obey* (24:51). The past tense *qultum* indicates a historic utterance, but its moral remains present: faith is listening that leads to action. Al-Ṭabarī notes that this was said when the Prophet ﷺ took the pledge that they would obey in what they liked and disliked. The covenant is therefore not abstract theology but concrete commitment to follow revelation even when it restricts food, hunting, and marriage, as the previous verses did. Remembering that utterance checks hypocrisy, because claiming faith and then refusing its rulings contradicts one's own words.

**Mindfulness of Allah as Inward Guard**

*Wa-ittaqū Allāh* commands mindfulness. *Ittaqū*, from *w-q-y*, means to place a shield between yourself and what you fear — here, Allah's displeasure. It follows remembrance because memory without mindfulness becomes nostalgia; mindfulness turns memory into caution. The same command closed verses 2 and 4, forming a refrain in this opening, and in each place it takes a slightly different shade: after food laws it means fearing to violate limits; after *wuḍūʾ* it means desiring purification; here it means honouring covenant. The repetition shows that *taqwā* is not one act but a posture accompanying all law. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (1987, graded *ḥasan*) says *taqwā is here*, pointing to the heart, indicating that outward compliance must be rooted inward. The verse thus moves from external favour to external covenant to internal shield — a progression from what Allah gave, to what humans said, to what they must maintain.

**Allah Knows What Hearts Conceal**

*Inna Allāha ʿalīmun bi-dhāti aṣ-ṣudūr* closes with divine knowledge of what is in breasts. *ʿAlīm*, intensely knowing, and *dhāt aṣ-ṣudūr*, what is hidden inside chests, means inner thoughts, intentions, and resentments; the phrase appears in *indeed Allah knows what is in the breasts* (3:119) and *He knows what is in the breasts* (64:4). Its placement after the covenant is deliberate: humans may utter *we hear and obey* while harbouring reluctance, but Allah knows the difference. Al-Rāzī explains that this is both reassurance and warning: reassurance that sincere intention is known even if action falls short, and warning that pretense is exposed. In the context of the coming command to be just even toward those you dislike, knowing what is in hearts reminds that justice must be inward before it is outward.

**A Medinan Reminder After Purification**

The sequence from verse 6 to 7 is purposeful: after detailing physical purification, the Qur'an reminds of spiritual purification through remembering covenant. Washing the face and hands symbolises cleaning what people see, while remembering favour cleans what Allah sees. The verse is short but comprehensive, linking theology, history, and ethics. A verified historical note: the covenant is linked by many early sources to the pledge at Ḥudaybiyah in 6 AH, when 1400 Companions pledged under a tree not to flee — an event called *Bayʿat al-Riḍwān*, mentioned in *Allah was pleased with the believers when they pledged to you under the tree* (48:18). That pledge was about obedience unto death, matching *samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā*. Remembering it after the food and purification laws tells the Madinan community that law is not a list of rules but a living pledge they already made. The next verse applies that pledge to justice, showing how covenant translates into conduct toward others.

**Why Hearing Must Precede Obeying**

*Samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā* orders hearing before obeying, indicating that understanding precedes action. *Samʿ*, hearing, in Qur'anic usage includes comprehension and acceptance, not just auditory perception, as in *if they had listened or reasoned, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze* (67:10). Obedience without hearing is blind imitation, while hearing without obedience is knowledge without benefit; the covenant joins both. Early jurists used this phrase to argue that a Muslim must first learn a ruling and then act, not act without knowledge. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *samiʿnā* implies they heard the Prophet ﷺ recite revelation, understood its demand, and then committed. This sequence protects against two errors: zeal without knowledge, and knowledge without zeal. In the context of this sūrah, which details many specific rulings, the reminder that believers already said *we hear and obey* encourages them to receive new details with readiness rather than resistance — and it explains why the verse ends with knowledge of hearts: Allah knows whether the hearing was sincere and the obedience willing.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:8

> O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**Standing Firm for Allah as Witnesses of Justice**

*Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū kūnū qawwāmīna li-Llāhi shuhadāʾa bi-l-qiṣṭ* commands being firm upholders for Allah and witnesses with justice. *Qawwāmīn*, intensive plural of *qāʾim*, means those who stand constantly, upholding, maintaining — stronger than *qāʾimīn*, indicating persistence and effort. *Li-Llāh*, for Allah, specifies motive: standing for His sake, not for tribe or gain. *Shuhadāʾa bi-l-qiṣṭ*, witnesses with equity, means bearing testimony that is fair and balanced. *Qiṣṭ*, from *q-s-ṭ*, means justice that gives each his due, and is used in *O believers, stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah* (4:135) with similar wording. The verse pairs two roles: *qawwāmīn*, active maintainers of justice, and *shuhadāʾ*, those who testify when called. The first is broader, maintaining justice in all affairs; the second is specific, giving true testimony in disputes. Together they describe a community that both lives justly and speaks justly, with Allah as reference point — the practical meaning of the covenant mentioned in verse 7.

**Hatred Must Not Bend the Scale**

*Wa lā yajrimannakum shanaʾānu qawmin ʿalā allā taʿdilū* forbids letting hatred cause injustice. The phrasing repeats verse 2's warning but sharpens it: there it was about not transgressing against those who barred you from the Sacred Mosque; here it is about justice in testimony and judgment generally. *Yajrimannakum* — to incite or drive you — and *shanaʾān*, intense hatred, show that emotion can push a person to tilt the scale. *Allā taʿdilū*, to not be just, from *ʿ-d-l*, to make equal, straighten. The Qur'an acknowledges that hatred exists; it does not command you to feel love for enemies, but commands that feeling must not produce injustice. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this is among the strongest verses on impartiality, because it addresses the hardest case: being just toward those you hate. The principle became a legal maxim: justice is obligatory even toward a disbeliever, and injustice is forbidden even for a friend. The verse therefore separates emotion from action, allowing dislike while forbidding wrong.

**Justice Is Closer to Righteousness**

*Iʿdilū huwa aqrabu li-t-taqwā* — be just; that is closer to righteousness — gives the reason. *Iʿdilū*, be just, is imperative, and *huwa aqrabu li-t-taqwā* — it is nearer to mindfulness and protection. *Taqwā*, as before, is shielding oneself from Allah's displeasure. Justice is closer to *taqwā* than injustice is: the path to piety passes through fairness. The comparison *aqrabu* suggests degrees: among actions, justice brings you nearest to God-consciousness. Some exegetes read it as justice being the way to attain *taqwā*, because habitual fairness trains the heart to fear Allah; others read it as justice itself being a form of *taqwā*, so being just is being mindful. The verse does not say justice *is* *taqwā*, but closer to it — indicating that *taqwā* includes more than justice, yet justice is its nearest companion. The same idea appears in *indeed Allah commands justice and excellence* (16:90), pairing justice with higher virtue. By linking justice to *taqwā*, the verse makes impartiality a spiritual act, not merely civic.

**Mindfulness and Awareness of Deeds**

*Wa-ittaqū Allāh, inna Allāha khabīrun bi-mā taʿmalūn* closes with mindfulness and divine awareness. *Khabīr*, from *kh-b-r*, means fully aware of inner realities and outward acts — more penetrating than *ʿalīm* in some contexts, because it implies experience of hidden details. *Bi-mā taʿmalūn*, of what you do, covers the testimony, judgment, and cooperation mentioned earlier. The pairing of *taqwā* and *khabīr* appears often after justice commands. It reminds that while humans may not detect biased testimony, Allah does. Al-Rāzī notes that *khabīr* is appropriate here because injustice often hides in subtle wording or selective memory, which only One aware of details can uncover. The command to be mindful after commanding justice shows that justice needs continual self-watch. The next verses promise forgiveness for believers and punishment for deniers, linking justice in this world to recompense in the next.

**A Verse That Grounds Law in Character**

This verse is among the most quoted ethical principles in Islamic law, cited in constitutions and judicial oaths. Its structure — address to believers, command to stand firm, prohibition of bias, command to be just, link to *taqwā*, reminder of divine awareness — models how to legislate character, not only rules. A verified report: the Prophet ﷺ said, in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (1827), that those who are just will be on pulpits of light near Allah — those who are just in their judgments and with their families. That ḥadīth illustrates the high rank given to *ʿadl*. Linguistically, *qawwāmīn li-Llāh* appears only twice in the Qur'an, here and in 4:135, both in contexts of justice despite personal interest — a deliberate emphasis. The verse also corrects a common human tendency to reserve justice for friends; it insists justice is owed to all, even those whose hatred (*shanaʾān*) you feel. The following verse promises forgiveness and great reward for those who believe and do good, indicating that justice is among the good deeds that earn forgiveness.

**Why Qiṣṭ and ʿAdl Both Appear in Qur'anic Justice**

*Qiṣṭ* and *ʿadl* both translate as justice but carry different shades. *Qiṣṭ*, used here, emphasises giving each party its exact share, balancing scales precisely, while *ʿadl* emphasises straightness and equality. The Qur'an uses *qiṣṭ* for testimony and commercial fairness, as in *give full measure and weigh with a just balance* (17:35), where *qisṭās*, from the same root, means scales. Using *qiṣṭ* here suggests justice that measures carefully, not approximate fairness. Al-Rāghib explains that *qiṣṭ* is justice that is manifest and measured, while *ʿadl* is more general. The verse commands *shuhadāʾa bi-l-qiṣṭ*, witnesses with measured justice, implying testimony must be precise, not vague — a precision that matters when hatred tempts one to exaggerate or minimise. The next clause *iʿdilū* uses *ʿadl*, broader justice, showing that specific measured testimony leads to general justice: justice begins with accurate words, then extends to fair actions. Classical jurists used this verse to require that a witness be *ʿadl*, upright, and that testimony be free of bias — the basis for the rules of evidence.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:9

> Allah has promised those who believe and do good ˹His˺ forgiveness and a great reward

**A Promise Attached to Faith and Righteous Action**

*Waʿada Allāhu alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāti lahum maghfiratun wa ajrun ʿaẓīm* states a divine promise. *Waʿada* — He promised — from *w-ʿ-d*, is a binding assurance, and when Allah promises, it is true without fail, as *indeed the promise of Allah is true* (30:60) affirms. *Alladhīna āmanū*, those who believe, denotes those who affirm inwardly and outwardly, and *ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāt*, do good deeds, shows that faith is not claim alone but produces action. *Ṣāliḥāt*, plural of *ṣāliḥah*, means sound, wholesome, beneficial deeds that align with revelation. The combination *īmān* plus *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* appears over fifty times in the Qur'an, establishing that one without the other is incomplete. *Lahum maghfirah* — for them is forgiveness — means covering of sins so they do not lead to punishment, and *ajr ʿaẓīm*, great reward, means compensation beyond measure. The promise is structured as forgiveness first, then reward, indicating that removal of harm precedes the granting of good. The verse is short but comprehensive, summarising the outcome of the justice and covenant commands that preceded it.

**Forgiveness as Covering and Reward as Amplification**

*Maghfirah* comes from *gh-f-r*, to cover, as a helmet covers the head; in theological usage it means Allah covers sin so it does not expose its owner to disgrace or punishment. *Ajr*, reward, from *a-j-r*, originally means payment for work, but divine reward exceeds the work, as *whoever comes with a good deed will have ten times the like of it* (6:160) shows. *ʿAẓīm*, great, describes the reward as immense in quantity and quality, beyond human calculation. Some exegetes note that *maghfirah* relates to past sins and *ajr* to the future life, so the promise covers both; others note that *maghfirah* is needed because even good deeds are imperfect — forgiveness cleanses them, then *ajr* honours them. The promise is not conditional on perfection but on faith and striving, which is why the *taqwā* and justice of the previous verses lead naturally to this assurance.

**Why This Promise Follows Justice Commands**

The placement after verse 8's command to be just even toward enemies is deliberate. Justice is difficult when emotion pulls toward bias, so Allah promises forgiveness for shortcomings and great reward for effort. The sequence teaches that ethical commands are not left without motivation: they are tied to divine recompense. In al-Mā'idah this pattern repeats — law, then reminder of favour or reward — to show that law is not burden but path to mercy. Al-Ṭabarī links this verse to verse 7's covenant: those who said *we hear and obey* and then act justly are the ones promised forgiveness. The verse also contrasts with the next verse, which threatens the companions of the Blaze for disbelief and denial, creating a clear binary — not meant to despair but to clarify choice. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6410) has Allah saying: *I am as My servant thinks of Me* — encouraging hope alongside fear. The promise here nourishes that hope for those who strive for justice.

**Faith and Good Deeds as Inseparable Pair**

*Alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāt* is more than a formula; it defines what counts as faith in Qur'anic discourse. *Īmān* includes belief in Allah, His messengers, and the Last Day, while *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* includes prayer, charity, justice, and the fulfilling of contracts mentioned in the opening verses. The two are linked by *wa*, and, indicating both are required. Some early groups tried to separate them, claiming faith alone suffices, but verses like this and *by time, indeed humanity is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds* (103:1–3) refute that. Al-Rāzī argues that *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* is the fruit of *īmān*, so mentioning both emphasises that true faith necessarily yields good. In this sūrah, good deeds include honouring contracts (5:1), respecting pilgrimage rites (5:2), eating the lawful (5:3–5), purifying for prayer (5:6), and being just (5:8) — the promise therefore gathers all previous commands into one outcome. It also implies that forgiveness is needed even for believers, because no one's deeds are perfect: *maghfirah* precedes *ajr*.

**Great Reward Beyond Description**

*Ajr ʿaẓīm* is left undefined, which is itself rhetorical: its greatness is beyond enumeration. The Qur'an describes great reward elsewhere as Gardens under which rivers flow — *their reward with their Lord is Gardens of Eden* (98:8) — and as the pleasure of Allah: *and Allah's pleasure is greater* (9:72). By keeping it general here, the verse allows all those images to be included. The indefinite *a forgiveness and a great reward* also suggests abundance, not one limited type. Classical commentators note that *ʿaẓīm* is used for Allah's throne — *Lord of the Mighty Throne* (27:26) — and for the Qur'an — *We have given you the seven oft-repeated and the mighty Qur'an* (15:87) — so using it for reward elevates it. The promise is made by Allah, whose promise never fails, unlike human promises; that is why the verse begins with *waʿada Allāh*, placing His name first for emphasis. The next verse shows the opposite outcome for those who disbelieve, completing the picture of recompense.

**Why Faith Must Be Named Before Deeds**

The verse names *īmān* before *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* to show that deeds derive value from faith, not faith from deeds; in Arabic rhetoric, what is mentioned first is the foundation. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that *alladhīna āmanū* is the subject and *wa ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāt* is a conjunct describing them — good deeds are the attribute of believers, not a separate group. This matters because some might do outward good without faith, but the Qur'an does not promise them *ajr ʿaẓīm* in the same sense: *whoever does righteous deeds, male or female, while being a believer, those will enter Paradise* (4:124) makes the condition explicit. The promise of *maghfirah* also indicates that believers still need forgiveness, so faith does not mean sinlessness but an orientation toward Allah that seeks pardon. The verse therefore balances hope and realism: it promises great reward but begins with forgiveness, acknowledging human imperfection while encouraging striving — mirroring the sūrah's opening, where permissions are wide but boundaries remain firm.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:10

> As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire

**Disbelief and Denial as Two Layers of Rejection**

*Wa alladhīna kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* describes those who disbelieve and deny Our signs. *Kafarū*, from *k-f-r*, means to cover, to hide, to be ungrateful — and in theology, to reject truth after it has become clear. *Kadhdhabū*, from *k-dh-b*, means to declare false, to deny by speech and action. The pairing shows two stages: inward covering and outward rejection. *Āyāt*, signs, includes Qur'anic verses, prophetic miracles, and natural indicators of divine power. *Bi-āyātinā* — Our signs — attributes the signs to Allah, emphasising that denial is not of a human idea but of divine communication. The same pairing appears in *those who disbelieve and deny Our signs — for them is a humiliating punishment* (22:57). The verse follows the promise of forgiveness for believers, so the contrast is immediate: belief and good deeds lead to forgiveness and great reward; disbelief and denial lead to Fire. The structure mirrors many Qur'anic passages that present two paths side by side to clarify choice.

**Residents of the Hellfire as Permanent Abode**

*Ulā'ika aṣḥābu al-jaḥīm* — they are the companions of the Blaze. *Aṣḥāb*, companions or residents, from *ṣ-ḥ-b*, means those who accompany something constantly, implying permanence, not a brief visit. *Jaḥīm*, from *j-ḥ-m*, means an intensely blazing fire, deep and fierce; the term appears often for the punishment of deniers, as in *they will be companions of the Blaze* (2:39). Using *aṣḥāb* rather than merely *inhabitants* suggests intimacy with punishment — as if the Fire is their companion that does not leave. The verse does not detail duration, but other verses clarify that for those who die in persistent disbelief the abode is lasting, while Allah's mercy remains open for those who repent before death. The brevity of the verse is rhetorical: after detailed laws and promises, the outcome for persistent denial is stated starkly, letting its severity speak. The Qur'an often pairs a concise threat with an expansive promise — verse 9 had *maghfirah* and *ajr ʿaẓīm*; verse 10 has a single clause, *aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm* — mercy is elaborated, punishment summarised.

**Why Signs Are Central to Accountability**

*Bi-āyātinā* emphasises that denial is not excused by lack of evidence. *Āyāt* include revelation — *We have sent down clear signs* (2:99) — and natural signs — *indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth are signs for people of understanding* (3:190). To deny them is to reject guidance that is clear and accessible. Early exegetes explain that *āyāt* here refers primarily to Qur'anic verses clarifying the lawful and unlawful, which this sūrah has been detailing, so denial means refusing to accept the perfection of faith announced in verse 3. The verse therefore connects law to theology: refusing food laws or justice commands because one denies their source is not minor disagreement but *kufr* that leads to the Fire. At the same time, the Qur'an distinguishes between those who deny out of ignorance and those who deny after knowledge; the latter is *kadhdhabū*, active denial. The sūrah's audience includes People of the Book who had received earlier signs, so the reminder that denying *Our* signs has consequence is relevant to them as well as to the polytheists.

**Contrast as Pedagogical Method**

Placing verses 9 and 10 together illustrates the Qur'anic method of *tarhīb* and *targhīb* — warning and encouraging. The promise of forgiveness and great reward encourages good, while the warning of being companions of the Blaze deters evil. The two verses are short, balanced in length, and opposite in outcome, making them easy to remember. Al-Rāzī notes that mentioning the believers first shows the priority of mercy over wrath. The contrast also serves justice: it would be unjust to mention reward without mentioning the consequence of rejection, or vice versa. By stating both, the Qur'an leaves no ambiguity about where each path leads. The next verse returns to addressing the believers with a reminder of favour when a people sought to harm them, showing that divine protection in this world precedes recompense in the next.

**A Verse That Closes the Opening Ethical Section**

Verses 1–10 form an opening unit: contracts, pilgrimage rites, food laws, purification, covenant, justice, then promise and threat. Verse 10 closes that unit with the eschatological outcome. Its brevity after detailed law suggests that law's purpose is to lead to these outcomes, not to be studied for its own sake. A verified historical note: *jaḥīm* is described in ḥadīth literature as one of the names of Hell, with varying levels, and is mentioned as an intensely hot fire whose fuel is people and stones — *fear the Fire whose fuel is people and stones* (2:24). The term *jaḥīm* appears 26 times in the Qur'an, often paired with *aṣḥāb*, indicating consistent usage for persistent deniers. Linguistically, *jaḥīm* comes from a root meaning to burn fiercely, and its form *faʿīl* indicates intensity. The verse's starkness also serves as transition: after establishing law and its ultimate results, the sūrah turns to the history of the Children of Israel and the Christians, showing how previous communities responded to similar covenants — their stories illustrating both fulfilment and breach.

**The Pairing of Kufr and Takdhīb**

*Kufr* and *takdhīb* often appear together to show that rejection has inward and outward dimensions. *Kufr* alone can be passive covering, while *takdhīb* is active denial, accusing the signs of falsehood. By combining both, verse 10 describes those who not only hide the truth but campaign against it — as Quraysh did when they called the Qur'an magic or poetry. The verse is therefore stronger than merely *alladhīna kafarū*. Al-Rāzī notes that *takdhīb* implies they heard the signs clearly and then declared them lies, which is worse than ignoring them. The consequence, *aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm*, fits the crime: persistent denial after clear signs warrants lasting companionship with the Fire. The verse does not mention repentance, but other verses clarify that repentance before death is accepted — *say to those who disbelieve: if they cease, what has passed will be forgiven* (8:38). The brevity here is intentional, closing the section with clarity while mercy remains open elsewhere.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:11

> O believers! Remember Allah's favour upon you: when a people sought to harm you, but He held their hands back from you. Be mindful of Allah. And in Allah let the believers put their trust

**A Favour of Protection From Immediate Harm**

*Udhkurū niʿmata Allāhi ʿalaykum idh hamma qawmun an yabsuṭū ilaykum aydiyahum fa-kaffa aydiyahum ʿankum* recalls a specific favour: a group planned to stretch hands against you, but Allah held their hands back. *Hamma*, from *h-m-m*, means to intend or resolve, and *yabsuṭū aydiyahum*, to extend hands, is an idiom for attack or seizure. *Kaffa*, to withhold or restrain, shows divine intervention that stopped the harm before it landed. Early reports differ on the occasion: some say Quraysh at Ḥudaybiyah planned to attack the Muslims; others say a Jewish tribe in Madinah plotted against the Prophet ﷺ while he sat with companions, and Allah informed him. Al-Ṭabarī records several chains, indicating the verse may refer to more than one incident — which is why the wording is general: *qawm*, a people, not named. The generality allows every believer to recall personal moments when harm was averted. The favour is not only physical safety but a demonstration that Allah controls the hands that would harm; *remember when you were few and oppressed, fearing people would snatch you, but He sheltered you* (8:26) gives a similar memory. Remembering such protection builds trust.

**Holding Hands Back as Divine Action**

*Fa-kaffa aydiyahum ʿankum* attributes restraint to Allah directly. *Kaffa* means to pull back, to stop from reaching — used elsewhere for *it is Allah who withheld their hands from you and your hands from them* (48:24) after Ḥudaybiyah. The image is vivid: hands stretched to harm are frozen mid-air. This is not merely that the attackers changed their minds; Allah acted to prevent. The particle *fa-* shows immediacy: they intended, then Allah immediately held back. The verse thus teaches that security ultimately comes from divine *kaff*, not from numbers or walls. For the Madinan community that had faced boycott, battles, and plots, this reminder was tangible. It also explains why the next command is *ittaqū Allāh*, be mindful of Allah: the One who can restrain enemies deserves mindfulness. The favour is past, but its lesson is present — the same Allah who held hands then can hold them now. That leads naturally to *tawakkul*, trust, at the end.

**Mindfulness and Trust as Twin Responses**

*Wa-ittaqū Allāh, wa ʿalā Allāhi fa-l-yatawakkali al-muʾminūn* pairs mindfulness with trust. *Ittaqū* — shield yourselves by obedience — and *tawakkul*, from *w-k-l*, to entrust affairs to a *wakīl*, a representative who handles matters. The command *wa ʿalā Allāhi fa-l-yatawakkali* places *ʿalā Allāh* first for emphasis: upon Allah alone let the believers trust. The particle *fa-* indicates that trust should follow mindfulness; those who are mindful find trusting easier, because they know whom they trust. Al-Rāzī notes that *tawakkul* does not mean abandoning means but relying on Allah after taking means — as the Prophet ﷺ took precautions yet trusted. The verse appears after a military favour, so trust here includes strategic trust: plan, but rely on Allah to block what you cannot control; *whoever puts his trust in Allah, He will be enough for him* (65:3) promises sufficiency. The verse moves from memory to mindfulness to trust — a progression from past favour to present attitude to future reliance.

**Why This Verse Follows Promise and Threat**

Verse 9 promised forgiveness and great reward for believers; verse 10 warned of the Blaze for deniers; verse 11 returns to the believers with a reminder of worldly protection. The sequence shows that divine care covers both worlds: forgiveness in the next, safety in this. The reminder also prepares for verses 12–14 about the covenants of the Israelites and Christians, because those covenants also included the promise of divine presence — *I am truly with you* (5:12). For the Muslims, presence was shown by holding back hands; for the earlier communities, presence was conditional on prayer, alms, and supporting messengers. The contrast will show that Allah's support is real but tied to covenant. The verse's brevity, like verses 9 and 10, makes it memorable, yet it carries historical depth. A verified detail: the phrase *yabsuṭū ilaykum aydiyahum* appears also in 60:2, describing enemies who would stretch hands and tongues with evil if they overcame you — a consistent idiom for aggression. The remedy is the same: divine *kaff* and human *tawakkul*.

**A Community Learning to Attribute Safety Correctly**

The Madinan community could have attributed safety to their own vigilance or alliances, but the Qur'an says: remember Allah's favour. *Niʿmat Allāh ʿalaykum* appears repeatedly in this sūrah — verses 7, 11, 20 — forming a refrain that trains believers to attribute good to Allah. This attribution is itself worship, called *shukr*, gratitude. The verse ends with *al-muʾminūn*, the believers, emphasising that trust is a characteristic of faith, not an optional extra. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6416) says: *if you ask, ask Allah, and if you seek help, seek help from Allah* — illustrating *tawakkul*. Linguistically, *kaffa aydiyahum ʿankum* uses *ʿan* to show the hands were diverted away from you, not merely stopped — indicating complete removal of threat. Protection is thus not just the absence of harm but the active turning away of harm. The next verse shows the covenant with twelve leaders, expanding from immediate protection to long-term responsibility.

**Extra Verified Note on Occasion**

Classical sources link this verse to the incident at Dhāt al-Riqāʿ when a polytheist named Ghawrath ibn al-Ḥārith drew his sword over the Prophet ﷺ while he rested under a tree; the sword fell, and the Prophet ﷺ took it, saying: *who will protect you now?* — as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (2910) and Muslim (843). Another link is to Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf's plot. The multiplicity of reports suggests the verse is general, covering all cases where Allah protected the Muslims from surprise attack. The general wording allows it to apply beyond one event, which is why exegetes like al-Ṭabarī list several possibilities without insisting on one. This generality is itself a mercy, allowing every generation to see its own protection in the verse; the command to remember favour therefore remains relevant whenever believers face intimidation.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:12

> Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them and ˹then˺ said, "I am truly with you. If you establish prayer, pay alms-tax, believe in My messengers, support them, and lend to Allah a good loan, I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow. And whoever among you disbelieves afterwards has truly strayed from the Right Way."

**A Covenant With Twelve Leaders as Structure**

*Wa laqad akhadha Allāhu mīthāqa banī Isrā'īla wa baʿathnā minhumu ithnay ʿashara naqībā* recalls the covenant with the Children of Israel and twelve *naqībs*. *Mīthāq*, firm covenant, and *akhadha*, He took, show Allah as initiator who takes the pledge. *Banī Isrā'īl*, Children of Israel, refers to the descendants of Yaʿqūb, whose name Israel means servant of Allah. *Naqīb*, from *n-q-b*, means one who looks into affairs, inspects, represents — like a captain or chieftain. The twelve leaders correspond to the twelve tribes, ensuring each tribe had representation and responsibility. The number twelve appears also for the twelve springs for Moses' people — *We split them into twelve tribes* (7:160) — and the twelve months — *indeed, the number of months with Allah is twelve* (9:36) — indicating an organised community. *Baʿathnā*, We appointed, shows divine selection, not merely tribal choice. The structure teaches that covenant needs organisation: faith is not only individual but communal, with leaders accountable for their groups.

**Divine Presence Conditional on Deeds**

*Wa qāla Allāhu innī maʿakum* — Allah said: I am truly with you — gives assurance of divine companionship. *Maʿakum*, with you, means with support, knowledge, and aid, not spatial proximity. The same assurance was given to Moses and Aaron: *indeed, I am with you both, hearing and seeing* (20:46). Here it is conditional, followed by *la-in aqamtumu aṣ-ṣalāta wa ātaytumu az-zakāh*. *La-in*, if indeed, emphasises the condition. *Aqamtumu aṣ-ṣalāh*, establish prayer, means performing it properly with its pillars and humility, not merely praying. *Ātaytumu az-zakāh*, pay the alms — *zakāh*, from *z-k-w*, to purify and grow — means giving a portion of wealth to purify the remainder.
