These two are pillars of practical faith. *Wa āmantum bi-rusulī wa ʿazzartumūhum* adds belief in the messengers and supporting them; *ʿazzartumūhum*, from *ʿ-z-r*, means to support with strength and honour, to prevent humiliation. *Wa aqraḍtumu Allāha qarḍan ḥasanā* — lend to Allah a good loan — means spending in His way, charity that will be returned multiplied; *qarḍ ḥasan* appears also in *who will lend to Allah a good loan so He multiplies it* (2:245).

**Forgiveness and Gardens as Reward for Covenant Fulfilment**

*La-ukaffiranna ʿankum sayyiʾātikum wa la-udkhilannakum jannātin tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār* promises forgiveness and entry to Gardens. *Ukaffiranna*, from *k-f-r*, to cover, here means to cover sins so they are erased, with the emphatic *nūn* for certainty. *Sayyiʾāt*, evil deeds, plural of *sayyiʾah*, misdeeds that accumulate. *Jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār*, Gardens under which rivers flow, is the standard Qur'anic image of Paradise, where water flows beneath, indicating abundance and permanence. The promise is conditional but assured with the double emphasis *la-…-anna*. This mirrors the promise to the Muslims in verse 9: forgiveness and great reward for faith and good deeds. The covenant with the Israelites therefore had the same core: prayer, alms, belief in messengers, support, charity — leading to forgiveness and Paradise. The difference is not in principle but in response, as the next verse will show the breach. The verse thus establishes the continuity of divine method across communities.

**Straying From the Right Way After Guidance**

*Fa-man kafara baʿda dhālika minkum fa-qad ḍalla sawāʾa as-sabīl* warns that whoever disbelieves after that has strayed from the right way. *Baʿda dhālika*, after that, means after the covenant and its clear conditions. *Kafara* here means rejecting or covering truth after knowing it. *Ḍalla*, strayed, from *ḍ-l-l*, to lose the way, and *sawāʾ as-sabīl*, the even or middle of the path, means the right way that is straight and balanced; the phrase appears in *whoever exchanges faith for disbelief has strayed from the right way* (2:108). The warning shows that after a covenant, disbelief is worse, because it is betrayal, not ignorance. The verse uses *minkum*, among you, addressing the Israelites directly, indicating that straying is individual responsibility even within a chosen community: the covenant was communal, but accountability is personal. This principle applies to Muslims as well, as verse 7 reminded them of their own covenant. The verse serves as both history and admonition: having leaders and promises does not guarantee success — fulfilment does.

**Continuity Between Communities**

This verse begins a historical section explaining why previous covenants were broken and why the new covenant with the Muslims matters. The twelve *naqībs* parallel the twelve chiefs among the Anṣār at ʿAqabah, showing a similar structure in early Islam. The conditions — prayer, *zakāh*, belief in messengers, support, the good loan — are the same as the Islamic pillars, indicating the continuity of religion. The difference lies in response: the Israelites broke the covenant, as verse 13 will say. A verified linguistic note: *naqīb* is also used for a leader who investigates and ensures compliance, and its plural *nuqabāʾ* appears in ḥadīth about the twelve leaders of the Anṣār; *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *naqīb* as one who is responsible for knowledge of his people and their affairs. The number twelve is thus not arbitrary but represents complete representation. The verse ends with the warning that straying after guidance is clear loss, preparing for the next verse's description of hardened hearts as the consequence of breach.

**Extra Verified Historical Detail**

The appointment of twelve leaders is linked to Moses selecting one leader per tribe before entering the Holy Land, as narrated in Numbers 13–14 and reflected in Qur'an 5:20–26. Al-Ṭabarī records that Moses ordered each *naqīb* to ensure his tribe's obedience. This historical context explains why the verse mentions the twelve before describing entry into the Holy Land in verse 21. The covenant's conditions also match Deuteronomy's calls to keep the commandments, support the prophets, and give charity. The Qur'anic retelling emphasises that the covenant was not merely law but relationship: *I am with you*. That phrase *maʿakum* appears for the Muslims in *indeed Allah is with those who are mindful and those who do good* (16:128), showing the same companionship offered to all who fulfil the conditions.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:13

> But for breaking their covenant We condemned them and hardened their hearts. They distorted the words of the Scripture and neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. You ˹O Prophet˺ will always find deceit on their part, except for a few. But pardon them and bear with them. Indeed, Allah loves the good-doers

**Breaking Covenant Leads to Curse and Hard Hearts**

*Fa-bi-mā naqḍihim mīthāqahum laʿannāhum wa jaʿalnā qulūbahum qāsiyah* explains the consequence: for breaking their covenant, We cursed them and made their hearts hard. *Bi-mā naqḍihim*, for their breaking — with the extra *mā* for emphasis — shows cause. *Naqḍ*, from *n-q-ḍ*, means to unravel a knot — the opposite of *ʿaqd* and *mīthāq*, which tie. *Laʿannāhum*, We cursed or condemned, from *l-ʿ-n*, means to expel from mercy and distance. *Qāsiyah*, hard, from *q-s-w*, means stern, unyielding, not soft to reminder. Hard hearts are described elsewhere: *then your hearts became hard after that, being like stones or even harder* (2:74), and *woe to those whose hearts are hardened against the remembrance of Allah* (39:22). The sequence is causal: breaking the covenant leads to curse, the curse manifests as a hard heart, and the hard heart leads to further sins like distorting words. The verse shows a moral psychology: betrayal hardens, and hardening enables more betrayal. The curse is not arbitrary; it is the natural outcome of persistent breach — a heart that repeatedly breaks promises loses its softness.

**Distorting Words and Forgetting a Portion**

*Yuḥarrifūna al-kalima ʿan mawāḍiʿihi* describes distorting words from their places. *Yuḥarrifūna*, from *ḥ-r-f*, means to change edge or side, to alter wording or meaning by shifting from its proper place. *Kalim*, words, refers to the Torah's words about the lawful and unlawful, and the descriptions of the Prophet ﷺ. *ʿAn mawāḍiʿihi*, from their places, indicates they moved words from the contexts where they were clear — by misreading, misinterpreting, or concealing. *Wa nasū ḥaẓẓan mimmā dhukkirū bihi* adds that they forgot a portion of what they were reminded of. *Nasū*, they forgot or neglected, and *ḥaẓẓ*, portion or share, shows they abandoned part of the guidance, not all — which made distortion easier, because partial knowledge is easier to twist. The same phrase appears for the Christians in the next verse: *they neglected a portion of what they were reminded* (5:14). The two sins are linked: forgetting leads to distorting, because when part of the reminder is lost, the remaining words are read without context; *they distort words after they have been placed properly* (2:75) describes a similar act.

**Persistent Deceit With Few Exceptions**

*Wa lā tazālu taṭṭaliʿu ʿalā khā'inatin minhum illā qalīlan minhum* tells the Prophet ﷺ: you will always find deceit among them, except a few. *Lā tazālu* — you will not cease — indicates continuity. *Taṭṭaliʿu*, you will come upon or discover, from *ṭ-l-ʿ*, to appear or overlook, means you will encounter. *Khā'inah*, deceit or treacherous act, is a feminine form indicating an act of betrayal. *Illā qalīlan minhum*, except a few among them, acknowledges a righteous minority, as *among the People of the Book is a community standing, reciting Allah's signs* (3:113) affirms. The verse therefore does not condemn all Israelites eternally but describes the prevailing pattern at the time of revelation. The deceit included plotting, breaking treaties, and misleading about scripture; *whenever they make a covenant, a party of them throws it away* (2:100). The exception *qalīlan* shows fairness: even in criticism, the Qur'an notes exceptions — a balance important for the coming command to pardon.

**Pardon and Forbearance Despite Deceit**

*Fa-ʿfu ʿanhum wa-iṣfaḥ* — pardon them and bear with them — commands forgiveness and overlooking. *ʿFu*, from *ʿ-f-w*, means to erase or pardon, as wind erases footprints, and *iṣfaḥ*, from *ṣ-f-ḥ*, means to turn away the page, to overlook. Together they mean forgive inwardly and show forbearance outwardly. This command was revealed when the Muslims had power in Madinah and could retaliate, yet were told to pardon. Some exegetes say this was before the permission to fight treacherous covenant-breakers, later qualified by verses about fighting those who break treaties; others say it remains the general ethic for dealing with People of the Book who do not fight. *Inna Allāha yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn* — Allah loves the good-doers — gives the motive: pardon is *iḥsān*, excellence. *Iḥsān* is doing good beyond obligation, as defined in the ḥadīth of Jibrīl in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (8): to worship Allah as if you see Him. The verse thus transforms political tension into spiritual opportunity: pardoning the deceitful is *iḥsān* that Allah loves.

**Hard Hearts as Warning for All Communities**

The description of hard hearts serves as a warning for the Muslims, who also have a covenant. The same disease can affect any community that breaks promises, distorts words, and forgets a portion of the reminder: *lest they become like those given the Book before, whose time was prolonged and whose hearts became hard* (57:16) — and the same verse prescribes the remedy: *has the time not come for believers that their hearts should soften to the remembrance of Allah?* The verse's placement after the mention of twelve leaders shows that structure alone does not protect; hearts must remain soft. A verified linguistic note: *qāsiyah* is an intensive form indicating hardness that is settled, not temporary, and is used for hearts that do not accept admonition. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes *qasā al-qalb* means the heart became hard and dry, lacking the moisture of mercy; the cure is *dhikr* and obedience, which bring the moisture back.

**Forgiveness as Strategy for Reform**

*Fa-ʿfu ʿanhum wa-iṣfaḥ* is not only a moral virtue but a practical strategy for reform. Pardon gives time for hard hearts to soften, while harshness would confirm their hostility. The Qur'an elsewhere commands: *repel evil with what is better, then the one who was your enemy becomes a close friend* (41:34). By commanding pardon after describing deceit, the verse teaches that the *muḥsinīn* are those who respond to betrayal with forbearance when forbearance can lead to guidance. Al-Ṭabarī records that the Prophet ﷺ pardoned many Jewish leaders after their plots were uncovered, hoping they would reflect. The love of Allah for the *muḥsinīn*, mentioned at the end, elevates pardon from weakness to excellence. The next verse shows that the Christians also neglected a portion, leading to enmity among themselves — extending the same pattern of breach and consequence across communities.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:14

> And from those who say, "We are Christians," We took their covenant, but they neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. So We let hostility and enmity arise between them until the Day of Judgment, and soon Allah will inform them of all they have done

**A Covenant Taken From Those Who Claim Christianity**

*Wa mina alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā akhadhnā mīthāqahum* begins like the previous verse but with distinct phrasing. *Min alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā* — from those who say we are Christians — notes the claim, not necessarily the reality, indicating that a name alone does not guarantee fidelity. *Naṣārā*, Christians, is from *naṣara*, to help, because they helped ʿĪsā — or from al-Nāṣirah, Nazareth. *Akhadhnā mīthāqahum*, We took their covenant, the same as with the Israelites, showing that the Christians also had a binding pledge: to follow the guidance brought by ʿĪsā and to believe in the coming messenger. The covenant included affirming *tawḥīd*, following the Gospel's call to worship Allah alone, and upholding the law. The Qur'an elsewhere says: *when Allah took the covenant of those given the Book: you must make it clear to people and not conceal it* (3:187). The Christian covenant therefore included not hiding truth and not exaggerating about ʿĪsā. The verse's wording *qālū*, they said, hints that the claim was more verbal than practical — which the next clause confirms.

**Neglecting a Portion of the Reminder**

*Fa-nasū ḥaẓẓan mimmā dhukkirū bihi* repeats the exact phrase from verse 13 about the Israelites: they forgot a portion of what they were reminded of. The repetition shows a pattern across communities: forgetting a portion leads to distortion and division. *Ḥaẓẓ*, portion, indicates they kept some and lost some — and the lost portion was enough to cause deviation. *Dhukkirū bihi*, they were reminded with it, is passive, showing the reminder came from Allah via messengers. For the Christians, the forgotten portion included pure monotheism and the prophecies of the final messenger, and for some it included the rulings that restrained exaggeration about ʿĪsā. The Gospel's central message of worshipping Allah alone was retained in words but neglected in interpretation, when some said *Allah is the Messiah* (5:17) or *Allah is the third of three* (5:73). The verse does not say they forgot everything, but a portion — which is more realistic and more dangerous, because partial retention allows the claim of continuity while the substance is altered.

**Hostility and Enmity as Consequence**

*Fa-aghraynā baynahumu al-ʿadāwata wa al-baghḍāʾa ilā yawmi al-qiyāmah* describes the result: We let hostility and enmity arise between them until the Day of Judgment. *Aghraynā*, from *gh-r-y*, means to incite, to cause to stick, to let cling — indicating that enmity was left to grow among them as the consequence of neglecting the reminder. *ʿAdāwah*, hostility, is outward enmity shown in fighting, and *baghḍāʾ*, hatred, is inward enmity in hearts — together covering inner and outer division. *Ilā yawmi al-qiyāmah*, until the Day of Judgment, indicates prolonged division, not a temporary dispute. History verifies it: Christian sects split over the nature of Christ — Jacobites, Nestorians, Melkites — and fought, and later Catholic, Orthodox, and Protestant divisions continued. The Qur'an notes similar division among the Israelites earlier, but here it emphasises that forgetting a portion of the reminder leads to internal enmity, not only curse. The cause is theological: when the core reminder about the unity of Allah is neglected, disagreements about secondary matters become irreconcilable, because no common foundation remains.

**Allah Will Inform Them of Their Deeds**

*Wa sawfa yunabbi'uhumu Allāhu bi-mā kānū yaṣnaʿūn* closes with future information: soon Allah will inform them of what they used to do. *Sawfa*, soon, indicates the near future relative to the Hereafter, and *yunabbi'uhum*, inform with news, from *n-b-ʾ*, to give important news, shows that the full reality of their deeds will be made clear. *Bi-mā kānū yaṣnaʿūn*, of what they used to do, includes both the neglect and the resulting enmity. The same closing appears in *Allah will inform them of what they did* (6:108-type usage) and *then to Allah is your return, and He will inform you of what you differed about* (5:48). The information on the Day of Judgment will be complete and undeniable, removing all dispute. The verse thus moves from covenant to neglect to division to final accounting — a pattern that also appeared for the Israelites, but with a different consequence: curse and hard hearts for one, hostility and hatred for the other. Both are forms of communal punishment for breach.

**Pattern of Covenant Breach Across Communities**

Verses 12–14 together form a unit showing that covenant is not ethnic privilege but responsibility. Twelve leaders for the Israelites, a covenant for the Christians — both with the condition of upholding the reminder. Both neglected a portion, leading to punishment that fits the crime: the Israelites distorted words and hardened, so curse and hard hearts; the Christians divided over theology, so enmity. The parallelism teaches the Muslims that their own covenant, mentioned in verse 7, also requires upholding all of the reminder, not a portion. A verified historical note: the term *naṣārā* appears fourteen times in the Qur'an, often with reference to their covenant and their claim of closeness to the believers, as in *you will find the closest in affection to the believers those who say we are Christians* (5:82) — the Qur'an distinguishes between the claim and the faithful among them. The warning about hostility until the Day of Judgment is observable in the Christian history of schisms, which began early and continue, fulfilling the description.

**Enmity Among Themselves as Divine Letting**

*Fa-aghraynā* indicates Allah let the enmity arise — not that He created hatred arbitrarily, but that He left them to the consequence of forgetting. *Aghrā*, to incite or cause to cling, is used when a person is left to what he chose; the same root appears in *We stirred enmity and hatred among them* (5:64) for another group. The letting is punishment that fits the neglect: they neglected the portion that would have united them, so they were left divided. The division is described as *ʿadāwah* and *baghḍāʾ*, outward hostility and inward hatred, covering both action and feeling. The phrase *ilā yawmi al-qiyāmah* shows longevity, and history confirms it: councils, excommunications, and wars among Christian groups persisted. The remedy is offered in the next verse: a Messenger who reveals what was hidden, and a light that guides to peace — directly countering the division. The next verse addresses the People of the Book directly, offering the remedy for forgetting.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:15

> O People of the Book! Now Our Messenger has come to you, revealing much of what you have hidden of the Scriptures and disregarding much. There certainly has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book

**Address to the People of the Book After the History of Breach**

*Yā ahla al-kitābi qad jāʾakum rasūlunā yubayyinu lakum kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna mina al-kitāb* calls the People of the Book directly. *Ahl al-kitāb* refers to the Jews and Christians given the Torah and Gospel. *Qad jāʾakum rasūlunā* — Our Messenger has come to you — with *qad* indicating already arrived, not a future expectation, and *rasūlunā*, Our Messenger, the possessive showing he is sent by Allah, not self-appointed. *Yubayyinu lakum*, revealing or making clear to you, from *b-y-n*, to separate, clarify, indicates his role is to clarify what was hidden. *Kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna mina al-kitāb* — much of what you used to hide of the Scripture — acknowledges concealment; *kuntum tukhfūna*, you used to hide, is imperfect, showing an ongoing practice of hiding the descriptions of the Prophet ﷺ, rulings like stoning, and monotheistic verses. The verse does not say he reveals everything hidden, but much — because some hidden matters are left as they are. *Wa yaʿfū ʿan kathīr* — and disregarding much — means he overlooks much that is not essential to clarify, showing mercy and focus. The balance between revealing and overlooking demonstrates prophetic wisdom: clarify what matters for guidance; ignore what would cause unnecessary dispute.

**Light and Clear Book as Dual Description**

*Qad jāʾakum mina Allāhi nūrun wa kitābun mubīn* announces that from Allah has come a light and a clear Book. *Nūr*, light, is interpreted as the Prophet ﷺ himself, or as Islam, or as the Qur'an; many exegetes say *nūr* is the Prophet ﷺ because he illuminates, while *kitābun mubīn* is the Qur'an that makes clear. Others say both refer to the Qur'an, which is both light and clear book. *Mubīn*, from *b-y-n*, means clear in itself and clarifying for others. The pairing of light and clear book appears elsewhere: *We have sent down to you a clear light* (4:174). Light guides in darkness; the book gives detailed instruction — together they provide both illumination and legislation. The phrase *mina Allāh*, from Allah, emphasises the source: this light is not human speculation but divine gift. For the People of the Book who had hidden parts of their books, the light reveals what was in shadow, and the clear book replaces distorted portions with preserved text. The verse therefore offers the remedy for the previous neglect: after forgetting a portion, a complete light now arrives.

**Revealing Hidden Scriptures as Prophetic Proof**

*Yubayyinu kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna* is also proof of prophethood, because an unlettered Arab could not know what Jewish and Christian scholars hid unless informed by Allah. Early reports mention that the Prophet ﷺ clarified that stoning is in the Torah, which some Jewish leaders tried to hide, as narrated in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635) about the stoning case brought to the Prophet ﷺ. He also clarified that the description of his coming is in their books: *those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find written in the Torah and the Gospel* (7:157). Revealing the hidden therefore serves two functions: correcting distortion and proving the messenger's truthfulness. *Wa yaʿfū ʿan kathīr* shows he does not expose every private concealment, focusing on what affects faith and law — a restraint that is itself prophetic character: not seeking to shame, but to guide. Al-Rāzī notes that *yubayyinu* is present tense, indicating ongoing clarification throughout the prophetic mission, not a one-time event.

**Why Light Precedes Book in Mention**

*Nūr* before *kitābun mubīn* may indicate that the personal example illuminates before the text is understood, or that the Qur'an itself is both light and book — light in guiding hearts, book in giving rulings. Light is needed to see the book, so mentioning light first is logical. The same order appears in *O people, proof has come to you from your Lord, and We sent down to you a clear light* (4:174). For the People of the Book, who had books but lacked the light to interpret them correctly after forgetting a portion, the new light is essential. The verse therefore addresses both scripture and interpretation: the clear book gives the correct text, the light gives the correct understanding. This dual gift addresses both previous diseases — distortion of words and forgetting of a portion. By sending a Messenger who is light and a book that is clear, Allah provides what the previous communities lost. The next verse describes how this light guides to ways of peace, showing the practical outcome of illumination.

**A Verse That Marks the Transition From History to Invitation**

Verses 12–14 narrated the breach; verse 15 offers the remedy. The address *yā ahla al-kitāb* is gentle, inviting, not condemning, coming after strong criticism — the Qur'anic method of critique followed by invitation. The verse is also among those that define the Prophet's role as *mubayyin*, clarifier, not only reciter: *We sent down to you the Reminder to make clear to the people what was sent down to them* (16:44) gives a similar role. A verified linguistic note: *mubīn* is an active participle meaning clarifying, not just clear, so *kitābun mubīn* is a book that clarifies truth from falsehood. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes *bāna* means both to become clear and to make clear. The verse's claim that the Messenger reveals the hidden was verified historically when Jewish scholars in Madinah acknowledged that the Prophet ﷺ knew matters they thought hidden, as recorded in the Sīrah.

**Revealing and Overlooking as Balanced Method**

*Yubayyinu kathīran* and *yaʿfū ʿan kathīr* show a balanced method: reveal much, overlook much. The balance prevents two extremes: exposing everything would humiliate; hiding everything would leave error. The Prophet ﷺ clarified what was essential for faith — monotheism, prophethood, the lawful — and overlooked details that were not central. Al-Qurṭubī notes that overlooking here means not holding them accountable for every concealment, but focusing on what leads to guidance. The same balance appears in the Qur'an's own storytelling: it mentions some details of previous books and leaves others. For the People of the Book, this balance is mercy: they are invited without being shamed for every hidden matter. The next verse details guidance to peace, continuing the invitation and showing the outcome of accepting the light.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:16

> through which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness and into light by His Will, and guides them to the Straight Path

**Guidance Through Light to Ways of Peace**

*Yahdī bihi Allāhu mani ittabaʿa riḍwānahu subula as-salām* describes guidance through the light and clear book. *Yahdī bihi* — He guides through it — with *bihi* referring to the *nūr* and *kitābun mubīn* of the previous verse. Allah as subject emphasises divine agency: guidance is from Allah, not from the book alone without His will. *Mani ittabaʿa riḍwānahu*, those who seek His pleasure — *riḍwān*, from *r-ḍ-w*, deep pleasure and acceptance — indicates that guidance requires seeking divine pleasure, not mere curiosity. *Subula as-salām*, ways of peace, is the plural of *sabīl* and *salām*, meaning paths that lead to peace, safety, and salvation. *Salām* is both peace and the name of Allah, *al-Salām*, and the abode of peace, *dār as-salām*, is Paradise. The plural *subul* indicates multiple ways: prayer, charity, justice, fulfilling contracts — all are ways of peace. *Allah invites to the abode of peace* (10:25) and *He guides to ways of peace* (5:16) show that peace is both path and destination. The verse therefore says the Qur'an guides the seekers of divine pleasure to all paths that produce peace in heart, family, and society.

**From Darknesses to Light by His Will**

*Wa yukhrijuhum mina aẓ-ẓulumāti ilā an-nūri bi-idhnihi* — brings them out of darknesses into light by His permission. *Ẓulumāt*, darknesses, is plural, indicating many darknesses: ignorance, disbelief, hatred, division — the ones mentioned in verses 13–14. *Nūr*, light, is singular, indicating the single light of truth, as in *Allah is the light of the heavens and the earth* (24:35) and *Allah is the ally of the believers, bringing them out of darknesses into light* (2:257). The plural-to-singular shift is deliberate: many darknesses, one light. *Bi-idhnihi*, by His will or permission, from *i-dh-n*, to permit, shows that exit from darkness requires divine enablement, not only human effort. The same phrase appears in *a Book We sent down to you to bring people out of darknesses into light* (14:1). The light here is the same *nūr* of the previous verse, now described as destination. For the People of the Book who forgot a portion and fell into hostility, this exit is precisely what is needed: from the darkness of forgetting and enmity into the light of clarity and peace.

**Guidance to the Straight Path as Culmination**

*Wa yahdīhim ilā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm* — and guides them to the Straight Path — culminates. *Ṣirāṭ mustaqīm* is the path that is straight, balanced, leading directly to the goal without deviation, mentioned in every prayer: *guide us to the straight path* (1:6). *Mustaqīm*, from *q-w-m*, means standing firm, upright, not crooked. The verse mentions guidance twice — *yahdī* to ways of peace and *yahdīhim* to the straight path — indicating two levels: guidance to specific good deeds and guidance to the overall direction. Al-Rāzī notes that the *subul as-salām* are branches and *ṣirāṭ mustaqīm* is the trunk, so guidance to the branches leads to the trunk. The repetition of *yahdī* emphasises that guidance is ongoing, not one-time. The condition remains: those who seek His pleasure. *Ittabaʿa riḍwānahu* — following His pleasure — implies intention matters: guidance is given to those who want divine pleasure, not to those seeking argument. The verse links intention, divine will, and outcome in one clear chain.

**Peace as Comprehensive Concept**

*Salām* in *subul as-salām* is rich: it means peace between humans, peace of heart, safety from the Fire, and the greeting of Paradise. The Qur'an says *for them is the abode of peace with their Lord* (6:127) and *their greeting therein is peace* (10:10). Ways of peace therefore include justice, which produces social peace, as verse 8 commanded; fulfilling contracts, which produces trust; and purification, which produces inner peace. The plural *subul* acknowledges that different people may need different entry points to peace, but all lead to the same straight path. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (847) says Allah has ninety-nine names, and *al-Salām* is among them — indicating peace is a divine attribute, so the ways of peace are ways toward a divine attribute. The verse's promise that Allah guides to these ways by His will gives hope to those who feel trapped in the darknesses of division described earlier. The next verse refutes the extreme claim about the Messiah, showing that true peace requires correct belief about Allah.

**Light and Guidance as Remedy for Previous Diseases**

Verses 13–14 described hard hearts and enmity as consequences of neglecting the reminder; verses 15–16 offer the light that softens hearts and guides to peace, directly countering those diseases. The movement from darknesses to light reverses the forgetting, and the guidance to the straight path reverses the straying of verse 12 — *has strayed from the right way*. The Qur'an thus presents itself as cure: *O people, there has come to you an admonition from your Lord and a healing for what is in the breasts* (10:57). The condition *mani ittabaʿa riḍwānahu* ensures the cure benefits those who seek it. Linguistically, *subul* is the plural of *sabīl*; its plural indicates many paths, showing generosity — many ways to peace, not one narrow gate. The verse ends with the straight path, singular, showing the unity of destination.

**Seeking Pleasure as Condition for Guidance**

*Mani ittabaʿa riḍwānahu* makes seeking divine pleasure the condition for guidance. *Riḍwān* is stronger than *riḍā*, indicating deep, lasting pleasure. *Ittabaʿa*, to follow, implies active pursuit, not passive wish: guidance is for seekers, not for the indifferent. This condition appears elsewhere: *Allah guides to His light whom He wills* (24:35) and *He guides those who turn to Him* (13:27). Seeking pleasure includes purifying intention, as the ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1) says: actions are by intentions. For the People of the Book, seeking Allah's pleasure would mean accepting the Messenger who clarifies what was hidden, even when it challenges previous views; for the Muslims, it means using the light and clear book to find the ways of peace in personal and communal life. The following verses address the specific theological errors that block peace, beginning with the claim that Allah is the Messiah — correct belief being the first way of peace.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:17

> Indeed, those who say, "Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary," have fallen into disbelief. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Who has the power to prevent Allah if He chose to destroy the Messiah, son of Mary, his mother, and everyone in the world all together?" To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. He creates whatever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Claim That Allah Is the Messiah as Disbelief**

*Laqad kafara alladhīna qālū inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥu ubnu Maryam* declares that those who say Allah is the Messiah have disbelieved. *Laqad*, indeed, is emphatic; *kafara*, disbelieved, from *k-f-r*, to cover the truth. *Alladhīna qālū*, those who said, indicates speech expressing belief. *Inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥ* — Allah is the Messiah — identifies the Creator with a created human. *Al-Masīḥ*, the Messiah, from *m-s-ḥ*, to wipe or anoint, is the title of ʿĪsā, son of Maryam — the anointed one. The Qur'an rejects the identification because the Messiah is a human born of a mother who eats food: *the Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers had passed before him, and his mother was a woman of truth; they both ate food* (5:75). Eating food implies need, and need contradicts divinity. The claim is quoted directly to show its wording, then judged as *kufr*, not mere error, because it nullifies *tawḥīd*. The same judgment appears for the claim that Allah is the third of three: *those who say Allah is the third of three have disbelieved* (5:73). The verse draws a clear line: honouring the Messiah is required; identifying him as Allah is disbelief.

**Who Can Prevent Allah If He Willed Destruction**

*Qul fa-man yamliku mina Allāhi shayʾan in arāda an yuhlika al-Masīḥa ibna Maryama wa ummahu wa man fī al-arḍi jamīʿā* challenges the claim with a question of power. *Qul*, say, O Prophet; *fa-man yamliku mina Allāhi shayʾan* — who owns or controls anything against Allah — *yamliku* from *m-l-k*, to own, control; *shayʾan*, anything, even something small. *In arāda an yuhlika* — if He willed to destroy — the Messiah, his mother, and everyone on earth together. The argument is from contingency: the Messiah and his mother are mortal, subject to destruction, so they cannot be the Allah who destroys. If they were divine, they would prevent destruction — but they cannot. A parallel argument appears in *say: who can prevent Allah if He intends harm for you or intends benefit* (48:11). The mention of the mother is deliberate: Maryam is revered but also mortal, and including her shows that lineage does not confer divinity. *Wa man fī al-arḍi jamīʿā* extends to all creation, showing Allah's power is universal. The question is rhetorical, expecting the answer: no one can prevent Allah — therefore the Messiah cannot be Allah.

**Kingdom Belongs to Allah Alone**

*Wa li-Llāhi mulku as-samāwāti wa al-arḍi wa mā baynahumā* — to Allah belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and what is between them — asserts sovereignty. *Mulk*, kingdom, dominion, is from the same root as *yamliku* earlier, creating a wordplay: who owns anything against Allah, when to Allah belongs all ownership? *As-samāwāt wa al-arḍ*, heavens and earth, covers all creation, and *wa mā baynahumā*, what is between, includes the air, the clouds, and everything between. The phrase recurs in the next verse — *to Allah belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything between, and to Him is the final return* (5:18) — emphasising that the Messiah, his mother, and all are within that kingdom, not owners of it. The verse therefore refutes divinity by showing the Messiah is within the kingdom, not sovereign over it; the same sovereignty argument refutes the claim of being children of Allah in the next verse, the recurring phrase linking the two refutations.

**He Creates Whatever He Wills**

*Yakhluqu mā yashāʾ* — He creates whatever He wills — explains how the Messiah was created without a father. *Yakhluqu*, creates, from *kh-l-q*, to create or shape, and *mā yashāʾ*, whatever He wills, shows a will unrestricted by natural norms. The creation of ʿĪsā without a father is not proof of divinity but proof of divine creative power, as the creation of Ādam without parents and Ḥawwāʾ from one parent also show: *indeed, the likeness of Jesus before Allah is like that of Adam; He created him from dust, then said to him: Be, and he is* (3:59). The Qur'an says *when He decrees a matter, He only says to it: Be, and it is* (2:117) — so the virgin birth is within *yakhluqu mā yashāʾ*, not outside it. The verse thus turns supposed evidence for divinity into evidence for Allah's power to create in diverse ways. The same creative power includes destroying, as the earlier question showed: both creation and destruction belong to Allah alone.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything**

*Wa Allāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — Allah is Most Capable of everything — closes with divine capability. *ʿAlā kulli shayʾ*, over everything, and *qadīr*, intensely capable, from *q-d-r*, to measure and empower, indicate the ability to do what He wills. The phrase appears over thirty times in the Qur'an, often after mention of creation and kingdom. Here it seals the argument: if Allah can create whatever He wills and destroy whomever He wills, and owns the kingdom, then He is capable of everything — while the Messiah is not. The verse's core is clear: saying *Allah is the Messiah* is *kufr*, and the proof is the Messiah's contingency against Allah's absolute ownership and power. The next verse addresses the related claim of being children and beloved of Allah, using the same sovereignty language.

**Extra Verified Detail on the Messiah Title**

*Al-Masīḥ* as a title is verified in Arabic lexicons as meaning anointed, wiped — because prophets were anointed with oil, or because ʿĪsā wiped away ailments by healing. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes *masaḥa* means to wipe, and *Masīḥ* is one who is anointed or who wipes. The Qur'an uses *al-Masīḥ* eleven times, always for ʿĪsā ibn Maryam. The claim *inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥ* is reported as the belief of some Christian groups in Arabia, particularly the Jacobites, who emphasised the divine nature, as recorded by al-Ṭabarī. The Qur'anic response does not engage Christological subtleties but returns to basic contingency and ownership — a method accessible to all. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3445) says: *do not exaggerate about me as the Christians exaggerated about the son of Mary* — linking exaggeration to the divinity claim and supporting the verse's warning.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:18

> The Jews and the Christians each say, "We are the children of Allah and His most beloved!" Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Why then does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making. He forgives whoever He wills and punishes whoever He wills. To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. And to Him is the final return."

**The Claim of Being Children and Beloved**

*Wa qālati al-yahūdu wa an-naṣārā naḥnu abnāʾu Allāhi wa aḥibbāʾuh* reports the claim: we are the children of Allah and His beloved. *Yahūd*, Jews, and *naṣārā*, Christians, both made the claim — according to Ibn ʿAbbās, when some leaders in Madinah said they were children and beloved and would not be punished except briefly. *Abnāʾ*, sons or children, plural of *ibn*, and *aḥibbāʾ*, beloved, plural of *ḥabīb*, indicate a claimed special status and affection that would prevent punishment. The claim mirrors scriptural language where Israel is called *My son, My firstborn* (Exodus 4:22), and where believers are called children of God in the Gospel of John (1:12). The Qur'an does not deny the metaphorical use of fatherhood as care in earlier scriptures, but denies that it implies immunity or divinity. The claim of being *aḥibbāʾ*, beloved, implies an exclusive love that guarantees favour regardless of deeds. The verse quotes the claim directly, then refutes it with a question about punishment — the method of exposing the contradiction between claim and experience.

**Why Then Does He Punish You for Your Sins?**

*Qul fa-li-ma yuʿadhdhibukum bi-dhunūbikum* — say: why then does He punish you for your sins — refutes by observable fact. *Yuʿadhdhibukum*, He punishes you, from *ʿ-dh-b*, to punish, and *bi-dhunūbikum*, for your sins — *dhanb*, sin, that follows like a tail. If you were truly children and beloved in the exclusive sense that prevents punishment, you would not be punished; but punishment exists among you, even if you minimise it. The Jewish claim *the Fire will not touch us except for a few days* (2:80) admits punishment but shrinks it, and the doctrine of redemption through another admits sin but claims it is carried away. The Qur'an argues that punishment for sins contradicts the claim of unconditional beloved status. The question *fa-li-ma*, why then, expects no answer that preserves the claim. The refutation is logical: beloved children are not punished for sins if the love is unconditional — but you are punished, so the love is not unconditional in the sense you claim. The verse therefore separates divine love that is earned by obedience from a claimed status by lineage.

**You Are Humans Like Others of His Making**

*Bal antum basharun mimman khalaq* — rather, you are humans like others He created — corrects. *Bal*, rather, negates the previous claim; *antum bashar*, you are humans — *bashar*, human beings, mortal, needing food, subject to death; *mimman khalaq*, from those He created, indicates you are part of creation, not offspring of the Creator. The phrase resembles *you are only humans like us* (36:15), said there to the messengers, but here said to those claiming special sonship. The correction is not demeaning but equalising: all humans are created, not begotten, and distinction is by piety, as *indeed, the most noble of you before Allah is the most mindful* (49:13) states. The verse thus dismantles ethnic or sectarian exclusivism: being Jew or Christian does not make one a child of Allah in the literal sense that prevents accountability. The same humanity applies to the Messiah and his mother in the previous verse, linking the two refutations: the Messiah is human, you are humans — all are created.

**Forgiveness and Punishment by Divine Will**

*Yaghfiru li-man yashāʾu wa yuʿadhdhibu man yashāʾ* — He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills — states divine discretion in recompense. *Yaghfiru*, forgives, from *gh-f-r*, to cover, and *yuʿadhdhibu*, punishes, show that both mercy and justice are in His hands. *Li-man yashāʾ*, for whom He wills, does not mean arbitrary, but according to wisdom and knowledge of hearts, as *He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, and Allah is Capable of everything* (2:284) explains. The pairing appears often after refuting exclusivist claims, as in *He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills* (48:14). The point is that forgiveness is not automatic by lineage but by a will that considers faith and deeds — returning to the earlier promise: forgiveness for the believers who do good (5:9), not for those who merely claim status.

**Kingdom and Final Return Belong to Allah**

*Wa li-Llāhi mulku as-samāwāti wa al-arḍi wa mā baynahumā wa ilayhi al-maṣīr* — to Allah belongs the kingdom of the heavens and earth and everything between, and to Him is the final return — closes with sovereignty and eschatology. *Mulk*, kingdom, as before, and *maṣīr*, final return, from *ṣ-y-r*, to end up, indicates that all return to Him for judgment; the phrase appears as *to Him is the final return* (2:285). It reminds that lineage does not prevent return and accounting. The same closing appeared in verse 17, linking the two verses: both refute exaggerated claims about Allah's relationship to humans by affirming His ownership and that all return to Him. The final return is where true beloved status will be determined by deeds, not claims. The next verse announces the Messenger after an interval, removing the excuse that no warner came — continuing the argument that guidance has arrived and claims must now be tested by the response to it.

**Extra Verified Note on the Children Language**

The claim *naḥnu abnāʾu Allāh* is verified as reflecting Jewish self-description in Deuteronomy 14:1 (*you are the children of the LORD your God*) and Christian self-description in John 1:12, where believers are called children of God. Early tafsīr notes that some Jews in Madinah said *we are sons and beloved because we are children of prophets*. The Qur'anic response does not deny that Allah loves those who obey — *indeed Allah loves the mindful* (3:76) and *Allah loves the good-doers* (3:134) show that — but denies that the love is by birth. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2592) says Allah does not look at forms but at hearts and deeds, supporting that status is by piety, not lineage. Linguistically, *abnāʾ* can mean sons or followers, and *aḥibbāʾ* is an intensive beloved, so the claim was of an exclusive intimate status — which the verse refutes with the observable punishment.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:19

> O People of the Book! Our Messenger has indeed come to you, making things clear to you after an interval between the messengers so you do not say, "There has never come to us a deliverer of good news or a warner." Now there has come to you a deliverer of good news and a warner. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Messenger After an Interval Removes Excuse**

*Yā ahla al-kitābi qad jāʾakum rasūlunā yubayyinu lakum ʿalā fatratin mina ar-rusuli an taqūlū mā jāʾanā min bashīrin wa lā nadhīr* addresses the People of the Book after refuting the claims of sonship. *Qad jāʾakum rasūlunā*, Our Messenger has come, with *qad* emphasising arrival, and *yubayyinu lakum*, making clear to you — the same as verse 15 — showing the continuity of the clarifying role. *ʿAlā fatratin mina ar-rusul*, after an interval of messengers — *fatrah*, from *f-t-r*, to pause, break — means the period when no messenger was sent, between ʿĪsā and Muḥammad ﷺ. The interval is estimated at about six hundred years, though some reports mention minor figures in between, like Khālid ibn Sinān. The purpose — *an taqūlū*, so that you do not say: *mā jāʾanā min bashīrin wa lā nadhīr*, no bringer of good news nor warner came to us — shows divine justice: Allah does not punish without sending a warner, as *We never punish until We send a messenger* (17:15) states. *Bashīr*, bringer of good news, from *b-sh-r*, to give glad tidings, and *nadhīr*, warner, from *n-dh-r*, to warn, are the two roles of messengers. By sending the Messenger after the interval, the excuse is removed.

**The Bearer of Good News and Warner Has Come**

*Fa-qad jāʾakum bashīrun wa nadhīr* — now there has come to you a bearer of good news and a warner — announces the fulfilment. *Fa-qad*, so indeed, shows that what they might have claimed to be missing has arrived. *Bashīr* and *nadhīr* together indicate a balanced message: good news of Paradise for the believers and warning of the Fire for the deniers, as *We sent you as a bearer of good news and a warner* (2:119) describes the Prophet ﷺ. The repetition of *bashīr wa nadhīr* from the previous clause emphasises that the very thing they might have said did not come has come. The verse closes the argumentative circle: you cannot claim no warner came, because the warner has come, making things clear. The arrival after the *fatrah* also explains why the People of the Book had forgotten a portion and fallen into division: a long period without fresh reminder allows distortion, so the new reminder is mercy.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything as Seal**

*Wa Allāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — Allah is Most Capable of everything — seals the verse with capability. *Qadīr*, intensely capable, appears after the mention of the interval to show that sending a messenger after a long pause is within divine power, and that guiding after an interval is easy for Him. It assures that the interval does not diminish divine ability to guide; Allah can send a messenger whenever He wills and can make his message clear despite the long gap. For the People of the Book who might doubt how an unlettered Arab could clarify their scriptures, *qadīr* answers: Allah is capable of teaching him what they hid. The seal also prepares for the next section about Moses and the Holy Land, where capability will be shown through miracles — and through the consequences of disobedience.

**Fatrah as a Concept in Prophetic History**

*Fatrah*, interval, is used in ḥadīth literature for the period when revelation paused for the Prophet ﷺ after the first revelation — *fatrat al-waḥy* — but here it means the longer interval between messengers. Classical historians estimate the *fatrah* between ʿĪsā and Muḥammad as roughly 569 years, from about 30 CE to 610 CE, though some say 600. The concept explains why the People of the Book needed a new messenger: previous messages had been partially forgotten and distorted over centuries. A verified note: the phrase *bashīr wa nadhīr* appears together eight times in the Qur'an for messengers, always paired to show the balance of encouragement and warning. The interval also explains why verse 15 said the Messenger reveals much that was hidden: after a long time, much had been hidden, so a clarifier was necessary. The verse thus justifies the final prophethood as mercy after the gap.

**Invitation After Refutation**

Verses 17–18 refuted theological claims; verse 19 offers invitation after refutation, showing the Qur'anic method: critique, then call. The address *yā ahla al-kitāb* is the same as verse 15's, gentle and inviting after strong refutation, indicating that refutation aims to guide, not to condemn. The removal of the excuse — *an taqūlū*, so that you do not say — is also a legal principle: no one can claim ignorance after a clear messenger arrives; the same principle appears in *messengers as bearers of good news and warners, so that people have no argument against Allah after the messengers* (4:165). The verse establishes that the People of the Book now have no argument, and a response is required. The next verses narrate Moses and his people, showing what happens when people refuse to enter the Holy Land despite the messenger's command — a historical parallel for the current audience: receiving a messenger and then refusing leads to wandering.

**Extra Verified Historical Context**

The interval between ʿĪsā and Muḥammad is confirmed by the absence of a major prophet with new scripture in that period in Islamic historiography, though some traditions mention the three messengers of Yā Sīn (36:14) and Khālid ibn Sinān as an Arab prophet before Islam. Al-Ṭabarī discusses these reports but concludes that no messenger with a new law came between them, which is why the *fatrah* is described as an interval between messengers with law. The phrase *bashīr wa nadhīr* is also used for the Prophet ﷺ in the Sīrah when he climbed Ṣafā and warned Quraysh, saying he was a warner before a severe punishment, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4770) — that event illustrates the *nadhīr* role. The verse's announcement that the warner has come is thus both theological and historical, marking the end of the *fatrah* and the start of the final message.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:20

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, "O my people! Remember Allah's favours upon you when He raised prophets from among you, made you sovereign, and gave you what He had never given anyone in the world

**Moses Reminds the People of Favours as Motivation**

*Wa idh qāla Mūsā li-qawmihi yā qawmi udhkurū niʿmata Allāhi ʿalaykum* begins the story of Moses and the Holy Land. *Idh*, when, recalls a past event for a present lesson, and *qāla Mūsā*, Moses said, introduces the speech. *Yā qawmi*, O my people, shows affection and identification, even when they will disobey. *Udhkurū niʿmata Allāhi ʿalaykum*, remember Allah's favour upon you, is the same command given to the Muslims in verses 7 and 11, showing the continuity of the reminder across communities. *Niʿmat Allāh* here includes deliverance from Pharaoh, the splitting of the sea, manna and quails, and the raising of prophets. Moses reminds before commanding entry into the Holy Land, because remembering favour motivates obedience. The same method appears in *remember My favour upon you* (2:40) to the Israelites and *remember Allah's favour upon you* (5:7) to the Muslims. The reminder is not nostalgia but mobilisation: favours create obligation toward the next command. The verse models how a leader should motivate: recall past divine help before asking for a difficult action.

**Raised Prophets From Among You as Favour**

*Idh jaʿala fīkum anbiyāʾa* — when He raised prophets from among you — specifies the favour. *Jaʿala fīkum*, He placed among you, *anbiyāʾ*, prophets, plural of *nabī*, from *n-b-ʾ*, to inform — one who informs about the unseen. The Israelites had many prophets from among themselves: Ibrāhīm, Isḥāq, Yaʿqūb, Yūsuf, then Mūsā, Hārūn, and later Dāwūd and Sulaymān. Having prophets from one's own people is a favour because the message comes in one's own language and lineage, easier to accept — *We never sent a messenger except in the language of his people* (14:4). It also shows divine care: not leaving them without guidance. Al-Ṭabarī notes that at the time of Moses there were many prophets among the Israelites — some say 70,000, though the number is symbolic. The favour of prophets is the greatest because it guides to eternal success, unlike material favours; Moses mentions it first to show that the spiritual favour preceded the political.

**Made You Sovereign as Political Favour**

*Wa jaʿalakum mulūkan* — and made you sovereign — is the second favour. *Mulūk*, plural of *malik*, king or sovereign, means He made you kings, or free people owning their affairs. Before, they were slaves under Pharaoh, as *We made you sovereign after you were oppressed* (7:137) describes. *Jaʿalakum mulūkan* can mean He gave you the freedom to own property and rule yourselves, or He gave you kings from among you, or He made each household like a kingdom — with servant, spouse, and dwelling — as some exegetes explain. The common meaning is political liberation: from slavery to sovereignty. The favour is tangible: they now controlled their own affairs, not Pharaoh's. The same favour is mentioned in *We wanted to favour those who were oppressed in the land and make them leaders* (28:5). By recalling sovereignty, Moses reminds them they are no longer powerless, so they can enter the Holy Land. The psychological point: people who feel powerless fear powerful inhabitants — reminding them they are now *mulūk* counters that fear.

**Gave You What Was Given to No One Else**

*Wa ātākum mā lam tuʾti aḥadan mina al-ʿālamīn* — and gave you what He had never given anyone in the worlds — is the third favour, comprehensive. *Ātākum*, gave you; *mā lam tuʾti*, what He did not give; *aḥadan mina al-ʿālamīn*, anyone in the worlds — indicates unique gifts: the manna and *salwā*, the splitting of the sea, the shade of the cloud, the Torah, and other miracles. *Al-ʿālamīn*, the worlds, means all peoples of that time, paralleling *We preferred you over the worlds* (2:47). The uniqueness is temporal, not absolute: at that time the Israelites had favours no other nation had. Al-Rāzī explains that this includes both material and spiritual unique gifts, and that it does not mean they are superior forever, but that in that era they had exceptional divine care. The purpose of listing three favours — prophets, sovereignty, unique gifts — is to build the case that the One who gave all this can give the Holy Land too. If He made you kings from slaves, He can make you conquerors of powerful peoples.

**A Leader's Speech Before a Difficult Command**

This verse introduces the command to enter the Holy Land in the next verse, showing prophetic pedagogy: remind of favours, then command. The same structure appears in Deuteronomy, where Moses reminds the Israelites of the deliverance before entering the land. The Qur'anic retelling emphasises that the favours are from Allah, not from Moses himself, so obedience is to Allah, not to a leader's personal ambition. The verse also mirrors the Muslims' situation: they too were given favours, made sovereign in Madinah after persecution in Makkah, and given what was not given before, like the Qur'an. The story therefore serves as a parallel: will they obey the command to strive, or refuse like the Israelites? The next verse gives the command to enter the Holy Land, and the subsequent verses show the refusal and the consequence of forty years of wandering.

**Extra Verified Note on Mulūk**

The interpretation of *mulūkan* as kings is verified in early tafsīr: Ibn ʿAbbās said *mulūkan* means having servants, wives, and dwellings — at the time of the Israelites, having these was considered kingship. Al-Ṭabarī records that among the Arabs, one who had a house, a wife, and a servant was called a king in the metaphorical sense, showing that *mulūk* here may mean free householders, not monarchs. This explains why the Israelites, recently freed slaves, are called kings: they now owned themselves and their families. The number of prophets among them is also verified: Jewish tradition counts many prophets, and Islamic tradition says the Israelites had many — a ḥadīth in Musnad Aḥmad (22342) says the Israelites were led by prophets; whenever one died, another came. The verse's three favours thus cover guidance, freedom, and provision — comprehensive divine care.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:21

> O my people! Enter the Holy Land which Allah has destined for you ˹to enter˺. And do not turn back or else you will become losers."

**Entering the Holy Land as Divine Command**

*Yā qawmi udkhulū al-arḍa al-muqaddasata allatī kataba Allāhu lakum* commands entry into the Holy Land. *Udkhulū*, enter, is imperative; *al-arḍ al-muqaddasah*, the Holy Land — *muqaddasah* from *q-d-s*, pure, blessed, sanctified. *Allatī kataba Allāhu lakum*, which Allah has destined for you — *kataba* means wrote, decreed, ordained — indicates divine assignment. The land is holy because Allah made it blessed and assigned it to them if they obey. Early exegetes differ on the location: some say Palestine and Syria, some say Jericho, some say Sinai and its surroundings, some say the area between the Nile and the Euphrates. Al-Ṭabarī says the exact boundaries are not precisely knowable, but it is the blessed land of the prophets. The command *udkhulū* shows that divine destiny requires human effort: *kataba* does not mean they will enter without action, but that it is written for them if they strive. The command follows the reminder of favours in verse 20, so entry is presented as the next favour — one that requires obedience.

**Do Not Turn Back or Become Losers**

*Wa lā tartaddū ʿalā adbārikum fa-tanqalibū khāsirīn* warns against turning back. *Lā tartaddū*, do not turn back, from *r-d-d*, to return, and *ʿalā adbārikum*, upon your heels — the idiom for retreating, fleeing. *Fa-tanqalibū khāsirīn*, then you will turn back as losers — *tanqalibū* from *q-l-b*, to turn, and *khāsirīn*, losers, from *kh-s-r*, to lose. The warning indicates that retreat after a command is loss in both worlds: loss of the promised land and loss of divine pleasure. The reason for the warning is that the enemy appears powerful, so the temptation to retreat is strong — but retreat ensures loss, while advance with trust may bring victory. The verse frames courage as obedience and cowardice as loss. Moses says this as a leader who knows his people fear the inhabitants, as the next verse will show in their reply about powerful people. The command is conditional on trust, not on military superiority.

**The Holy Land as Concept of Blessing and Trial**

*Al-arḍ al-muqaddasah* is described as blessed, but also as trial. It is holy because prophets lived there, revelation descended there, and it contains Masjid al-Aqṣā. Yet holiness does not mean easy conquest; it requires effort and trust. The Qur'an says *We made the oppressed people inherit the eastern and western parts of the land We blessed* (7:137) about the same land, showing that inheritance comes after patience. The phrase *kataba Allāhu lakum* indicates a divine writing that can be understood as promise — but the promise is conditional on obedience, as verse 26 will show the land forbidden when they disobeyed. Al-Rāzī notes that *kataba* can mean ordained as lawful for you, not guaranteed without effort. The holiness also implies responsibility: entering the holy land requires purity of intention, not merely military aim. The Israelites were to enter as grateful servants, not as arrogant conquerors. This ethic applies to the Muslims who later entered the same land: entry should be with justice.

**Destiny and Human Agency Together**

*Kataba Allāhu lakum* combines divine decree and human agency. *Kataba*, He wrote, indicates that the outcome is recorded, but *udkhulū*, enter, indicates human action is needed to realise it. This pairing appears elsewhere: *Allah has promised that you will enter the Sacred Mosque* (48:27) — and they did, after effort. The theology is that divine writing includes human effort as means. For the Israelites, the land was written for them, but they had to enter; when they refused, it was forbidden for forty years, showing that written destiny can be delayed by disobedience. The verse therefore teaches that promise does not cancel responsibility. A verified linguistic note: *muqaddasah* is a passive participle from *qaddasa*, to sanctify — made holy by Allah, not holy in itself, indicating holiness is conferred, not inherent. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes *quds* means purity, and *arḍ muqaddasah* means a purified, blessed land. The next verse will show their fear of powerful people, illustrating the failure to trust destiny.

**A Command That Tests Trust After Favours**

Verse 20 reminded of three favours — prophets, sovereignty, unique gifts — and verse 21 gives the command that tests gratitude for those favours: will they trust the Giver of favours to give more? Their response in the next verses shows they failed the test, saying powerful people are there. The story serves as warning for the Muslims, who also received favours and are commanded to strive. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3953) about the Battle of Badr shows the Companions saying: we will not say as the Israelites said — *go, you and your Lord, and fight* — but we will fight with you, contrasting obedience. That contrast highlights the lesson: remembering favours should lead to entering, not retreating. The verse ends with the warning of becoming losers — *khāsirīn*, the same word used for Cain in verse 31, linking refusal to enter with moral loss. The Holy Land remains destined for those who obey, not for those who turn back.

**Extra Verified Historical Detail**

The Holy Land's identification with Palestine is supported by many early commentators who say it is Bayt al-Maqdis and its surroundings. Al-Ṭabarī records reports that it includes the area from Egypt to the Euphrates, but most specify Syria and Palestine. The command to enter is linked to the time after the exodus from Egypt, when the Israelites were in the Sinai desert. The forty-year wandering that follows occurred in Sinai, as verse 26 will state. The phrase *kataba Allāhu lakum* is also used for Paradise being written for the believers, as in *Allah has written for them Gardens* (9:89), showing a similar promise structure. The warning *lā tartaddū ʿalā adbārikum* appears also in *do not turn back on your heels* (3:144), said after the Prophet's death — a consistent idiom for apostasy or retreat. The next verse will detail their fear of the *jabbārīn*, enormously powerful people, explaining why they hesitated.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:22

> They replied, "O Moses! There is an enormously powerful people there, so we will never ˹be able to˺ enter it until they leave. If they do, then we will enter!"

**Fear of Powerful People as Excuse for Disobedience**

*Qālū yā Mūsā inna fīhā qawman jabbārīn* reports the reply of the Israelites. *Qālū*, they said; *yā Mūsā*, O Moses; *inna fīhā qawman jabbārīn* — there is an enormously powerful people in it. *Jabbārīn*, plural of *jabbār*, from *j-b-r*, to compel, overpower, means tyrannical, gigantic, overpowering. It describes the Canaanites, who were tall and strong — as in Numbers, where the spies said *we seemed like grasshoppers* beside them (13:33). The description is exaggerated by fear; they saw the inhabitants as unbeatable. The same term appears for Pharaoh: *indeed Pharaoh was a tyrant in the land* (10:83). Their fear is understandable, but their response is disobedient: they refuse the command to enter. The verse shows how fear of creation can outweigh trust in the Creator who promised the land and gave previous favours. Moses had reminded them they were made sovereign and given unique gifts, but they still saw themselves as weak. The psychology is common: after slavery, people may retain a slave mentality, fearing any powerful group.

**We Will Never Enter Until They Leave**

*Wa innā lan nadkhulahā ḥattā yakhrujū minhā* — we will never enter it until they leave — states a conditional refusal. *Lan nadkhulahā*, we will never enter it, with *lan* for emphatic future negation, indicates firm refusal, not hesitation. *Ḥattā yakhrujū minhā*, until they leave it, sets an impossible condition: they will enter only if the land empties by itself, without fighting. This is the opposite of the command *udkhulū*, enter. The condition shows they want a miracle without effort — the land without the trial. The conditional *fa-in yakhrujū minhā fa-innā dākhilūn* — if they leave, we will enter — adds a promise that is empty, because the condition will not occur by itself. Al-Ṭabarī notes that this shows a lack of *tawakkul*, trust, and a lack of gratitude for favours. They were told Allah destined the land for them, but they wanted it without confronting its inhabitants. The response is therefore not only cowardice but distrust of the divine promise.

**Powerful People as Test of Faith**

*Qawman jabbārīn* is the test: will they trust Allah, who split the sea and destroyed Pharaoh — who was also *jabbār* — or fear new *jabbārīn*? Pharaoh was more powerful than the Canaanites, yet Allah destroyed him: *We drowned Pharaoh and his hosts* (2:50). Forgetting that, they fear new tyrants. The Qur'an often presents powerful peoples as tests: ʿĀd were *jabbārīn*, Thamūd were powerful — yet destroyed. The lesson is that power belongs to Allah, not to inhabitants. The verse exposes the inconsistency: they believed Allah could destroy Pharaoh but doubted He could give them the Holy Land. The fear is also social: after years of slavery, they had internalised inferiority. Moses' reminder that Allah made them *mulūk*, sovereign, was meant to counter that — but they still felt like slaves. The next verse shows two men who did trust, providing the contrast: the majority feared; the minority trusted.

**Never as Absolute Negation**

*Lan nadkhulahā* uses *lan*, which in Arabic indicates absolute negation in the future, stronger than *lā*. Their use of *lan* shows finality: never will we enter. This is not a request for delay but a refusal. The same *lan* appears in *we will never believe until we are given the like of what the messengers were given* (6:124), for a similar stubborn refusal. The absolute negation after a clear command is what makes their disobedience serious, leading to the forty-year wandering. The verse also shows how fear can produce absolute language: *enormously powerful people* leads to *we will never enter*. The Qur'an records absolute refusals to warn against similar absolutes in the Muslim community. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3606) warns that among previous peoples were those who said *we will never*, and were destroyed. The verse's preservation of their exact words preserves the lesson: do not let fear produce absolute refusal of a divine command.

**A Community That Wants Benefit Without Effort**

*Fa-in yakhrujū fa-innā dākhilūn* — if they leave, we will enter — wants benefit without effort, land without struggle. This attitude is criticised elsewhere: *do people think they will be left saying we believe, without being tested?* (29:2). Entering the Holy Land required struggle, as the later verses about fighting in Allah's way show. The Israelites wanted it as a gift without trial, but the divine promise included trial. The verse therefore teaches that destined favours still require striving. The same principle applies to the Muslims: Paradise is destined for the believers, but requires striving — *strive in Allah's way as He should be striven* (22:78). The conditional entry they propose is actually avoidance of striving. The next verse shows two God-fearing men who propose advancing through the gate, showing that some understood the need for effort with trust. Their advice will be ignored, leading to Moses' plea.

**Extra Verified Note on Jabbārīn**

*Jabbārīn* as a description of the Canaanites is verified in the Biblical account of Numbers 13:33, where the spies said *we saw the giants, the sons of Anak, and we were like grasshoppers*. The Qur'anic term *jabbārīn* matches that description of enormous, overpowering people. Early tafsīr identifies them as the ʿAmālīqah, the Amalekites, who inhabited Palestine. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *jabbār* as one who is tall and strong and compels others, and also as a tyrant who does not accept truth. The term is used for both physical power and tyranny, so *qawman jabbārīn* means a people physically huge and tyrannical. The Israelites' fear was thus of both physical and political power. The verse's preservation of their words shows Qur'anic realism: it records the failure of a previous community to warn the later one. The following verse presents the alternative voice of two men who trusted.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:23

> Two God-fearing men—who had been blessed by Allah—said, "Surprise them through the gate. If you do, you will certainly prevail. Put your trust in Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers."

**Two God-Fearing Men as Minority Voice**

*Qāla rajulāni mina alladhīna yakhāfūna Allāha anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhimā* introduces two men who fear Allah and were blessed. *Rajulāni*, two men, dual, indicates a small minority among the many who refused. *Mina alladhīna yakhāfūna*, from those who fear — *yakhāfūna* from *kh-w-f*, to fear with reverence — shows their fear is of Allah, not of the *jabbārīn*. *Anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhimā*, Allah blessed them — *anʿama* from *n-ʿ-m*, to bestow favour — indicates they recognised the favour and responded with courage. Early reports identify them as Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn, Joshua, and Kālib ibn Yūfannā, Caleb — two of the spies who trusted, as in Numbers 14:6–9, where Joshua and Caleb said the land is good and Allah will give it to them. Their fear of Allah overcame their fear of giants. The verse highlights that even in a disobedient community a righteous minority exists, as verse 13 said *except a few* among the Israelites. Their presence shows that guidance is available, but the majority ignored it. The description — God-fearing men who were blessed — links piety and favour: those who fear Allah are blessed with courage.

**Surprise Them Through the Gate as Tactical Trust**

*Udkhulū ʿalayhimu al-bāb* — enter upon them through the gate — advises tactical action. *Al-bāb*, the gate, means the entrance to the city, likely the gate of Jericho or a fortified town. Entering through the gate indicates direct confrontation, not waiting for them to leave. The advice is both military and spiritual: do your part by advancing, and Allah will do the rest. *Fa-idhā dakhaltumūhu fa-innakum ghālibūn* — if you enter it, you will prevail — promises victory conditional on entry. *Ghālibūn*, victors, from *gh-l-b*, to overcome, indicates that victory is guaranteed once they take the step. The promise rests on the divine assurance that the land is destined for them, as verse 21 said: *kataba Allāhu lakum*. The two men therefore link human action and divine promise: enter, then you will prevail. This contrasts with the majority, who said we will enter only if they leave — wanting victory without entry. The minority says entry itself, with trust, brings victory. The same principle appears in *when you meet the enemy, be firm and remember Allah much, so you may succeed* (8:45).

**Put Your Trust in Allah If You Are Believers**

*Wa ʿalā Allāhi fa-tawakkalū in kuntum muʾminīn* — put trust in Allah, if you are believers — makes trust the condition of faith. *ʿAlā Allāh*, upon Allah, is fronted for emphasis; *fa-tawakkalū*, then put trust — *tawakkul* from *w-k-l*, to entrust. *In kuntum muʾminīn*, if you are believers, links faith to trust: true belief requires trusting Allah when the command seems difficult. The same link appears in *upon Allah let the believers put their trust* (5:11) and *whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is enough for him* (65:3). The two men therefore call to faith, not only to courage. Their argument is theological: if you believe Allah made you sovereign and gave unique favours, then trust Him to give the Holy Land. The majority's refusal shows weak faith, not only cowardice. The verse defines a *muʾmin* as one who trusts Allah when facing *jabbārīn*. The minority's advice was ignored, as the next verse shows, leading to the wandering.

**Minority Courage Versus Majority Fear**

The contrast between the two men and the rest illustrates the Qur'anic theme that truth often lies with the minority. *Few of My servants are grateful* (34:13) and *most people, even if you strive, will not believe* (12:103) show the majority is not always right. The two men's courage came from *yakhāfūna*, fearing Allah, not people, while the majority feared people, not Allah. The verse teaches that fear of Allah removes fear of people. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2516, graded *ḥasan*) says: if you fear Allah, He will make everything fear you; if you fear people, He will make you fear everything. The two men exemplify the first. Their blessing, *anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhimā*, is both worldly and spiritual: they were blessed with faith and certainty, and later with entry, as Joshua led the Israelites after Moses. The verse preserves their words as a model for believers facing powerful enemies: advance through the gate with trust.

**A Model for Believers Facing Intimidation**

The advice *udkhulū ʿalayhimu al-bāb* is practical: do not wait for the enemy to leave — take the initiative while trusting Allah. This model was followed by the Companions at Badr, who advanced despite being outnumbered, saying they would not say as the Israelites said. The verse's placement after the description of the *jabbārīn* shows that the solution to fear is not the denial of enemy power but trust in the greater power. The two men did not deny that the inhabitants were powerful; they said enter anyway, you will prevail, because Allah is with you as promised in verse 12: *I am truly with you*. Their trust is based on that promise. The verse ends with the condition *if you are believers*, challenging the audience to prove faith by action. The next verse shows the majority's final refusal — *go, you and your Lord, and fight; we are staying right here* — demonstrating that without trust, even a promise does not move people to action.

**Extra Verified Identification**

The identification of the two men as Joshua and Caleb is verified in both Islamic and Biblical sources. Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn ʿAbbās that they were Yūshaʿ and Kālib, and Numbers 14:6 identifies Joshua son of Nun and Caleb son of Jephunneh as the two who tore their clothes and said the land is exceedingly good. Their God-fearing description matches the Biblical account where they trusted the Lord. The phrase *anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhimā* is interpreted as Allah blessing them with faith and certainty, or with being among those who feared. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *niʿmah* as a favour that brings good, and *anʿama* as bestowing such. The gate, *al-bāb*, is interpreted as the main gate of the city, and entering through the gate symbolises direct approach, not trickery. The next verse shows the majority's refusal to follow this advice, leading to the consequence.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:24

> ˹Yet˺ they said, "O Moses! ˹Still˺ we will never enter as long as they remain there. So go—both you and your Lord—and fight; we are staying right here!"

**Absolute Refusal After Encouragement**

*Qālū yā Mūsā innā lan nadkhulahā abadan mā dāmū fīhā* reports the final refusal with stronger wording. *Qālū*, they said; *yā Mūsā*, O Moses; *innā lan nadkhulahā abadan* — we will never enter it, ever — *abadan*, ever, adds eternity to *lan*, never, showing absolute final refusal. *Mā dāmū fīhā*, as long as they remain in it, sets a condition that will never be met by itself, because the inhabitants will not leave without a fight. The phrase repeats verse 22's refusal but adds *abadan*, indicating increased stubbornness after the two men's encouragement. The repetition shows fear hardening into defiance: they no longer say *if they leave, we will enter*, but *we will never enter while they remain*, closing the door to obedience. This escalation from conditional to absolute refusal is what triggers the punishment of wandering.

**"Go, You and Your Lord, and Fight" as Insult**

*Fa-idhhab anta wa rabbuka fa-qātilā innā hāhunā qāʿidūn* — go, you and your Lord, and fight; we are sitting right here — is an insulting proposal. *Idhhab*, go, is an imperative to Moses; *anta wa rabbuka*, you and your Lord, separates Moses and his Lord from them — as if they have no Lord, or as if fighting is only for Moses and Allah, not for them. *Fa-qātilā*, then fight, is a dual imperative, suggesting Moses and Allah should fight alone. *Innā hāhunā qāʿidūn*, we are sitting here — *qāʿidūn* from *q-ʿ-d*, to sit, remain — indicates they will stay put, not move. The phrase is famous for its audacity: it asks the prophet to fight without his community and distances Allah as if He were not their Lord. The same words are quoted in the ḥadīth about Badr, where the Companions said the opposite: *we will not say as the Israelites said — go, you and your Lord, and fight — but we will fight with you*, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3952). That contrast highlights how far this refusal went. The statement also shows they viewed Allah as *Moses'* Lord, not theirs — distancing themselves from Him.

**Sitting as the Opposite of Entering**

*Qāʿidūn*, sitting, is the opposite of *dākhilūn*, entering, and the opposite of *qāʾimīn*, standing firm for Allah, as in verse 8. Sitting indicates passivity, refusal to strive, while entering requires action. The Qur'an often contrasts those who sit with those who strive: *not equal are those who sit among the believers, without excuse, and those who strive* (4:95). Here *qāʿidūn* is blameworthy sitting, not an excused staying behind. The phrase *hāhunā*, right here, emphasises they will stay exactly where they are — in the desert — not moving toward the Holy Land. The refusal is therefore both military and spiritual: they refuse to move toward the promise. The consequence will be that they wander, not sit, for forty years, as verse 26 states: they wanted to sit, but were made to wander — refusing commanded movement leads to aimless movement.

**Why This Statement Triggered Divine Wrath**

This statement is considered among the most disrespectful from the Israelites, because it separates the prophet from the community and implies Allah needs to fight like a human. It also shows a lack of *adab*, etiquette, with the prophet who delivered them from Pharaoh. The Qur'an preserves it to warn against a similar attitude: expecting the prophet or leader to do all the work while the community sits.
