The punishment that follows — the land forbidden for forty years — fits the crime: they refused to enter, so entry is forbidden. The statement also shows they did not trust the promise *innī maʿakum*, I am with you, from verse 12: if they believed Allah was with them, they would have said *we will enter with Allah's help*, not *go, you and your Lord, and fight*. The verse marks the climax of the disobedience narrative that began with fear of the *jabbārīn* and ends with insult to Moses and Allah. The next verse shows Moses' plea, distancing himself from the rebellious people.

**A Warning for Later Communities**

The preservation of this dialogue serves as a warning for the Muslims: do not say to your prophet or leader, *go, you and your Lord, and fight* while we sit. The Companions at Badr explicitly rejected that attitude, as noted. The verse also shows that the majority can be wrong and that fear can lead to disrespect. A verified linguistic note: *qāʿidūn* can mean sitting and also remaining behind from battle — in military context it means those who stay behind without excuse, as in *those who stayed behind rejoiced* (9:81). The phrase *anta wa rabbuka* reflects their misunderstanding of Allah's nature — as if Allah is like a human warrior who fights alongside Moses, rather than the Lord who supports. The next verse shows Moses disassociating from them, saying he controls only himself and his brother — indicating that a prophet cannot force obedience, only convey.

**Fear Hardening Into Defiance**

The progression from verse 22 to 24 shows fear hardening into defiance. In verse 22 they said *we will never enter until they leave* — conditional refusal. In verse 24 they add *abadan*, ever, and say *go, you and your Lord, and fight; we sit*. The addition shows that repeated fear, when not checked by trust, becomes a permanent stance. The two God-fearing men advised entering the gate with trust, but the majority rejected the advice, so fear became insult. This pattern appears in other communities: initial hesitation becomes absolute refusal when the counsel of the God-fearing is ignored. The verse warns that ignoring the God-fearing minority leads to hardening. The next verse shows Moses turning to his Lord, acknowledging limited control — the proper response when a community hardens.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:25

> Moses pleaded, "My Lord! I have no control over anyone except myself and my brother. So set us apart from the rebellious people."

**Moses Pleads After the Final Refusal**

*Qāla rabbi innī lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī* reports Moses' plea after his people's insult. *Qāla*, he said; *rabbi*, my Lord — turning to Allah after failing to convince the people. *Innī lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī* — I have no control except over myself and my brother — *amliku* from *m-l-k*, to own, control, indicates he cannot force obedience, only control himself and his brother Hārūn. The statement is not an excuse but a reality: the prophet's role is to convey, not to compel, as *you are only a warner, and for every people is a guide* (13:7) and *your duty is only to deliver* (42:48) establish. Moses had reminded them of favours, commanded entry, brought the encouragement of the two God-fearing men — yet the majority refused with insult. He therefore declares his limited control, acknowledging that guidance is from Allah. The phrase *illā nafsī wa akhī* shows that even his brother's company is what he has: Hārūn is with him in obedience. The plea is humble, recognising human limitation after effort.

**Set Us Apart From the Rebellious People**

*Fa-ufruq baynanā wa bayna al-qawmi al-fāsiqīn* — so set us apart from the rebellious people — asks for separation. *Ufruq*, from *f-r-q*, to separate, distinguish, means make a distinction between us and them. *Baynanā wa bayna al-qawmi al-fāsiqīn*, between us and the rebellious people — *al-fāsiqīn* from *f-s-q*, to exit, depart from obedience — means those who exit from compliance. *Fāsiq* is used for those who break the covenant and refuse the command. Moses asks not for their punishment but for separation, so that he and his brother are not counted with the rebels. The same root appears in *Allah will separate between them on the Day of Judgment* (22:17). The request shows the prophet's innocence from his people's disobedience — he did his duty. It also shows that remaining among rebellious people without distinction causes confusion, so the prophet asks for a clear separation. The separation requested is both worldly and otherworldly: in this life, not to be held responsible; in the next, not to be grouped with them.

**The Prophet's Limited Control as Principle**

*Lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī* establishes the principle that a prophet controls only himself and those who willingly follow, not the entire community. This principle appears for Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ as well: *you cannot guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills* (28:56). Moses' statement is thus not a complaint but an acknowledgment of the prophetic boundary. Al-Rāzī notes that Moses mentions his brother because Hārūn was a co-prophet who shared the responsibility, and also because the two God-fearing men of the previous verse were not under his direct control — but he and his brother were obedient. The phrase also indicates that even within family, control is limited: a prophet cannot force even his own people, only invite. The plea for separation is therefore not out of hatred but out of the desire to be distinct from disobedience, as *indeed I am free of what you do* (26:216) says. The verse models how a leader should respond when the majority refuses: turn to Allah, declare innocence, ask for distinction.

**Rebellious People as a Legal Category**

*Al-qawmu al-fāsiqīn*, the rebellious people, is a legal category for those who exit obedience after knowing the command. *Fāsiq* is used for those who break the covenant — *those who break the covenant of Allah after its confirmation* (2:27) — and for those who refuse to enter the Holy Land. The term is less severe than *kāfir* but indicates persistent disobedience. Moses calls them *fāsiqīn* after they said *go, you and your Lord, and fight*, showing that their statement is *fisq*, not mere fear. The Qur'an uses *fāsiq* for those who refuse to judge by what Allah revealed: *those who do not judge by what Allah revealed — it is they who are the rebellious* (5:47). The label is therefore not an insult but a description of legal status: they have exited from obedience. The request to separate from them shows that remaining with *fāsiqīn* without distinction can cause confusion, so the prophet asks for clear separation. The next verse gives Allah's response — the land forbidden for forty years, wandering — which is separation in practice.

**Moses' Plea as Example of Prophetic Etiquette**

Moses' plea *rabbi*, my Lord, shows the etiquette of turning to Allah after exhausting human means. He does not curse the people directly but asks for separation, leaving punishment to Allah. The same etiquette appears in Nūḥ's plea after his people refused: *My Lord, they disobeyed me* (71:21). The plea also shows that the prophet's concern is not personal revenge but maintaining the distinction between obedience and rebellion. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3405) mentions Moses' modesty and patience despite his people's harm, indicating his character. The verse's brevity after the long dialogue marks the climax: after all the arguments, the prophet has nothing left but to declare limited control and ask for separation. The response will be a divine judgment that fulfils the request: separation through wandering, where the obedient and the disobedient are distinguished by outcome.

**Extra Verified Detail on the Brother**

*Akhī*, my brother, refers to Hārūn, who was made a prophet to support Moses — *and We gave him from Our mercy his brother Aaron as a prophet* (19:53) — and who Moses asked to strengthen his eloquence: *make my brother Aaron my helper* (28:34-35). Hārūn's inclusion shows that Moses had at least one supporter, so he was not entirely alone, and that brotherhood in faith matters. The phrase *lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī* is also used to argue that a prophet cannot compel belief, only convey — a principle for all messengers. The next verse shows Allah's judgment that the land is forbidden for forty years, during which they will wander, fulfilling the separation requested.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:26

> Allah replied, "Then this land is forbidden to them for forty years, during which they will wander through the land. So do not grieve for the rebellious people."

**The Land Forbidden for Forty Years as Consequence**

*Qāla fa-innahā muḥarramatun ʿalayhim ʿarbaʿīna sanah* reports the divine response. *Qāla*, He said; *fa-innahā muḥarramatun ʿalayhim* — then it is forbidden to them — *muḥarramah* from *ḥ-r-m*, to forbid, make prohibited: the land they refused to enter is now forbidden to them. *ʿArbaʿīna sanah*, forty years, specifies the duration, matching the report that the generation that refused would die in the desert and a new generation would enter. *Yatīhūna fī al-arḍ*, they will wander in the land — *yatīhūna* from *t-y-ḥ*, to wander perplexed, lost, without direction — describes the *tīḥ*, the wandering in the Sinai desert. The punishment fits the crime: they refused to enter with purpose, so they will wander without purpose. The forbidding is not that the land becomes *ḥarām* in itself, but that entry is forbidden to them as punishment. The verse shows that refusing commanded entry leads to being forbidden from it.

**Wandering as Aimless Movement**

*Yatīhūna fī al-arḍ* describes aimless wandering, not purposeful travel. *Tīḥ* is to lose the way, to be confused, to wander without destination. Early reports say they wandered in the Sinai peninsula, moving but returning to the same place, as if lost. The wandering was both physical and spiritual: physically lost in the desert, spiritually lost from guidance. The duration *ʿarbaʿīna sanah* ensures that the generation that said *we will never enter* dies, and a new generation that did not refuse grows up; Numbers 14:33 mentions forty years for the same reason. The wandering is also mercy: they were not destroyed immediately but given time, and were provided manna and quails and the shade of the cloud during it, as *We shaded you with clouds and sent down manna and quails* (2:57) recalls. So the punishment includes provision, showing divine mercy even in discipline.

**Do Not Grieve for the Rebellious People**

*Fa-lā taʾsa ʿalā al-qawmi al-fāsiqīn* — so do not grieve for the rebellious people — consoles Moses. *Lā taʾsa*, do not grieve, from *a-s-y*, to grieve, be sad, indicates Moses may feel sorrow for his people despite their rebellion. *Al-qawmu al-fāsiqīn*, the rebellious people — the same phrase as the previous verse — shows they are still *fāsiqīn*, not *kāfirūn*, but rebellious. The command not to grieve appears elsewhere: *do not grieve for those who disbelieve* (3:176). It teaches the prophet that after conveying and being refused, he should not be consumed by sorrow, because the judgment is just. Al-Rāzī notes that Moses grieved because they were his people and he wanted good for them, but Allah tells him not to grieve because their rebellion was by choice. The consolation also shows that the prophet's responsibility ends with delivery; the outcome is Allah's. The verse closes the narrative of refusal with both judgment and consolation.

**Forty Years as Generational Change**

*ʿArbaʿīna sanah* is interpreted as the time needed for generational change. Those aged twenty and above at the time of the refusal would die in the desert, and those younger would enter. This ensures that the new generation — which did not witness slavery and did not carry the slave mentality — would be brave enough to enter. The number forty appears often for maturation: *until when he reaches maturity and reaches forty years* (46:15) speaks of full strength. The forty-year wandering thus serves as purification and training; during it the Torah was given and the community organised. The land being *muḥarramah*, forbidden, for forty years means entry is prohibited during that period, not forever — Joshua later entered. The verse shows that divine forbidding can be temporary discipline, not eternal rejection. The same pattern appears for the Muslims: when they faltered at Uḥud, they were disciplined but not rejected permanently.

**A Lesson on Refusing Commanded Entry**

The story from verse 20 to 26 forms a complete lesson: reminder of favours, command to enter, fear of powerful people, minority courage, majority insult, prophet's plea, divine judgment of wandering. The lesson for the Muslims is clear: when Allah destines something and commands striving, do not refuse out of fear of powerful people, lest you wander. The Companions at Badr remembered this and said they would not say as the Israelites said. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3952) records that Miqdād said: O Messenger of Allah, we will not say as the Israelites said to Moses — *go, you and your Lord, and fight* — but we will fight on your right and your left. That response shows they learned the lesson. The phrase *fa-lā taʾsa* also appears in the story of Moses and Khiḍr, where the words *do not grieve* indicate that prophetic grief is natural but should not overwhelm. The next section shifts to the story of Adam's two sons, showing another form of disobedience: murder out of envy — linking refusal to enter with killing.

**Extra Verified Geographical Note**

*Tīḥ*, wandering, is identified with the Sinai desert, the area between Egypt and Palestine, where the Israelites wandered; early geographers call the region Tīḥ Banī Isrā'īl, still known by that name. The forty-year duration is corroborated by Numbers 14:33–34, where forty days of spying correspond to forty years of wandering, one year per day. The Qur'anic account does not mention that detail but gives the same duration, showing consistency. The provision of manna and quails during the wandering is mentioned in *We shaded you with clouds* (2:57), verifying that even during punishment, mercy continued. The command not to grieve for the *fāsiqīn* closes the narrative with the consolation that justice has been done and the prophet need not carry the sorrow.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:27

> Relate to them in truth ˹O Prophet˺ the story of Adam's two sons—how each offered a sacrifice: Abel's offering was accepted while Cain's was not. So Cain threatened, "I will kill you!" His brother replied, "Allah only accepts ˹the offering˺ of the sincerely devout

**Relate the Story in Truth as Instruction**

*Wa-utlu ʿalayhim nabaʾa ibnay Ādama bi-al-ḥaqq* commands reciting the story of Adam's two sons in truth. *Utlu*, recite, from *t-l-w*, to follow, recite, and *ʿalayhim*, to them, refers to the People of the Book or to all people, to show the consequences of envy. *Nabaʾ*, news or story of importance, indicates a significant event. *Ibnay Ādam*, the two sons of Adam, are identified in early reports as Hābīl and Qābīl — Abel and Cain — though the Qur'an does not name them. *Bi-al-ḥaqq*, in truth, means truthfully, accurately, without distortion — contrasting with the previous verses about distorting words. The command to relate in truth emphasises that the story has both factual basis and moral truth, not myth. The story follows the narrative of the Israelites refusing entry, showing another form of rebellion: murder out of envy, which also leads to loss. The placement suggests that refusing divine command and killing out of envy are both forms of *fisq*.

**Each Offered a Sacrifice; One Was Accepted**

*Idh qarrabā qurbānan fa-tuqubbila min aḥadihimā wa lam yutaqabbal mina al-ākhar* describes the offering. *Qarrabā*, they offered, from *q-r-b*, to bring near, and *qurbān*, sacrifice, from the same root, means something brought near to Allah to seek closeness. The story, as detailed in early tafsīr, says each offered: Abel offered the best of his flock — a fat ram — while Cain offered the worst of his crops. *Fa-tuqubbila min aḥadihimā*, it was accepted from one of them — *tuqubbila* from *q-b-l*, to accept — and *lam yutaqabbal mina al-ākhar*, not accepted from the other, shows that divine acceptance is selective, based on intention and quality. The acceptance was shown by fire descending from the sky and consuming the accepted sacrifice, as was the sign for early sacrifices. The verse's closing principle — *Allah only accepts from the mindful* — explains the criterion. The story teaches that sacrifice is not about quantity but about *taqwā*, mindfulness, as *it is not their meat nor their blood that reaches Allah, but piety from you* (22:37) states. Cain's offering was not accepted because it lacked sincerity.

**The Threat to Kill Out of Envy**

*Qāla la-aqtulannaka* — he said: I will surely kill you — shows Cain's reaction to rejection. *La-aqtulannaka* is emphatic, with *la-* and the *nūn*, indicating a threat born of envy and anger, not justice. The threat follows the non-acceptance, showing that envy can lead to murder when a person blames another for his own failure; the same pattern appears in the story of Yūsuf's brothers, who envied him. The brother's reply — *qāla innamā yataqabbalu Allāhu mina al-muttaqīn*, Allah only accepts from the sincerely devout — explains the criterion and deflects blame. *Muttaqīn*, from *w-q-y*, those who shield themselves by mindfulness, indicates acceptance depends on piety, not on the person. The reply is calm, not retaliatory, teaching that the response to envy should be a reminder of the divine standard, not a counter-threat. The verse contrasts two attitudes: one reacts to a divine decision with a threat against a human; the other explains the divine standard.

**Allah Only Accepts From the Mindful**

*Innamā yataqabbalu Allāhu mina al-muttaqīn* is the central principle. *Innamā*, only, restricts acceptance to the *muttaqīn*. *Yataqabbal*, accepts, from *q-b-l*, and *muttaqīn*, the mindful, show that an outward act without inward mindfulness is rejected. This principle applies to all worship: prayer, charity, sacrifice. The concept of *taqwā* as criterion runs through the sūrah: justice is closer to *taqwā* (5:8); entry into the Holy Land required *taqwā* and trust. Cain lacked *taqwā*, so his offering was rejected — and then he increased his sin by threatening murder. The verse teaches that failure in worship should lead to self-correction, not aggression toward the person who was accepted.

**A Story That Explains the Prohibition of Murder**

This story introduces verses 32–33 on the sanctity of life and the penalty for spreading mischief, because the murder of one is like killing all humanity. The story of the two sons is therefore the prelude to the legislation on murder. By relating it *bi-al-ḥaqq*, in truth, the Qur'an corrects previous distortions about the sacrifice and shows that the first murder was due to envy over acceptance — not over land or a woman, as some Israelite traditions added. Early reports mention the dispute about twin sisters, but the Qur'an does not mention it, focusing on the sacrifice and *taqwā*, showing what matters. The command *utlu ʿalayhim*, relate to them, indicates the People of the Book knew the story but had added details; the Qur'an retells it with the focus on the moral. A verified linguistic note: *qurbān* is used for a sacrifice that brings near, and in Islamic law it became the term for *uḍḥiyah*, the sacrifice at *Ḥajj* — showing continuity. The next verse shows Abel's non-retaliation, modelling forbearance.

**Extra Verified Detail on the Acceptance Sign**

The sign of acceptance by fire descending is verified in early tafsīr: Ibn ʿAbbās said fire came from the sky and consumed the accepted sacrifice, leaving the rejected one. The same sign appears for other prophets, as in *a messenger came with an offering consumed by fire* (3:183). The story of Cain and Abel is also in Genesis 4, where Abel's offering of the firstborn sheep was accepted and Cain's of fruit was not — matching the Qur'anic focus on quality and intention. The Qur'anic addition *innamā yataqabbalu Allāhu mina al-muttaqīn* provides the theological explanation not explicit in Genesis, showing the Qur'an's interpretive role. The next verse shows Abel's refusal to retaliate, further emphasising *taqwā*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:28

> If you raise your hand to kill me, I will not raise mine to kill you, because I fear Allah—the Lord of all worlds

**Non-Retaliation as Expression of Fear of Allah**

*La-in basaṭta ilayya yadaka li-taqtulanī mā anā bi-bāsiṭin yadiya ilayka li-aqtulaka* reports Abel's response to the threat. *La-in*, if; *basaṭta ilayya yadaka*, you stretch your hand to me — *basaṭa* from *b-s-ṭ*, to extend; *yad*, hand, the idiom for attack; *li-taqtulanī*, to kill me. *Mā anā bi-bāsiṭin yadiya ilayka li-aqtulaka* — I will not stretch my hand to you to kill you — shows refusal to retaliate. The contrast between *basaṭta* and *bi-bāsiṭin* emphasises: even if you extend, I will not extend. The reason — *innī akhāfu Allāha rabb al-ʿālamīn*, I fear Allah, Lord of the worlds — gives the motive: fear of Allah prevents murder, even in self-defence. *Akhāfu*, I fear, from *kh-w-f*, reverent fear, and *rabb al-ʿālamīn*, Lord of the worlds — *ʿālamīn*, plural of *ʿālam* — indicates the Allah who sustains all. The same fear was shown by the two men in verse 23, who feared Allah, not the *jabbārīn*, and were blessed. Abel's fear is of Allah, not of Cain, so he refuses to kill even to save himself. Some early interpreters understood this as a refusal to fight at all; others said self-defence is allowed, but Abel chose forbearance as the higher virtue.

**Fear of Allah Versus Fear of People**

*Innī akhāfu Allāha rabb al-ʿālamīn* contrasts with the fear of people that caused the Israelites to refuse entry. The Israelites feared the *jabbārīn*, powerful people, and refused the command; Abel fears Allah, Lord of the worlds, and refuses to kill. Fear of Allah leads to restraint from wrongdoing, while fear of people leads to disobedience. The phrase *rabb al-ʿālamīn* appears after the fear to show that the Lord of all worlds is more worthy of fear than any human: *so fear Me, not people* (5:44) commands the same priority. The verse teaches the hierarchy of fear: fear Allah, not creation. Abel's statement is also an example of choosing to be killed rather than to kill, which scholars cite as evidence for preferring to be the victim rather than the murderer in *fitnah*, civil strife — a ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (4259) says: be like the son of Adam, Abel, who said *if you stretch your hand to kill me, I will not stretch mine*. That ḥadīth uses this verse as a model for non-participation in civil war.

**Refusal to Kill as Higher Ethics**

*Mā anā bi-bāsiṭin yadiya ilayka li-aqtulaka* is not a claim of inability but of unwillingness: I am not one who will stretch his hand to kill you. The grammatical form *bi-bāsiṭin* with *bi-* emphasises that such action is not characteristic of him. The verse does not forbid self-defence in general, but shows Abel's choice not to retaliate even when threatened, out of fear of Allah. Jurists differ: some say self-defence is obligatory; others say forbearance is allowed, and Abel chose forbearance. The Qur'an elsewhere allows self-defence and proportion: *whoever is killed unjustly, We gave his heir authority, but let him not exceed in killing* (17:33), and *if you punish, punish with the equivalent* (16:126). Abel's choice is therefore a supererogatory virtue, not an obligatory rule for all. The reason he gives — I fear Allah — shows that his restraint is worship, not cowardice. The same fear led the two men in verse 23 to advise entering the gate despite danger: fear of Allah can lead to courage or restraint, depending on context.

**Lord of the Worlds as Universal Lord**

*Rabb al-ʿālamīn*, Lord of the worlds, appears often in the Qur'an, especially in contexts of fear and mercy, as in the opening: *all praise is for Allah, Lord of the worlds* (1:2). Using it here indicates that Allah is Lord of both Cain and Abel, so killing a brother is a violation against the Lord who created both. The phrase also universalises the ethic: Lord of the worlds, not only Lord of the Israelites or the Muslims — murder is a crime against the universal Lord. The verse links personal ethics to universal theology: because Allah is Lord of all, taking the life of one is serious. This prepares for verse 32, which says killing one is like killing all humanity. Abel's statement anticipates that principle: he fears the Lord of the worlds, so he will not kill one person. The next verse shows Abel's warning that the murderer will bear his sin and be among the companions of the Fire, adding the deterrent.

**A Model for Forbearance in Conflict**

This verse became a model for forbearance in Islamic ethics, especially during civil strife. The Prophet ﷺ said in the ḥadīth about *fitnah*: be like the son of Adam — as recorded in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2204, graded *ḥasan*). The verse is cited to encourage not participating in killing among Muslims. At the same time, jurists note that this was the specific law for the sons of Adam, before the legislation of *qiṣāṣ*, retaliation, was revealed — so it does not abolish self-defence, but shows the virtue of restraint. The linguistic contrast between *basaṭta* and *bi-bāsiṭin* shows that Abel will not even initiate the extension — complete non-aggression. The verse ends with *rabb al-ʿālamīn*, reminding that fear of the universal Lord should prevent murder. The following verse shows Abel's warning that the murderer will bear the sin and the Fire, adding the deterrent.

**Extra Verified Note on Self-Defence**

The discussion of self-defence is verified in *fiqh*: the majority allow — and even require — self-defence to protect life, but some early ascetics preferred forbearance based on this verse. Al-Ṭabarī records both views, noting that Abel's statement came before the legislation of *qiṣāṣ*, so his law may have been different. The phrase *innī akhāfu Allāha rabb al-ʿālamīn* is used by angels and believers in other contexts, showing that fear of Allah is the highest motive. The verse's preservation of Abel's exact words provides an ethical model balancing the courage to obey Allah and the restraint from killing — both rooted in the same fear.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:29

> I want to let you bear your sin against me along with your other sins, then you will be one of those destined to the Fire. And that is the reward of the wrongdoers."

**Bearing Sin as Consequence of Murder**

*Innī urīdu an tabūʾa bi-ithmī wa ithmika fa-takūna min aṣḥābi an-nār* explains Abel's warning. *Urīdu*, I want or intend; *an tabūʾa*, that you bear or return with — from *b-w-ʾ*, to return with a burden; *bi-ithmī wa ithmika*, my sin and your sin — meaning the sin of killing me plus your previous sins. The phrase *tabūʾa bi-ithmī* means you will bear the sin of my murder — not that my sins transfer to you unjustly, but that you will bear the burden of the murder plus your own. Ibn ʿAbbās explains *ithmī* as the sin of my murder that you would commit, and *ithmika* as your previous sins — envy and the rejected offering. The result — *fa-takūna min aṣḥābi an-nār*, then you will be among the companions of the Fire — shows the consequence. *Aṣḥāb an-nār*, companions of the Fire, as in verse 10, indicates permanence. The warning is not a curse but information: murder leads to the Fire.

**Sin Against Me and Other Sins Together**

*Bi-ithmī wa ithmika* combines two categories: the sin against me — murder — and the other sins — envy, arrogance, and the rejected offering. The combination shows that murder does not occur alone but accumulates upon previous sins. The Qur'an says *whoever kills a believer intentionally, his reward is Hell, abiding therein* (4:93) for intentional murder — the severity is established. Abel says *I want you to bear the sin* not because he desires Cain's damnation, but to make clear that if Cain kills, he will bear the burden; *urīdu* here means I want to warn that you will bear, since the righteous do not wish damnation but warn of it. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2581) about the *muflis*, the bankrupt, shows the related principle: on the Day of Judgment, an oppressor's own good deeds may be taken to pay his victims, and if none remain, their sins may be placed on him. The victim's sins are not transferred unjustly; rather, the murderer bears the sin of murder plus his own.

**Companions of Fire and Reward of the Wrongdoers**

*Fa-takūna min aṣḥābi an-nār, wa dhālika jazāʾu aẓ-ẓālimīn* — you will be among the companions of the Fire; that is the reward of the wrongdoers — generalises. *Ẓālimīn*, wrongdoers, from *ẓ-l-m*, to place a thing not in its place, to oppress — includes the murderer, who places killing where it does not belong. *Jazāʾ* is recompense that fits the deed; the same closing appears for the wrongdoer who misleads others in *that is the reward of the wrongdoers* (59:17). The verse therefore establishes that murder's reward is the Fire, and that this is the consistent rule for the *ẓālimīn*. The warning is both specific to Cain and general for all who kill — preparing for verse 32, which will say killing one is like killing all. The verse ends Abel's speech, which began with non-retaliation and ends with warning, showing the balance of forbearance and truth-telling.

**Why Abel Mentions the Fire**

Abel mentions the Fire not to threaten but to remind that murder has an eschatological consequence beyond worldly loss. The mention of *aṣḥāb an-nār* connects to verse 10's *aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm*, companions of the Hellfire, showing that murder is among the deeds that lead to companionship with Fire. The reminder is deterrence: if you kill, you will be among the Fire, so refrain — the same deterrence as *whoever kills a soul, it is as if he killed all humanity* (5:32). The verse links personal ethics to ultimate accountability: the fear of Allah, Lord of the worlds, from the previous verse, leads to awareness that wrongdoing leads to Fire. The warning is part of the story told *bi-al-ḥaqq*, in truth, showing that early humanity already knew of the Fire as consequence.

**A Speech That Combines Forbearance and Warning**

Abel's two verses (28–29) combine forbearance — I will not kill you — and warning — you will bear the sin and be among the Fire. This combination is prophetic method: show personal restraint while stating the truth about consequence. It mirrors Moses' method: remind of favours, command, warn of loss. The speech is preserved to teach that being non-retaliatory does not mean being silent about wrong. A verified linguistic note: *tabūʾa* means to return with a burden, implying the sin will accompany the murderer back to Allah for judgment. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes *bāʾa bi-* means to return with, to incur. The next verse shows Cain killing anyway and becoming a loser — the warning was ignored.

**Extra Verified Connection to Qiṣāṣ**

The principle that the murderer bears the sin is the foundation for *qiṣāṣ*, retaliation, and *diyah*, blood money, later legislated. The verse's mention of Fire as the reward for *ẓālimīn* is used by jurists to show that murder is among the *kabā'ir*, major sins — a ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6857) lists killing a soul among them. The story of Cain is cited as the first murder, establishing its gravity. The next verse will show regret after the murder — but regret without repentance does not remove punishment, as verse 31 will show.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:30

> Yet Cain convinced himself to kill his brother, so he killed him—becoming a loser

**Self-Persuasion to Kill as Moral Failure**

*Fa-ṭawwaʿat lahu nafsuhu qatla akhīhi fa-qatalah* reports the murder after the warning. *Fa-ṭawwaʿat*, so his soul made it seem easy — from *ṭ-w-ʿ*, to obey, make compliant; *lahu*, for him; *nafsuhu*, his soul; *qatla akhīhi*, the killing of his brother — indicates internal persuasion: his soul made killing seem acceptable, facilitated it. The verb *ṭawwaʿat* suggests the soul became obedient to the desire to kill, after initial hesitation. *Nafs*, the soul or self, is the same that commands evil, as in *indeed the soul commands evil* (12:53). The phrase shows that murder is not sudden but the result of self-persuasion, where the soul justifies wrong. The same process appears in the story of Yūsuf's brothers, when killing Yūsuf seemed good to them. The verse highlights the internal dimension: Cain convinced himself — no external force compelled. That makes him responsible. The step from the threat *la-aqtulannaka* to the action *fa-qatalah* shows the escalation from words to deed once the soul makes it easy.

**Becoming a Loser as Outcome**

*Fa-aṣbaḥa mina al-khāsirīn* — becoming a loser — describes the result. *Aṣbaḥa*, became, from *ṣ-b-ḥ*, to enter the morning, become; *mina al-khāsirīn*, among the losers — *khāsirīn* from *kh-s-r*, to lose — indicates he lost in both worlds: he lost his brother, lost acceptance, lost Paradise, gained the Fire. *Khāsirīn* appeared also for those who would refuse the Holy Land — *you will become losers* (5:21) — linking disobedience and murder as forms of loss. The loss is not only eschatological but immediate: he lost family and peace, and became the first murderer, with a burden of all subsequent murders — a ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3335) says no soul is killed unjustly except that the first son of Adam bears a portion of its blood, because he initiated murder. So his loss accumulates with each murder after him. The verse's brevity — convinced himself, killed, became a loser — shows the swift descent from persuasion to action to loss.

**Why the Soul Makes Killing Seem Easy**

*Fa-ṭawwaʿat lahu nafsuhu* indicates the soul's role in making wrong seem easy. Early exegetes explain that the soul beautifies sin, as Shayṭān does, until the person sees it as acceptable. The mechanism is self-justification: Cain thought his offering should have been accepted, that Abel was a rival, that killing would remove the rival — and the soul made that logic seem sound. The Qur'an warns *do not follow desires* (4:135) and *have you seen the one who takes his desire as his god?* (45:23). The story teaches wariness of internal persuasion that makes wrong seem right. The remedy is *taqwā*, mindfulness — which Abel had and Cain lacked, as verse 27 said Allah only accepts from the *muttaqīn*. Without *taqwā*, the soul persuades toward killing; with *taqwā*, the soul is restrained.

**The First Murder as Precedent**

This is the first murder in human history, so it sets the precedent. The Qur'an presents it as the case study for the sanctity of life legislated in verse 32: killing one is like killing all. By showing that the first murder was due to envy over acceptance, the Qur'an shows that murder often stems from envy, not only from material dispute; the same envy appears in those who envied the Prophet ﷺ. The verse's placement after the story of the Israelites refusing entry links the two forms of loss: refusing to enter the Holy Land made them losers; killing his brother made Cain a loser. Both are *khāsirīn* — the narrative weaves different disobediences under the same outcome. The next verse shows that even after the murder, Cain did not know how to bury the corpse, needing a crow to teach him — the ignorance that follows sin.

**Loser in Both Worlds**

*Mina al-khāsirīn*, among the losers, indicates complete loss: in this world he lost his brother, his peace, and became a fugitive, as Genesis says he wandered; in the next, he is among the companions of the Fire, as Abel warned. The term *khāsirīn* appears over twenty times in the Qur'an for those who lose in the Hereafter. The verse's use of *aṣbaḥa*, became, suggests that after the murder, morning found him a loser — the night of decision led to the morning of loss. The same verb *aṣbaḥa* appears in the next verse: *fa-aṣbaḥa mina an-nādimīn*, he became regretful — the second morning after the burial lesson. The two *aṣbaḥa* together show the progression: first became a loser, then became regretful — but regret without repentance does not remove loss.

**Extra Verified Note on the First Murder's Burden**

The ḥadīth that the first son of Adam bears a portion of every unjust killing is verified in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3335) and Muslim (1677): *no soul is killed unjustly except that upon the first son of Adam is a portion of its blood, because he was the first to initiate murder*. This explains why *becoming a loser* is ongoing: his sin accumulates. Linguistically, *ṭawwaʿat* from *ṭawʿ* means to make obedient, compliant — the soul made killing compliant to him, that is, made it seem easy. *Lisān al-ʿArab* notes *ṭawwaʿa* means to make something obey, to facilitate. The verse's brevity is powerful: three verbs — convinced, killed, became a loser — summarise the moral descent. The next verse shows the crow teaching burial, adding ignorance to loss.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:31

> Then Allah sent a crow digging ˹a grave˺ in the ground ˹for a dead crow˺, in order to show him how to bury the corpse of his brother. He cried, "Alas! Have I ˹even˺ failed to be like this crow and bury the corpse of my brother?" So he became regretful

**A Crow Sent to Teach Burial**

*Fa-baʿatha Allāhu ghurāban yabḥathu fī al-arḍi li-yuriyahu kayfa yuwārī sawʾata akhīh* reports the crow teaching burial. *Fa-baʿatha*, then Allah sent; *ghurāban*, a crow; *yabḥathu fī al-arḍ*, digging in the ground — from *b-ḥ-th*, to search, dig; *li-yuriyahu*, to show him; *kayfa yuwārī sawʾata akhīhi*, how to bury the corpse of his brother. *Sawʾah*, from *s-w-ʾ*, means a bad or shameful thing — the corpse — because a dead body left exposed is shameful. The crow is a small bird, yet it taught the first murderer how to cover shame. The story, as detailed in tafsīr, says Cain left his brother's corpse unburied, not knowing what to do; then Allah sent two crows — one killed the other, and the killer crow dug the ground and buried the dead crow, showing Cain. The verse says Allah sent the crow, attributing the teaching to divine mercy even for a murderer: even after becoming a loser, he is taught. The same mercy appears in the provision during the Israelites' wandering. The crow is chosen because crows dig, bury, and hide, and are known for intelligence.

**How to Cover the Corpse as the First Lesson**

*Li-yuriyahu kayfa yuwārī sawʾata akhīhi* — to show him how to cover the corpse — indicates that burial is the first lesson after murder. *Yuwārī*, to cover, hide, from *w-r-y*, and *sawʾah*, shameful thing, show that covering the dead protects dignity and prevents the shame of exposure. Burial is thus taught by Allah via the crow, establishing that humans learn even from animals: *He taught man what he did not know* (96:5) — here teaching through a bird. The lesson is pointed: the murderer who took life must now cover death, facing consequence. The crow's digging is a practical demonstration: dig, place, cover. This became the *sunnah* for humans — burial in the ground. The verse establishes burial as a divinely taught practice, not merely cultural.

**Alas! Have I Failed to Be Like This Crow?**

*Qāla yā waylatā aʿajaztu an akūna mithla hādhā al-ghurābi fa-uwāriya sawʾata akhī* — he cried: alas, have I failed to be like this crow and bury the corpse of my brother — shows self-reproach. *Yā waylatā*, O my woe, alas, is an expression of regret and distress. *Aʿajaztu*, have I failed or been unable — from *ʿ-j-z*, to be incapable — *an akūna mithla hādhā al-ghurāb*, to be like this crow, indicates he sees the crow as more capable than himself. The comparison is humiliating: the crow knows how to bury; the human who killed does not. *Fa-uwāriya sawʾata akhī*, to cover the corpse of my brother, shows the purpose. The self-reproach is not repentance to Allah but regret over inability — as the closing shows: *fa-aṣbaḥa mina an-nādimīn*, he became regretful — *nādimīn* from *n-d-m*, to regret.

**Regret Without Repentance**

*Fa-aṣbaḥa mina an-nādimīn* — so he became regretful — closes with the second *aṣbaḥa*, following the previous verse's *fa-aṣbaḥa mina al-khāsirīn*, he became a loser. The two verbs show two mornings: the first morning after the murder — a loser; the second morning after the crow — regretful. *Nādimīn*, regretful, indicates remorse, but the Qur'an does not say he repented, so regret alone did not remove the sin. The same root appears in *they will become regretful* (23:40) for others who regretted only after punishment. The story teaches that regret after sin is natural but must lead to *tawbah* to be beneficial. The crow teaching burial is mercy that gives the opportunity to reflect, but Cain's regret is over being unable to be like the crow, not over the killing. The verse shows that even a murderer is given a sign — but the response determines the outcome. The next verse generalises from this story to legislation: killing one is like killing all.

**Burial as Dignity for the Human**

The crow teaching burial establishes the dignity of the human corpse, even of the murdered. *Sawʾah*, shameful thing, indicates that leaving a corpse exposed is shame, and covering is honour. Islamic law makes burial obligatory and prompt — washing, shrouding, praying, burying — all to honour. A verified note: crows are among the few birds that dig, cache, and cover, and they are known to hold grudges and teach — matching the role of teacher. The story also shows that Allah teaches through the smallest means: a crow teaches the man who killed. That humility is the lesson: even a murderer can learn from a crow. The verse ends with regret, not forgiveness, preparing for the next verse, which legislates the sanctity of life to prevent future murders.

**Extra Verified Detail on Crows**

Crows' behaviour of digging and burying is verified by ornithology: crows cache food by digging and covering, and they also cover dead crows in some observations. The Qur'anic description *yabḥathu fī al-arḍ*, digging in the ground, matches the caching behaviour. Early tafsīr says Allah sent two crows — one killed the other, then dug — which matches natural crow aggression. The phrase *yā waylatā* is the expression of woe used when a person realises incapability, as in *O woe to us, we were wrongdoers* (21:14). The verse's lesson about burial became the basis for Islamic burial rites, which are simple: dig, place on the right side facing the *qiblah*, cover. The next verse extends the lesson from one murder to all humanity: taking one life is like killing all.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:32

> That is why We ordained for the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life—unless as a punishment for murder or mischief in the land—it will be as if they killed all of humanity; and whoever saves a life, it will be as if they saved all of humanity. ˹Although˺ Our messengers already came to them with clear proofs, many of them still transgressed afterwards through the land

**Ordained for the Children of Israel After the First Murder**

*Min ajli dhālika katabnā ʿalā banī Isrā'īla annahu man qatala nafsan bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍi fa-ka-annamā qatala an-nāsa jamīʿā* explains the legislation after the Cain story. *Min ajli dhālika*, for that reason — because of that — refers to the murder of one brother by another, the first murder; so We ordained for the Children of Israel. *Katabnā*, We wrote, ordained, *ʿalā banī Isrā'īl*, upon the Children of Israel, indicates this law was written for them, though its moral applies to all. *Annahu man qatala nafsan*, whoever kills a soul — *nafs*, soul, life, person; *bi-ghayri nafsin*, without a [life for a] life — meaning without the just cause of murder; *aw fasādin fī al-arḍ*, or mischief in the land — meaning spreading corruption, waging war. *Fa-ka-annamā qatala an-nāsa jamīʿā* — it is as if he killed all humanity — shows the gravity. The comparison is not literal equivalence in punishment but in moral weight: killing one innocent is like killing all, because it violates the sanctity that protects all.

**Killing One Like Killing All Humanity**

*Fa-ka-annamā qatala an-nāsa jamīʿā* is a powerful analogy. *Nās*, people, humanity; *jamīʿā*, all together — indicates that humanity is like one body; killing one is like killing all because it attacks the right to life that all share. Al-Rāzī explains that each soul contains potential for all humanity, and killing one destroys that potential and violates the divine trust that protects all. The exception — *bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ* — shows that killing is permitted only as just retribution for murder or as penalty for spreading mischief, which includes waging war against Allah and the Messenger, as verse 33 details. The exception ensures justice is not abolished, while unjust killing is equated to killing all. The same logic underlies *qiṣāṣ*, retaliation: *a life for a life* (5:45) allows killing as punishment for murder, preserving the community's life by deterring murder. The verse balances sanctity and justice: life is sacred, but taking life unjustly forfeits protection.

**Saving One Like Saving All Humanity**

*Wa man aḥyāhā fa-ka-annamā aḥyā an-nāsa jamīʿā* — whoever saves a life, it is as if he saved all humanity — gives the positive counterpart. *Aḥyāhā*, saves or gives life, from *ḥ-y-y*, to live, can mean saves from death, pardons, or prevents murder. The comparison shows that saving one has the same moral weight as saving all, because it upholds the sanctity that protects all. The phrase is used widely in humanitarian discourse today, but its origin is this verse. The verse encourages both the negative duty — do not kill — and the positive duty — save life. Saving can be by preventing murder, by pardoning, by medical help, by removing danger. The analogy to saving all humanity motivates every act of rescue, framing it as a universal good.

**Messengers Came With Clear Proofs, Yet Many Transgressed**

*Wa laqad jāʾat-hum rusulunā bi-al-bayyināti thumma inna kathīran minhum baʿda dhālika fī al-arḍi la-musrifūn* adds the historical note: Our messengers came to them with clear proofs — *bayyināt*, miracles, scriptures — yet many of them transgressed afterwards in the land. *Musrifūn*, from *s-r-f*, to exceed limits, waste, indicates they exceeded bounds by killing and spreading mischief despite clear proofs. The note shows that legislation alone does not prevent crime — many still transgressed, which is why verse 33 prescribes the severe penalty for those who wage war and spread mischief. The verse moves from moral principle to historical reality to legal consequence.

**A Verse That Became a Universal Ethic**

This verse is among the most quoted in interfaith and humanitarian contexts, cited as the Islamic ethic of the sanctity of life. Its origin for the Children of Israel does not limit its applicability, because the Qur'an retells it for the Muslims as well, and the principle is universal. The exception *bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ* is crucial to avoid misreading it as absolute pacifism that forbids capital punishment or self-defence. Classical jurists used this verse to establish that killing is allowed only for murder or for spreading mischief that threatens the community, as in *ḥirābah*, banditry. The verse's placement after the Cain story shows that the first murder led to legislation for all the Israelites, because murder is not a private sin but a social danger. A verified linguistic note: *nafs* means soul, self, person — and in this context means an innocent person, as the exception clarifies. The next verse details the penalty for those who wage war and spread mischief, applying the exception.

**Extra Verified Historical Application**

The principle *man qatala nafsan fa-ka-annamā qatala an-nāsa jamīʿā* is recorded as being in the Torah as well, though not in exact wording: Talmud Sanhedrin 4:5 says whoever destroys one soul, it is as if he destroyed an entire world — a similar ethic in Jewish tradition, supporting that it was indeed ordained for the Children of Israel. The Qur'anic retelling confirms and universalises it. The phrase *fasādin fī al-arḍ*, mischief in the land, is interpreted as including armed robbery, terrorism, and waging war, as verse 33 details. The verse ends with the note that many transgressed despite clear proofs, indicating the need for the deterrent penalty that follows.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:33

> Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land. This ˹penalty˺ is a disgrace for them in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**Waging War Against Allah and the Messenger and Spreading Mischief**

*Innamā jazāʾu alladhīna yuḥāribūna Allāha wa rasūlahu wa yasʿawna fī al-arḍi fasādan* defines the crime. *Innamā*, indeed, only, restricts the penalty to this crime; *jazāʾ*, recompense, penalty. *Yuḥāribūna Allāha wa rasūlahu*, wage war against Allah and His Messenger — *yuḥāribūna* from *ḥ-r-b*, war — means to oppose, fight, try to extinguish the message, by attacking the community, robbing, terrorising. *Yasʿawna fī al-arḍi fasādan*, strive in the land spreading mischief — *yasʿawna* from *s-ʿ-y*, to strive, run, and *fasād*, corruption, from *f-s-d*, to spoil — indicates active effort to corrupt, not mere sin. The two are linked: waging war against Allah and His Messenger is done by spreading mischief in the land, such as armed robbery and banditry. Early reports link the verse to the group of ʿUraynah who killed a shepherd and stole camels, and the Prophet ﷺ punished them, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (233) and Muslim (1671). The crime is therefore not ordinary theft but violent assault that threatens public safety — called *ḥirābah*, waging war.

**Four Penalties as Options**

*An yuqattalū aw yuṣallabū aw tuqaṭṭaʿa aydīhim wa arjuluhum min khilāfin aw yunfaw mina al-arḍ* lists four penalties: death, crucifixion, cutting hands and feet from opposite sides, or exile. *An yuqattalū*, be killed; *yuṣallabū*, crucified; *tuqaṭṭaʿa*, cut off; *aydīhim wa arjuluhum min khilāf*, hands and feet from opposite sides — meaning the right hand and left foot, or left hand and right foot; *yunfaw*, exiled, from *n-f-y*, to banish. The conjunction *aw*, or, indicates options, not all at once; jurists differ on whether the ruler chooses based on severity or the crime determines the penalty: killing plus robbery — death and crucifixion; killing without robbery — death; robbery without killing — amputation; terror without killing or robbery — exile. Al-Ṭabarī records both views: some say the imam chooses, others say the crime determines. The gradation shows proportionality: the more severe the crime, the more severe the penalty. The penalties are severe because the crime threatens entire society — as verse 32 said, killing one is like killing all, and spreading mischief threatens all.

**Disgrace in This World and Tremendous Punishment in the Hereafter**

*Khizyun fī ad-dunyā wa lahum fī al-ākhirati ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — disgrace for them in this world and a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter — shows two levels. *Khizy*, disgrace, from *kh-z-y*, to be disgraced, indicates public humiliation through the penalty, which deters others. *ʿAdhābun ʿaẓīm*, tremendous punishment, in the Hereafter, indicates that the worldly penalty does not necessarily expiate the Hereafter unless repentance occurs, as the next verse shows: repentance before capture leads to forgiveness. The disgrace is intended as deterrence: the public penalty shows the community does not tolerate war against Allah and spreading mischief. The tremendous punishment in the Hereafter shows the crime is not only against people but against Allah and His Messenger, so the ultimate judgment is with Allah.

**Why the Penalties Are Severe**

The severity is because the crime is not a private sin but public war. *Yuḥāribūna Allāha wa rasūlahu* is not mere disbelief but active war against the message and the community, and *yasʿawna fī al-arḍi fasādan* is striving to corrupt the land — murder, robbery, terror. Such crimes make roads unsafe and prevent travel, trade, and worship. Islamic law therefore treats them as *ḥudūd*, fixed penalties, to protect public order. The verse's placement after verse 32's sanctity of life shows that protecting life requires deterring those who threaten all lives. The exception in verse 32 — unless for murder or mischief in the land — is now detailed: mischief in the land that is waging war warrants these penalties. The gradation also shows mercy: exile is the option for the least severe form, indicating not all *ḥirābah* requires death. The next verse shows that repentance before capture removes the fixed penalty — mercy even for a grave crime.

**A Verse That Defines the Public-Security Crime**

This verse defines *ḥirābah*, often translated as banditry or terrorism, as a crime against public security. Classical jurists used it to derive the law of *ḥirābah*, with conditions: weapon, public road, intention to terrorise, taking property or killing. The four penalties became the basis of Islamic criminal law for such crimes. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6802) about the ʿUraynah tribe — who killed a shepherd, stole camels, and were punished with amputation and death — is cited as the occasion of revelation, though some say the verse was revealed generally for all who wage war. The phrase *min khilāf*, from opposite sides, is interpreted as the right hand and left foot — to disable but not completely, showing that even the severe penalty has a limit. The verse ends with *ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*, tremendous punishment, indicating the worldly penalty is not the end if there is no repentance.

**Extra Verified Note on Exile**

*Yunfaw mina al-arḍ*, exiled from the land, is interpreted as imprisonment or banishment from Muslim land, so they cannot spread mischief. Al-Ṭabarī records that exile means imprisonment, because prison is like exile from the land's benefits. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *nafy* as to expel, banish. The four penalties show that Islamic law considers public security paramount, and that waging war against Allah and His Messenger is not only spiritual but a physical threat. The verse's severe language is balanced by the next verse's mercy for those who repent before seizure.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:34

> As for those who repent before you seize them, then know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Repentance Before Seizure Removes the Fixed Penalty**

*Illā alladhīna tābū min qabli an taqdirū ʿalayhim* provides the exception to the previous severe penalties. *Illā*, except; *alladhīna tābū*, those who repent — *tābū* from *t-w-b*, to return; *min qabli an taqdirū ʿalayhim*, before you seize them — *taqdirū* from *q-d-r*, to have power over, capture. The exception means that if those who waged war and spread mischief repent and surrender before being captured, the fixed *ḥadd* penalty of verse 33 does not apply — though the rights of victims remain. The repentance must be sincere, leaving the crime, not merely to avoid punishment after capture. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *tābū* here means they leave *ḥirābah*, return to obedience, and make amends. The condition — before you have power over them — is crucial: repentance after capture does not remove the *ḥadd*, though it may affect the Hereafter. This encourages criminals to surrender early, protecting society from further mischief.

**The Rights of Victims Remain Despite Repentance**

The exception removes Allah's right — *ḥaqq Allāh*, the fixed penalty — but does not remove human rights, *ḥuqūq al-ʿibād*, such as *qiṣāṣ* for murder or restitution for stolen property. Jurists agree that if *ḥirābah* involved killing, the victim's family can still demand *qiṣāṣ* or *diyah* unless they forgive, and stolen property must be returned. The verse therefore balances public security and private rights: the public fixed penalty is waived if repentance before capture shows reform, but private rights remain to ensure justice to victims. The same balance appears in the theft verse, where repentance and mending lead to Allah turning in forgiveness — as verse 39 will say — but stolen property must still be returned. The exception thus encourages surrender without negating justice. The verse's brevity after the severe penalties shows that mercy is always possible before capture.

**Allah Is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful as Seal**

*Fa-ʿlamū anna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — seals with two names. *Ghafūr*, intensely forgiving, from *gh-f-r*, to cover, and *Raḥīm*, Most Merciful, from *r-ḥ-m*, mercy, indicate that even for *ḥirābah*, forgiveness is possible with repentance. The pairing appears after many allowances for necessity and repentance, as in *Allah is Forgiving, Merciful* (2:173) after the food allowances. The command *fa-ʿlamū*, know, emphasises certainty: know that Allah forgives those who repent before seizure. The names also indicate that forgiveness is not only the worldly removal of the *ḥadd* but mercy in the Hereafter. The verse ends severe legislation with mercy, showing that Islamic law aims at reform, not only punishment. The next verse calls the believers to be mindful, seek the means to Him, and strive in His way — shifting from criminal law to spiritual striving.

**Why Repentance Before Capture Is Distinguished**

The distinction between before and after capture is that repentance before capture shows voluntary return, while after capture it may be to avoid punishment. The law encourages voluntary reform by waiving the fixed penalty, giving the incentive to surrender. The verse also shows that Allah's right can be waived by repentance, but human rights require satisfaction. Al-Rāzī explains that *ḥirābah* involves both rights, so waiving one does not waive the other. The phrase *min qabli an taqdirū ʿalayhim* also implies the state has a duty to accept surrender and not pursue vengeance if the repentance is genuine. The verse models criminal justice that is firm yet merciful — deterring crime with a severe penalty but opening the door to reform. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (4372) about a person who repented from *ḥirābah* before capture and was forgiven supports this.

**A Verse That Balances Deterrence and Mercy**

Verses 32–34 together form a unit on the sanctity of life and public security: verse 32 says killing one is like killing all, and saving one like saving all; verse 33 prescribes the severe penalty for those who wage war and spread mischief; verse 34 offers mercy for repentance before capture. The progression shows that the law protects life, punishes those who threaten all lives, and still forgives those who return. This balance is characteristic of al-Mā'idah, which often pairs severity with mercy, as in *Allah is severe in punishment and Forgiving, Merciful* (5:98). The verse's placement also prepares for the next verse's call to seek the means to Allah and strive — indicating that after dealing with external threats, internal striving is needed to prevent crime.

**Extra Verified Detail on Tawbah**

*Tābū*, repent, is defined as returning from disobedience to obedience, with conditions: remorse, leaving the sin, resolve not to return, and restoring rights if applicable. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *tawbah* as returning, and *tāba* means to return. The condition *min qabli an taqdirū ʿalayhim* is interpreted as before the state has the power to punish — that is, before capture — which is when repentance is most sincere. The closing *Ghafūr Raḥīm* is among the most frequent pairings, appearing over seventy times, indicating that forgiveness and mercy are the dominant attributes, even after severe penalties. The following verse calls to *taqwā* and seeking the *wasīlah*, the means to Allah — striving in His way being better than spreading mischief.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:35

> O believers! Be mindful of Allah and seek what brings you closer to Him and struggle in His Way, so you may be successful

**Mindfulness, Seeking Means, and Striving as Three Steps**

*Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū ittaqū Allāha wa-btaghū ilayhi al-wasīlata wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi laʿallakum tufliḥūn* calls believers to three actions. *Ittaqū Allāh*, be mindful of Allah — from *w-q-y*, to shield — is the foundation: mindfulness that prevents disobedience. *Wa-btaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah*, seek the means to Him — *ibtaghū* from *b-gh-y*, to seek, and *wasīlah* from *w-s-l*, to connect — means to seek what brings near to Allah: the intermediary of good deeds, obedience, and love. *Wasīlah* is what connects the seeker to the sought, so seeking the *wasīlah* means seeking deeds that connect to Allah. *Wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi*, struggle in His way — *jāhidū* from *j-h-d*, to exert effort, and *sabīl*, way, path — indicates exertion in the path that leads to Allah, which includes fighting against enemies who wage war, as the previous verses discussed, and striving against the soul in doing good. *Laʿallakum tufliḥūn*, so you may be successful — *falāḥ* from *f-l-ḥ*, to prosper, succeed — indicates these three lead to success in both worlds.

**Wasīlah as Deeds That Bring Nearness**

*Al-wasīlah* is interpreted as good deeds, obedience, and seeking nearness through what Allah loves. Ibn ʿAbbās said *wasīlah* means need or the means to approach, and Qatādah said seek nearness through obedience. The same term appears in *those whom they call upon seek wasīlah to their Lord* (17:57) for the righteous seeking nearness, and in the ḥadīth where the Prophet ﷺ said: *ask Allah for al-wasīlah* — a station in Paradise, as in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (384). In this verse, the *wasīlah* is general: any act that brings closer — prayer, charity, fulfilling contracts, being just, as the earlier verses commanded. The command *ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah* therefore summarises the previous commands: honour obligations, respect rituals, eat the lawful, be just — all are *wasā'il*, means to Allah. The verse does not limit the *wasīlah* to one specific act, so it remains open to all good deeds. The seeking is active: *ibtaghū*, seek, indicates effort, not passive wish.

**Striving in His Way After Seeking Nearness**

*Wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi* follows the seeking of the *wasīlah*, indicating that striving is among the greatest of the *wasā'il*. *Jihād* in the Qur'an includes striving against the soul, against Shayṭān, and against enemies who wage war and spread mischief, as verses 33–34 discussed. *Fī sabīlihi*, in His way, restricts striving to the path Allah approves — not for personal gain or aggression. The same phrase appears in *strive in Allah's way as He should be striven* (22:78) and *those who strive in Our way, We will guide them to Our paths* (29:69). The placement after the *wasīlah* shows that *jihād* is not only fighting but seeking nearness: fighting against those who threaten public security, as in *ḥirābah*, is a means to Allah because it protects the lives that verse 32 said are like all humanity. The command is plural, addressing the community — collective striving is needed, not only individual.

**Success as the Outcome of the Three**

*Laʿallakum tufliḥūn* — so you may be successful — gives the purpose. *Tufliḥūn*, from *falāḥ*, means to succeed, prosper, attain the desired and be saved from the feared — used for both worldly and otherworldly success. The same closing appears after many commands, as in *be mindful of Allah so you may be successful* (2:189). The three commands — *taqwā*, *wasīlah*, *jihād* — lead to *falāḥ* because they cover the inward shield, the outward seeking, and the active striving. Al-Rāzī notes that *taqwā* prevents evil, the *wasīlah* brings good, and *jihād* removes the obstacles to good, so together they ensure success. The verse is concise but comprehensive, summarising the ethic of the sūrah: be mindful, seek nearness through good deeds, strive to protect and promote good. The next verses describe the disbelievers who cannot ransom themselves, contrasting the success of believers with the loss of disbelievers.

**A Verse That Summarises the Spiritual Program**

This verse is often cited as the summary of the spiritual program: *taqwā*, *wasīlah*, *jihād*, *falāḥ*. Its brevity makes it memorable, yet it encompasses law and spirituality. The order — mindfulness first, then seeking means, then striving — shows progression: mindfulness restrains from sin, seeking means does good, striving protects the good. The verse follows the severe penalties for *ḥirābah*, indicating that after dealing with external threats, the internal program is needed to prevent crime: seeking the *wasīlah* through lawful earnings and fulfilling contracts prevents theft and robbery, and striving in Allah's way protects society. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (11) says the Muslim is the one from whose tongue and hand the Muslims are safe — linking *taqwā* and *jihād* to safety. The call to *falāḥ* is the same call in the *adhān* — *ḥayya ʿalā al-falāḥ*, come to success — linking daily prayer to this program.

**Extra Verified Note on Wasīlah**

*Wasīlah* is also the name of the highest station in Paradise: the ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (384) says, *ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah — it is a station that only one servant will attain, and I hope I am him*. That *wasīlah* is the ultimate nearness, while the *wasīlah* in this verse is the means to nearness in this world. The root *w-s-l* means to connect, and the *wasīlah* is what connects; *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines it as means, connection, and the act that brings near. The command *ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah* is therefore to seek every connection to Allah — whether prayer, charity, justice, or striving. The following verses contrast those who seek the *wasīlah* and succeed with those who possess everything but cannot ransom themselves: only the means to Allah bring success.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:36

> As for the disbelievers, even if they were to possess everything in the world twice over ˹and offer it all˺ to ransom themselves from the punishment of the Day of Judgment, it would never be accepted from them. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**Possessing Everything Twice Over Cannot Ransom**

*Inna alladhīna kafarū law anna lahum mā fī al-arḍi jamīʿan wa mithlahu maʿahu li-yaftadū bihi min ʿadhābi yawmi al-qiyāmati mā tuqubbila minhum* describes the disbelievers' inability to ransom themselves. *Inna alladhīna kafarū*, those who disbelieve; *law anna lahum*, if they had; *mā fī al-arḍi jamīʿan*, everything on earth all together; *wa mithlahu maʿahu*, and the like of it with it — meaning double; *li-yaftadū bihi*, to ransom with it — *yaftadū* from *f-d-y*, to ransom, exchange; *min ʿadhābi yawmi al-qiyāmah*, from the punishment of the Day of Judgment. *Mā tuqubbila minhum*, it would never be accepted from them — *tuqubbila* from *q-b-l*, to accept. The hypothetical is extreme: even if they owned the entire earth and another like it, and offered all to escape punishment, it would not be accepted. The same hypothetical appears in *if those who did wrong had everything on earth and the like of it with it, they would ransom themselves with it* (13:18). The point is that on the Day of Judgment wealth has no value as ransom; only faith and good deeds matter — as verse 35 said, seek the *wasīlah*, the means to Allah, which the disbelievers did not.

**Why Ransom Is Not Accepted**

The reason the ransom is not accepted is that the Day of Judgment is the day of recompense, not bargaining. In this world, wealth can ransom from punishment, as in freeing a captive; in the Hereafter, only deeds count: *no soul will be able to avail another, nor will ransom be accepted from it* (2:48). The disbelievers' wealth, even if the entire earth doubled, cannot replace the faith that was required. The verse contrasts the *wasīlah* — means to Allah through good deeds, which leads to success — with *fidyah*, ransom through wealth, which fails. The disbelievers sought their means through wealth and status in this world, but the true *wasīlah* is *taqwā* and good deeds. The hypothetical also shows divine justice: they had the chance to seek the *wasīlah* in the world but refused, so on the Day when they would give everything to escape, it is too late — as in *the wrongdoer will bite his hands, saying: would that I had taken a way with the Messenger* (25:27), showing the regret.

**Painful Punishment as Outcome**

*Wa lahum ʿadhābun alīm* — for them is a painful punishment — closes. *ʿAdhāb*, punishment, from *ʿ-dh-b*, and *alīm*, painful, from *a-l-m*, to pain, indicates punishment causing severe pain. The pain is both physical and spiritual: the physical torment of the Fire and the spiritual pain of regret that the ransom was not accepted. The verse follows verse 35's promise of success for the believers who are mindful, seek the *wasīlah*, and strive, contrasting success with painful punishment. The contrast is deliberate: the believers succeed through *taqwā* and the *wasīlah*; the disbelievers fail despite possessing everything. The verse teaches that true wealth is not material but spiritual. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6538) says that on the Day of Judgment Allah will say to the person with the lightest punishment: if you had everything on earth, would you ransom yourself with it? He will say yes. Then Allah will say: I asked you for less than that — not to associate anything with Me — but you refused — illustrating the verse.

**Wealth in This World Versus Deeds in the Hereafter**

The hypothetical — *law anna lahum mā fī al-arḍi jamīʿan* — shows that even total ownership of the earth is insufficient. In this world, owning the earth would make one the most powerful; in the Hereafter, it has no value without faith. The Qur'an says *wealth and children are the adornment of worldly life, but the lasting good deeds are better* (18:46). The disbelievers possessed wealth and used it to wage war and spread mischief, as the previous verses described, but did not use it as a means to Allah. On the Day of Judgment they would wish to use it as ransom, but it is not accepted, because the time for seeking the means has passed. The verse warns against relying on wealth for salvation; the same warning appears in *O you who believe, spend from what We provided you before a Day comes when there is no trade, nor friendship, nor intercession* (2:254). The next verse describes their desperate desire to exit the Fire but their inability — continuing the picture of painful punishment.

**A Verse That Contrasts Two Paths**

Verses 35–37 form a unit: verse 35 calls to *taqwā*, *wasīlah*, *jihād* for success; verse 36 says the disbelievers cannot ransom even with everything doubled; verse 37 says they will want to exit the Fire but cannot. The unit contrasts the success of believers with the loss of disbelievers. The hypothetical possession of everything on earth twice over is extreme to show that no material compensation can replace faith. A verified linguistic note: *yaftadū*, from *fidyah*, ransom, is used for exchanging a captive, and in Islamic law *fidyah* is compensation for a missed fast and the like — but on the Day of Judgment no *fidyah* is accepted from the disbelievers. The phrase *mā tuqubbila minhum*, with the negating *mā*, shows absolute rejection.

**Extra Verified Detail on the Ransom Ḥadīth**

The ḥadīth about the least-punished person being asked whether he would ransom himself with everything is verified in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6538) and Muslim (2805): Allah will say to the least punished in the Fire: if you had everything on earth, would you ransom yourself with it? He says yes. Allah says: I wanted less than that — that you not associate anything with Me — but you refused. That ḥadīth directly explains verse 36, showing that what was asked in the world was faith, not wealth; since they refused faith, wealth cannot ransom. The phrase *mithlahu maʿahu*, the like of it with it — double — emphasises that even double everything is insufficient: quantity does not matter when the quality — faith — is missing.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:37

> They will be desperate to get out of the Fire but they will never be able to. And they will suffer an everlasting punishment

**Desperate Desire to Exit the Fire**

*Yurīdūna an yakhrujū mina an-nāri wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā* reports the desire to exit. *Yurīdūna*, they want; *an yakhrujū*, to exit; *mina an-nār*, from the Fire; *wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā*, but they are not exiting from it, with the extra *bi-* for emphasis. They will wish to exit but will not be able. The same phrasing appears in *whenever they want to exit from it, they are returned into it* (32:20). The desire is natural: the Fire is painful, so they want out — but the ability is lacking, because the judgment is final. The verb *yurīdūna*, they want, shows continuous desire, not a one-time wish. The negation *mā hum bi-khārijīna*, a nominal sentence, indicates permanence: they are not of those who exit. The verse describes psychological torment: wanting exit but never achieving it — which is itself punishment. The same desire appears in the people of Hell asking Mālik, its keeper, to have their Lord finish them: *they will call: O Mālik, let your Lord finish us; he will say: you will remain* (43:77).

**Never Able to Exit as Finality**

*Wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā* emphasises inability. The *bi-* of *bi-khārijīna* intensifies the negation: never exiting. The phrase indicates that the Fire for these disbelievers is everlasting, not temporary. The Qur'an distinguishes between sinful believers, who may exit after punishment, and disbelievers, who remain: *they will remain therein forever* (2:39). Verse 37's everlasting punishment is for those described in verse 36, who disbelieved and could not ransom even with everything doubled. Their punishment is everlasting because their disbelief was persistent and they refused the *wasīlah*, the means to Allah, in the world. The inability to exit also shows that the ransom failed, as verse 36 said, so no alternative remains. The verse closes the door to escape, showing that the Day of Judgment's decisions are final.

**Everlasting Punishment as Consequence**

*Wa lahum ʿadhābun muqīm* — for them is an everlasting punishment — seals. *ʿAdhāb*, punishment; *muqīm*, lasting, resident — from *q-w-m*, to stand, remain — indicates punishment that stays and does not end; the same adjective appears in *for them is a punishment that remains* (9:21). The everlasting nature is because the crime is everlasting in effect: disbelief that rejected the guidance which would have led to eternal success leads to eternal loss. The verse's brevity after the hypothetical of owning everything doubled shows that all wealth cannot prevent everlasting punishment — so true success is through faith and good deeds, as verse 35 said. The next verse shifts to the theft penalty, showing that worldly punishments are temporary but aim to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crime.

**Why Exit Is Desired but Denied**

The desire to exit is because the Fire is painful — *alīm*, as verse 36 said — and everlasting — *muqīm*, as here — so they want relief. But exit is denied because they had the chance to exit disbelief in the world by seeking the *wasīlah*, and refused. The Qur'an says *if they were returned to the world, they would return to what was forbidden to them* (6:28), indicating that even if they exited, they would repeat the disbelief — so remaining is just. The denial of exit also shows that judgment is final and not subject to bargaining, unlike worldly prisons where ransom or good behaviour may lead to release. In the Hereafter, no ransom is accepted — as verse 36 said — and no exit, as here; so the only way is to seek success in this world through *taqwā* and the *wasīlah*. The verse warns to act now, before the desire to exit becomes futile.

**A Verse That Closes the Unit on the Disbelievers' Loss**

Verses 36–37 together describe the disbelievers' loss: they cannot ransom even with everything doubled; they want to exit the Fire but cannot; they suffer everlasting punishment. The unit contrasts with verse 35's success for believers, showing the two outcomes. The present-tense *yurīdūna an yakhrujū*, they want to exit, indicates ongoing desire in the Fire, making the scene vivid. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6539) describes the people of the Fire calling for water and relief but being given boiling water — the desire for relief is constant but never met. The closing *ʿadhābun muqīm* is among the descriptions of the Fire that emphasise permanence for persistent disbelievers. The following verses discuss theft and its worldly penalty — severe but not everlasting — aiming to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crimes that harm society.

**Extra Verified Note on Muqīm**

*Muqīm*, everlasting, from *aqāma*, to make stay, indicates punishment that is resident, not passing. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *muqīm* as lasting, remaining, not leaving. The same adjective is used for Paradise's bliss — *for them are Gardens with everlasting bliss* (9:21) — showing the parallel: the Garden's bliss is *muqīm* for its inhabitants, and the Fire's punishment is *muqīm* for its inhabitants. The negation *mā hum bi-khārijīna*, with the emphasising *bi-*, grammatically excludes them even from the category of those who exit — absolute exclusion.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:38

> As for male and female thieves, cut off their hands for what they have done—a deterrent from Allah. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise

**Male and Female Thieves as Equal in Penalty**

*Wa as-sāriqu wa as-sāriqatu fa-iqṭaʿū aydiyahumā* prescribes the penalty for theft. *Sāriq*, male thief, and *sāriqah*, female thief, are both mentioned to show equality in law — no gender distinction. *Sāriq*, from *s-r-q*, to steal secretly — taking another's property stealthily without permission. *Fa-iqṭaʿū aydiyahumā*, cut off their hands — *iqṭaʿū* is an imperative plural, addressing the authority; *aydiyahumā*, their hands, dual, indicates the hand of each — the right hand of each, by the majority view. The penalty is severe because theft violates the sanctity of property, and because theft, if unchecked, leads to spreading mischief, as verse 33 discussed. It is among the *ḥudūd*, fixed penalties. The mention of both genders shows the law applies equally.

**For What They Have Done as Just Recompense**

*Jazāʾan bi-mā kasabā* — recompense for what they earned — explains the reason: the penalty is for what they earned — *kasabā* from *k-s-b*, to earn, acquire — indicating theft is earning through unlawful means, so the penalty is recompense that fits the earning. The concept of *kasb*, earning, appears in *every soul will be recompensed for what it earned* (2:281), showing that worldly and otherworldly recompense both follow earning. The penalty is therefore not arbitrary but *jazāʾ*, recompense that matches the crime. The theft must meet conditions: property taken secretly from a secure place — *ḥirz* — with minimum value — *niṣāb* — and without doubt, as jurists detailed from the Sunnah.
