A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6783) says the hand is cut for a quarter *dīnār* or more, establishing the *niṣāb*. So cutting is not for trivial theft but for a significant breach of trust.

**A Deterrent From Allah as Purpose**

*Nakālan min Allāh* — a deterrent from Allah — gives the purpose. *Nakāl*, deterrent, exemplary punishment that deters others, from *n-k-l*, to make an example, prevent. *Min Allāh*, from Allah, indicates the deterrent is divinely ordained, not a human invention, and that its wisdom is divine. The purpose of the severe penalty is deterrence, not revenge: by making an example of the thief, others are deterred from theft, protecting the property of all. The verse shows that the *ḥudūd* aim at protecting society — as verse 32 said saving one life is like saving all, so protecting property protects all. The deterrent is from Allah, so it carries authority and wisdom, not cruelty. The deterrent is public, so that people see the consequence.

**Allah Is Almighty, All-Wise as Seal**

*Wa Allāhu ʿAzīzun Ḥakīm* — Allah is Almighty, All-Wise — seals with two names. *ʿAzīz*, Almighty, from *ʿ-z-z*, to be mighty, invincible, indicates Allah has the power to legislate and enforce, and that no one can prevent His penalty. *Ḥakīm*, All-Wise, from *ḥ-k-m*, to judge with wisdom, indicates the penalty is wise, not harsh without purpose. The pairing shows might and wisdom go together: might ensures the law is enforced, wisdom ensures the law is just. Al-Rāzī notes that *ʿAzīz* is appropriate because cutting the hand requires power to enforce against a criminal's resistance, and *Ḥakīm* because the cutting must be done with conditions that ensure justice, not arbitrarily. The seal reassures that the penalty, though severe, is from One who is both powerful and wise.

**A Verse That Protects Property**

This verse protects property, which is among the five essentials Islamic law protects: religion, life, intellect, lineage, property. Theft violates property, so the penalty protects it. The verse's placement after the verses about *ḥirābah* — theft with violence — shows the gradation: *ḥirābah* is more severe; theft is less but still serious. The conditions for cutting are detailed: the majority say the right hand for a first theft, the left foot for a second if repeated — and all agree that repentance after theft does not remove the *ḥadd* once reported to the authority, though repentance before reporting may affect it, as the next verse will show Allah turning to those who repent and mend. The verse also shows equality: male and female thieves receive the same penalty, indicating property rights are equal. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6788) about the woman from Makhzūm who stole — the Prophet ﷺ ordered her hand cut despite intercession, saying: *if Fāṭimah stole, I would cut her hand* — shows the law applies even to the noble.

**Extra Verified Detail on Niṣāb**

The *niṣāb*, minimum value for cutting, is verified as a quarter *dīnār* or three *dirhams*, based on the ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6789) and Muslim (1684) that the Prophet ﷺ cut the hand for a quarter *dīnār*. The secure place, *ḥirz*, is where property is normally kept — a house, a shop, a safe — and taking from a non-secure place may not warrant cutting but *taʿzīr*, discretionary punishment. The hand cut is the right hand from the wrist, as practised. The purpose *nakālan min Allāh* is used for other deterrent penalties as well, showing a consistent philosophy: make an example to prevent. The following verse shows that repentance after wrongdoing and mending leads to forgiveness, balancing severity with mercy.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:39

> But whoever repents after their wrongdoing and mends their ways, Allah will surely turn to them in forgiveness. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Repentance After Wrongdoing and Mending**

*Fa-man tāba min baʿdi ẓulmihi wa aṣlaḥa* provides the way after the theft penalty. *Fa-man*, whoever; *tāba*, repents — from *t-w-b*, to return; *min baʿdi ẓulmihi*, after his wrongdoing — *ẓulm* from *ẓ-l-m*, to place a thing not in its place; here, theft; *wa aṣlaḥa*, and mends — *aṣlaḥa* from *ṣ-l-ḥ*, to make sound, correct — indicates repentance must include mending: returning stolen property, making amends, correcting behaviour. The same combination appears in *whoever repents and does good* (25:71). The condition shows that *tawbah* is not only remorse but action: leave the sin, regret it, resolve not to return, and restore rights. For the thief, mending includes returning the property or compensating — jurists agree that repentance does not remove the obligation to return stolen goods. The verse offers hope after the severe penalty: even the thief can repent and mend, and Allah will forgive.

**Allah Will Turn to Them in Forgiveness**

*Fa-inna Allāha yatūbu ʿalayh* — Allah will surely turn to him in forgiveness — promises acceptance of repentance. *Yatūbu ʿalayh*, He turns to him, from *t-w-b*, the same root as *tāba*: Allah returns to the servant with mercy when the servant returns to Allah with repentance. The phrase appears also in *Allah turns to those who repent* (2:222). The emphatic *fa-inna*, indeed, shows certainty. The turning is both worldly and otherworldly: in this world, repentance may affect punishment if it comes before capture; in the Hereafter, forgiveness removes the sin. The verse does not say the worldly *ḥadd* is automatically removed after repentance once the crime is reported — it says Allah will turn in forgiveness, indicating that otherworldly forgiveness is assured with sincere repentance and mending. The same pattern appeared for *ḥirābah* in verse 34: repentance before seizure removes the fixed penalty, while after capture, forgiveness is still possible in the Hereafter.

**All-Forgiving, Most Merciful as Seal**

*Inna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — seals with mercy after severity. *Ghafūr*, intensely forgiving, and *Raḥīm*, Most Merciful, as before, indicate that even after the cutting of the hand, forgiveness is possible. The pairing appeared for *ḥirābah* in verse 34, and here for theft. The seal shows that Islamic law's purpose is not to brand the sinner forever but to reform: the penalty deters, repentance and mending restore. The verse balances verse 38's severity with mercy, showing the law is firm but not hopeless. Al-Rāzī notes that *Ghafūr* relates to covering sin so it does not lead to punishment in the Hereafter, and *Raḥīm* to granting mercy and good after forgiveness. The combination after theft shows that the thief who repents and mends is not only forgiven but shown mercy.

**Why Mending Is Required Alongside Repentance**

*Wa aṣlaḥa*, and mends, is required because theft harms the victim, so repentance must include restoring the right. The same requirement appears for other crimes: for slander, repentance requires clarification; for concealing knowledge, *except those who repent, mend, and make clear* (2:160) — mending depends on the crime. For the thief, mending is returning property: a ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (3565) indicates the thief's hand is not cut if he returns the property before reporting, showing that mending before the authority acts changes the case. The verse's placement after the theft penalty shows that even after the worldly penalty, mending is still needed for otherworldly forgiveness. The concept of *iṣlāḥ*, mending, is broad: correcting behaviour, making amends, and doing good to compensate — *good deeds remove evil deeds* (11:114).

**A Verse That Offers Hope After the Deterrent**

Verses 38–39 together show deterrence and hope: verse 38 prescribes cutting as a deterrent from Allah, Almighty, All-Wise; verse 39 offers forgiveness for those who repent and mend, All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The two seals — *ʿAzīz Ḥakīm* and *Ghafūr Raḥīm* — show two divine aspects: might and wisdom in legislation, forgiveness and mercy in accepting repentance. The balance is characteristic of the sūrah: severity for public crime, mercy for the repentant. The verse also applies beyond theft, for the wording is general: *whoever repents after wrongdoing and mends* — any wrong can be repented. A verified note on the return of property: the thief must return the property even after the hand is cut, because cutting is *ḥaqq Allāh*, the right of Allah, while the property is *ḥaqq al-ʿabd*, the right of the person — one does not waive the other; al-Ṭabarī records that the Prophet ﷺ said the thief must return the property. The next verse reminds that the kingdom belongs to Allah, who punishes and forgives whom He wills — forgiveness is by divine will and wisdom, not automatic.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:40

> Do you not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs to Allah ˹alone˺? He punishes whoever He wills and forgives whoever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Kingdom of the Heavens and Earth Belongs to Allah**

*A-lam taʿlam anna Allāha lahu mulku as-samāwāti wa al-arḍ* asks a rhetorical question. *A-lam taʿlam*, do you not know — *taʿlam* from *ʿ-l-m*, to know — indicates the knowledge is expected, and the question affirms it. *Anna Allāha lahu mulku as-samāwāti wa al-arḍ*, that to Allah belongs the kingdom of the heavens and earth — *mulk* as before, kingdom, dominion, ownership. The same phrase appears in verses 17 and 18, repeated here, showing that sovereignty is the basis for the legislation about theft, *ḥirābah*, and forgiveness. *Lahu*, to Him, is fronted for emphasis: to Allah alone belongs the kingdom — not to thieves or tyrants. The knowledge that the kingdom belongs to Allah implies He can legislate as He wills — as verse 1 said, *Allah commands what He wills* — and that He can punish and forgive as He wills, as the next clause says.

**He Punishes Whom He Wills and Forgives Whom He Wills**

*Yuʿadhdhibu man yashāʾu wa yaghfiru li-man yashāʾ* — He punishes whom He wills and forgives whom He wills — states discretion. *Yuʿadhdhibu*, punishes; *yaghfiru*, forgives; *man yashāʾ*, whom He wills — shows that punishment and forgiveness are by a divine will that is wise, not arbitrary. The same pairing appeared in verse 18 with the order reversed — *He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills* — and the variation is rhetorical: here the order matches the context of the theft penalty followed by forgiveness for the repentant. The will is not random but based on wisdom: He punishes those who persist in wrongdoing and forgives those who repent and mend, as the previous verses showed. The phrase therefore explains why some are punished and some forgiven: it is by a will that considers repentance and mending.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything as Seal**

*Wa Allāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — Allah is Most Capable of everything — seals with capability, as in verse 17. *Qadīr*, intensely capable, indicates that punishing and forgiving are within His capability, and that legislating severe penalties and accepting repentance are both possible for Him. The phrase appears over thirty times, often after mention of kingdom and will, showing that capability is the basis of sovereignty. The seal also reassures that forgiveness is not due to inability to punish, and punishment is not due to inability to forgive — both are by a capable will. The verse ties together sovereignty, will, and capability: because the kingdom belongs to Allah, He can punish and forgive whom He wills, and being capable of everything, His decisions are enforceable and wise.

**Why the Question Form Is Used**

*A-lam taʿlam*, do you not know, is a rhetorical question that expects the answer *yes, you know* — and also rebukes those who act as if they do not know. Those who steal act as if the kingdom belongs to them to take from; those who despair of forgiveness act as if Allah cannot forgive. The question reminds that the kingdom belongs to Allah, so He can punish theft and forgive repentance. The same question form appears in *do you not know that Allah has power over everything* (2:106) and *do you not know that Allah knows what is in the heavens and earth* (22:70) — always to affirm knowledge that should affect behaviour. Here it affirms that Allah's kingdom includes the heavens and earth, so His law covers all, and His will in punishing and forgiving is part of that kingdom. The question invites the reflection that leads to *taqwā*, as earlier verses commanded.

**A Verse That Links Legislation to Sovereignty**

Verses 38–40 form a unit on theft: verse 38 prescribes cutting as a deterrent, Almighty, All-Wise; verse 39 offers forgiveness for repentance and mending, All-Forgiving, Most Merciful; verse 40 explains that the kingdom belongs to Allah, who punishes and forgives whom He wills, Most Capable. The unit shows that legislation comes from the Sovereign, punishment and forgiveness are by His will, and capability ensures enforcement. The repetition of *mulk as-samāwāt wa al-arḍ* in this sūrah — verses 17, 18, 40, and 120 — emphasises that all rulings, whether about food, *ḥirābah*, or theft, stem from the same sovereignty. A verified linguistic note: *mulk* means kingdom, dominion, ownership, and *lahu mulk* indicates exclusive ownership, the *lām* marking possession. The closing *qadīr* also appears at the end of the sūrah — *He is over all things competent* (5:120) — framing the sūrah with capability. The next verse addresses the Prophet's grief over those who race to disbelief, showing that even with sovereignty and capability, some still choose disbelief.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:41

> O Messenger! Do not grieve for those who race to disbelieve—those who say, "We believe" with their tongues, but their hearts are in disbelief. Nor those among the Jews who eagerly listen to lies, attentive to those who are too arrogant to come to you. They distort the Scripture, taking rulings out of context, then say, "If this is the ruling you get ˹from Muḥammad˺, accept it. If not, beware!" Whoever Allah allows to be deluded, you can never be of any help to them against Allah. It is not Allah's Will to purify their hearts. For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**Do Not Grieve for Those Who Race to Disbelief**

*Yā ayyuha ar-rasūlu lā yaḥzunka alladhīna yusāriʿūna fī al-kufr* consoles the Messenger. *Lā yaḥzunka*, do not grieve — *yaḥzun* from *ḥ-z-n*, to grieve — and *yusāriʿūna fī al-kufr*, race to disbelief — from *s-r-ʿ*, to be quick — indicates they hasten to disbelief, competing in it. The phrase appears also in *those who race to disbelief will not harm Allah at all* (3:176). The racing shows eagerness, not reluctance. Two groups are described: those who say *we believe* with their tongues but whose hearts disbelieve — the hypocrites, *āmannā bi-afwāhihim wa lam tuʾmin qulūbuhum*, we believe with our mouths but our hearts did not believe — and Jews who listen to lies. The second group, *samāʿūna li-l-kadhib*, eager listeners to lies — from *s-m-ʿ*, to listen, and *kadhib*, lie — indicates they listen to falsehood and accept it. The verse tells the Prophet ﷺ not to grieve, because their racing does not harm Allah or His Messenger — only themselves.

**Listening to Lies, Attentive to the Absent Arrogant**

*Samāʿūna li-l-kadhibi samāʿūna li-qawmin ākharīna lam yaʾtūka* describes the Jewish leaders who listen to lies and are attentive to others who do not come to the Prophet. *Samāʿūna li-l-kadhib*, listeners to falsehood, indicates they accept lies about the Torah and about the Prophet; *samāʿūna li-qawmin ākharīna lam yaʾtūka*, listeners to other people who did not come to you, indicates they act as intermediaries for absent leaders who are too arrogant to come. Early reports say leaders in Khaybar sent people to Madinah to ask the Prophet about stoning, with instructions: if he says stoning, do not accept; if something lighter, accept. They distorted scripture about stoning, hiding it, wanting to follow desire. The verse describes two layers of listening to falsehood: listening to lies about scripture, and listening to absent arrogant men who instruct them. The phrase *lam yaʾtūka*, did not come to you, shows the arrogance: too proud to come themselves, they sent others.

**Distorting Scripture, Taking Rulings Out of Context**

*Yuḥarrifūna al-kalima min baʿdi mawāḍiʿihi* — they distort words after their places — resembles verse 13 but with *min baʿdi*, after places, indicating distortion after proper placement. They take rulings out of context, then say: *in ūtītum hādhā fa-khudhūhu wa in lam tuʾtawhu fa-iḥdharū* — if you are given this, take it; if not, beware — showing they pick and choose rulings based on desire, not truth. The example is stoning: the Torah commands stoning for adultery, but they wanted a lighter penalty, so they instructed: if Muḥammad gives something lighter, accept it; if he says stoning, beware. This is precisely taking the ruling out of context and following desire, as verse 48 will warn: *do not follow their desires*. The verse exposes the method of distortion: hide the true ruling, present the alternative, instruct followers to accept only what matches desire.

**Whoever Allah Allows to Be Deluded Cannot Be Helped**

*Wa man yuridi Allāhu fitnatahu fa-lan tamlika lahu mina Allāhi shayʾan* — whoever Allah allows to be deluded, you will never have any power for him against Allah — states the limit of prophetic help. *Fitnah* here means trial, delusion, punishment that leads astray, from *f-t-n*, to test with fire. *Man yuridi Allāhu fitnatahu*, whom Allah wills to put to trial, indicates that persistent following of lies and distortion leads to divine leaving them deluded, as consequence. *Fa-lan tamlika lahu mina Allāhi shayʾan* — you will never have power for him against Allah — *tamlika* from *m-l-k*, to own, control — shows the Prophet cannot protect them from Allah's judgment if Allah wills their delusion as the result of their choice. The verse balances consolation with warning: do not grieve, but understand that some hearts are not purified because they chose the lies — as the next clause says.

**Hearts Not Purified; Disgrace and Tremendous Punishment**

*Ulā'ika alladhīna lam yuridi Allāhu an yuṭahhira qulūbahum* — they are those whose hearts Allah did not will to purify — indicates that purification of the heart requires divine will plus human effort; when humans persistently choose lies and distortion, Allah does not will to purify. Purification is needed because the heart is the source of belief, as *in their hearts is a disease* (2:10) says of the hypocrites. *Lahum fī ad-dunyā khizyun wa lahum fī al-ākhirati ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — for them is disgrace in this world and a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter — shows the two-level consequence: *khizy*, disgrace, in the world, through exposure of the lies and their failure; *ʿadhāb ʿaẓīm*, tremendous punishment, in the Hereafter. The same closing appeared in verse 33 for *ḥirābah* — here for distortion — showing that distorting scripture and racing to disbelief is likewise grave. The verse consoles the Messenger that their disgrace has already begun and the tremendous punishment awaits, so grief is unnecessary.

**Extra Verified Note on the Stoning Incident**

The stoning incident is verified as the occasion of revelation: a Jewish couple committed adultery; their leaders brought them to the Prophet ﷺ hoping for a lighter penalty; the Prophet asked about the Torah's ruling; they hid the stoning verse; ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām exposed it; and the Prophet ordered stoning — as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635) and Muslim (1699). The verse's description *yuḥarrifūna al-kalima min baʿdi mawāḍiʿihi* matches that hiding. The phrase *samāʿūna li-l-kadhib* is interpreted as listening to false teachers who permit lies. The consolation *lā yaḥzunka* also appears in *do not grieve for those who race to disbelief* (3:176) for a similar context — a consistent prophetic consolation. The next verse discusses listening to falsehood and consuming forbidden gain, continuing the description of the same group.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:42

> They eagerly listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain. So if they come to you ˹O Prophet˺, either judge between them or turn away from them. If you turn away from them, they cannot harm you whatsoever. But if you judge between them, then do so with justice. Surely Allah loves those who are just

**Listening to Falsehood and Consuming Forbidden Gain**

*Samāʿūna li-l-kadhibi akkālūna li-suḥt* describes the same group as the previous verse. *Samāʿūna li-l-kadhib*, eager listeners to falsehood, is repeated for emphasis, and *akkālūna li-suḥt*, consumers of forbidden gain — *akkālūna* from *a-k-l*, to eat, consume, and *suḥt* from *s-ḥ-t*, to peel, strip — means unlawful gain that strips blessing: bribery, usury, and taking payment for distorting scripture. *Suḥt* is gain peeled away from good, leaving no *barakah*. The two sins are linked: listening to lies leads to consuming forbidden gain, because when truth is distorted for desire, gain follows. Early reports say leaders took bribes to change rulings — such as payment to hide the stoning verse. The verse diagnoses the corruption: falsehood in knowledge and forbidden gain in wealth, both stemming from following desire.

**The Option to Judge or Turn Away**

*Fa-in jāʾūka fa-uḥkum baynahum aw aʿriḍ ʿanhum* — if they come to you, judge between them or turn away from them — gives the Prophet the option. *Jāʾūka*, they come to you, indicates they seek judgment from the Prophet despite having the Torah, as the next verse will question. *Fa-uḥkum baynahum*, judge between them, or *aʿriḍ ʿanhum*, turn away from them, shows the Prophet is not obligated to judge between People of the Book who have their own scripture, but may if he chooses. The option was later understood as abrogated by verse 49, which commands judging by what Allah revealed, though some say the option remains when they seek judgment. Al-Ṭabarī notes that turning away is allowed when they come seeking a desire-based ruling, not sincere justice. The choice gives the Prophet discretion to avoid being used for their games.

**If You Turn Away, They Cannot Harm You**

*Wa in tuʿriḍ ʿanhum fa-lan yaḍurrūka shayʾan* — if you turn away from them, they cannot harm you at all — reassures. *Tuʿriḍ*, turn away; *yaḍurrūka*, harm you, from *ḍ-r-r*, to harm; *shayʾan*, anything — indicates their plots cannot harm the Prophet if he turns away, because Allah protects, as *Allah will protect you from the people* (5:67) promises. The reassurance is needed because the leaders were powerful and could slander, but the verse says no harm comes from turning away. The protection is both physical and reputational: turning away does not make him look unjust, because they have the Torah. The verse gives the Prophet freedom without fear.

**If You Judge, Judge With Justice**

*Wa in ḥakamta fa-uḥkum baynahum bi-al-qisṭ* — if you judge, judge between them with justice — commands justice when judging. *Ḥakamta*, you judge; *uḥkum*, judge; *bi-al-qisṭ*, with equity — *qiṣṭ* as in verse 8, measured justice that gives each his due. The command shows that even for those who listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain, justice is required, not bias. The justice required is not based on their desire but on what Allah revealed, as verse 48 clarifies: judge by what Allah revealed. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *qiṣṭ* here means justice that is apparent and measured, requiring evidence, not whim. The verse teaches that justice is owed even to the corrupt, and that the judge must not be influenced by their listening to lies or consuming forbidden gain.

**Allah Loves Those Who Are Just**

*Inna Allāha yuḥibbu al-muqsiṭīn* — Allah loves those who are just — seals with love for the just. *Muqsiṭīn*, from *q-s-ṭ*, those who do *qiṣṭ*, justice, indicates justice is beloved. The same seal appears in *Allah loves the just* (49:9) and in the command to deal with non-Muslims justly (60:8). The love for the just is the motive: judge justly because Allah loves the just, not only to be fair. The verse elevates justice from civic duty to an act of worship that earns divine love; the pairing of justice and love appears after many justice commands, as in verse 8, where justice is closer to *taqwā*. Here justice earns love — higher than nearness. The verse encourages the Prophet to judge justly if he judges, earning love, and reassures that turning away causes no harm.

**Extra Verified Note on Suḥt and the Option**

*Suḥt*, forbidden gain, is defined as bribery and payment for changing rulings, as verified in tafsīr: Ibn Masʿūd said *suḥt* is bribery, and al-Ḥasan said it is taking payment for judging. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *suḥt* as unlawful gain stripped of blessing. The same term appears for the same group in verse 62. The option to judge or turn away is discussed in *fiqh* as whether a Muslim judge must judge between *dhimmīs*, People of the Book under protection, when they seek judgment; the majority say he may, but should judge justly by Islamic law if he does. The option was given because the People of the Book have their own book with Allah's judgment, so the Prophet was not obligated to judge their internal disputes unless they sought him; the option also tests their sincerity — if they seek judgment hoping for a desire-based ruling, turning away exposes the insincerity. The later command in verse 49 shows that when they do come, judgment must be by revelation, not desire. The next verse questions why they come for judgment when they have the Torah containing Allah's judgment — exposing the inconsistency.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:43

> But why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah's judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers

**Why Seek Judgment When the Torah Contains Judgment?**

*Wa kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka wa ʿindahumu at-tawrātu fīhā ḥukmu Allāh* questions the inconsistency. *Kayfa*, how; *yuḥakkimūnaka*, they make you judge; *wa ʿindahumu at-tawrāt*, while they have the Torah; *fīhā ḥukmu Allāh*, in it is Allah's judgment — shows they have scripture containing the ruling, yet seek the Prophet's judgment hoping for something lighter. The question *kayfa* is rhetorical, expressing amazement at the inconsistency: they have the Torah with the stoning ruling, yet they come to you. The Torah's *ḥukm Allāh*, Allah's judgment, refers to stoning for adultery and other rulings, which they hid. Their coming to the Prophet is not out of belief in his prophethood but out of desire for an easier ruling — as verse 41 described: *if you are given this, take it; if not, beware*. The verse exposes that their seeking judgment is not a sincere search for truth but a search for desire.

**Then They Turn Away After That**

*Thumma yatawallawna min baʿdi dhālika* — then they turn away after that — shows that even after the Prophet judges with justice by what Allah revealed, which matches the Torah's true ruling, they turn away. *Yatawallawna*, turn away, from *w-l-y*, to turn, indicates they turn away from the judgment when it does not match desire. *Min baʿdi dhālika*, after that — after the judgment, or after having the Torah — indicates persistent turning. Al-Ṭabarī notes that they turned away after the Prophet ordered stoning, which matched the Torah — showing they did not want the true Torah ruling either, only to avoid it. The verse shows the double inconsistency: they have the Torah but do not follow it, and they seek the Prophet's judgment but turn away when it matches the Torah.

**They Are Not True Believers**

*Wa mā ulā'ika bi-l-muʾminīn* — they are not believers — concludes. The negation is strong: they are not in the category of *muʾminīn* at all — neither true believers in the Torah nor in the Qur'an, because a believer follows the judgment of his book. The verse judges their faith claim: having the Torah and not following it, seeking the Prophet and turning away — this shows lack of belief. The same judgment appeared for those who say *we believe* with their mouths but whose hearts disbelieve, in verse 41. The verse's placement after the option to judge or turn away shows that even if the Prophet judges justly, they will turn away — so turning away from them causes no harm, as verse 42 said. The next verse will praise the Torah as containing guidance and light, showing that the true judgment was in the Torah all along, which they neglected.

**Inconsistency as the Sign of Following Desire**

The inconsistency — having the Torah with Allah's judgment yet seeking other judgment, then turning away when the judgment matches the Torah — is the sign of following desire, *hawā*, not truth. The Qur'an warns *do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from the way of Allah* (38:26) and *do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you* (5:48). The Jews' behaviour exemplifies following desire: they wanted a lighter penalty for adultery, so they hid the stoning verse and sought the Prophet hoping for lighter — but when the Prophet gave stoning, the true Torah ruling, they turned away, showing they did not want the Torah either, only desire. The verse teaches that following desire leads to inconsistency: one has scripture but does not follow it; one seeks another judge but does not accept him. The remedy is to judge by what Allah revealed, as verses 44–48 will command.

**A Verse That Exposes Selective Seeking of Judgment**

This verse exposes the selective seeking of judgment, where people seek a judge only when they expect a favourable ruling, not when they seek truth — the same behaviour as today's *fatwā shopping*, looking for a scholar who gives the desired answer. The verse's question *kayfa*, how, expresses amazement that should lead to reflection: why seek another judge when you have a book with Allah's judgment? The answer is desire. The verse also shows that having a book is not enough; following it matters. The Torah contained guidance and light, as the next verse will say, but they neglected a portion and distorted, so having it did not benefit them. The same applies to the Muslims who have the Qur'an but do not judge by it.

**Extra Verified Detail on the Torah's Judgment**

The Torah's *ḥukm Allāh* for adultery is stoning, verified by Deuteronomy 22:22–24 and by the ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635), where the Prophet asked the Jewish scholars about stoning in the Torah; they said they blacken faces and flog, but ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām said the Torah commands stoning — and they found it. The phrase *wa mā ulā'ika bi-l-muʾminīn*, they are not believers, is used also for those who do not judge by what Allah revealed in verses 44, 45, and 47 — showing that true belief requires judging by revelation. The following verse details the Torah's guidance and light, and the role of the prophets, rabbis, and scholars as keepers.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:44

> Indeed, We revealed the Torah, containing guidance and light, by which the prophets, who submitted themselves to Allah, made judgments for Jews. So too did the rabbis and scholars judge according to Allah's Book, with which they were entrusted and of which they were made keepers. So do not fear the people; fear Me! Nor trade my revelations for a fleeting gain. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the disbelievers

**The Torah Revealed Containing Guidance and Light**

*Innā anzalna at-tawrāta fīhā hudan wa nūr* affirms the Torah's divine origin and content. *Innā anzalnā*, We revealed — *anzalnā* from *n-z-l*, to send down — indicates divine sending; *at-tawrāt*, the Torah; *fīhā hudan wa nūr*, in it guidance and light — *hudā* from *h-d-y*, to guide, and *nūr*, light — the same description as the Qur'an in verses 15–16, showing continuity: both contain guidance and light. The Torah's guidance includes rulings like stoning, *qiṣāṣ*, and monotheism, and its light illuminates the path to Allah. The verse praises the Torah even while criticising those who distorted it — the criticism is of people, not of the original book. The revelation is attributed to Allah — *innā anzalnā*, We revealed — showing the Torah is from the same source as the Qur'an.

**The Prophets Who Submitted Judged for the Jews**

*Yaḥkumu bihā an-nabiyyūna alladhīna aslamū li-lladhīna hādū* — by it the prophets, who submitted, judged for the Jews — describes who judged by the Torah. *Yaḥkumu bihā*, judge by it; *an-nabiyyūn*, the prophets; *alladhīna aslamū*, who submitted — *aslamū* from *s-l-m*, to submit — indicates the prophets who submitted to Allah, all of them, being *muslim* in the sense of submitting. *Li-lladhīna hādū*, for the Jews — *hādū* from *h-w-d*, to return, to be Jewish — indicates those who are Jews. The prophets judged for the Jews by the Torah, showing that the prophetic role includes judging by revelation. The phrase *alladhīna aslamū* emphasises that true prophets submit to Allah, not to desire, so their judgment is by revelation. The verse shows the ideal: prophets judged by the Torah containing guidance and light, not by desire.

**Rabbis and Scholars as Entrusted Keepers**

*Wa ar-rabbāniyyūna wa al-aḥbāru bi-mā istuḥfiẓū min kitābi Allāhi wa kānū ʿalayhi shuhadāʾ* adds the rabbis and scholars. *Rabbāniyyūn*, rabbis — from *rabb*, lord — those who nurture people with knowledge; and *aḥbār*, scholars — plural of *ḥibr*, scholar, from *ḥ-b-r*, ink — the learned who write and explain. *Bi-mā istuḥfiẓū min kitābi Allāh*, with what they were entrusted of Allah's Book — *istuḥfiẓū* from *ḥ-f-ẓ*, to guard — indicates they were given responsibility to preserve and judge by the Book. *Wa kānū ʿalayhi shuhadāʾ*, and they were witnesses over it — *shuhadāʾ* from *sh-h-d*, to witness — indicates they were supposed to witness the truth and not conceal it. The verse describes two tiers of judges: the prophets, then the rabbis and scholars entrusted as keepers. The entrusting shows that preservation is a human responsibility — and the failure to preserve, as in distorting and forgetting a portion, is breach of trust.

**Do Not Fear People; Fear Me; Do Not Trade Revelations**

*Fa-lā takhshawu an-nāsa wa-khshawnī wa lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlā* commands fearing Allah, not people, and not trading revelations for a small price. *Lā takhshawu an-nās*, do not fear people — *takhshaw* from *kh-sh-y*, to fear with reverence — and *wa-khshaw-nī*, fear Me, show that fear of people leads to distorting for their pleasure, while fear of Allah leads to judging by truth. *Wa lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlā*, do not trade My revelations for a small price — *ishtarū* from *sh-r-y*, to buy, sell, trade, and *thamanan qalīlā*, a small price — indicates worldly gain like bribery, status, or favour, which is small compared to the Hereafter. The same warning appears in *do not trade My revelations for a small price* (2:41) to the Israelites. The verse warns scholars not to distort out of fear of people or for worldly gain — exactly what *samāʿūna li-l-kadhibi akkālūna li-suḥt* in verse 42 did: listened to lies and consumed forbidden gain, trading revelations for a small price.

**Those Who Do Not Judge by What Allah Revealed Are Disbelievers**

*Wa man lam yaḥkum bi-mā anzala Allāhu fa-ulā'ika humu al-kāfirūn* gives a severe judgment. *Man lam yaḥkum*, whoever does not judge; *bi-mā anzala Allāhu*, by what Allah revealed; *fa-ulā'ika humu al-kāfirūn*, they are the disbelievers — indicates that refusing to judge by revelation out of rejection, or believing other judgment is better, is disbelief. The same phrasing appears with variations for *ẓālimūn*, wrongdoers, in verse 45, and *fāsiqūn*, rebellious, in verse 47, showing degrees: disbelief if one denies the obligation to judge by revelation; wrongdoing if one judges unjustly; rebellion if one exits obedience. Early exegetes like Ibn ʿAbbās said this is not the disbelief that expels from Islam for every sin, but *kufr dūna kufr*, lesser disbelief — when one judges by other than revelation while believing revelation is better but following desire. The verse warns against both denying revelation and following desire over it.

**Extra Verified Note on Rabbāniyyūn and Aḥbār**

*Rabbāniyyūn* is defined as those who nurture people with knowledge, and *aḥbār* as scholars who write — two roles: nurturing and writing. The phrase *istuḥfiẓū min kitābi Allāh*, entrusted with the Book, shows that preservation of the Torah was entrusted to scholars, unlike the Qur'an, which Allah promised to preserve Himself: *We will preserve it* (15:9). The difference explains why the Torah was distorted — entrusted to humans who failed — while the Qur'an is preserved by Allah. The command *lā takhshawu an-nāsa wa-khshaw-nī* is a principle for scholars: fear Allah, not people, when judging. The next verse details the *qiṣāṣ* in the Torah — an example of the Allah's judgment they hid.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:45

> We ordained for them in the Torah, "A life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth—and for wounds equal retaliation." But whoever waives it charitably, it will be atonement for them. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the wrongdoers

**Life for Life, Eye for Eye in the Torah**

*Wa katabnā ʿalayhim fīhā anna an-nafsa bi-an-nafsi wa al-ʿayna bi-al-ʿayni wa al-anfa bi-al-anfi wa al-udhuna bi-al-udhuni wa as-sinna bi-as-sinni wa al-jurūḥa qiṣāṣ* reports the ordainment in the Torah. *Katabnā ʿalayhim*, We ordained for them — *katabnā* from *k-t-b*, to write, ordain; *fīhā*, in it, the Torah. *Anna an-nafsa bi-an-nafs*, a life for a life — *nafs*, soul, life — indicates retaliation for murder: a life taken for a life taken unjustly. *Wa al-ʿayna bi-al-ʿayn*, eye for eye, and so on for nose — *anf*; ear — *udhun*; tooth — *sinn*; and *jurūḥ*, wounds — *qiṣāṣ*, equal retaliation. The same law appears in Exodus 21:23–25 and Leviticus 24:19–20, showing the Torah indeed contained *qiṣāṣ*. The verse proves the Torah had a just retaliation law — which Jewish leaders in Madinah avoided, seeking lighter penalties for adultery and, as some reports say, keeping differential blood money based on tribal status. The ordainment shows justice requires equality: life for life regardless of status; eye for eye regardless of tribe.

**Wounds Equal Retaliation as Precision**

*Wa al-jurūḥa qiṣāṣ* — wounds [require] equal retaliation — generalises: all wounds have equal retaliation. *Qiṣāṣ*, from *q-ṣ-ṣ*, to follow, track, because retaliation follows the crime exactly. The phrase indicates retaliation must be equal, not excessive, as *if you punish, punish with the equivalent of what you were harmed* (16:126) states. The precision — eye for eye, nose for nose — shows the Torah's law was detailed and just, not vague. The law of *qiṣāṣ* is both deterrent and just: it deters murder and injury by threatening the equal, and it satisfies the victim's family with justice. The Qur'an confirms *qiṣāṣ* for the Muslims as well: *O believers, qiṣāṣ is prescribed for you in murder* (2:178) and *in qiṣāṣ is life for you* (2:179) — retaliation preserves life by deterring killing. The Torah's ordainment is the same principle the Qur'an confirms.

**Waiving Charitably as Atonement**

*Fa-man taṣaddaqa bihi fa-huwa kaffāratun lahu* — whoever waives it charitably, it is an atonement for him — offers the alternative: waiving retaliation as charity is atonement. *Taṣaddaqa bihi*, waives charitably — from *ṣ-d-q*, to give charity, to be truthful — indicates forgiving *qiṣāṣ* as an act of charity, not obligation. *Fa-huwa kaffāratun lahu*, it is an atonement for him — *kaffārah* from *k-f-r*, to cover — an atonement that covers sin, for the one who waives. The same concept appears in *if you pardon, it is closer to righteousness* (2:237). The verse encourages forgiveness as the higher virtue, though *qiṣāṣ* is allowed. The atonement is for the one who waives: his sins are covered because he waived his right — forgiving is charity that benefits the forgiver in the Hereafter; early reports say when the victim's family waives *qiṣāṣ*, it is expiation for their sins.

**Those Who Do Not Judge by Revelation Are Wrongdoers**

*Wa man lam yaḥkum bi-mā anzala Allāhu fa-ulā'ika humu aẓ-ẓālimūn* — those who do not judge by what Allah revealed are the wrongdoers. *Ẓālimūn* from *ẓ-l-m*, to wrong, place a thing not in its place. This is the second of the trio: verse 44 said *kāfirūn*, disbelievers, for those who deny the obligation to judge by revelation; verse 45 says *ẓālimūn*, wrongdoers, for those who judge unjustly — not giving equal retaliation; verse 47 will say *fāsiqūn*, rebellious, for those who exit obedience. The three describe different aspects of the same failure: disbelief in rejecting revelation, wrongdoing in giving unequal judgment, rebellion in exiting obedience. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *ẓālimūn* here refers to those who judge with inequality — such as giving different penalties based on status, as Jewish leaders did. The verse condemns unequal justice.

**A Verse That Shows the Torah's Justice and Encourages Forgiveness**

This verse shows the Torah contained the just law of equality, which the Jewish leaders violated by having unequal penalties, and that the Qur'an confirms the same law while encouraging forgiveness as charity that is atonement. The balance between *qiṣāṣ* and forgiveness — the law allows both justice and mercy — appears in the verse itself: *a life for a life, but whoever waives charitably, it is atonement*. The verse's placement after verse 44's praise of the Torah as guidance and light shows an example of that guidance: life for life, eye for eye. The next verse discusses the Gospel confirming the Torah and containing guidance and light, continuing the chain of revelation: Torah, then Gospel, then Qur'an — each confirming the previous and containing guidance.

**Extra Verified Note on Qiṣāṣ**

*Qiṣāṣ*, retaliation, is defined as following the crime with the equal — from *qaṣṣa*, to follow, track — because the avenger follows the criminal's act. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *qiṣāṣ* as retaliation that follows exactly. The eye-for-eye law also appears in the Code of Hammurabi, but the Torah's version is more just because it limits retaliation to the equal, not the excessive. The Qur'anic confirmation of *qiṣāṣ* for the Muslims in 2:178–179 shows continuity. The encouragement to waive as charity is verified in a ḥadīth where the Prophet ﷺ said: *no one waives retaliation seeking Allah's pleasure except that Allah raises him in honour* — Sunan al-Tirmidhī (1391, graded *ḥasan*). The next verse discusses the Gospel as confirmation of the Torah.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:46

> Then in the footsteps of the prophets, We sent Jesus, son of Mary, confirming the Torah revealed before him. And We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light and confirming what was revealed in the Torah—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing

**In the Footsteps of the Prophets, Jesus Sent Confirming the Torah**

*Wa qaffaynā ʿalā āthārihim bi-ʿĪsā ibni Maryama muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi mina at-tawrāt* describes Jesus following the previous prophets. *Qaffaynā*, We sent following — from *q-f-w*, to follow footsteps; *ʿalā āthārihim*, on their traces — indicates Jesus came after the prophets who judged by the Torah, following their path, not bringing an entirely new law that abrogates everything, but confirming. *Bi-ʿĪsā ibni Maryam*, with Jesus son of Mary, the lineage emphasising humanity and the virgin birth; *muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi mina at-tawrāt*, confirming what was before him of the Torah — *muṣaddiq* from *ṣ-d-q*, to confirm truth — indicates the Gospel confirms the Torah's truth, not contradicts it. The same phrasing appears for the Qur'an confirming the previous scriptures: *confirming what was before it* (3:3). The confirmation shows continuity: Torah, then Gospel, then Qur'an — each confirms the previous, so those who have the Torah should recognise the Gospel, and those who have both should recognise the Qur'an.

**The Gospel Given Containing Guidance and Light**

*Wa ātaynāhu al-injīla fīhi hudan wa nūr* — We gave him the Gospel, in it guidance and light — repeats the description used for the Torah in verse 44: *fīhā hudan wa nūr* — showing the Gospel also contains guidance and light. *Injīl*, Gospel, from the Greek *euangelion*, good news, contains guidance for monotheism and ethics, and the light that illuminates the path. The Gospel's guidance includes the call to worship Allah alone, to be mindful, and to prepare for the coming messenger: *when Jesus said: O Children of Israel, I am the messenger of Allah to you, confirming the Torah before me and bringing good news of a messenger after me, whose name is Aḥmad* (61:6). The light in the Gospel illuminates, but over time — as verses 13–14 said — a portion was forgotten and words distorted, so the light dimmed, requiring the new light and clear book, the Qur'an, of verses 15–16.

**Confirming the Torah, a Guide and Lesson for the God-Fearing**

*Wa muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi mina at-tawrāti wa hudan wa mawʿiẓatan li-l-muttaqīn* — confirming the Torah, a guide and lesson for the God-fearing — adds two more roles. *Muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi* shows the Gospel confirms the Torah, not abrogating its monotheism; *hudan*, guide, and *mawʿiẓah*, lesson, admonition — from *w-ʿ-ẓ*, to admonish — for *muttaqīn*, the God-fearing, indicates the Gospel guides and admonishes those who fear Allah. The same phrase appears for the Qur'an: *a guide and admonition for the God-fearing* (3:138). The restriction *li-l-muttaqīn*, for the God-fearing, shows guidance benefits those who fear, not those who follow desire, as the previous verses about distorting and consuming forbidden gain showed. The Gospel's confirmation of the Torah also means true followers of the Torah should accept the Gospel — and true followers of both should accept the Qur'an, which confirms both.

**The Chain of Revelation as Confirmation**

The sequence Torah, Gospel, Qur'an — each confirming the previous — shows the chain of revelation: each confirms the truth of the previous and adds. The confirmation is not that the later book copies the earlier, but that it confirms its core: monotheism, justice, accountability. The differences are in law, *sharīʿah*, and way, *minhāj*, as verse 48 will say: *to each We ordained a law and a way*. The Gospel's law was somewhat lighter than the Torah's, easing some restrictions — *I have come to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden* (3:50), Jesus says. The Qur'an then confirms both and is the protector, *muhaymin*, over them, as verse 48 will say. The verse therefore places Jesus in the prophetic chain — not as divine, but as messenger confirming the Torah and bringing the Gospel with guidance and light.

**A Verse That Defines Jesus' Role**

This verse defines Jesus' role as messenger in the footsteps of the prophets, confirming the Torah, bringing the Gospel with guidance and light, a guide and lesson for the God-fearing. It counters the claims that Allah *is* the Messiah (5:17) and that the Messiah is the third of three (5:73), by showing Jesus as messenger with a book, not divine. The same definition appears in *the Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger* (5:75). The verse also shows the Gospel, like the Torah, contained guidance and light — the People of the Book had light, but neglected a portion, leading to enmity and distortion, as earlier verses said. The remedy is the new light and clear book that guides to ways of peace, as verses 15–16 said. The next verse commands the people of the Gospel to judge by what Allah revealed in it, showing that they too must judge by revelation, not desire.

**Extra Verified Note on Qaffaynā**

*Qaffaynā ʿalā āthārihim*, We sent following on their traces, is used for sending messenger after messenger — the same verb appears in *then We sent, following in their footsteps, Our messengers* (23:44, 57:27). The name *Injīl* is Arabicised from Greek and is used twelve times in the Qur'an, always as the book given to Jesus, containing guidance and light. The phrase *hudan wa nūr* appears for the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an — all three share the description — but the Qur'an is also described as *nūran mubīnā*, a clear light, emphasising its clarity.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:47

> So let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed in it. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the rebellious

**The People of the Gospel Must Judge by What Allah Revealed**

*Wa l-yaḥkum ahlu al-injīli bi-mā anzala Allāhu fīh* commands the people of the Gospel to judge by revelation. *L-yaḥkum*, let them judge — or so that they judge; *ahlu al-injīl*, people of the Gospel, the Christians; *bi-mā anzala Allāhu fīh*, by what Allah revealed in it — indicates the Gospel contains Allah's judgment that they must follow, just as the Torah contained Allah's judgment the Jews must follow, as verse 44 said. The command parallels the previous commands to judge by the Torah, showing each community must judge by its own book as long as it is preserved and not abrogated. The Gospel's judgments include monotheism, justice, and preparation for the coming messenger. The verse establishes that the People of the Gospel have the responsibility to judge by what Allah revealed in the Gospel — not by distorted interpretations that claim divinity for the Messiah.

**Those Who Do Not Judge Are Rebellious**

*Wa man lam yaḥkum bi-mā anzala Allāhu fa-ulā'ika humu al-fāsiqūn* — those who do not judge by what Allah revealed are the rebellious — the third in the trio of judgments. *Fāsiqūn* from *f-s-q*, to exit, rebel, indicates exiting from obedience. The trio is: verse 44 *kāfirūn*, disbelievers, for denying the obligation; verse 45 *ẓālimūn*, wrongdoers, for judging unjustly; verse 47 *fāsiqūn*, rebellious, for exiting obedience. The three describe the same act from different angles: disbelief in rejecting revelation, wrongdoing in unjust judgment, rebellion in exiting obedience. Early exegetes like Ibn ʿAbbās said these are not three separate groups but three descriptions of those who do not judge by revelation, with severity depending on belief and action. The verse warns the Christians, like the Jews before, that not judging by the Gospel is *fisq*, rebellion.

**Judging by the Gospel Means Following Its Monotheism**

Judging by what Allah revealed in the Gospel means following its call to worship Allah alone and to follow the messenger who confirms the Torah — not following later doctrines that claim Allah *is* the Messiah or the third of three. The Gospel's true judgment includes *Hear, O Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is One* (Mark 12:29), Jesus saying *I am going to my Father and your Father* (John 20:17), indicating distinction, and *worship the Lord your God* (Matthew 4:10). The Qur'an says *they did not follow the Gospel as it should be followed* (57:27) and *they neglected a portion* (5:14). Judging by the Gospel therefore means returning to its monotheistic core and its prophecy of the coming messenger, Aḥmad — *bringing good news of a messenger after me, whose name is Aḥmad* (61:6). The command *l-yaḥkum ahlu al-injīl* is a call to follow the true Gospel, not the distorted one.

**A Verse That Completes the Parallel for Two Communities**

Verses 44–47 form a parallel: verse 44 — the Torah with guidance and light, prophets and rabbis judged by it, do not fear people but fear Me, do not trade revelations, those who do not judge are disbelievers; verse 45 — life for life ordained, waiving is atonement, those who do not judge are wrongdoers; verse 46 — Jesus confirming the Torah, the Gospel with guidance and light, a guide and lesson for the God-fearing; verse 47 — let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah revealed, those who do not are rebellious. The parallel shows both communities have the same responsibility, the same failure, and the same categories of judgment for failure. The next verse brings the Qur'an as confirmation and protector over the previous books, commanding the Prophet to judge by what Allah revealed and not follow desires — completing the chain: Torah, Gospel, Qur'an — each to be judged by, but the Qur'an the final protector.

**Why Fāsiqūn Here**

*Fāsiqūn*, rebellious, is used for the People of the Gospel who do not judge by the Gospel, while *kāfirūn* was used for the People of the Torah in verse 44 and *ẓālimūn* in verse 45. The variation may indicate that for the Christians, not judging by the Gospel is *fisq*, exiting from obedience, because the Gospel's main call is to follow and prepare for the final messenger — exiting that is rebellion. Al-Rāzī notes that *fisq* is exiting from obedience after knowing, which fits Christians who knew the Gospel's monotheism but exited to the trinity. The three terms together cover all who do not judge by revelation, regardless of community — true belief requires judging by revelation, not by desire.

**Extra Verified Note on Ahl al-Injīl**

*Ahl al-injīl*, people of the Gospel, appears only here in the Qur'an — a specific address to Christians to judge by the Gospel. The command *l-yaḥkum* can be read as imperative — let them judge — or as purpose — so that they judge; both mean they must judge by it. The phrase *fa-ulā'ika humu al-fāsiqūn* is among the verses used to discuss whether ruling by other than revelation is major or minor *kufr*, with Ibn ʿAbbās explaining it can be *kufr dūna kufr*, lesser disbelief, if done while believing revelation is better but following desire. The next verse introduces the Qur'an as confirmation and protector, commanding the Prophet to judge by it.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:48

> We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ this Book with the truth, as a confirmation of previous Scriptures and a supreme authority on them. So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you. To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you with what He has given ˹each of˺ you. So compete with one another in doing good. To Allah you will all return, then He will inform you ˹of the truth˺ regarding your differences

**The Book With Truth as Confirmation and Protector**

*Wa anzalnā ilayka al-kitāba bi-al-ḥaqqi muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi mina al-kitābi wa muhayminan ʿalayh* introduces the Qur'an. *Anzalnā ilayka al-kitāb*, We revealed to you the Book; *bi-al-ḥaqq*, with truth — the Qur'an is revealed with truth, containing truth, and for truth. *Muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi mina al-kitāb*, confirming what was before it of the Book — *muṣaddiq* as before, confirming the truth of the Torah and Gospel — and *muhayminan ʿalayh*, protector, guardian, trustee over them — *muhaymin* from *h-y-m-n*, to protect, watch over, also the name of Allah, *al-Muhaymin*, the Guardian. *Muhaymin* means the Qur'an is the guardian that testifies to the truth of the previous books, preserves and judges over them: what agrees with the Qur'an is true; what contradicts is distorted. The Book with truth confirms the previous scriptures' monotheism and justice, and protects by clarifying what was hidden and distorted, as verses 15 and 41 described.

**Judge Between Them by What Allah Revealed**

*Fa-uḥkum baynahum bi-mā anzala Allāh* commands the Prophet to judge between the People of the Book by what Allah revealed — the Qur'an — not by their distorted desires. *Fa-uḥkum*, so judge; *baynahum*, between them; *bi-mā anzala Allāh*, by what Allah revealed — when they come for judgment, as in verses 42–43, he should judge by the Qur'an, which contains the true judgment matching the Torah and Gospel's originals. *Wa lā tattabiʿ ahwā'ahum ʿammā jāʾaka mina al-ḥaqq* — do not follow their desires away from the truth that has come to you — warns against following *hawā*, desire: *ʿammā jāʾaka mina al-ḥaqq*, away from the truth that came to you — *ḥaqq*, truth, from *ḥ-q-q*, to be true, real. The same warning is repeated in verse 49, showing that following desire is the main danger when judging between People of the Book who seek desire-based rulings — as verse 41 said: *if you are given this, take it; if not, beware*. The command to judge by revelation and not follow desire is the core of the previous verses about not trading revelations for a small price.

**To Each We Ordained a Law and a Way**

*Li-kullin jaʿalnā minkum shirʿatan wa minhājā* — to each of you We ordained a law and a way — explains the plurality of laws. *Li-kullin*, to each community; *jaʿalnā*, We made, ordained; *shirʿatan*, law — from *sh-r-ʿ*, to legislate, open a way; *minhājā*, way, clear path — from *n-h-j*, to be clear, open — indicates each community has its own law and way, though the core *tawḥīd* is the same. *Shirʿah* is the law, *sharīʿah*; *minhāj* is the way that is clear and smooth. The verse acknowledges that different communities have different ritual and legal formulations appointed by Allah — part of divine wisdom, not contradiction. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *shirʿah* and *minhāj* refer to distinct laws for each messenger, while the religion, *dīn*, is one.

**If Allah Had Willed, He Would Have Made You One Community**

*Wa law shāʾa Allāhu la-jaʿalakum ummatan wāḥidah* — if Allah had willed, He would have made you one community — states that diversity is by divine will, not inability. *Law shāʾa*, if He willed; *la-jaʿalakum*, He would have made you; *ummatan wāḥidah*, one community — indicates Allah could have made all one community with one law but did not, for wisdom. The same counterfactual appears in *He would have made you one community, but He leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills* (16:93) and *had Allah willed, He would have made them one community* (42:8). The will not to make one community is to test, as the next clause says — not to create division for its own sake, but to test obedience within diversity, as *We made you into nations and tribes that you may know one another* (49:13) explains diversity as a means to know, not to despise.

**Tested With What He Gave, Competing in Good Deeds**

*Wa lākin li-yabluwakum fī mā ātākum* — but to test you in what He gave you — gives the purpose: *li-yabluwakum*, to test you — from *b-l-w*, to test, try; *fī mā ātākum*, in what He gave you — the different laws and ways are a test of obedience and gratitude. *Fa-istabiqū al-khayrāt* — so compete in good deeds — commands competition in *khayrāt*, good deeds, plural of *khayr*, good. The competition is in good deeds, not in theology — while laws differ, good deeds are the common ground where communities compete. The same command appears in *compete in good deeds* (2:148). *Ilā Allāhi marjiʿukum jamīʿan fa-yunabbi'ukum bi-mā kuntum fīhi takhtalifūn* — to Allah is your return, all together, and He will inform you of what you differed about — shows the final resolution of theological differences is with Allah in the Hereafter, not to be forced in the world: *marjiʿ* from *r-j-ʿ*, to return, and *yunabbi'ukum*, inform — from *n-b-ʾ*, to give important news — Allah clarifies the differences on the Day of Judgment.

**Extra Verified Note on Muhaymin**

*Muhaymin*, protector, guardian, is verified as meaning witness, keeper, trustworthy guardian — from the root *h-y-m-n*, to protect. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *muhaymin* as the guardian who watches, and *al-Muhaymin* as the name of Allah who watches over creation. The description of the Qur'an as *muhayminan ʿalayh*, protector over the previous books, means it is the criterion that judges what is true in them. The phrase *li-kullin jaʿalnā shirʿatan wa minhājā* is used in discussions of religious pluralism, as it acknowledges distinct laws and ways appointed by Allah. The call *fa-istabiqū al-khayrāt*, compete in good deeds, is cited as the basis for interfaith competition in virtue, not conflict. The next verse repeats the command to judge by revelation and warns against being lured away — continuing the emphasis on judging by truth, not desire.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:49

> And judge between them ˹O Prophet˺ by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires. And beware, so they do not lure you away from some of what Allah has revealed to you. If they turn away ˹from Allah's judgment˺, then know that it is Allah's Will to repay them for some of their sins, and that many people are indeed rebellious

**Judge by What Allah Revealed; Do Not Follow Desires**

*Wa ani iḥkum baynahum bi-mā anzala Allāhu wa lā tattabiʿ ahwā'ahum* repeats the command of the previous verse with emphasis. *An iḥkum*, and judge; *baynahum*, between them; *bi-mā anzala Allāh*, by what Allah revealed; *wa lā tattabiʿ ahwā'ahum*, do not follow their desires — *ahwāʾ*, plural of *hawā*, desire, whim — shows that judging must be by revelation, not by the desire of People of the Book who seek favourable rulings. The repetition from verse 48 emphasises the importance, because the temptation to follow desires is strong when judging between powerful groups. The command is to the Prophet ﷺ but also to all judges after him: judge by revelation, not desire. The same warning appeared for Dāwūd: *do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from the way of Allah* (38:26). The verse reinforces that justice requires following revelation, not desire.

**Beware They Do Not Lure You Away**

*Wa-iḥdharhum an yaftinūka ʿan baʿḍi mā anzala Allāhu ilayk* — beware, lest they lure you away from some of what Allah revealed to you — warns of the attempt to lure. *Iḥdharhum*, beware of them — from *ḥ-dh-r*, to be cautious; *an yaftinūka*, lest they tempt or lure you — *yaftinūka* from *f-t-n*, to test with trial, to lure away; *ʿan baʿḍi mā anzala Allāhu ilayk*, from some of what Allah revealed to you. They will try to make him leave part of the revelation, not necessarily all — by suggesting compromise: accept part, leave part. Early reports say leaders tried to persuade the Prophet not to order stoning or to give a lighter penalty, offering to follow him if he did, as in verse 41's *if you are given this, take it*. The warning shows that even leaving *some* of revelation for desire is dangerous, because revelation is interconnected. The command to beware shows that even the Prophet needed caution against being lured — so others need it more.

**If They Turn Away, Allah Will Repay for Sins**

*Fa-in tawallaw fa-ʿlam annamā yurīdu Allāhu an yuṣībahum bi-baʿḍi dhunūbihim* — if they turn away, know that Allah wills to repay them for some of their sins — explains the consequence of turning away. *Tawallaw*, turn away — from *w-l-y*, to turn — indicates they turn away from judgment by revelation. *Fa-ʿlam*, know; *annamā yurīdu Allāhu*, that Allah wills; *an yuṣībahum*, to afflict them — *yuṣībahum* from *ṣ-w-b*, to hit, afflict; *bi-baʿḍi dhunūbihim*, for some of their sins — indicates turning away leads to punishment for some sins in this world, as part of recompense, with more in the Hereafter. The phrase *for some sins* shows that not all sins are punished immediately, but some are, as warning. The affliction can be through defeat, exposure, or other worldly punishment. The verse says: if they turn away from your just judgment, know that their turning away is itself the cause of Allah afflicting them for some sins — so you need not grieve.

**Many People Are Rebellious**

*Wa inna kathīran mina an-nāsi la-fāsiqūn* — many people are indeed rebellious — notes the prevalence of *fisq*, rebellion. *Kathīran mina an-nās*, many people; *la-fāsiqūn*, indeed rebellious — *fāsiqūn* as before, those who exit obedience — indicates that many, not only the People of the Book, are rebellious, so turning away is not surprising. The same phrase appears in verse 81 for a similar context. The observation explains why some turn away: many are rebellious, following desire rather than revelation. The verse consoles the Prophet that turning away is common, not a personal failure — and warns believers not to be among the many rebellious, but among the few who judge by revelation and compete in good deeds, as verse 48 said.

**A Verse That Repeats the Command for Emphasis**

This verse repeats the command to judge by revelation and not follow desires for the third time in two verses (48–49), showing emphasis. The repetition is not redundant but adds new elements: beware lest they lure you away from *some* of what was revealed; and if they turn away, know Allah will repay them for some sins. The structure mirrors the earlier warnings about distorting and forgetting a portion: small deviation leads to larger. The closing *many are rebellious* appears after similar warnings, indicating that rebellion is widespread, so adherence to revelation requires firmness. A verified note on *yaftinūka*: from *fitnah*, a trial that tempts away, it is used for attempts to make the Prophet compromise, as in *they almost tempted you away from what We revealed to you* (17:73), where the polytheists tried to make him compromise. Early tafsīr reports that some said: if you judge with stoning, we will not follow; but if you judge with flogging, we will follow — trying to lure him to leave part of the revelation. The next verse asks whether they seek the judgment of ignorance, contrasting revelation with *jāhiliyyah*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:50

> Is it the judgment of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance they seek? Who could be a better judge than Allah for people of sure faith

**Is It the Judgment of Ignorance They Seek?**

*A-fa-ḥukma al-jāhiliyyati yabghūn* questions whether they seek the judgment of ignorance. *A-fa-*, is it, an interrogative with *fa-*, so; *ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah*, judgment of ignorance — *jāhiliyyah* from *j-h-l*, ignorance, the era before Islam marked by ignorance of divine guidance, tribal custom, following desire; *yabghūn*, they seek — from *b-gh-y*, to seek, desire — indicates they desire the judgment of ignorance instead of Allah's judgment. The question is rhetorical, expressing disapproval: do they seek *jāhiliyyah* judgment after having the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an with guidance and light? The same seeking appeared in *do you see them turning to the judgment of ṭāghūt* (4:60) for similar seeking of non-divine judgment. *Jāhiliyyah* judgment is characterised by following desire, tribal bias, and inequality — as the earlier verses showed the leaders had differential penalties based on status, which is *jāhiliyyah*. The verse asks: after all this guidance, do they still prefer ignorance?

**Who Is a Better Judge Than Allah for People of Sure Faith**

*Wa man aḥsanu mina Allāhi ḥukman li-qawmin yūqinūn* — who is better than Allah in judgment for people of sure faith — asserts Allah's judgment is best. *Man aḥsanu*, who is better — *aḥsan* from *ḥ-s-n*, to be good, beautiful; *ḥukman*, in judgment — indicates no one judges better than Allah. *Li-qawmin yūqinūn*, for people who are certain — *yūqinūn* from *y-q-n*, certainty — indicates those with sure faith recognise Allah's judgment as best, while those without certainty seek *jāhiliyyah*. The question expects the answer: no one is better than Allah in judgment. The comparison is not between Allah and humans in general, but between Allah's judgment based on knowledge, wisdom, and mercy, and *jāhiliyyah* judgment based on desire and ignorance. For people of certainty — who have *yaqīn* — Allah's judgment is clearly better.

**Jāhiliyyah as System Versus Islam as System**

*Al-jāhiliyyah*, ignorance, is not only lack of knowledge but a system of values: tribalism, following desire, inequality — as in *do not display yourselves as in the former jāhiliyyah* (33:33) and *ḥamiyyata al-jāhiliyyah*, the pride of ignorance (48:26). Islam is presented as a system of knowledge, justice, equality — as verses 8, 42, and 44–48 showed: be just, judge with *qiṣṭ*, judge by what Allah revealed. The contrast is between two systems: *jāhiliyyah* and Islam, ignorance and guidance. The verse asks which system they seek, implying that seeking *jāhiliyyah* after guidance is regression. The verse completes the argument that began with the Torah, the Gospel, the Qur'an: each contains guidance and light, and judging by them is best — seeking other than them is ignorance.

**For People of Sure Faith as Qualifier**

*Li-qawmin yūqinūn*, for people who have certainty, qualifies who recognises the best judgment. *Yūqinūn*, those with *yaqīn*, certainty, indicates that certainty of faith leads to recognising Allah's judgment as best; without *yaqīn*, a person may doubt and seek *jāhiliyyah*. The phrase suggests Allah's judgment appears best to those with certainty, because they know Allah's wisdom and mercy, while those without certainty may be swayed by desire. The verse links epistemology and law: certainty leads to accepting divine judgment; doubt leads to seeking ignorance.

**A Verse That Closes the Section on Judging by Revelation**

Verses 41–50 form a unit on judging by revelation: verse 41 — do not grieve for those who race to disbelief, distort, listen to lies; verse 42 — they listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain; judge or turn away; if you judge, with justice; verse 43 — why seek you when the Torah has Allah's judgment; they turn away; not believers; verse 44 — the Torah with guidance and light, prophets and rabbis judged by it, do not fear people, fear Me, do not trade revelations, those who do not judge are disbelievers; verse 45 — life for life, waiving is atonement, those who do not judge are wrongdoers; verse 46 — Jesus confirming the Torah, the Gospel with guidance and light; verse 47 — let the people of the Gospel judge, those who do not are rebellious; verse 48 — the Qur'an with truth, confirmation and protector, judge by what Allah revealed, do not follow desires, to each a law and way, tested, compete in good; verse 49 — judge by revelation, beware luring from some, if they turn away Allah will repay for some sins, many are rebellious; verse 50 — is it the judgment of ignorance they seek? Who is better than Allah for people of sure faith. The unit closes with the rhetorical question that sums it up: seeking *jāhiliyyah* is seeking worse; Allah's judgment is best for those with certainty.

**Extra Verified Note on Jāhiliyyah**

*Jāhiliyyah* is defined as the period before Islam and also as an attribute that can return — a ḥadīth says four matters of *jāhiliyyah* remain among the community. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *jahl* as the opposite of *ʿilm*, knowledge, and *jāhiliyyah* as ignorance — but in Qur'anic usage it also means following desire and tribal custom. The phrase *aḥsanu ḥukman*, better in judgment, shows the superiority of divine law. The closing *li-qawmin yūqinūn* indicates certainty is needed to appreciate divine judgment, and certainty comes from knowledge and reflection: *We made from them leaders guiding by Our command when they were patient and certain of Our signs* (32:24) — patience and certainty lead to guidance. The following verses discuss alliances, warning not to take the Jews and Christians as protectors to the exclusion of believers — also part of judging by revelation versus following desire.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:51

> O believers! Take neither Jews nor Christians as guardians—they are guardians of each other. Whoever does so will be counted as one of them. Surely Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**The Prohibition of Taking Jews and Christians as Guardians**

*Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū lā tattakhidhū al-yahūda wa an-naṣārā awliyāʾ* prohibits taking Jews and Christians as *awliyāʾ*. *Awliyāʾ*, plural of *walī* — from *w-l-y*, to be near, protect, ally — means protectors, allies, close friends to whom one turns for protection and loyalty to the exclusion of the believers. *Lā tattakhidhū*, do not take, indicates the prohibition of taking them as exclusive guardians. *Baʿḍuhum awliyāʾu baʿḍ* — they are guardians of each other — explains that the Jews and Christians are allies of each other against the Muslims, though they differ among themselves, as verses 13–14 showed enmity among Christians and hardened hearts among Jews — but against the Muslims they ally. The prohibition is not against kind dealings — *Allah does not forbid you from being kind and just to those who did not fight you* (60:8) allows kindness — but against taking them as protectors to the exclusion of believers: taking them as allies against Muslims, or relying on them for protection instead of Allah and the believers. The same prohibition appears in *do not take the disbelievers as allies instead of the believers* (4:144).

**Whoever Does So Is Counted Among Them**

*Wa man yatawallahum minkum fa-innahu minhum* — whoever allies with them among you is among them — warns that taking them as guardians makes one counted as part of them. *Yatawallahum*, allies with them — from *w-l-y*, the same root — indicates taking as *walī*. *Fa-innahu minhum*, he is among them, means in ruling and loyalty, not necessarily in faith — but counted with them in opposition. Early reports say some hypocrites in Madinah allied with the Jewish tribes for protection, fearing the Muslims might lose, as verse 52 will describe those with sickness in hearts racing for guardianship. The warning is that alliance for protection out of fear of a turn of fortune leads to being counted among them. The verse distinguishes between permissible dealings and the impermissible alliance that compromises loyalty.

**Allah Does Not Guide the Wrongdoing People**

*Inna Allāha lā yahdī al-qawma aẓ-ẓālimīn* — Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people — seals with the lack of guidance for *ẓālimīn*. *Ẓālimīn*, wrongdoers — from *ẓ-l-m*, to place a thing not in its place — indicates those who place alliance where it does not belong, taking disbelievers as guardians instead of believers. *Lā yahdī*, does not guide, means does not guide to success or the right path, because they chose the wrong alliance. The same seal appears after warnings about alliances: *Allah does not guide the disbelieving people* (5:67) for a similar context. The lack of guidance is the consequence of wrongdoing, not arbitrary denial. The verse warns that taking Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians is *ẓulm* that prevents guidance. The next verse describes those with sickness in hearts racing for guardianship, fearing a turn of fortune — the motive for the prohibited alliance.

**The Context of Madinan Alliances**

The verse was revealed when some Muslims — particularly hypocrites and some with weak faith — sought alliances with the Jewish tribes and Christians for protection, fearing the Muslims might be defeated, as verse 52 says: *we fear a turn of fortune will strike us*. The prohibition is about political alliance that compromises loyalty to the Muslim community, not about personal kindness or business. Classical jurists distinguish between *muwālāt*, alliance of loyalty and protection, which is prohibited, and *muʿāmalah*, dealings like trade and kindness, which is allowed — as *be kind and just* (60:8) permits. The phrase *baʿḍuhum awliyāʾu baʿḍ* indicates the Jews and Christians, despite their differences, ally against the Muslims, so taking them as guardians to the exclusion of believers is unwise.

**Guardianship Belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and the Believers**

The prohibition is clarified by verse 55, which says your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and the believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility. That verse defines the true *awliyāʾ*: Allah, the Messenger, and the believers. The prohibition in verse 51 is therefore about taking Jews and Christians as guardians *instead of* those three, not forbidding all interaction. The verse establishes the loyalty hierarchy: Allah first, then His Messenger, then the believers; taking others as guardians to the exclusion violates the hierarchy. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (3052) says the Muslim community is one hand against the others, indicating internal alliance. The closing — Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people — indicates choosing the wrong guardians is *ẓulm* that blocks guidance.

**Extra Verified Note on Awliyāʾ**

*Awliyāʾ*, plural of *walī*, is defined as protector, ally, close friend, guardian — from *w-l-y*, to be near. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *walī* as one who is near and protects. The prohibition is interpreted as forbidding taking them as allies in war and as protectors to the exclusion of believers, not forbidding kindness, as *Allah does not forbid you from being kind and just* (60:8) clarifies. The phrase *baʿḍuhum awliyāʾu baʿḍ* is verified as describing the alliance of Jews and Christians against the Muslims despite their internal enmity, as verses 13–14 showed. The next verse describes those with sickness racing for guardianship, fearing a turn of fortune.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:52

> You see those with sickness in their hearts racing for their guardianship, saying ˹in justification˺, "We fear a turn of fortune will strike us." But perhaps Allah will bring about ˹your˺ victory or another favour by His command, and they will regret what they have hidden in their hearts

**Sickness in Hearts Racing for Guardianship**

*Fa-tarā alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun yusāriʿūna fīhim* describes the hypocrites with disease in their hearts racing to ally with them. *Tarā*, you see; *alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun*, those with sickness in hearts — *maraḍ* from *m-r-ḍ*, disease; here the spiritual disease of hypocrisy, doubt, weak faith; *yusāriʿūna fīhim*, racing among them, for their guardianship — *yusāriʿūna* from *s-r-ʿ*, to race, hasten — indicates eagerness to ally with the Jews and Christians, as verse 51 prohibited. The sickness is doubt about Islam's victory, so they seek protection from the other side to secure themselves if the Muslims lose. The same phrase appears for the hypocrites in *in their hearts is a disease, so Allah increased their disease* (2:10). The racing shows anxiety: they hasten to secure alliances before the outcome is clear. The verse diagnoses the motive: sickness in heart leads to racing for the guardianship of disbelievers.

**We Fear a Turn of Fortune Will Strike Us**

*Yaqūlūna nakhshā an tuṣībanā dā'irah* — they say: we fear a turn of fortune will strike us — gives the justification. *Nakhshā*, we fear — from *kh-sh-y*, to fear; *an tuṣībanā*, that it will afflict us — *tuṣībanā* from *ṣ-w-b*, to hit, afflict; *dā'irah*, turn of fortune, circle of bad luck — from *d-w-r*, to turn — means a calamity that turns, such as defeat, famine, or loss. They fear the Muslims will be defeated and then the Jews and Christians will take revenge, so they secure the alliance now to be safe. The excuse reveals lack of trust in Allah's promise of victory — as verse 11 said Allah held hands back, and verse 56 will say Allah's party will prevail. The fear of *dā'irah* is natural, but seeking protection from disbelievers instead of Allah shows the sickness. The Qur'an says elsewhere *they fear people as they fear Allah or even more* (4:77) for a similar weak faith.

**Perhaps Allah Will Bring Victory or a Favour**
