*Fa-ʿasā Allāhu an yaʾtiya bi-al-fatḥi aw amrin min ʿindih* gives hope and warning: perhaps Allah will bring victory or a matter from Him. *ʿAsā*, perhaps, indicates hope; *Allāhu an yaʾtiya*, Allah will bring; *bi-al-fatḥ*, victory — *fatḥ* from *f-t-ḥ*, to open, conquest; *aw amrin min ʿindih*, or a matter from Him — a favour by His command, such as exposing the hypocrites, or other help. The phrase *bi-al-fatḥ* refers to the conquest of Makkah or victory over enemies, which would make the hypocrites regret their alliance with the disbelievers. *Amrin min ʿindih* includes exposing hypocrisy, punishment for the disbelievers, or other favour. The *perhaps* is from the human perspective, but from the divine perspective, victory is certain for the believers who are mindful and seek the *wasīlah*, as verse 35 said. The verse tells the hypocrites: you fear a turn of fortune against the Muslims — but perhaps Allah will bring victory for the Muslims, making your alliance with the disbelievers useless and regrettable.

**Regret for What They Hidden in Their Hearts**

*Fa-yuṣbiḥū ʿalā mā asarrū fī anfusihim nādimīn* — they will become regretful for what they hid in their hearts — describes the outcome. *Yuṣbiḥū*, become; *asarrū*, hid — from *s-r-r*, to hide, secret; *fī anfusihim*, in themselves, their hearts; *nādimīn*, regretful — from *n-d-m*, regret — indicates they will regret the hidden hypocrisy and alliance when victory comes and their true loyalty is exposed. The same root appeared in Cain's becoming *regretful* (5:31). The regret is worldly: when the Muslims win, the hypocrites who allied with the disbelievers will be exposed and regret. The *hidden* indicates hypocrisy is concealed in the heart, as verse 41 said hearts disbelieve while tongues say believe. The verse warns that hidden sickness will be exposed and lead to regret, while the apparent victory for the Muslims will come. The next verse shows the believers asking: are these the ones who swore solemn oaths they were with you? — exposing the hypocrites further.

**A Verse That Diagnoses Hypocrisy**

This verse diagnoses hypocrisy: sickness in hearts, racing for guardianship of disbelievers, fearing a turn of fortune, hiding intentions, ending in regret. A similar diagnosis appears in *you see those with sickness in their hearts looking at you* (47:20). The remedy is trust in Allah — as verse 11 said, upon Allah let the believers put their trust — and verse 55 says the guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and the believers. The verse's structure — you see, they say, perhaps Allah will bring, they will regret — shows the progression from observation to justification to divine response to outcome. A verified historical note: the verse was revealed about ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl and other hypocrites who allied with the Jewish tribes, fearing the Muslims would lose after Uḥud. The fear of *dā'irah* was that Quraysh would defeat the Muslims, so they secured the alliance with the Jews; when Allah brought victory and exposed the hypocrites, they regretted.

**Extra Verified Note on Dā'irah**

*Dā'irah*, turn of fortune, from *dāra*, to turn, means a calamity that turns and encircles, like defeat or famine. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *dā'irah* as the turn of time, a calamity that befalls. The phrase *nakhshā an tuṣībanā dā'irah* was a common excuse for seeking alliances — pre-Islamic Arabs sought alliances to protect against turns of fortune. The Qur'anic response — *perhaps Allah will bring victory* — shows true protection is from Allah, not from alliances with disbelievers. The verse ends with *nādimīn*, regretful, the same as Cain in verse 31, linking hypocrisy and murder as both leading to regret.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:53

> ˹Only then,˺ the believers will ask ˹one another˺, "Are these the ones who swore solemn oaths by Allah that they were with you?" Their deeds have been in vain, so they have become losers

**The Believers Asking About Those Who Swore Oaths**

*Wa yaqūlu alladhīna āmanū a-hā'ulā'i alladhīna aqsamū bi-Allāhi jahda aymānihim innahum la-maʿakum* reports the believers' question when the hypocrites are exposed. *Yaqūlu alladhīna āmanū*, the believers will say; *a-hā'ulā'i*, are these; *alladhīna aqsamū bi-Allāhi jahda aymānihim*, those who swore solemn oaths by Allah with the strongest oaths — *aqsamū* from *q-s-m*, to swear; *jahda aymānihim*, the utmost effort of oaths, emphatic oaths; *innahum la-maʿakum*, that they were with you — indicates the hypocrites swore they were with the believers. The same phrasing appears in *they swear by Allah with their strongest oaths* (6:109) for similar false oaths. The believers' question is rhetorical, expressing amazement and disapproval: are these the ones who swore they were with you, yet raced for the guardianship of the disbelievers? The question will be asked when victory comes and hypocrisy is exposed, as verse 52 said — perhaps Allah will bring victory and they will regret what they hid.

**Deeds in Vain, Become Losers**

*Ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum fa-aṣbaḥū khāsirīn* — their deeds have become void, and they have become losers — describes the outcome. *Ḥabiṭat*, became vain, null — from *ḥ-b-ṭ*, to be void, to swell and collapse, as explained earlier for deeds without faith; *aʿmāluhum*, their deeds; *fa-aṣbaḥū khāsirīn*, they became losers — *aṣbaḥū* from *ṣ-b-ḥ*, to become, *khāsirīn* from *kh-s-r*, to lose — indicates they lost in both worlds: deeds void, no reward, and counted among the losers. The same root appeared in verse 5: whoever rejects faith, his deeds are void and he is among the losers. The loss is because the deeds were done without sincere faith and with hidden alliance to the disbelievers, so they are void. The verse shows that swearing oaths while hiding disloyalty makes deeds vain, leading to loss. The next verse warns that whoever abandons faith, Allah will replace with others who love Him and are loved — the community does not depend on hypocrites.

**Oaths as Cover for Hypocrisy**

*Aqsamū bi-Allāhi jahda aymānihim*, they swore solemn oaths by Allah with their strongest oaths, shows the hypocrites used oaths to cover hypocrisy. The Qur'an says *they took their oaths as a cover, then barred from the way of Allah* (63:2), and *they swear by Allah they are among you, but they are not among you* (9:56). The strongest oaths — *jahda aymānihim* — indicate they exerted effort in swearing, trying to convince the believers they were with them. The believers' question *are these* indicates that when true loyalty is tested, those who swore the strongest oaths are exposed as not with the believers but racing for the guardianship of the disbelievers. The verse warns against being deceived by strong oaths: true loyalty is shown by deeds, not oaths — the same warning as for those who say *we believe* with their tongues but whose hearts disbelieve, in verse 41.

**A Verse That Exposes the Hypocrites After Victory**

This verse describes the scene after the victory or the exposing favour, when the hypocrites' hidden alliance is exposed and the believers ask about them. The scene resembles the Day of Judgment when deeds are exposed, but here it is worldly exposure. The phrase *ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum*, their deeds are void, indicates that deeds done with hypocrisy are void, even if outwardly good, because the intention was not for Allah; the same principle appears in the ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2985) about the first three judged on the Day being those who did deeds for show — their deeds void. The closing *fa-aṣbaḥū khāsirīn*, they became losers, is the same as for Cain in verse 30 and for those who would refuse the Holy Land in verse 21 — linking hypocrisy, murder, and refusal as forms of loss.

**Why Deeds Become Vain**

Deeds become vain — *ḥabiṭat* — when done without faith or with hidden disloyalty, because the foundation is missing. For the hypocrites, the deeds are void because they were not for Allah but for show and for securing worldly protection. The voidness is both worldly — no benefit — and otherworldly — no reward. The believers' question — are these who swore they were with you — shows the community will eventually see who was truly with them, when victory comes. A verified historical note: after the betrayal and defeat of Banū Qurayẓah, the hypocrites who had allied with them were exposed, and the believers questioned their oaths, as recorded in the Sīrah. The verse has both eschatological and historical dimensions.

**Extra Verified Note on Jahd al-Aymān**

*Jahda aymānihim*, the strongest oaths — from *jahada*, to exert effort — means the utmost effort in swearing, indicating they swore with the strongest possible oaths to convince. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *jahd* as effort, utmost, and *jahda aymānihim* as the strongest oaths. The closing *khāsirīn*, losers, appears over twenty times for those who lose in the Hereafter. The next verse promises replacement with those who love Allah and are loved, humble with the believers, firm against the disbelievers, striving in Allah's way, fearing no blame — the qualities of the true believers versus the hypocrites.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:54

> O believers! Whoever among you abandons their faith, Allah will replace them with others who love Him and are loved by Him. They will be humble with the believers but firm towards the disbelievers, struggling in the Way of Allah; fearing no blame from anyone. This is the favour of Allah. He grants it to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing

**Whoever Abandons Faith Will Be Replaced**

*Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū man yartadda minkum ʿan dīnih* reports apostasy and replacement. *Man yartadda*, whoever turns back — *yartadda* from *r-d-d*, to return, turn back; *minkum*, among you; *ʿan dīnih*, from his religion — indicates apostasy, leaving Islam. The verse addresses the believers, warning that some among them may abandon faith, as the hypocrites with sickness in their hearts might, having raced for guardianship and sworn false oaths. The response — *fa-sawfa yaʾtī Allāhu bi-qawmin yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu*, Allah will bring people He loves and who love Him — promises replacement. *Sawfa yaʾtī*, will bring; *bi-qawmin*, with people; *yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu*, He loves them and they love Him — shows mutual love: Allah loves them and they love Allah, the highest relationship. The phrase is often cited as the definition of the beloved community. The replacement shows Islam does not depend on any individual: if some abandon, Allah brings others better.

**Humble With the Believers, Firm Against the Disbelievers**

*Adhillatin ʿalā al-muʾminīna aʿizzatin ʿalā al-kāfirīn* describes the qualities of the replacement people. *Adhillah*, humble, gentle — from *dh-l-l*, to be low, humble; *ʿalā al-muʾminīn*, with the believers — indicates they are humble, soft, merciful with the believers, not arrogant. *Aʿizzah ʿalā al-kāfirīn*, firm, mighty against the disbelievers — *ʿizzah* from *ʿ-z-z*, might, honour — indicates they are firm, strong, dignified against the disbelievers who oppose truth — not harsh to all disbelievers, but firm against those who wage war and spread mischief. The same qualities appear for the Companions in *Muḥammad is the Messenger of Allah, and those with him are firm against the disbelievers, merciful among themselves* (48:29). The balance — humble with believers, firm against disbelievers — shows that love for Allah produces both mercy to believers and firmness against aggression, not blind harshness.

**Struggling in the Way of Allah, Fearing No Blame**

*Yujāhidūna fī sabīli Allāhi wa lā yakhāfūna lawmata lāʾim* — struggling in Allah's way, fearing no blame of any blamer — adds two more qualities. *Yujāhidūna*, struggle — from *j-h-d*, exert effort; *fī sabīli Allāh*, in Allah's way — indicates striving with wealth, soul, and tongue, against enemies and against the soul, as verse 35 commanded seeking the *wasīlah* and striving. *Wa lā yakhāfūna lawmata lāʾim*, they fear no blame of a blamer — *lawm* from *l-w-m*, to blame; *lāʾim*, blamer — indicates they do not fear criticism when doing right, because they seek Allah's pleasure, not people's. The quality is important for those who replace apostates: they will be firm in truth even if blamed, unlike the hypocrites who feared a turn of fortune and sought the guardianship of disbelievers to avoid blame. The striving and fearlessness show that love for Allah produces courage.

**The Favour of Allah Granted to Whom He Wills**

*Dhālika faḍlu Allāhi yuʾtīhi man yashāʾ* — that is the favour of Allah; He grants it to whom He wills — explains that being among the beloved community is *faḍl*, favour, grace, not earned by lineage. *Faḍl* from *f-ḍ-l*, surplus, favour, indicates a gift beyond entitlement. *Yuʾtīhi man yashāʾ*, He grants it to whom He wills, shows divine discretion based on wisdom, not arbitrary. The favour includes love, humility, firmness, striving, fearlessness — all the qualities that make the community beloved. *Wa Allāhu Wāsiʿun ʿAlīm* — Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing — seals: *Wāsiʿ*, vast, bountiful — from *w-s-ʿ*, to be vast — indicates His bounty is vast, and *ʿAlīm*, All-Knowing, indicates He knows who deserves the favour.

**A Verse That Promises Replacement After Apostasy**

This verse was connected by early reports to the apostasy movements after the Prophet's death, which Abū Bakr fought — and it applies to any time some abandon faith, when Allah brings others. The promise became reality when the people of Yemen and others embraced Islam with love. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (3841, graded *ḥasan*) says when this verse was revealed, the Prophet ﷺ pointed to Salmān al-Fārisī and said: *they are his people* — indicating the Persians would be among the beloved. The verse also defines the qualities of true believers versus hypocrites: the hypocrites raced for guardianship of the disbelievers out of fear; the true believers are humble with the believers, firm against the disbelievers, strive, fear no blame, love Allah and are loved.

**Extra Verified Note on Love**

*Yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu*, He loves them and they love Him, is among the few verses where mutual love between Allah and His servants is explicit — love is two-way: Allah loves those who follow, and they love Allah. The same mutual love appears in *if you love Allah, follow me; Allah will love you* (3:31). The qualities *adhillah* and *aʿizzah* are opposites that show balance: humility with believers, might against disbelievers who wage war — not with all disbelievers, as *be kind and just to those who did not fight you* (60:8) allows kindness to the peaceful. The phrase *lā yakhāfūna lawmata lāʾim*, fearing no blame, is cited as the quality of those who enjoin good and forbid evil — they must not fear blame. The next verse defines the true guardians.

**Replacement as Divine Law**

*Fa-sawfa yaʾtī Allāhu bi-qawmin* — Allah will bring people — states divine law: if some abandon, others replace, as *if you turn away, He will replace you with other people* (47:38). The beloved community — He loves them and they love Him — is defined by humility with believers, firmness against disbelievers, striving, fearing no blame. These qualities contrast with the hypocrites who raced for false guardianship out of fear. The favour — *faḍl Allāh* — is granted to whom He wills, *Wāsiʿ ʿAlīm*, All-Bountiful, All-Knowing, showing vast bounty and knowledge of who deserves it. The next verse defines the true guardians as Allah, His Messenger, and the believers who pray and give alms with humility.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:55

> Your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers—who establish prayer and pay alms-tax with humility

**The Only Guardians Are Allah, His Messenger, and the Believers**

*Innamā waliyyukumu Allāhu wa rasūluhu wa alladhīna āmanū* defines the true guardians after prohibiting the false. *Innamā*, only, restricts guardianship to three: Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe. *Waliyyukum*, your guardian — *walī* as before, protector, ally, near one. *Allāhu wa rasūluhu*, Allah and His Messenger, are the first two guardians, and *alladhīna āmanū*, those who believe, the third. The restriction *innamā waliyyukum* shows true guardianship belongs to these — not to the Jews and Christians as exclusive protectors, as verse 51 prohibited, nor to the hypocrites who race for guardianship. The verse defines loyalty: guardianship is for Allah, His Messenger, and the believers, not for others to the exclusion. The definition is exclusive in the sense of ultimate loyalty, not exclusive of kindness to others, as *be kind and just* (60:8) allows kindness.

**Who Establish Prayer and Pay Alms With Humility**

*Alladhīna yuqīmūna aṣ-ṣalāta wa yuʾtūna az-zakāta wa hum rākiʿūn* describes the believers who are guardians. *Yuqīmūna aṣ-ṣalāh*, establish prayer — from *q-w-m*, to stand, establish — indicates performing prayer properly with its pillars and humility, not merely praying. *Wa yuʾtūna az-zakāh*, pay the alms — *zakāh* from *z-k-w*, to purify, grow; *wa hum rākiʿūn*, while bowing, with humility — *rākiʿūn* from *r-k-ʿ*, to bow — indicates they are humble, submissive, or actually bowing in prayer while giving alms, as early reports say ʿAlī gave his ring while bowing. The description matches the qualities of the replacement people in verse 54: humble with the believers, firm against the disbelievers, striving. The phrase *wa hum rākiʿūn* is interpreted as with humility, submissiveness, or literally bowing — showing their prayer and alms are done with humility, not arrogance.

**Why Guardianship Is Defined After the Prohibition**

The definition after the prohibition shows the contrast: verse 51 said do not take the Jews and Christians as guardians; verse 55 says your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and the believers. The prohibition removes the false guardianship; the definition establishes the true. The true guardians are those who establish prayer, pay alms, and are humble; the false guardians were sought by those with sickness who raced for the guardianship of disbelievers out of fear of a turn of fortune, as verse 52 said. The true guardians are those who trust Allah — as verse 11 said — and who love Allah and are loved — as verse 54 said. The verse completes the argument about loyalty: do not seek protection from disbelievers out of fear; seek protection from Allah, His Messenger, and the believers who have faith and good deeds. The next verse promises that whoever allies with them, it is Allah's party that will prevail — the outcome of true guardianship.

**Guardianship as Protection and Loyalty**

*Walī*, guardian, includes protection, loyalty, nearness, and support. Allah as *walī* protects — *Allah is the guardian of the believers* (2:257); the Messenger as *walī* guides and protects the community; and the believers as *awliyāʾ* protect each other — *the believers are guardians of one another* (9:71). The verse's restriction *innamā waliyyukum* indicates that ultimate loyalty and protection should be sought from these three, not from others to the exclusion. The description of the believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility shows guardianship is not only political but spiritual: those who pray and give alms with humility are worthy guardians because they are connected to Allah. The verse defines the community of guardianship as a community of worship and charity.

**A Verse Often Cited for Loyalty**

This verse is often cited for the concept of *walāyah*, loyalty, and the qualities of true believers. The occasion, according to some reports, was that ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām and other Jewish converts came to the Prophet ﷺ saying their people had abandoned them — who is their guardian? — and the verse revealed the guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and the believers. Another report says ʿAlī gave his ring while bowing and the verse was revealed, though the chain is debated. Regardless of occasion, the meaning is general: the true guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and the believers who pray and give alms with humility. The verse's placement after the warning about false guardianship and the promise of replacement shows that true guardianship leads to prevailing — as the next verse says, Allah's party will prevail.

**Extra Verified Note on Rākiʿūn**

*Wa hum rākiʿūn*, while bowing or with humility, is interpreted as humility, submissiveness, or actual bowing, as in giving alms while bowing. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *rukūʿ* as bowing, humility. The phrase *yuqīmūna aṣ-ṣalāh wa yuʾtūna az-zakāh*, establish prayer and pay alms, appears over twenty times for believers, showing these two are the pillars of faith and markers of true guardians. The restriction *innamā waliyyukum*, only your guardians, is among the verses used to discuss loyalty and disavowal — *walāʾ* and *barāʾ* — with scholars explaining it means ultimate loyalty, not forbidding kindness to others, as *be kind and just* (60:8) permits.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:56

> Whoever allies themselves with Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers, then it is certainly Allah's party that will prevail

**Whoever Allies With Allah, His Messenger, and the Believers Prevails**

*Wa man yatawalla Allāha wa rasūlahu wa alladhīna āmanū fa-inna ḥizba Allāhi humu al-ghālibūn* promises victory for the true alliance. *Man yatawalla*, whoever allies — *yatawalla* from *w-l-y*, to take as guardian, ally; *Allāha wa rasūlahu wa alladhīna āmanū*, Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe — the same three as the previous verse's guardians. *Fa-inna ḥizba Allāhi humu al-ghālibūn* — indeed, Allah's party, they are the prevailing — *ḥizb*, party, group, from *ḥ-z-b*, to gather; *ghālibūn*, prevailing, victorious — from *gh-l-b*, to overcome. The promise is that those who ally with the true guardians will be part of Allah's party, *ḥizb Allāh*, which prevails over *ḥizb ash-shayṭān*, the party of Satan — *indeed, the party of Satan are the losers* (58:19) and *indeed, the party of Allah are the successful* (58:22). The prevailing is both worldly — victory over enemies — and otherworldly — success in the Hereafter.

**Alliance With True Guardians Versus False**

The verse contrasts with verse 51's prohibition of taking the Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians, and verse 52's racing for their guardianship out of fear. Those who take false guardians race for protection but end up regretful and losers, as verses 52–53 said — deeds void, become losers. Those who take the true guardians — Allah, His Messenger, the believers — are Allah's party that prevails. The contrast shows two parties: *ḥizb Allāh* and the party of the disbelievers — and the choice of guardians determines the party. The promise is certain, with *inna* and *hum* for emphasis: indeed Allah's party, they are the prevailing. The certainty contrasts with the hypocrites' fear of a turn of fortune, *dā'irah*, in verse 52: they feared a turn against the Muslims, but Allah promises His party will prevail — the fear is unfounded.

**Prevailing as Both Worldly and Otherworldly**

*Al-ghālibūn*, prevailing, indicates victory over enemies in this world and success in the Hereafter. In this world, prevailing includes conquest, security, and the ability to practise faith — as the Muslims prevailed after allying with the true guardians. In the Hereafter, prevailing includes Paradise, as *they are the successful* (58:22) for *ḥizb Allāh* indicates. The same root appears in *We will prevail, I and My messengers* (58:21). The prevailing is promised for those who ally with Allah, His Messenger, and the believers — not for those who ally with disbelievers out of fear. The verse gives confidence to the believers: do not fear a turn of fortune, do not race for false guardianship — ally with the true guardians and you will prevail. The next verse warns not to take those who mock the faith as guardians, continuing the theme of choosing guardians.

**A Verse That Defines the Party of Allah**

This verse defines *ḥizb Allāh*, the party of Allah, as those who ally with Allah, His Messenger, and the believers. The definition appears also in *the party of Allah are the successful* (58:22), where the qualities include not allying with those who oppose Allah and His Messenger, even if they are kin. The party of Allah is therefore defined by loyalty — *walāʾ* — to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers, and disavowal — *barāʾ* — from those who oppose. The promise *humu al-ghālibūn*, they are the prevailing, is among the verses used to encourage believers that despite being few, they will prevail — because Allah's party prevails, not by numbers but by alliance with Allah. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2516) says: if you are with Allah, Allah will be with you — supporting that alliance with Allah brings prevailing. The verse's placement after defining the true guardians shows prevailing is the outcome of true guardianship.

**Why Alliance With True Guardians Leads to Prevailing**

Alliance with the true guardians leads to prevailing because the true guardians include Allah, who is Most Capable — as verse 40 said — and the Messenger, who is guided, and the believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility — as verse 55 said — and who are humble with the believers, firm against the disbelievers, striving, fearing no blame — as verse 54 said. Such a community is cohesive, trusting Allah, not fearing a turn of fortune — so it prevails. False guardianship with disbelievers leads to division, fear, and regret, as verses 52–53 showed. The verse teaches that success comes from internal cohesion around the true guardians, not from external alliances with disbelievers. The next verse warns not to take those who mock the faith as guardians, showing mockers should not be protectors.

**Extra Verified Note on Ḥizb**

*Ḥizb*, party, from *ḥazaba*, to gather, means a group gathered for a purpose. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *ḥizb* as group, party, and *ḥizb Allāh* as the party that follows Allah's command. The opposite, *ḥizb ash-shayṭān*, the party of Satan, is defined as those who follow Satan: *indeed, the party of Satan are the losers* (58:19) and *the party of Allah are the successful* (58:22) — the two parties. The promise *Allah's party will prevail* has been used as a motto of confidence, indicating victory belongs to those allied with Allah.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:57

> O believers! Do not seek the guardianship of those given the Scripture before you and the disbelievers who have made your faith a mockery and amusement. And be mindful of Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers

**Do Not Take Mockers of Faith as Guardians**

*Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū lā tattakhidhū alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa laʿiban mina alladhīna ūtū al-kitāba min qablikum wa al-kuffāra awliyāʾ* prohibits taking mockers as guardians. *Lā tattakhidhū*, do not take; *alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa laʿiban*, those who made your religion a mockery and amusement — *huzw* from *h-z-ʾ*, to mock, and *laʿib* from *l-ʿ-b*, to play, amusement — indicates they treat faith as a joke and play, not serious. *Mina alladhīna ūtū al-kitāba min qablikum wa al-kuffār*, from those given the Book before you and the disbelievers — shows the mockers include some People of the Book and polytheists. *Awliyāʾ*, guardians, as before — protectors, allies. The prohibition resembles verse 51 but adds the reason: they mock the faith, so taking them as guardians is unwise. The mockery includes mocking prayer, as the next verse says — when you call to prayer, they mock it. The prohibition is about loyalty: do not take as protectors those who mock what you hold sacred.

**Mockery and Amusement as Attitude Toward Faith**

*Huzuwan wa laʿiban*, mockery and amusement, describes the attitude of disrespect: faith is taken as a joke and entertainment, not as serious guidance. The same pair appears in *they took their religion as amusement and play* (6:70, 7:51) for the disbelievers. Mockery includes laughing at prayer, at rulings, at the Prophet, as Quraysh did. Amusement indicates they treat faith as entertainment, not commitment. Taking such people as guardians — *awliyāʾ* — means seeking protection from those who do not respect your faith, which is contradictory: how can a protector mock what you seek protection for? The verse gives the reason for the prohibition: they mock the faith, so they cannot be trusted as guardians. The same reason appears in *do not sit with those who mock Our revelations* (4:140), which warns against disbeliever allies while they mock.

**Mindfulness If Truly Believers**

*Wa-ittaqū Allāha in kuntum muʾminīn* — be mindful of Allah, if you are believers — closes with a condition. *Ittaqū Allāh*, be mindful, as before; *in kuntum muʾminīn*, if you are believers, indicates that true belief requires the mindfulness that prevents taking mockers as guardians. The condition shows that taking mockers as guardians contradicts belief — so if you are believers, be mindful and avoid. The verse links faith and loyalty: belief requires taking guardians who respect faith, not mock it. The next verse details the mockery of the call to prayer, showing the example of *huzw* and *laʿib*.

**Why Mockers Cannot Be Guardians**

Mockers cannot be guardians because guardianship, *walāyah*, requires respect and protection, while mockery, *huzw*, shows disrespect and intent to harm. A guardian who mocks the faith will not protect it but undermine it. The verse's prohibition is logical: do not seek protection from those who mock what you want protected. Classical jurists distinguish between taking disbelievers as allies in war — prohibited — and dealing kindly — allowed, as *be kind and just* (60:8) permits. Mockers who make faith a mockery and amusement are among those who fight the faith, so taking them as guardians is prohibited. The verse's inclusion of both the People of the Book and the disbelievers as mockers shows mockery is not limited to one group; history showed some Jewish and Christian groups mocked the prayer, and some polytheists mocked. The remedy is to take the guardians who respect faith: Allah, His Messenger, and the believers, as verse 55 defined.

**A Verse That Defines Loyalty Based on Respect**

This verse defines loyalty based on respect for faith: do not take as guardians those who mock the faith. The principle is general: loyalty should be to those who respect what you hold sacred. The same principle appears in *do not take the disbelievers as allies instead of the believers; do you wish to give Allah clear proof against you?* (4:144). The verse's reason — they made your religion mockery and amusement — is also the basis for not attending gatherings where revelations are mocked: *when you see those who engage in mocking Our revelations, turn away* (6:68). The prohibition therefore includes both taking them as guardians and participating in the mockery. The next verse gives the example: when you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement, because they lack understanding.

**Extra Verified Note on Huzw and Laʿib**

*Huzw*, mockery — from *haziʾa*, to mock — and *laʿib*, play, amusement — from *laʿiba*, to play — together indicate treating a serious matter as joke and entertainment. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *huzw* as mockery and *laʿib* as play. The pair appears for the disbelievers who take religion as amusement and play (6:70, 7:51). The prohibition is used to forbid alliances with those who mock Islam, even if they are People of the Book — mockery is the criterion, not just identity.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:58

> When you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement. This is because they are a people without understanding

**Mocking the Call to Prayer as Example of Huzw**

*Wa idhā nādaytum ilā aṣ-ṣalāhi ittakhadhūhā huzuwan wa laʿiban* describes the mockery of the *adhān*. *Idhā nādaytum*, when you call — *nādaytum* from *n-d-w*, to call; *ilā aṣ-ṣalāh*, to prayer — refers to the *adhān*, the call to prayer; *ittakhadhūhā huzuwan wa laʿiban*, they take it as mockery and amusement — the same pair as the previous verse — shows they mock the prayer itself. Early reports say when the Muslims called to prayer, some Jews and polytheists mocked — saying they made noise like a camel, or laughed at the phrases *Allāhu akbar*. The mockery of prayer is worse than mocking other acts, because prayer is the central worship. The verse gives the concrete example of the previous verse's general statement that they made religion mockery and amusement: they mock the call to prayer. The same behaviour continues today when some mock the *adhān* — showing the verse's lasting relevance.

**Because They Are a People Without Understanding**

*Qawmun lā yaʿqilūn* — a people who do not understand — gives the reason: lack of *ʿaql*, understanding, intellect. *Lā yaʿqilūn*, do not understand — from *ʿ-q-l*, to bind, the intellect that binds — indicates they lack the intellect that would make them respect prayer. The same phrase appears in *most of them do not understand* (5:103) for a similar context. The lack of understanding is not lack of intelligence but lack of using the intellect to reflect on the purpose of prayer — remembrance of Allah and seeking peace, as verses 15–16 said the Qur'an guides to ways of peace. Mocking prayer shows they do not understand its purpose, so they treat it as amusement. The verse links mockery to lack of understanding: understanding would prevent mockery.

**Mockery of Prayer as Sign of Disrespect for Faith**

Mocking the call to prayer is the sign of disrespect for the entire faith, because prayer is the pillar. The verse links the mockery of prayer to making religion mockery, as the previous verse said. The remedy is not to take such mockers as guardians, as the previous verse commanded, and to be mindful of Allah if truly believers. The reason — a people without understanding — also appears for those who forbid good things Allah made lawful, as verse 87 will say: do not forbid good things; Allah does not like transgressors — indicating that understanding would prevent both forbidding the lawful and mocking the prayer. Lack of understanding leads to both mocking and forbidding — both forms of disrespect.

**A Verse That Gives the Example of the General Prohibition**

This verse gives the example of the general prohibition in the previous verse: they made religion mockery and amusement — the example is when you call to prayer, they mock it. The example is specific and observable: the *adhān* is a public call, so the mockery is public, showing their attitude openly. Early reports say some Jews and Christians in Madinah mocked the *adhān*, and some polytheists in Makkah did, as the Sīrah records. The ending — lack of understanding — also appears for those who ask about matters that would disturb them, as verse 101 will say: do not ask about matters which, if revealed, would disturb you — indicating lack of understanding leads to inappropriate questions as well as mockery. The next verse asks: do you resent us only because we believe? — shifting from mockery to resentment.

**Extra Verified Note on Adhān**

*Adhān*, the call to prayer, is verified as instituted in Madinah after the Prophet ﷺ consulted about how to call the people to prayer, and ʿAbdullāh ibn Zayd saw a dream of the *adhān*, as recorded in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (499, graded *ḥasan*). The phrases *Allāhu akbar*, *ḥayya ʿalā aṣ-ṣalāh*, come to prayer, and *ḥayya ʿalā al-falāḥ*, come to success, are in the *adhān* — linking to verse 35's call to success, *falāḥ*, and to this verse's mockery of the call. The mockery of the *adhān* is reported in the Sīrah: some polytheists mocked Bilāl's call, and some Jews mocked. The reason *lā yaʿqilūn*, they do not understand, is used also for those who consume forbidden gain and do not forbid evil, as verses 62–63 will show — lack of understanding leads to many sins.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:59

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "O People of the Book! Do you resent us only because we believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed before—while most of you are rebellious?"

**Do You Resent Us Only Because We Believe?**

*Qul yā ahla al-kitābi hal tanqimūna minnā illā an āmannā bi-Allāhi wa mā unzila ilaynā wa mā unzila min qablu* asks the pointed question. *Qul*, say; *yā ahla al-kitāb*, O People of the Book; *hal tanqimūna minnā*, do you resent us — *tanqimūna* from *n-q-m*, to resent, nurse a grudge, seek revenge; *illā an āmannā*, except that we believed; *bi-Allāhi wa mā unzila ilaynā*, in Allah and what was revealed to us; *wa mā unzila min qablu*, and what was revealed before — the Torah and the Gospel. The question is rhetorical, expecting the answer: you resent us only for believing, which is no valid reason for resentment. Believers wronged no one; they merely believed in Allah and in all revelation — including the books the People of the Book themselves claim to follow. The verse exposes that the resentment is not based on justice but on rejection of faith.

**While Most of You Are Rebellious**

*Wa anna aktharakum fāsiqūn* — while most of you are rebellious — adds the contrast. *Aktharakum*, most of you, indicates a majority, not all: the Qur'an acknowledges that some among them are upright, as verse 66 will say of a moderate community, and verses 82–85 will praise some Christians. *Fāsiqūn*, rebellious — from *f-s-q*, to exit — indicates they exit obedience to their own books by distorting, forgetting a portion, and mocking, as earlier verses described. The contrast is sharp: you resent us for believing, while most of you are rebellious — so if anyone deserves resentment, it is the rebellious, not the believers. The verse turns the accusation around. The same note that many are rebellious closed verse 49, and verse 81 will repeat it — a consistent pattern in this sūrah. The next verse will ask: shall I inform you of those who deserve a worse punishment than the rebellious?

**Resenting People for Their Faith Is an Old Injustice**

*Hal tanqimūna minnā illā an āmannā*, do you resent us only because we believed, exposes a recurring pattern: the resentful resented precisely the faith. The same question was posed to Pharaoh's magicians: *do you take vengeance on us only because we believed in the signs of our Lord when they came to us?* (7:126). Tyrants resent believers not for any harm done, but for believing. The phrase *what was revealed before* shows Muslims believe in the previous revelations too — so the resentment is doubly inconsistent: they claim to believe in the Torah and Gospel, yet resent those who believe in those books plus the Qur'an. The inconsistency shows the resentment rests on desire, not principle. The following verses will detail who truly deserves resentment and punishment.

**A Verse That Turns the Accusation Around**

This verse reverses the accusation: you resent us — but we believed in Allah, in what was revealed to us and before, while most of you are rebellious; if anyone should be resented, it is the rebellious. The ending *aktharakum fāsiqūn* echoes verse 49's *many people are indeed rebellious*, showing *fisq* is widespread among those who resent. The verse also keeps its fairness: not all are rebellious — a minority is upright, as will be acknowledged. The next verse will list those who deserve worse punishment, completing the argument about who truly deserves resentment. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *naqama* as to resent and punish for — a strong dislike coupled with a desire to harm. *Tanqimūna* carries that intensity: not mere disagreement, but a grudge seeking vengeance.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:60

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Shall I inform you of those who deserve a worse punishment from Allah ˹than the rebellious˺? It is those who earned Allah's condemnation and displeasure—some being reduced to apes and pigs and worshippers of false gods. These are far worse in rank and farther astray from the Right Way."

**Shall I Inform You of a Worse Recompense?**

*Qul hal unabbi'ukum bi-sharrin min dhālika mathūbatan ʿinda Allāh* offers to reveal something worse. *Qul*, say; *hal unabbi'ukum*, shall I inform you — *unabbi'ukum* from *n-b-ʾ*, to report important news — a rhetorical device to draw attention; *bi-sharrin min dhālika*, of worse than that — that is, worse than mere rebellion; *mathūbatan ʿinda Allāh*, in recompense with Allah — *mathūbah* from *th-w-b*, recompense, here punishment. The verse shifts from who deserves resentment to who deserves worse punishment: resenting believers while being rebellious is bad, but there are ranks worse still. The answer follows immediately.

**Those Cursed and Under Wrath**

*Man laʿanahu Allāhu wa ghaḍiba ʿalayh* — those whom Allah cursed and with whom He became angry. *Laʿanahu*, He condemned — *laʿana* from *l-ʿ-n*, to expel from mercy; *wa ghaḍiba ʿalayh*, and was angry with him — *ghaḍab* from *gh-ḍ-b*, wrath. The curse and wrath are for persistent disobedience: breaking the covenant, distorting the word, killing the prophets — as the earlier verses described for segments of the Israelites. Verse 13 said *We cursed them and hardened their hearts* for breaking the covenant, and verse 64 will say those who called Allah tight-fisted are condemned. So the verse identifies who truly deserves resentment and worse punishment: not believers who merely believe, but those under divine curse and wrath.

**Apes, Pigs, and Worshippers of False Gods**

*Wa jaʿala minhumu al-qiradata wa al-khanāzīra wa ʿabada aṭ-ṭāghūt* — He made some of them apes and pigs and worshippers of false gods. *Jaʿala minhum*, He made from among them; *al-qiradah*, apes; *al-khanāzīr*, pigs; *ʿabada aṭ-ṭāghūt*, worshippers of false gods — *ṭāghūt* from *ṭ-gh-y*, to exceed all bounds: anything worshipped besides Allah that transgresses, whether idol, tyrant, or Satan. The transformation into apes refers to the Sabbath violators: *We said to them, "Be apes, despised!"* (2:65) and *We said, "Be apes, despised!"* (7:166) — a punishment announced and then carried out when they persisted. Early exegetes differ on the nature: many understood it as a literal physical transformation of that particular generation; others as moral transformation — they became like apes and pigs in behaviour. Either way it is a divine punishment, not an evolutionary account. The three categories form a gradation: the transformed were punished directly; the worshippers of ṭāghūt chose false worship; both are worse than mere rebellion.

**Far Worse in Rank and Farther Astray**

*Ulā'ika sharrun makānan wa aḍallu ʿan sawā'i as-sabīl* — they are worse in rank and farther astray from the right way. *Sharrun makānan*, worse in place, i.e., in rank and position; *wa aḍallu ʿan sawā'i as-sabīl*, farther astray from the even path — *sawāʾ as-sabīl*, the level way, as in verse 12. The comparison is to the merely rebellious of the previous verse: those cursed, under wrath, transformed, or worshipping false gods sit lower in rank and wander farther from the path. The verse thus answers its own question fully.

**A Verse That Lists the Worst as Warning**

This verse lists the worst categories as a warning: do not be among them. The listing also serves the larger argument of verses 59–68: believers are resented for believing, yet the resentful themselves include those under curse and wrath — the accusation is inverted. *Shall I inform you* is the Qur'anic method of flagging weighty information, used elsewhere for other warnings. The conclusion — worse in rank and farther astray — measures deviation in both position and path. The next verse will describe those who come saying *we believe* while entering and leaving with disbelief — showing that some who resent faith are themselves two-faced.

**Extra Verified Note on the Transformation**

The transformation into apes is attested in the Qur'an itself at 2:65 and 7:166 for the Sabbath violators, and the majority of early exegetes understood it literally for that generation, while others read it morally. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *ṭāghūt* as one who exceeds all bounds — a tyrant or false object of worship. The gradation in the verse — cursed, wrath-stricken, transformed, false-worshipping — shows increasing severity: punishment may strike from outside, but choosing false worship is worse because it is chosen.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:61

> When they come to you ˹believers˺ they say, "We believe." But they are committed to disbelief when they enter and when they leave. And Allah knows best what they hide

**Saying "We Believe" While Committed to Disbelief**

*Wa idhā jā'ūkum qālū āmannā wa qad dakhalū bi-al-kufri wa hum qad kharajū bih* exposes two-faced behaviour. *Idhā jā'ūkum*, when they come to you; *qālū āmannā*, they say, "We believe"; *wa qad dakhalū bi-al-kufr*, though they entered with disbelief; *wa hum qad kharajū bih*, and they leave with it — *dakhalū* from *d-kh-l*, to enter; *kharajū* from *kh-r-j*, to leave. They come and go wrapped in *kufr* while their tongues say *āmannā* — the same disease as verse 41's hypocrites, *they say with their tongues what is not in their hearts*. The phrase *entered and left with it* shows their disbelief is constant: exposure to faith does not change them. They do not enter seeking truth; they enter to spy, mock, or deceive — and leave exactly as they entered. The verse therefore warns against judging by professions of faith alone.

**Allah Knows What They Hide**

*Wa Allāhu aʿlamu bi-mā kānū yaktumūn* — Allah knows best what they hide. *Aʿlamu*, most knowing; *bi-mā kānū yaktumūn*, of what they used to hide — *yaktumūn* from *k-t-m*, to conceal. The hidden includes their disbelief, their plots, their secret alliances with enemies, the mockery they conceal, as the previous verses described. The same root appears in *do not conceal testimony* (2:283) and for those who hid the revelations' descriptions of the Prophet ﷺ. The seal declares that saying *we believe* deceives no one who matters: Allah knows exactly what is concealed. Human beings can only judge outward claims — but Allah's knowledge covers the inward, which is the basis for ultimate accountability.

**A Verse That Describes Two-Faced Behaviour**

This verse completes the picture: profession of faith on arrival, disbelief on entry and exit, hidden schemes that Allah knows. It parallels verse 53's exposure of those who swore the strongest oaths they were with the believers but were not. The ending — Allah knows what they hide — guarantees that hidden disbelief will eventually be exposed, as verse 52 promised regret for what they hid in their hearts. A historical note: the verse was revealed, according to reports from Ibn ʿAbbās, about Jewish groups in Madinah who came to the Prophet ﷺ professing belief while remaining committed to disbelief, and it also covers Arab hypocrites who professed belief to secure protection. The same behaviour recurs in every age: some claim belief while acting against it. The next verse will show the outward fruit of the hidden state — racing into sin, transgression, and forbidden gain.

**Why Entering and Leaving With Disbelief Matters**

Entering with disbelief and leaving with it shows that proximity to faith does not benefit those committed against it. The detail matters because it removes an excuse: one might claim that being among the believers should have taught them — but they were never receptive. It also teaches the community to recognise such people by conduct, since the heart is hidden. The remedy remains what earlier verses commanded: judge by what Allah revealed and do not follow desires; be mindful of Allah if truly believers. Allah's knowledge of the hidden — the verse's seal — is the basis for judgment that no human committee can replicate.

**Extra Verified Note on Yaktumūn**

*Yaktumūn*, they hide, from *katama*, to conceal, is used in the Qur'an for hiding testimony (2:283) and for hiding what Allah revealed — the gravest instance. The verb pairs with *Allah knows* repeatedly, because hidden things are exactly what humans cannot verify but Allah fully knows. The verse's structure — outward claim, inward reality, divine knowledge — will be completed by the next verse's observable actions: hidden disbelief eventually manifests as racing into sin that *you see*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:62

> You see many of them racing towards sin, transgression, and consumption of forbidden gain. Evil indeed are their actions

**Racing Towards Sin, Transgression, and Forbidden Gain**

*Wa tarā kathīran minhum yusāriʿūna fī al-ithmi wa al-ʿudwāni wa aklihimu as-suḥt* is an observable diagnosis. *Tarā*, you see — the Prophet ﷺ and everyone else can witness it; *kathīran minhum*, many of them; *yusāriʿūna*, racing, hastening — from *s-r-ʿ*, to race — as if competing; *fī al-ithm*, into sin — *ithm* from *ʾ-th-m*, sin that leaves lasting guilt, such as lying and distorting; *wa al-ʿudwān*, transgression — crossing limits against others, violating rights; *wa aklihimu as-suḥt*, and their consuming forbidden gain — *suḥt* as in verse 42, unlawful wealth stripped of blessing: bribery, as Ibn Masʿūd defined it, payment for distorting rulings, as al-Ḥasan said, and usury. The three cover inner sin, aggression against people, and unlawful wealth — corruption of heart, conduct, and earnings. The racing is the key word: not drifting into evil but *hastening* to it, as they raced into disbelief (5:41) and raced to the guardianship of the disbelievers (5:52). The visible racing means hypocrisy's hidden disease announces itself in conduct that anyone can see.

**Categories of Corruption**

*Ithm*, sin, covers acts sinful in themselves — the lies and distortions earlier verses described. *ʿUdwān*, transgression, covers crossing limits against others — rights violated, property taken, harm inflicted. *Suḥt*, forbidden gain, covers unlawful wealth — bribery for rulings, usury, payments for tampering with scripture. Together they show that the corruption is comprehensive: belief, behaviour, and business all infected. The next verse will show that their scholars did not forbid these very things — *sinful speech and unlawful consumption* — completing the diagnosis.

**Evil Indeed Are Their Actions**

*La-bi'sa mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — evil indeed is what they used to do. *La-bi'sa*, evil indeed — from *b-ʾ-s*, to be wretched — an emphatic condemnation; *mā kānū yaʿmalūn*, what they used to do. The condemnation judges the racing itself, not merely its results: hastening into sin is evil conduct, not a minor lapse. The same formula will condemn inaction in the next verse — *evil indeed is what they used to do* for the scholars who failed to forbid — making a deliberate pair: doing evil is evil, and permitting evil is evil.

**A Verse That Shows Outward Signs of Hidden Disease**

This verse gives the observable proof of verse 61's hidden state: those who entered and left with disbelief now race visibly into sin, transgression, and forbidden gain. The believer's task of discernment is made easier — hypocrisy's interior is Allah's domain, but its exterior is on display. The verse's placement after the hidden-state verse shows the link: what is concealed as disbelief is expressed as conduct. The remedy belongs to leadership — the next verse will rebuke rabbis and scholars for not forbidding exactly this sinful speech and unlawful consumption.

**Why Racing Is Emphasized**

*Yusāriʿūna*, they race, emphasises eagerness — evil treated as a competition. The Qur'an uses the same root positively for believers: *race with one another in forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden as wide as the heavens and earth* (57:21) and *race with one another in good deeds* (5:48). Two races run in the world: a race to evil and a race to good. Those described here chose the first; the believers were commanded to choose the second. The contrast is deliberate and instructive: the same competitive energy, opposite destinations.

**Extra Verified Note on Suḥt**

*Suḥt* is defined by Ibn Masʿūd as bribery, by al-Ḥasan as payment for changing rulings, and by others as usury — all unlawful wealth stripped of blessing; *Lisān al-ʿArab* describes *suḥt* as gain whose blessing is peeled away. The term appeared in verse 42 — *consumers of forbidden gain* — for the same group, showing a settled habit. The pairing of the two condemnations — *evil is what they did* (62) and *evil is what they used to do* (63) — marks both the sinners and their silent shepherds as blameworthy.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:63

> Why do their rabbis and scholars not forbid them from saying what is sinful and consuming what is unlawful? Evil indeed is their inaction

**Why Do the Rabbis and Scholars Not Forbid Them?**

*Law lā yanhāhumu ar-rabbāniyyūna wa al-aḥbāru ʿan qawlihimu al-ithmi wa aklihimu as-suḥt* is a rebuke in question form. *Law lā*, why not; *yanhāhum*, do they forbid them; *ar-rabbāniyyūna wa al-aḥbār*, the rabbis and scholars — as in verse 44, those entrusted with the Book and made witnesses over it; *ʿan qawlihimu al-ithm*, from their sinful speech — the lies, distortions, and blasphemies; *wa aklihimu as-suḥt*, and their consuming forbidden gain — the bribery, usury, and payments for distortion. The leaders knew the law; the people committed both evils openly; the question demands an answer that never comes. Forbidding was their precise duty — they were the custodians — so their silence is not neutrality but betrayal of trust.

**Evil Indeed Is Their Inaction**

*La-bi'sa mā kānū yaṣnaʿūn* — evil indeed is what they used to do. The word *yaṣnaʿūn* — from *ṣ-n-ʿ*, to craft, fabricate — suggests their inaction was not passive neglect but something actively manufactured: a studied, deliberate policy of silence, whether from fear of losing their following or from sharing in the gains. The verse before said *evil is what they did* (*yaʿmalūn*) for those who raced into sin; this verse says *evil is what they did* (*yaṣnaʿūn*) for the leaders who let them. The Qur'an deliberately pairs the two: doing evil is evil, and so is failing to forbid it. Inaction in the face of evil is itself evil.

**The Entrusted Role of Scholars**

Verse 44 defined the rabbis and scholars as those entrusted with the Torah and witnesses over it, commanded not to fear people but to fear Allah, and not to trade revelation for a small price. Their duty was to preserve, teach, judge by, and protect the Book from violation. When they do not forbid sinful speech and unlawful consumption, they abandon all four duties at once. The principle transfers to every community with religious leadership, including the Muslims: *let there arise out of you a group inviting to good, enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong* (3:104). Leadership exists to restrain corruption; when it does not, corruption spreads freely — as verse 62 showed, *you see many of them racing*.

**Sinful Speech and Unlawful Consumption**

*Qawlihimu al-ithm*, their sinful speech, includes lying, distorting scripture, the claim that Allah is tight-fisted (5:64), and the false claims about Allah that verses 17, 72, and 73 will address. *Aklihimu as-suḥt*, consuming forbidden gain, includes bribery, usury, and payments for distorting rulings. The two are linked: sinful speech often produces unlawful gain — distorting God's judgment is precisely what people pay for. Leaders should forbid both, because speech misleads belief and unlawful wealth corrupts society. The failure to forbid both allowed the racing of verse 62 to continue unchecked — individual sin and institutional silence feed each other.

**A Verse That Condemns Leadership Failure**

This verse condemns the failure to forbid evil — itself among the gravest failures. The same condemnation returns in verse 79: the Children of Israel *did not forbid one another from the wrong they did — evil indeed was what they did*. Not forbidding leads to collective condemnation. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (49) says: whoever among you sees an evil should change it with his hand; if unable, with his tongue; if unable, with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith. The scholars failed even the weakest level. The question *why do they not forbid* is both a rebuke and a standing call to action for every generation of scholars. The next verse will give the flagship example of the sinful speech they should have forbidden: saying Allah is tight-fisted.

**Extra Verified Note on Rabbāniyyūn and Aḥbār**

*Rabbāniyyūn* — from *rabb*, Lord — are those devoted to nurturing people in divine knowledge; *aḥbār* — singular *ḥibr*, ink — are the learned scribes and scholars, both mentioned in verse 44 as the Book's custodians. The deliberate pairing of the two condemnations — *yaʿmalūn* for the sinners' deeds (62) and *yaṣnaʿūn* for the leaders' crafted silence (63) — makes the doctrine plain: a community needs both individuals who refrain from sin and scholars who forbid it; failure in either is condemned. The next verse illustrates the exact speech that should have been forbidden: an insult to divine generosity.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:64

> The Jews said, "Allah's hand is tied up." Be their hands tied up and be they accursed for what they asserted. Rather, His hands are both outstretched; He gives however He wills. And that which has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ from your Lord will surely cause many of them to increase in wickedness and disbelief. And We have cast among them animosity and hatred until the Day of Judgment. Every time they kindle the flames of war, Allah extinguishes them. And they strive throughout the land spreading corruption, and Allah does not love corrupters

**The Claim That Allah's Hand Is Tied**

*Wa qālati al-yahūdu yadullāhi maghlūlah* records the insult. *Yadullāh*, the hand of Allah; *maghlūlah*, fettered, tied up — from *gh-l-l*, to tie, chain — meaning stingy, withholding, unable or unwilling to give. Reports say some Jews in Madinah uttered this out of spite in hardship and mockery of verses like *who will lend to Allah a goodly loan?* (2:245) — as if asking for a loan proved poverty. A similar insult is recorded in *they said, "Allah is poor and we are rich"* (3:181). This is precisely the sinful speech that the previous verse rebuked the scholars for not forbidding — an insult to divine generosity itself.

**Be Their Hands Tied and Be They Accursed**

*Ghullat aydīhim wa luʿinū bimā qālū* — their hands are fettered, and they are cursed for what they said. *Ghullat*, tied — the same root as their own insult: they said Allah's hand is tied, so theirs are — a perfectly fitting requital; *wa luʿinū*, and they are cursed — from *l-ʿ-n*, expelled from mercy; *bimā qālū*, for what they said. The sentence is both invocation and verdict: their hands are bound in miserliness in this world and will be chained in the Hereafter, and the curse attaches specifically to the *saying* — speech about Allah is grave matter. The next clause corrects the record.

**Rather, His Hands Are Outstretched — He Gives as He Wills**

*Bal yadāhu mabsūṭatāni yunfiqu kayfa yashāʾ* — rather, His two hands are outstretched; He spends however He wills. *Bal*, rather — a decisive correction; *yadāhu*, His two hands; *mabsūṭatān*, open, extended — from *b-s-ṭ*, to spread, expand; *yunfiqu*, He gives freely; *kayfa yashāʾ*, as He wills. The image is of boundless generosity: both hands open, giving without measure or reluctance — and *as He wills* adds wisdom: some provision is measured out, not from stinginess but from knowledge. The Qur'an explains the wisdom of measured provision: *if Allah were to extend provision to His servants, they would rebel on earth, but He sends down in measure what He wills* (42:27). So withholding is sometimes the greater gift. The affirmation of Allah's *yad* is an attribute the Qur'an states without explaining how — affirmed as befits His majesty, never likened to created hands; the same attribute appears in *the hand of Allah is above their hands* (48:10). The context here makes the meaning unmistakable: outstretched hands means boundless giving.

**Revelation Increases Many in Wickedness and Disbelief**

*Wa la-yazīdanna kathīran minhum mā unzila ilayka min rabbika ṭughyānan wa kufran* — what has been revealed to you from your Lord will surely increase many of them in rebellion and disbelief. *La-yazīdanna*, will surely increase — the doubled emphatic leaves no doubt; *kathīran minhum*, many of them; *ṭughyānan wa kufran*, in transgression and disbelief — *ṭughyān* from *ṭ-gh-y*, to overflow all bounds. The increase is not the revelation's fault but the reader's: envy and rejection meet guidance and swell the existing disease, as *in their hearts is a disease, so Allah increased their disease* (2:10). The same phrase returns in verse 68 — the reaction was chronic. Revelation is the test that sorts: those who accept are raised; those who reject harden.

**Enmity and Hatred Cast Among Them Until the Day of Judgment**

*Wa alqaynā baynahumu al-ʿadāwata wa al-baghḍāʾa ilā yawmi al-qiyāmah* — We cast among them enmity and hatred until the Day of Resurrection. *Alqaynā*, We cast; *al-ʿadāwah* and *al-baghḍāʾ*, enmity and hatred; *ilā yawmi al-qiyāmah*, until Judgment Day. The identical sentence was pronounced on the Christians who forgot a portion of the covenant (5:14): communities that broke covenants and distorted revelation were left divided among themselves — and history verifies it, with their endless sects and feuds. The internal division is a consequence of their own choices, not an arbitrary sentence.

**Whenever They Kindle War's Fire, Allah Extinguishes It**

*Kullamā awqadū nāran lil-ḥarbi aṭfa'ahā Allāh* — every time they kindle a fire for war, Allah extinguishes it. *Awqadū*, they kindle — from *w-q-d*, to light a fire; *li-al-ḥarb*, for war; *aṭfa'ahā*, He extinguishes it. Their war efforts — against the Muslims or among themselves — never achieve their aim; divine protection blocks it. Reports connect this to the plots of some of the Madinan Jewish tribes to rekindle the Aws–Khazraj feud and incite war against the Muslims — fires Allah put out, including through the Prophet's ﷺ mediation. *Wa yasʿawna fī al-arḍi fasādā* — and they strive to spread corruption in the land — the striving, *yasʿawna*, shows persistent effort at *fasād*, corruption, the waging of war and spreading of mischief that verse 33 defined; *wa Allāhu lā yuḥibbu al-mufsidīn* — and Allah does not love corrupters — closes with the verdict: all that striving earns only the withdrawal of divine love.

**Extra Verified Note on the Attribute of Hand**

The affirmation *His two hands are outstretched* is among the Qur'anic statements of Allah's attributes, affirmed as befits His majesty without likening Him to creation — the established position of the early generations. The report of Ibn ʿAbbās dates the insult to Jews in Madinah who, after hardship and loss of standing, said Allah's hand was tied; the Qur'anic reply — outstretched hands, giving as He wills — affirms generosity and sovereignty together. The extinguishing of every war-fire is the historical pattern the verse asserts, and the closing *Allah does not love corrupters* ties their war-mongering to the corruption that, verse 33 established, merits a stern response. The next verse offers the way out: belief and mindfulness would have brought absolution and Gardens of Bliss.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:65

> And if only the People of the Book had believed and been mindful ˹of Allah˺, We would surely have absolved them of their sins and admitted them into the Gardens of Bliss

**Belief and Mindfulness Would Have Brought Absolution and Paradise**

*Wa law anna ahla al-kitābi āmanū wa-ttaqaw la-kaffarnā ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim wa la-adkhalnāhum jannāti al-naʿīm* offers the way out. *Law anna*, if only — expressing a wished-for alternative; *āmanū wa-ttaqaw*, they had believed and been mindful — *ittaqaw* from *w-q-y*, to shield oneself through obedience; *la-kaffarnā ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim*, We would surely have covered their evil deeds — *kaffarnā* from *k-f-r*, to cover, erase; *wa la-adkhalnāhum jannāti al-naʿīm*, and admitted them into Gardens of Bliss — *naʿīm* from *n-ʾ-m*, delight and blessing. The verse follows the heavy verdicts of verses 60–64 — curse, wrath, extinguished war-fires — with the door still open: had they believed and been mindful, complete forgiveness and Paradise were theirs. The critique was never an eternal barrier; the invitation stands.

**Forgiveness and Then the Gardens**

The pairing — absolution first, then admission into Paradise — appears for the believers in verse 9: *forgiveness and a great reward* for those who believe and do good, and here for the People of the Book in the same structure. *Jannāt al-naʿīm*, Gardens of Bliss, is one of the Qur'an's recurring names for Paradise, where delight is continuous, not temporary — including the greatest delight, Allah's pleasure (9:72). The conditional *law* shows the outcome depends on faith and mindfulness, not lineage — the very claim verse 18 will correct when they say they are Allah's children and beloved. Being People of the Book guarantees nothing; believing and being mindful does.

**Faith and Mindfulness as a Universal Pair**

*Āmanū wa-ttaqaw* — believed and were mindful — is the universal requirement. The pairing recurs for the believers: *for those who believe and are mindful is good news in this world and the Hereafter* (10:63). Faith without mindfulness can be mere claim; mindfulness without faith can be ritual without root; joined, they are the complete state that earns forgiveness and Paradise. The verse therefore universalises salvation: it never was a birthright, and it remains accessible — the People of the Book of any generation who truly believe and are mindful receive exactly this promise.

**A Verse That Offers Hope After Criticism**

This verse is the Qur'anic method in miniature: critique followed immediately by invitation, as verses 15–19 did when the sūrah's opening gave the Messenger bringing light after describing covenant-breaking. The placement matters: after listing the worst categories and their verdicts, the very next sentence says forgiveness was available all along. The invitation is not limited to the past — People of the Book today who believe and are mindful attain the same Gardens. The next verse will extend the promise to worldly provision: observing the Torah, the Gospel, and the final revelation would have opened provision from above them and from beneath their feet.

**Extra Verified Note on Gardens of Bliss**

*Jannāt al-naʿīm*, Gardens of Bliss, appears several times in the Qur'an as the abode of the righteous — *in Gardens of Bliss* (56:12), *on thrones, facing one another* (37:44). The promise of absolution, *la-kaffarnā ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim*, appears also for the believers generally: *We will remove their evil deeds* (29:7). The past-tense conditional — *had they believed* — shows the door was open and is open still; the offer was not withdrawn with the verdicts.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:66

> And if only they had observed the Torah, the Gospel, and what has been revealed to them from their Lord, they would surely have eaten from above them and from beneath their feet. Among them are a moderate community, yet many of them do nothing but evil

**Observing All Revelation Would Have Brought Provision From Every Direction**

*Wa law annahum aqāmū at-tawrāta wa al-injīla wa mā unzila ilayhim min rabbihim la-akalū min fawqihim wa min taḥti arjulihim* extends the promise to this world. *Law annahum aqāmū*, had they established — *aqāmū* from *q-w-m*, to set upright, establish properly, as in *establish prayer*: follow with its requirements, not merely recite; *at-tawrāta wa al-injīla wa mā unzila ilayhim min rabbihim*, the Torah, the Gospel, and what was revealed to them from their Lord — the Qur'an, which came to all people, addressed to them as much as anyone. *La-akalū min fawqihim wa min taḥti arjulihim*, they would have eaten from above them and from beneath their feet — an idiom for abundance from every direction: rain and sky's blessing from above, crops, water, and earth's produce from below. The same idiom of overwhelming provision appears in *if they had followed the right way, We would have given them abundant water* (72:16) and *had the people of the towns believed and been mindful, We would have opened blessings from heaven and earth* (7:96). The promise covers both worlds: Gardens of Bliss in the Hereafter (verse 65) and full provision in this one.

**Establishing All Three**

The three — Torah, Gospel, and the final revelation — form a chain: the Torah first, the Gospel confirming it, the Qur'an confirming both and guarding over them as *muhaymin* (verse 48). Observing all three means following true monotheism as taught in each, and recognising the final messenger the earlier books describe. To hold two books while rejecting the one confirming them is not observance but selectivity. Had they established all three, they would have recognised the Prophet ﷺ, followed him, and received provision from above and below; their refusal brought the opposite — revelation increasing many in rebellion, and enmity cast among them (verse 64). The verse thus offers the alternative history: abundance instead of enmity.

**Among Them Is a Moderate Community**

*Minhum ummatun muqtaṣidah* — among them is a community that is upright and moderate. *Ummah* here means a group; *muqtaṣidah* from *q-ṣ-d*, the middle, balanced way — those who follow the truth with justice, neither extreme nor negligent. The Qur'an keeps its record fair: it does not paint every individual with the same brush. *Wa kathīrun minhum sāʾa mā yaʿmalūn* — but most of them, evil is what they do — the majority persist in the conduct the previous verses catalogued: racing into sin, forbidden gain, the claim that Allah is tight-fisted, kindling war. The same fairness appears in *they are not all alike; among the People of the Book is an upright community* (3:113), and verses 82–85 will name believers among the Christians whom Allah is pleased with. The upright minority among them are those who truly observed their books and recognised the final revelation.

**A Verse That Links Observance to Provision**

This verse teaches that guidance brings worldly benefit as well as otherworldly reward: divine law includes the rule that justice and obedience open the earth's blessings. The observance commanded is *iqāmah* — establishing, judging by, living by — the same standard the opening verses of this section demanded: judge by what Allah revealed. The ending — a moderate community exists, but most do evil — balances hope with realism: goodness among them is real and should be acknowledged; the general trend, however, was decline. The next verse will turn to the Messenger: convey everything revealed, and Allah will protect you from the people.

**Extra Verified Note on Muqtaṣidah**

*Muqtaṣid*, from *iqtaṣada*, to follow a middle, just course, denotes the balanced person; *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses it as one who holds the middle path. *Eating from above and below* is an established Arabic idiom for comprehensive provision — the sky's yield and the earth's. The verse's structure — a full conditional promise plus an acknowledgment of the upright minority — shows both the extent of what was lost and the fairness in distinguishing those who kept faith with their books.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:67

> O Messenger! Convey everything revealed to you from your Lord. If you do not, then you have not delivered His message. And Allah will protect you from the people. Indeed, Allah does not guide the people who disbelieve

**Convey Everything Revealed**

*Yā ayyuhā ar-rasūlu balligh mā unzila ilayka min rabbik* is one of the weightiest commands in the Qur'an. *Yā ayyuhā ar-rasūl*, O Messenger — the title of office itself; *balligh*, convey — from *b-l-gh*, to deliver a message to its destination, complete and unaltered; *mā unzila ilayka min rabbik*, what has been revealed to you from your Lord — all of it. The command follows the verses about the People of the Book: revelation increases many of them in rebellion, scholars stay silent, fires of war are kindled. Against that whole backdrop comes: convey everything anyway. Nothing may be held back for fear of reaction — not the hard rulings on food and penalties, not the verdicts on those who resent belief.

**If You Do Not, You Have Not Delivered the Message**

*Wa in lam tafʿal fa-mā ballaghta risālatah* — and if you do not, then you have not conveyed His message. The warning is severe: holding back any part is counted as failing the entire mission, because the message is one interconnected whole. This verse was revealed at the Farewell Pilgrimage, on the Day of ʿArafah, concerning the rights and rulings being declared openly — when the Prophet ﷺ feared some might harm him over them. Its position near the end of the mission shows its function: the final, complete delivery of everything, with nothing softened or omitted. The Messenger's duty is to deliver, not to compel acceptance — as *your duty is only to convey clearly* (5:99) states.

**Allah Will Protect You From the People**

*Wa Allāhu yaʿṣimuka mina an-nās* — and Allah will protect you from the people. *Yaʿṣimuka*, He will guard you — from *ʿ-ṣ-m*, to protect, preserve. The promise answered a real fear: a verified report in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (3046, graded *ḥasan*) says the Prophet ﷺ used to be guarded by companions; when this verse came he told them to leave, saying *Allah has promised to protect me*. The protection is both physical — no harm will reach him — and mission-protective: no one will be able to stop the delivery. It parallels the earlier signs in this sūrah: Allah held back the hands about to harm the believers (5:11), and extinguishes every war-fire his enemies kindle (5:64). Protection belongs to Allah, so fear belongs to no one else — the exact lesson of verse 44: *do not fear people; fear Me*.

**Allah Does Not Guide the Disbelieving People**

*Inna Allāha lā yahdī al-qawma al-kāfirīn* — indeed, Allah does not guide the disbelieving people. The seal closes the circuit: convey everything, be protected while doing it, and know that those who still disbelieve after complete delivery have chosen their state. The same seal followed the warning about wrong alliances in verse 51 — *Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people*. The lack of guidance is the consequence of persistent choice, not an arbitrary barrier. The Messenger's grief must therefore not turn into despair: the next verse will tell him *do not grieve over the disbelieving people*.

**A Verse That Commands Complete Delivery Despite Opposition**

This verse defines prophetic duty: complete conveyance, regardless of opposition, under divine protection. It also defines the standard for everyone who transmits the message after him — *convey from me, even a single verse* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3461) — though the Prophet's ﷺ duty was total and guarded. The verse's placement, after the increase in rebellion and before the address *you have nothing to stand on unless you establish the Torah, the Gospel, and what was revealed*, shows the logic: the delivery must be complete so that the people are left without excuse. Guidance is offered fully; those who reject it thereafter disbelieve by choice.

**Extra Verified Note on Balligh**

*Balligh*, convey, from *balāgh*, arrival at the goal, denotes delivery that reaches and is understood. The warning *you have not conveyed the message* shows partial delivery equals non-delivery for a prophet entrusted with a complete guidance. The report of the guards being dismissed after this verse — Tirmidhī 3046 — is the earliest testimony to how literally the promise of protection was received. The Day of ʿArafah occasion, on the Farewell Pilgrimage, is reported in the ḥadīth collections' commentaries on this verse, tying it to the open declaration of the faith's final rulings.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:68

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "O People of the Book! You have nothing to stand on unless you observe the Torah, the Gospel, and what has been revealed to you from your Lord." And your Lord's revelation to you ˹O Prophet˺ will only cause many of them to increase in wickedness and disbelief. So do not grieve over the people who disbelieve

**You Have Nothing to Stand On Unless You Establish All Three**

*Qul yā ahla al-kitābi lastum ʿalā shay'in ḥattā tuqīmū at-tawrāta wa al-injīla wa mā unzila ilaykum min rabbikum* delivers the verdict plainly. *Qul*, say; *lastum ʿalā shay'*, you are not upon anything — an Arabic idiom meaning you have no valid foundation, no standing, no case; *ḥattā tuqīmū*, until you establish — from *q-w-m*, set upright, live by with all requirements, as in establishing prayer; *at-tawrāta wa al-injīla wa mā unzila ilaykum min rabbikum*, the Torah, the Gospel, and what was revealed to you from your Lord — the Qur'an, which was revealed to them as much as to anyone. The verse answers the claims of religious authority: whatever they assert about their standing before Allah, they have nothing to stand on while they follow a fraction of revelation. The condition repeats verse 66's promise — establish all three and provision comes from above and below — now stated as the minimum for any claim to validity.

**The Chain of Books Must Be Followed Whole**

The three are mentioned together because they form one chain of guidance: the Torah first, the Gospel confirming it, the Qur'an confirming both and guarding over them (verse 48). Establishing them means holding their true monotheism and justice, and following the final messenger they describe. Selective religion — holding the old books while rejecting the confirmation and protector sent with them — is not observance at all. The same standard applies symmetrically: no community stands on anything except living by the revelation it has received.

**Revelation Increases Many in Wickedness and Disbelief**

*Wa la-yazīdanna kathīran minhum mā unzila ilayka min rabbika ṭughyānan wa kufran* repeats verse 64's sentence word for word: what has been revealed to you from your Lord will surely increase many of them in rebellion and disbelief. The verbatim repetition drives home that the reaction was not an episode but a settled pattern — every new revelation deepened the divide, because envy and rejection feed on the very light that would have healed them, as *in their hearts is a disease, so Allah increased their disease* (2:10). The repetition also frames the command that follows: knowing this reaction, convey everything anyway (verse 67), and do not despair when the predicted reaction occurs.

**So Do Not Grieve Over the Disbelieving People**

*Fa-lā taʾsa ʿalā al-qawmi al-kāfirīn* — so do not grieve over the disbelieving people. *Lā taʾsa*, do not grieve — from *ʾ-s-y*, sorrow that weighs down. The consolation completes the series in this sūrah: *do not grieve over the rebellious people* (5:26, concerning those who refused the Holy Land) and *do not grieve over the people who race into disbelief* (5:41). The Messenger's duty is delivery; the outcome belongs to Allah. Since complete conveyance has occurred and the reaction is foretold, grief serves no purpose — their state is their own choice. The sequence — convey, expect the increase, refuse grief — is the Prophet's ﷺ standing instruction for dealing with rejection.

**A Verse That Defines Valid Standing**

The verse reduces every claim of religious authority to one test: establishing the whole of revelation.
