For the People of the Book that means Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an together; the phrase *you have nothing to stand on* strips away every other basis — lineage, names, inherited prestige. The next verse will state the universal principle of salvation: whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteousness has no fear and no grief — true standing is by faith and deeds, not labels.

**Extra Verified Note on "Not Upon Anything"**

*Lastum ʿalā shay'* is a strong Arabic idiom: not upon anything = without foundation or valid standing. *Tuqīmū*, establish, from *aqāma*, to set upright, is the same verb used for establishing prayer and for observing the Torah and Gospel in verse 66 — always meaning living by something with its full requirements, not merely possessing it. The closing consolation *do not grieve* is used in the Qur'an for prophets facing rejection, marking grief as natural but futile after complete delivery.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:69

> Indeed, the believers, Jews, Sabians, and Christians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good—there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**Whoever Believes and Does Good Has No Fear and No Grief**

*Inna alladhīna āmanū wa alladhīna hādū wa aṣ-ṣābi'ūna wa an-naṣārā* names four groups: the believers — the Muslims; the Jews — *hādū* from *h-w-d*; the Sabians — *aṣ-ṣābi'ūn*, a scriptural group of uncertain identity discussed below; and the Christians. Then the universal principle: *man āmana bi-Allāhi wa al-yawmi al-ākhiri wa ʿamila ṣāliḥan* — whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteousness. The result: *fa-lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn* — no fear upon them, nor will they grieve. The reward is comprehensive: no fear of the future punishment, no grief over what they left behind. The same grouping and principle appeared in *the believers, Jews, Christians, and Sabians—whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good will have their reward with their Lord, no fear upon them, nor will they grieve* (2:62). The principle is identical in both verses: salvation is by true faith and righteous deeds, not by label.

**What the Principle Requires**

Belief in Allah means belief in His oneness — and, since Allah's oneness cannot be separated from His messengers, belief in all of His messengers, including the final one, just as verse 12's covenant required belief in the messengers. Belief in the Last Day means belief in accountability, the reality verses 36–37 described: the disbelievers will wish to exit the Fire and will never leave it. Righteous deeds include everything the sūrah has commanded — establishing prayer, paying alms, judging justly, keeping contracts. Whoever has all three is the one named here.

**Why This Verse Follows "You Have Nothing to Stand On"**

Verse 68 said the People of the Book have nothing to stand on unless they establish the Torah, the Gospel, and the final revelation. This verse immediately states the positive side of the same coin: among those very groups, whoever truly believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good has no fear and no grief. The two verses together define standing: not lineage, not names, not heritage — but real faith and real deeds. Verse 65 promised the People of the Book that belief and mindfulness would bring absolution and Gardens of Bliss; this verse generalises the promise to any individual among them who meets the condition.

**Early Exegetes on the Condition**

Early commentators debated the scope: does the verse speak of those who lived before the final Prophet ﷺ and died on pure monotheism, or does it include people of every era? The majority read it as referring to those who believed in their prophet faithfully and, when the final messenger came, believed in him as well — because believing in Allah includes believing in the messenger He sent, as the covenant of verse 12 required. The verse therefore is not a licence to reject the final messenger and still claim the reward; it is the standing assurance that whoever meets the full condition — from any of the four groups — is safe. This is the fairness the sūrah maintains throughout: categories are criticised for conduct, never individuals condemned without their deeds.

**Extra Verified Note on the Sabians**

*Al-ṣābi'ūn*, the Sabians, are a group mentioned three times in the Qur'an, always in these universal-principle contexts. Early sources record several identifications — a people who followed a scripture, a group between Judaism and Christianity, or star-worshippers; al-Ṭabarī lists the views and notes the uncertainty. What matters for the verse is the principle, not their identity: the offer is open *whoever* they were. The formula *no fear upon them, nor will they grieve* appears more than ten times in the Qur'an for those with true faith and deeds — the twin negation meaning safety in the Hereafter from punishment and regret.

**No Fear and No Grief as the Complete Reward**

*Lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn* is the Qur'an's shorthand for complete salvation: no fear of what is coming, no grief over what is past. It is promised to those who believe and are mindful (10:62–63) and to those who spend in Allah's way and do not follow their spending with reminders (2:262). Placed here, after the hard verdicts of verses 60–68, it restores the balance: the critique was never a closed door — for individuals who truly believe and do good, the door has always been open. The next verse begins the history: the covenant with the Children of Israel and their treatment of the messengers sent to them.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:70

> Indeed, We took a covenant from the Children of Israel and sent them messengers. Whenever a messenger came to them with anything their souls did not desire, they denied some ˹of the messengers˺ and killed others

**The Covenant and the Messengers**

*Laqad akhadhnā mīthāqa banī Isrā'īla wa arsalnā ilayhim rusulan* recalls the double favour. *Laqad akhadhnā*, We did take; *mīthāq*, a firm covenant — the same word as verse 12's covenant at Mount Sinai, with its duties of worship, alms, and belief in the messengers; *banī Isrā'īl*, the Children of Israel; *wa arsalnā ilayhim rusulan*, and We sent them messengers — plural, one after another. No community received more guidance: a covenant, an unbroken chain of prophets, scriptures renewed century after century. The pairing matters: the covenant was not left as ink — messengers kept coming to renew and enforce it. Guidance was abundant; excuse was impossible, as verse 19 said of the final interval: a messenger came so that no one could claim no warner came.

**Whenever a Messenger Brought What Their Souls Did Not Desire**

*Kullamā jāʾahum rasūlun bimā lā tahwā anfusuhum farīqan kadhdhabū wa farīqan yaqtulūn* records the response. *Kullamā*, whenever — every single time; *jāʾahum rasūlun*, a messenger came to them; *bimā lā tahwā anfusuhum*, with what their souls did not desire — *tahwā* from *h-w-y*, to incline, desire; *farīqan kadhdhabū*, a group they denied — from *k-dh-b*, to cry lies; *wa farīqan yaqtulūn*, and a group they killed. The test of every messenger was the same: did he bring what their souls already wanted? If yes, they accepted; if the message forbade unlawful gain, commanded justice, or exposed their corruption, they rejected it — some by denial, some by murder. The identical pattern is stated for their history: *whenever a messenger came to them with what their souls did not desire, some they called liars and some they killed* (2:87). Acceptance by desire is the exact attitude verse 41 diagnosed in the hypocrites: *if you are given this, take it; if not, beware*.

**A History of Denial and Killing**

The Qur'an records the crime repeatedly: *they killed the prophets without right* (2:61), *they killed the prophets unjustly* (3:21), and *they disbelieved for saying against Mary a tremendous slander, and for saying we killed the Messiah* (4:155–157). Zakariyyā and Yaḥyā were killed according to the reports; an attempt was made on ʿĪsā — *they did not kill him, nor did they crucify him* (4:157) — though they intended it. Two levels of rejection appear in the verse: denial is grave, killing is graver. The lesson is systematic: following desire leads, step by step, to the worst of crimes. A community that filters revelation through appetite ends up silencing — by word or by sword — the very men sent to save it.

**Why This Follows the Universal Principle**

Verse 69 stated the universal path to salvation: belief in Allah, the Last Day, and righteous deeds. Verse 70 opens the historical case study of how that path was obstructed: the Children of Israel received covenant and messengers in abundance, yet the desire-governed response — deny some, kill others — blocked their own path. The failure was never lack of guidance; it was appetite. For the Muslim reader the warning transfers directly: the same test applies whenever revelation commands what the soul dislikes. Following desire over guidance is the root from which both rejection and violence grew. The next verse will describe the inner state behind the crimes: they assumed no consequences, and so became blind and deaf — until mercy gave them another chance, and most relapsed.

**Extra Verified Note on Desire as the Criterion**

*Bimā lā tahwā anfusuhum*, with what their souls did not desire, is the verse's engine: the criterion of acceptance was desire, not truth. The Qur'an elsewhere says the same of their fathers: *when they did not desire guidance they were given hearts that did not understand* (for the general pattern, cf. 7:179). Denial and killing, *kadhdhabū* and *yaqtulūn*, are the far ends of one process that begins with the soul's discomfort at being corrected. The sequence in the sūrah — covenant (12), forgotten portions (13–14), distorted words (13, 41), desire-filtered rulings (41), denial and killing (70) — is one continuous decline, each step feeding the next.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:71

> They assumed there would be no consequences, so they turned a blind eye and a deaf ear. Yet Allah turned to them in forgiveness ˹after their repentance˺, but again many of them became blind and deaf. And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do

**Assuming No Consequences, Then Blind and Deaf**

*Wa ḥasibū allā takūna fitnatun fa-ʿamū wa ṣammū* exposes the mindset behind the crimes. *Ḥasibū*, they assumed — from *ḥ-s-b*, to reckon; *allā takūna fitnah*, that there would be no *fitnah* — here, no punishment or consequence for denying and killing the messengers. Then *fa-ʿamū wa ṣammū*, so they became blind and deaf — *ʿamū* from *ʾ-m-y*, to be blind; *ṣammū* from *ṣ-m-m*, to be deaf. The blindness is moral, not physical: they chose not to see the signs and not to hear the warnings, exactly like *deaf, dumb, blind — so they do not return* (2:18). The assumption came first: whoever believes no consequence will ever arrive has no reason to open eyes or ears. Carelessness about consequences is itself the trial — it locks the senses against correction.

**Then Allah Turned to Them in Forgiveness**

*Thumma tāba Allāhu ʿalayhim* — then Allah turned to them in forgiveness. *Tāba* from *t-w-b*, to return: Allah turned back to them in mercy when they returned to Him. Reports connect this to the great national repentances — after the calf, *then Allah turned to you in forgiveness* (2:54) — and the principle is the same as verse 39: whoever repents after wrongdoing and mends conduct, Allah accepts his repentance. The verse is astonishing in its mercy: even after denial, killing, blindness, and deafness, the door of *tawbah* stood open for the community that walked through it. Mercy is shown to invite repentance, never to excuse persistence.

**Then Many Became Blind and Deaf Again**

*Thumma ʿamiya kathīrun minhum wa ṣammū* — then many of them became blind and deaf again. *Thumma*, then — marking relapse after forgiveness; *kathīrun minhum*, many of them — the majority, not all, since a moderate community persisted (verse 66). Forgiveness does not immunise against relapse: hard hearts (verse 13), forgotten portions (verses 13–14), and desire-governed hearing (verse 41) pull back toward blindness unless the heart is kept soft. The verse traces the full cycle — assume no consequences, go blind and deaf, receive forgiveness upon repentance, relapse — and so teaches that one repentance is not a lifetime insurance; steadfastness and mindfulness must be continual, as verse 7 commanded: *remember My favour upon you and My covenant, and be mindful*.

**Allah Is All-Seeing of What They Do**

*Wa Allāhu Baṣīrun bimā yaʿmalūn* — and Allah is All-Seeing of what they do. *Baṣīr* from *b-ṣ-r*, to see: the seal answers the opening assumption. They assumed no consequence; Allah sees every deed — the denial, the killing, the blindness, the repentance, the relapse. Nothing in the cycle escapes His sight, and the seeing is joined to recompense. The closing name answers the closing sin: the blind assumed invisibility; the All-Seeing guarantees it otherwise.

**A Verse That Traces a Cycle**

The verse compresses centuries into one sentence: presumption → blindness → divine forgiveness → relapse → divine observation. The same cycle appears in the Qur'an's account of other communities, and the final repetition — *many became blind and deaf again* — explains why the earlier verdicts (curse, wrath, hardened hearts) had to be pronounced: repeated relapse after repeated pardon exhausts mercy's ordinary course. The remedy named by the sūrah is continual mindfulness, not episodic remorse. The next verse will show a new arena for the same blindness: theology — those who said Allah is the Messiah.

**Extra Verified Note on Fitnah Here**

*Fitnah* — from *fatana*, to test and purify as gold is tested by fire — carries several meanings in the Qur'an: trial, discord, punishment, enticement. Here it means punishment or consequence: *they assumed there would be no punishment*. *Lisān al-ʿArab* confirms the usage. The assumption *allā takūna fitnah* is the exact opposite of *iḥsān* — living as though one sees Allah and knows He sees. Between assumption of impunity and certainty of being seen lies the entire difference between the blind and the mindful. The following verses carry the blindness into doctrine: the claims that Allah is the Messiah (72) or the third of three (73).
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:72

> Those who say, "Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary," have certainly fallen into disbelief. The Messiah ˹himself˺ said, "O Children of Israel! Worship Allah—my Lord and your Lord." Whoever associates others with Allah ˹in worship˺ will surely be forbidden Paradise by Allah. Their home will be the Fire. And the wrongdoers will have no helpers

**The Claim That Allah Is the Messiah Is Disbelief**

*Laqad kafara alladhīna qālū inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥu ibnu Maryam* restates the verdict first given in verse 17. *Laqad kafara*, they have certainly disbelieved — the doubled emphasis shows the gravity; *inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥ*, Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary. The claim collapses the Creator into a creature: it identifies the Lord of the worlds with a man who was born, ate, and slept. Verse 17 already refuted it by contingency — if Allah willed, He could destroy the Messiah, his mother, and everyone on earth. Here the refutation comes from the Messiah's own words.

**The Messiah's Own Testimony: Worship Allah, My Lord and Your Lord**

*Qāla al-Masīḥu ibnu Maryama yā banī Isrā'īla uʿbudū Allāha rabbī wa rabbakum* — the Messiah, son of Mary, said: O Children of Israel, worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord. The Qur'an quotes the man deified to destroy the deification — the argumentative masterstroke, since Christians accept the Messiah's authority. *Rabbī wa rabbakum*, my Lord and your Lord: he has a Lord, they have a Lord, and the two are one — so he is a worshipper alongside them, not the object of worship. This is his message everywhere in the Qur'an: *indeed, Allah is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him; this is a straight path* (3:51, 19:36), and it is the message of every messenger: *We sent no messenger before you without revealing to him: there is no god but Me, so worship Me* (21:25). The Gospel's own record agrees: Jesus teaches *Hear, O Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is one* (Mark 12:29). Whoever says Allah *is* the Messiah says the opposite of what the Messiah said.

**Whoever Associates — Paradise Forbidden, the Fire the Abode**

*Innahu man yushrik bi-Allāhi fa-qad ḥarrama Allāhu ʿalayhi al-jannata wa maʾwāhu an-nār* — whoever associates others with Allah, Allah has forbidden him Paradise, and his refuge is the Fire. *Yushrik* from *sh-r-k*, to share: directing worship to another alongside Allah — whether saying Allah is the Messiah, or worshipping anything else. *Ḥarrama*, He has made forbidden — the entry barred permanently for one who dies in that state, as *Allah does not forgive that partners be associated with Him, but forgives less than that for whom He wills* (4:48) establishes: repentance before death erases it; death in it does not. *Maʾwāhu an-nār*, his abode is the Fire — *maʾwā* from *ʾ-w-y*, a place of return, a home, not a stopover. *Wa mā liẓ-ẓālimīna min anṣār* — and the wrongdoers have no helpers — *ẓālimīn* because shirk is the greatest *ẓulm*, as *association is a tremendous wrong* (31:13) says; *anṣār*, helpers: on that Day no patron, no intercessor, no ally can aid them.

**A Verse That Refutes Deification With Its Own Authority**

The method is as instructive as the verdict: the opponent's accepted authority — the Messiah himself — is quoted against the claim. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3445) carries the same warning from the Prophet ﷺ: *do not exaggerate about me as the Christians exaggerated about the son of Mary, for I am only a servant, so say the servant of Allah and His messenger*. Exaggerated veneration is the well-worn road to deification; the verse blocks it at the source. The repetition from verse 17 marks the claim as settled doctrine in the Qur'an, not a passing remark.

**Extra Verified Note on Shirk and Its Consequence**

*Shirk* is the one sin Allah names as unforgivable when unrepented at death (4:48); every lesser sin is subject to His will. The sentence here — Paradise forbidden, Fire the abode, no helpers — is the legal and theological core of that doctrine. The phrase *the wrongdoers have no helpers* recurs for those who wrong themselves by association (2:270, 3:192). The next verse extends the verdict to the Trinitarian formula: Allah as third of three.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:73

> Those who say, "Allah is the third of three," have certainly fallen into disbelief. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except One God. If they do not desist from what they say, a painful punishment will afflict those who disbelieve among them

**"Allah Is the Third of Three" Is Disbelief**

*Laqad kafara alladhīna qālū inna Allāha thālithu thalāthah* states the verdict for the Trinitarian claim. *Thālithu thalāthah*, third of three — the doctrine that God is one "in three," with Allah counted as one member of a triad. The Qur'an judges the statement as *kufr* for the same reason as the previous verse: it destroys *tawḥīd*, the absolute oneness that is the first truth of every revelation. If divinity is shared, it is not absolute; if Allah is one of three, He is not the One God. The formula "third of three" captures precisely what is wrong: it does not merely add to Allah — it *positions* Him within a plurality.

**There Is No God Except the One God**

*Wa mā min ilāhin illā ilāhun wāḥid* — there is no god except One God. The correction is a single sentence, the whole of *tawḥīd* compressed: no deity exists, none is worshipped rightfully, none creates or provides — except the One. Compare *your God is one God; there is no god but He, the Most Merciful* (2:163) and *say: He is Allah, the One* (112:1). The oneness is not merely arithmetic: it belongs to essence, attributes, lordship, and the right to worship. Against that, any triad — however mystically described — is false by definition. The Qur'an's refutation is characteristically simple: count as they will, the total of gods is One.

**If They Do Not Desist, a Painful Punishment**

*Wa in lam yantahū ʿammā yaqūlūna la-yamassanna alladhīna kafarū minhum ʿadhābun alīm* — if they do not desist from what they say, a painful punishment will surely touch those of them who disbelieve. *Yantahū* from *n-h-y*, to desist, stop; *la-yamassanna*, will surely touch — the doubled emphatic; *ʿadhābun alīm*, painful punishment. Two features deserve attention. First, the door is left open: *if they desist* — the claim can be retracted; this is an invitation framed as a threat. Second, the punishment is specified for *those who disbelieve among them* — those who, after the warning is clear, persist. The condition prepares the very next verse: *will they not repent to Allah and seek His forgiveness? And Allah is Forgiving, Merciful* (5:74).

**A Verse That Refutes the Trinity**

This verse, with verses 72 and 17, forms the Qur'an's complete answer to the deification of the Messiah: saying Allah *is* the Messiah (72), saying Allah is *third of three* (73), and the contingency argument of verse 17. The doctrine of the Trinity developed after Jesus, through councils — Jesus himself, by the Qur'an's testimony and the Gospel's Shemaʿ, called to the worship of Allah alone, *my Lord and your Lord* (5:72). The verse returns to the first principle: *no god except One God* — the foundation of the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an alike, as verses 44 and 46 established that each contains guidance and light to the same Lord. One God is not a Muslim peculiarity; it is the original teaching they all claim to uphold.

**Extra Verified Note on "Third of Three"**

*Thālithu thalāthah* occurs only here in the Qur'an, matching the earlier unique phrase *the Messiah, son of Mary, is only a messenger* (5:75) that follows. The pairing of verdict and correction — *laqad kafara* then *mā min ilāhin illā ilāhun wāḥid* — is the sūrah's pattern: every severe judgment is followed by the pure statement of truth it protects. The warning's phrasing, *a painful punishment will touch those who disbelieve among them*, leaves room precisely for those who never understood, were never taught, or turn back before death — the mercy embedded inside the severity. The next verse is the door: repentance and forgiveness.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:74

> Will they not turn to Allah in repentance and seek His forgiveness? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Will They Not Repent and Seek Forgiveness?**

*A-fa-lā yatūbūna ilā Allāhi wa yastaghfirūnah* — will they not then turn to Allah in repentance and seek His forgiveness? *Yatūbūna*, turn — *tāba* from *t-w-b*, to return; *yastaghfirūnah*, seek His forgiveness — *istighfār* from *gh-f-r*, to cover. After two verses of the heaviest verdicts — Paradise forbidden, the Fire the abode, painful punishment promised — the Qur'an turns with a question that is pure invitation: will they not turn? The question expects and urges the answer yes. Even the gravest claims — Allah is the Messiah, Allah is third of three — are survivable, if the claimant returns before death. No sin outbids repentance.

**Tawbah and Istighfār Together**

The verse pairs the two movements of repentance. *Tawbah* is the turn: abandoning the sin, resolving against it, returning to obedience — a change of direction. *Istighfār* is the plea: asking Allah to cover the sin so it never surfaces in judgment. Both are needed: turning without seeking covering may leave the record exposed; seeking covering without turning is insincerity. The same pairing appears in *seek forgiveness of your Lord and turn to Him* (11:3). Here the order runs turn, then seek — direction first, then covering.

**The Seal: Forgiving, Merciful**

*Wa Allāhu Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — and Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. *Ghafūr*, intensely covering; *Raḥīm*, continuously merciful — the pair that seals most repentance invitations in the Qur'an, including in this very sūrah: after the warning about war-mongers, *unless they repent before you overpower them — know that Allah is Forgiving, Merciful* (5:34); after the theft penalty, *whoever repents after his wrongdoing and mends, Allah accepts his repentance; Allah is Forgiving, Merciful* (5:39). The pattern is deliberate: warning and door always travel together, so that the severity of the verdict never produces despair. The doctrine of 4:48 — Allah forgives everything except unrepented association — stands behind the verse: repentance from *shirk* itself is not only possible but accepted.

**Hope Placed Immediately After Condemnation**

The placement is the Qur'anic rhythm this sūrah has repeated: critique, then invitation — the Messenger bringing light after the covenant-breakers (15–19); absolution offered after the curse-list (65); universal salvation after "no standing" (68–69). Here the rhythm reaches its most delicate: after two verses declaring the Trinity-claim disbelief, the very next sentence asks, gently, *will they not turn?* The purpose of condemnation is never despair; it is correction. A person cannot repent from a sin he does not know is a sin — the warning exists so that the turning can happen.

**Extra Verified Note on the Invitation's Scope**

The question *a-fa-lā yatūbūna*, will they not turn, is addressed to the claimants themselves — meaning the claim is retractable within a lifetime. Reports of the Prophet's ﷺ dealings confirm the practice: invitation first, always. *Lisān al-ʿArab* defines *tawbah* as the servant's return from sin and *istighfār* as the request for covering; the two are named together because a return without a plea and a plea without a return are each half-healed. The next verse will strengthen the invitation with the plainest facts about the Messiah — that he was only a messenger, that his mother was a truthful woman, and that they both ate food.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:75

> The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger; ˹many˺ messengers had ˹come and˺ gone before him. His mother was a woman of truth. They both ate food. See how We make the signs clear to them, yet see how they are deluded ˹away from the truth˺

**No More Than a Messenger**

*Mā al-Masīḥu ibnu Maryama illā rasūlun qad khalat min qablihi ar-rusul* — the Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger; messengers had passed before him. *Mā...illā*, no more than — the restrictive double negative; *qad khalat min qablihi ar-rusul*, messengers had gone before him — *khalat* from *kh-l-w*, to pass away. A chain of prophets — Ibrāhīm, Mūsā, Dāwūd, Zakariyyā, Yaḥyā — came before him with the same message and the same human nature. He is a link in that chain, its crown in some respects, but a link: one messenger among the sent. To make the final link divine while the rest remain human has no basis in the structure of prophethood itself.

**His Mother Was a Woman of Truth**

*Wa ummuhu ṣiddīqah* — and his mother was a woman of truth. *Ṣiddīqah*, intensely truthful — the feminine of *ṣiddīq*, the title of the highest truthfulness, borne elsewhere by Abū Bakr aṣ-Ṣiddīq. Maryam is honoured with one of the Qur'an's highest titles — she was *chosen above the women of the worlds* (3:42), the *woman of truth who lied against neither her Lord nor her son's mission*. But the honour is prophetic and human: a truthful woman, devout, served by miracles — not a goddess, not a member of a godhead. The verse guards her honour precisely by guarding her humanity: veneration that deifies is not honour but distortion.

**They Both Ate Food**

*Kānā yaʾkulāni aṭ-ṭaʿām* — they both used to eat food. Here is the decisive, undeniable, everyday proof. Eating means hunger, dependence, digestion: the need to be sustained. God needs nothing — *He feeds and is not fed* (6:14), *Allah, the Eternal Refuge; He neither begets nor is born* (112:2–3). Mother and son both stood in need of bread; the Lord of the worlds does not. The argument's power is its accessibility: no council, no philosophical training required — anyone who has seen a hungry child can verify it. The Qur'an habitually refutes elaborate falsehood with the plainly observable, as it does with the idols that *cannot create a fly even if they gathered for it* (22:73).

**See How We Make Clear, Then See How They Delude**

*Unẓur kayfa nubayyinu lahumu al-āyāti thumma unẓur annā yuʾfakūn* — see how We make the signs clear to them, then see how they are deluded. *Unẓur*, see — twice, a double summons to observe; *nubayyinu* from *b-y-n*, to make plain; *yuʾfakūn* from *ʾ-f-k*, to veer into delusion. The first *see* surveys the evidence: the Messiah's own testimony (verse 72), the One God (verse 73), the open invitation (verse 74), the plain humanity of mother and son (this verse). The second *see* surveys the response: despite total clarity, they turn away. The delusion is therefore not an information problem — it is a will problem, as the earlier verses traced: desire-filtered hearing (41), blindness upon assuming impunity (71). Clarity has been given to the utmost; what remains is choice.

**A Verse That Refutes Doctrine With Facts**

The sequence of facts is a complete dismantling: *only a messenger* — so not divine in office; *messengers before him* — so not unique in kind; *mother a woman of truth* — so she too is human; *they both ate food* — so both are dependent. Complex theology is answered with biography. The method teaches every reader: test grand claims against plain facts. The next verse draws the practical conclusion: why worship, besides Allah, what possesses neither harm nor benefit for you?

**Extra Verified Note on Ṣiddīqah**

*Ṣiddīqah*, from *ṣadaqa*, to be truthful, denotes one saturated with truthfulness; the Qur'an uses it for Maryam here and for Ibrāhīm as *ḥanīfan musliman* (say, *a truthful one*, 19:41). The title dignifies without deifying — the exact balance the verse enforces. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṭaʿām*, food, as what is eaten; *kānā yaʾkulāni*, they both used to eat, states habitual practice — not one meal but a lifelong dependence. The double *unẓur*, see, mirrors the Qur'an's repeated *do you not see?* — the invitation to reason that runs from the first page to the last.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:76

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "How can you worship, besides Allah, what holds no power to harm or benefit you? And Allah ˹alone˺ is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing"

**Why Worship What Cannot Harm or Benefit?**

*Qul a-taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi mā lā yamliku lakum ḍarran wa lā nafʿā* reduces false worship to absurdity with one question. *Qul*, say; *a-taʿbudūna*, do you worship — from *ʿ-b-d*, to serve, worship; *min dūni Allāh*, besides Allah, instead of Allah; *mā lā yamliku lakum*, what possesses for you — *yamliku* from *m-l-k*, to own, control; *ḍarran wa lā nafʿā*, neither harm nor benefit. The objects — the deified Messiah, the exalted mother, and every idol — control nothing for their worshippers: they cannot shield from harm or confer benefit, because beings who need food (verse 75) lack the wealth, power, and knowledge to govern anything, let alone the universe. The identical question was posed to idolaters: *do you worship besides Allah what possesses no harm or benefit for you, while Allah is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing?* (21:66). Worship — the highest act of the heart — is rationally owed only to the One who governs what worship seeks.

**The Criterion of Harm and Benefit**

*Ḍarr* and *nafʿ*, harm and benefit, together cover the whole of power: the ability to hurt one's enemy and help one's friend — the two things worship asks of its object. The Qur'an applies the test everywhere: idols *possess no power over harm or benefit* (10:18), and even *for themselves* they *possess neither harm nor benefit, nor death, nor life, nor resurrection* (25:3). Now the same test is turned on the deification of humans: what eats cannot own either. The criterion is deliberately public — test your object of worship; can it help you or harm you? If not, the worship is misdirected, however elevated the object's status.

**Allah, the All-Hearing, All-Knowing**

*Wa Allāhu huwa as-Samīʿu al-ʿAlīm* — and Allah, He alone is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing. *Samīʿ* from *s-m-ʿ*, to hear; *ʿAlīm* from *ʿ-l-m*, to know. The names are chosen for the argument: worship is prayer, and prayer needs hearing; worship is supplication, and supplication needs knowledge of the supplicant's state. The One who hears every cry and knows every need *can* harm and *can* benefit, by wisdom. The pair *as-Samīʿ al-ʿAlīm* follows refutations of false worship repeatedly in the Qur'an — the false object cannot hear; the true One hears all. The contrast completes the syllogism: humans who eat cannot hear all nor know all; Allah hears and knows all; therefore worship belongs to Allah alone.

**A Verse That Continues the Refutation**

The verse follows the proof of humanity (75) with the proof of irrationality (76): if they both ate food, they cannot govern harm and benefit; if they cannot govern harm and benefit, worshipping them is incoherent. The word *mā*, "what," is also deliberate — it strips the objects to things rather than persons: worship besides Allah is, functionally, the worship of *what* — things without power. The next verse will name the root of the distortion: exceeding limits in religion and following the desires of those who strayed before.

**Extra Verified Note on the Names**

*As-Samīʿ al-ʿAlīm* appears together more than thirty times, typically after arguments about power and worship — hearing and knowledge being the attributes worship presupposes. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *mā la yamliku ḍarran wa lā nafʿā* as owning nothing of harm or benefit — the Quranic idiom of impotence. The verse's question *a-taʿbudūna*, do you worship, expects a negative answer: the practice, once examined, cannot survive its own reasoning. The next verse locates the origin of such worship: *ghuluw*, excess, and inherited desire.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:77

> Say, "O People of the Book! Do not go to extremes in your religion beyond the truth, and do not follow the desires of a people who went astray before ˹you˺, misled many, and strayed from the Right Way"

**Do Not Exceed Limits in Your Religion Beyond the Truth**

*Qul yā ahla al-kitābi lā taghlū fī dīnikum ghayra al-ḥaqq* warns against the disease diagnosed across the last verses. *Lā taghlū*, do not exceed — *taghlū* from *gh-l-w*, to overstep every bound; *fī dīnikum*, in your religion; *ghayra al-ḥaqq*, beyond the truth. The truth was just stated: the Messiah is only a messenger, his mother a truthful woman, both ate food, worship belongs to Allah alone (72–76). Exceeding it means exalting the messenger into a god — the exact *ghuluw* of verses 17, 72, and 73. The parallel warning to the People of the Book appeared earlier: *do not exceed limits in your religion, and do not say about Allah except the truth; the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, is only a messenger of Allah* (4:171). The counsel cuts both ways: exceeding beyond truth includes exaggeration — and by implication, equally untruthful is reduction that denies the messenger what Allah gave him. The safe road is the truth itself, exactly as much as revelation states.

**Do Not Follow the Desires of a People Who Strayed Before**

*Wa lā tattabiʿū ahwāʾa qawmin qad ḍallū min qablu* identifies the source of the excess: do not follow the desires — *ahwāʾ*, plural of *hawā*, whim — of a people who strayed before. *Ḍallū* from *ḍ-l-l*, to wander from the way. The excess did not originate with each new generation; it was inherited — desires of earlier generations, encoded into doctrine and passed on as if they were revelation. The verse unmasks the lineage of the error: what is followed is not truth but the *ahwāʾ* of those who went before — the same desire-following this sūrah has condemned in verse 41, 49, and 77.

**They Misled Many and Strayed From the Even Way**

*Wa aḍallū kathīran wa ḍallū ʿan sawāʾi as-sabīl* — they misled many, and they strayed from the even way. *Aḍallū kathīran*, they caused many to stray; *ḍallū ʿan sawāʾi as-sabīl*, they themselves strayed from the level path — *sawāʾ as-sabīl*, the even way, as in verses 12 and 60. A double loss is recorded: the innovators lost their own way, and dragged multitudes with them. Error in religion is rarely private; doctrine outlives its authors. That is precisely why the warning is issued *before* the following occurs: each generation stands at the fork — revive the truth, or inherit and transmit the straying.

**A Verse That Warns Against Extremism in Religion**

*Ghuluw*, exceeding limits in religion, is among the most warned-against diseases. A ḥadīth in Sunan an-Nasāʾī (3057, graded *ṣaḥīḥ*) says: *beware of excess in religion, for nothing destroyed those before you except excess in religion*. The deification of prophets is its classic case — *the Jews said ʿUzayr is the son of Allah, and the Christians said the Messiah is the son of Allah* (9:30) — and its prevention is stated here: stay within *al-ḥaqq*, the truth. Honour the Messiah as messenger, honour the mother as truthful, worship the One Lord — the middle that neither inflates nor deflates.

**Extra Verified Note on Ghuluw**

*Ghuluw*, from *ghalā*, to boil over, exceed — *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses as passing beyond every limit. The phrase *ahwāʾa qawmin qad ḍallū min qabl*, desires of a people who strayed before, traces doctrinal error to *hawā* rather than revelation — the Qur'an's consistent diagnosis: *who is further astray than one who follows his desire without guidance from Allah?* (28:50). The double outcome — *they misled many and strayed* — shows error's multiplication: the strayer becomes a guide, the misled a multitude. The next verse records the verdict such conduct earned: the condemnation pronounced by Dāwūd and ʿĪsā upon the disbelievers among the Children of Israel.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:78

> The disbelievers among the Children of Israel were condemned on the tongues of David and Jesus, son of Mary. That was because they disobeyed and transgressed

**Condemned on the Tongues of David and Jesus**

*Luʿina alladhīna kafarū min banī Isrā'īla ʿalā lisāni Dāwūda wa ʿĪsā ibni Maryam* records a condemnation from within their own history. *Luʿina*, they were cursed — *laʿana* from *l-ʿ-n*, expelled from mercy; *alladhīna kafarū min banī Isrā'īl*, those who disbelieved among the Children of Israel — the qualifier *among* matters: not the whole nation, the disbelievers among them; *ʿalā lisāni Dāwūda wa ʿĪsā ibni Maryam*, on the tongue of Dāwūd and ʿĪsā son of Mary — *lisān*, tongue: the curse was pronounced by these two prophets themselves, in their revelations and supplications — in the Zabūr and the Gospel. The point is devastating for any argument from heritage: the condemnation did not come from an outsider — it came from Dāwūd, their own king and prophet, and from ʿĪsā, the very Messiah they exalted. Their own revered authorities condemned the disbelievers among them.

**Because They Disobeyed and Transgressed**

*Dhālika bimā ʿaṣaw wa kānū yaʿtadūn* — that was because they disobeyed and were transgressing. *Bimā ʿaṣaw*, for their disobeying — *ʿaṣaw* from *ʿ-ṣ-y*; *wa kānū yaʿtadūn*, and they used to transgress — *yaʿtadūn* from *ʿ-d-w*, to overstep limits, the habitual imperfect showing persistence. The curse is for conduct, not lineage — the Qur'anic rule throughout: *that was for their covenant-breaking* (5:13), *for their disbelief and uttering against Mary a tremendous slander* (4:156). The record this sūrah has built is the content of that disobedience: refusing the Holy Land (21–26), distorting words (13, 41), consuming forbidden gain (42, 62), not forbidding evil (63), the claim that Allah is tight-fisted (64), the deification of the Messiah (72–73). No single act triggered the curse; a persistent pattern did.

**Why David and Jesus Specifically**

The two prophets bracket their history. Dāwūd is linked to the Zabūr, which contains the condemnations of the disobedient among Israel; ʿĪsā belongs to the Gospel era and its warnings. Reports connect Dāwūd's curse with the Sabbath violators — the same whom the Qur'an records as *be apes, despised* (2:65, 7:166) — and ʿĪsā's with those who disbelieved after the Table Spread, the *mā'idah* that gives this sūrah its name, upon whom came a punishment unlike any other (5:115). Together they show the condemnation spanned the centuries: no era of disobedience lacked its warning voice from within.

**A Verse That Sets Up the Specific Charge**

This verse states the general ground — disobedience and transgression — and the next verse names the emblematic instance: *they did not forbid one another from the wrong they did* (5:79). The sequence mirrors verses 62–63: the people raced into sin and their scholars did not forbid it; both were declared evil. Here both are gathered into the verdict of curse — sinners cursed for sinning, and a community cursed for abandoning the duty of correction. The chain from verse 70 to 79 is unbroken: covenant and messengers (70), blindness and relapse (71), doctrinal excess (72–77), and now the sentence pronounced by their own prophets.

**Extra Verified Note on the Tongues of the Prophets**

*ʿAlā lisāni Dāwūda wa ʿĪsā* — on the tongue of Dāwūd and ʿĪsā — is unique in the Qur'an: no other curse is attributed to two prophets by name. The attribution makes the condemnation *scriptural* — belonging to the Zabūr and Gospel traditions the Children of Israel and Christians themselves honour — even where the present texts no longer carry the same wording, as verse 15 indicated the Messenger reveals much of what was concealed of the Book. The reason clause *bimā ʿaṣaw wa kānū yaʿtadūn* also appears, nearly word for word, for earlier verdicts (2:61), underlining that the cause is always the conduct. The next verse delivers the specific example of the transgression: they did not forbid one another from the wrong they did — and *evil indeed was what they used to do*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:79

> They did not forbid one another from the wrong they committed. Evil indeed was what they did

**They Did Not Forbid One Another**

*Kānū lā yatanāhawna ʿan munkarin faʿalūh* names the emblematic transgression. *Kānū lā yatanāhawna*, they used not to restrain one another — *yatanāhawna*, the reciprocal form of *n-h-y*, to forbid: each restraining the other, a community checking itself; *ʿan munkarin faʿalūh*, from the wrong they used to commit — *munkar* from *n-k-r*, the rejected: what sound nature and revelation both recoil from. Sin was being committed openly, and no one said stop. This is the specific charge behind verse 78's general verdict of curse, and it echoes verse 63's rebuke: *why do their rabbis and scholars not forbid them?* — there the scholars' silence, here the whole community's. The evil was double: the *munkar* itself, and the silence around it.

**Evil Indeed Was What They Did**

*La-biʾsa mā kānū yafʿalūn* — evil indeed was what they used to do. The verse completes the deliberate trilogy of this section: *evil are their deeds* for those racing into sin (62), *evil is what they used to do* for the scholars who did not forbid (63), *evil was what they did* for the community that did not restrain one another (79). Doing evil is evil; permitting evil is evil; and in both sentences the *not forbidding* is condemned as an action — *mā kānū yafʿalūn*, what they *used to do* — as if their doing was precisely their not doing. Silence in the face of wrong, when one has the power to speak, is itself recorded as conduct.

**The Duty Being Enforced**

The verse enforces one of the community's central duties. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (49) says: whoever among you sees a wrong, let him change it with his hand; if unable, with his tongue; if unable, with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith. The same duty commands Muslims: *let there arise out of you a nation inviting to good, enjoining right and forbidding wrong* (3:104), *you are the best nation brought forth for mankind, enjoining right and forbidding wrong* (3:110), *cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression* (5:2). The Children of Israel failed this duty, and the failure earned curse; the warning transfers to every community: the identity of "best nation" is conditional on doing what they failed to do.

**Why Silence Is Condemned**

Silence in the face of wrong is condemned because wrong is never static — it spreads. Verse 62 showed the result when nothing restrains: *you see many of them racing into sin, hostility, and forbidden gain*. Not forbidding is a form of the cooperation verse 2 prohibits: cooperation by permission, by silence, by folding the hands. It also signals the death of *taqwā* in a community: people who love Allah's bounds restrain each other; people who have stopped caring watch each other fall. The chain of this section — race into sin (62), scholars silent (63), curse pronounced by their own prophets (78), no one restraining one another (79) — shows the progression: when correction dies, everything downstream of correction dies with it.

**A Verse That Prepares the Next Charge**

The next verse names another wrong that no one forbade: *you see many of them taking the disbelievers as allies* (5:80) — an observable *munkar* that flourished precisely because no one restrained anyone. The sequence teaches where corruption ends: with uncensured alliances, earned wrath, and lasting punishment. It also teaches where renewal begins: with the hand, the tongue, and at minimum the heart.

**Extra Verified Note on Munkar**

*Munkar*, from *ankara*, to reject as unknown or alien, denotes what is disapproved by both revelation and sound nature — the counterpart of *maʿrūf*, the known good. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses it as what is not recognised as good. *Lā yatanāhawna* is the Qur'an's only use of this reciprocal verb form for forbidding: mutuality is the point — not a police force, but neighbours restraining neighbours. The condemnation *la-biʾsa mā kānū yafʿalūn* closes the unit on the Children of Israel's failures with the same formula that opened their charges, ring-composing the account of a community cursed for sins no one tried to stop.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:80

> You see many of them allying with those who disbelieved. Evil indeed is what their souls have sent ahead for them — Allah's displeasure fell on them, and in the punishment they will remain forever

**You See Many of Them Allying With the Disbelievers**

*Tarā kathīran minhum yatawallawna alladhīna kafarū* is another observable scene. *Tarā*, you see; *kathīran minhum*, many of them; *yatawallawna*, they take as allies and guardians — the *walāyah* root that verses 51–57 regulated; *alladhīna kafarū*, those who disbelieved — here including the polytheists who fought the believers. Many among the People of the Book — and the hypocrites among the Muslims — were taking the *kuffār* as protectors, inverting the loyalty order verse 51 established. The *you see* matters: after verse 79 condemned a community that never forbade wrong, here is the wrong itself, visible to everyone — alliances with the enemies of the believers, formed for protection and gain, flourishing because no one restrained anyone. It parallels verse 52's *you see those with sickness in their hearts racing to them* — the same scene from the hypocrites' side.

**Evil Is What Their Souls Have Sent Ahead**

*Biʾsa mā qaddamat lahum anfusuhum* — evil is what their souls have sent ahead for them. *Qaddamat* from *q-d-m*, to advance, send forward: deeds are a forwarding — every life ships its acts ahead to arrive before the sender. The image is the Qur'an's standard accounting: *they will find whatever they did present* (99:6). Here the shipment is the alliances themselves — bargains struck for worldly safety that will arrive as evidence. *Biʾsa*, evil/wretched — the same emphatic that sealed the last four verses of condemnation.

**Allah's Displeasure Fell on Them**

*An sakhiṭa Allāhu ʿalayhim* — that Allah's displeasure has fallen upon them. *Sakhaṭ* from *s-kh-ṭ*, intense anger — stronger than mere displeasure: the anger of one whose command was traded away. The wrath is earned — *fell on them* for the alliances — and it is the same wrath the sūrah has traced through this whole people's history: curse, wrath upon wrath (2:90). The verb's tense — has fallen — makes it already accomplished: the bargain that promised safety delivered wrath.

**And in the Punishment They Will Remain**

*Wa fī al-ʿadhābi hum khālidūn* — and in the punishment they will abide forever. *Khālidūn* from *kh-l-d*, to remain eternally. The permanence belongs to those who die in the state — persistence without repentance fixes the abode, just as Paradise is permanent for those who die in faith. The verse's four clauses trace one arc: the visible alliance (you see), the wretched forwarding (what their souls sent ahead), the fallen wrath (Allah's displeasure), the fixed address (abiding in punishment). The next verse explains the root of it all: belief would have prevented the alliances entirely.

**A Verse That Completes the Loyalty Unit**

Verses 51–81 form the sūrah's great loyalty unit: do not take the People of the Book as guardians (51); the sick race to them (52); oaths do not prove loyalty (53); apostates are replaced by those who love Allah (54); your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and the believers (55); Allah's party prevails (56); do not take mockers as guardians (57–58); and now the verdict on those who did ally: wrath, and permanence in punishment. The unit's architecture is a single choice with two outcomes — Allah's party prevails, or wrath and Fire. The next verse names what would have prevented the wrong choice: belief in Allah, the Messenger, and the revelation.

**Extra Verified Note on the Wrath**

*Sakhaṭ Allāh* — Allah's displeasure — is the Qur'an's term for anger that attaches to specific conduct; here, the alliance with disbelievers against the believers, the act verse 55's true guardianship was defined against. The pairing *biʾsa mā qaddamat... an sakhiṭa Allāhu ʿalayhim* also appears for the calf-worshippers (2:92) — forwarded deeds, fallen wrath — tying this page to the same history. *Khālidūn fī al-ʿadhāb*, abiding in the punishment, marks the Hereafter outcome for the persistent, the counterpart of *khālidīna fīhā*, abiding in the Gardens, that verse 85 will promise the believing Christians — one verb, two eternities, two destinations.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:81

> And had they believed in Allah and the Prophet and what has been revealed to him, they would never have taken them ˹the disbelievers˺ as allies. But most of them are rebellious

**Belief Would Have Prevented the Alliances**

*Wa law kānū yuʾminūna bi-Allāhi wa an-nabiyyi wa mā unzila ilayhi mā ittakhadhūhum awliyāʾ* diagnoses the root. *Law kānū yuʾminūna*, had they believed — the conditional states a fact by its opposite: they did not truly believe; *bi-Allāhi wa an-nabiyyi wa mā unzila ilayhi*, in Allah and the Prophet and what has been revealed to him — the Qur'an; *mā ittakhadhūhum awliyāʾ*, they would never have taken them as allies. The logic is tight: real belief includes loyalty to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers (verse 55); real belief trusts Allah's protection (verses 11, 67) rather than seeking safety from the disbelievers; real belief obeys the prohibition of verses 51 and 57. Whoever allies with the *kuffār* against the believers has thereby shown that at least one of the three pillars is missing. The claim *we believe* (verse 61) is refuted by the alliance itself.

**But Most of Them Are Rebellious**

*Wa lākinna kathīran minhum fāsiqūn* — but most of them are rebellious. *Fāsiqūn* — those who exit obedience — is the running diagnosis of this sūrah: *many people are rebellious* (49), *most of you are rebellious* (59), and now again. The rebellion is the sūrah's whole catalogue: covenant-breaking (12–13), distortion (13, 41), desire-filtered rulings (41), racing into sin (62), forbidden gain (62–63), silent scholars (63), the tight-fisted claim (64), unrestrained evil (79), disloyal alliances (80). Alliance with disbelievers is not an isolated lapse; it is one link in a chain that begins with exiting obedience. *Kathīran minhum*, most of them — not all, the Qur'an's persistent fairness: a moderate community exists (66), and some Christians weep at the truth (83–84).

**A Verse That Links Faith and Loyalty**

The verse welds two things together that modern readers often separate: creed and loyalty. Belief in Allah, the Messenger, and the revelation is not merely internal assent; it has a loyalty structure — who protects you, whom you trust, whom you stand with. The three named objects of belief are exactly the three true guardians of verse 55. To believe in them is to seek refuge with them; to ally with their enemies is to announce that the belief is not there. The next verses will show the other side of the coin — among the People of the Book, those whose knowledge and humility made them nearest to the believers in affection.

**Why the Conditional Is Mercy Too**

*Law kānū yuʾminūna... mā ittakhadhūhum* — had they believed, they would never have — is also an open path: the alliance can be undone by believing. Nothing in the verse says the error is irredeemable for the living; the permanence of punishment (verse 80) belongs to those who persist to the end. For the believer reading this, the verse is protective instruction: fortify belief in Allah, His Messenger, and His revelation, and the fear that drives false alliances — *we fear a turn of fortune* (52) — dissolves. The next verse turns from the bitter to the gentlest in the sūrah: those who found the nearest affection.

**Extra Verified Note on the Conditional**

*Law* with the past tense — *law kānū yuʾminūna* — in Arabic always signals a counterfactual: the condition did not hold. Its placement after verse 80's verdict of wrath explains the wrath without excusing it: the alliances flowed from unbelief, not from misunderstanding. *Fāsiqūn* closes the unit on failure with the word that opened it — the same diagnosis, disobedience to obedience, framing the history as one continuous exit. The sūrah now pivots: after four pages of charges, five verses of hope (82–86).
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:82

> You will surely find the most bitter towards the believers to be the Jews and those who associate others ˹with Allah˺; and you will find the nearest of them in affection to the believers those who say, "We are Christians." That is because among them are priests and monks, and because they are not arrogant

**The Most Bitter in Enmity**

*La-tajidanna ashadda an-nāsi ʿadāwatan lilladhīna āmanū al-yahūda wa alladhīna ashrakū* foretells a pattern the Prophet ﷺ would live. *La-tajidanna* — the doubled emphatic: you will most surely find; *ashadda an-nāsi ʿadāwatan*, the strongest of people in enmity; *lilladhīna āmanū*, to the believers; *al-yahūda wa alladhīna ashrakū*, the Jews and those who associate partners with Allah — the polytheists. The forecast matched the Madinan experience: the hostile Jewish tribes who broke pacts and allied with Quraysh, and the Quraysh polytheists who fought the Muslims at Badr, Uḥud, and the Trench. The verse speaks of general dispositions, not every individual — the sūrah has just said *most of them are rebellious*, not all, and the very next verses will name believers among the Christians. Scripture criticises conduct and trend, never condemns individuals unseen.

**The Nearest in Affection**

*Wa la-tajidanna aqrabahum mawaddatan lilladhīna āmanū alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā* — and you will most surely find the nearest of them in affection to the believers are those who say, "We are Christians." *Aqrabahum mawaddatan*, nearest of them in affection — *mawaddah* from *w-d-d*, love and warmth. The phrase *those who say* is precise: it is about the self-identified Christians' disposition, not a theological endorsement. History verified the verse: the Negus (an-Najāshī), the Christian king of Abyssinia, sheltered the persecuted Muslims when Makkah gave them no refuge; Christian delegations listened to the Qur'an with open hearts (as the next verse records); and the Qur'an keeps its fairness — among the Christians, the nearest; among all groups, whoever believes and does good is saved (69).

**Because Among Them Are Priests and Monks**

*Dhālika bi-anna minhum qiṣṣīsīna wa ruh-bānan* — that is because among them are priests and monks. *Qiṣṣīsīn*, priests — from *q-ṣ-ṣ*, to narrate, relate scripture; *ruhbān*, monks — from *r-h-b*, awe and fear of Allah, those devoted to worship. The reasoning is instructive: affection for the believers grows where knowledge of scripture persists. Priests who spend their lives narrating God's word and monks absorbed in devotion carry the residual knowledge of the Torah and Gospel — knowledge that resonates with the Qur'an when heard, as the next verse will show. Communities that keep learning and devotion alive keep channels open to guidance; hostility grows where scripture is possessed but abandoned.

**And Because They Are Not Arrogant**

*Wa annahum lā yastakbirūn* — and because they are not arrogant. *Lā yastakbirūn* from *k-b-r*, to swell with pride. Arrogance is the Qur'an's named barrier to faith — *those who are arrogant will answer*... the leaders said *we heard this before* and disbelieved; knowledge without humility calcifies. The Christians' lack of arrogance meant recognition could mature into acceptance: they wept at the truth they knew (next verse). Contrast the leaders who *knew* the Prophet ﷺ as they knew their own sons (verse 41's import) yet rejected from arrogance and envy. Knowledge plus humility opens; knowledge plus arrogance closes. This is the verse's deepest instruction: the difference between those nearest and those most bitter was not merely doctrine — it was pride.

**A Verse That Maps Attitudes, With Reasons**

The verse is remarkable for giving its own sociology: enmity and affection are traced to causes — devotion, learning, humility. It is the Qur'anic basis for gradation among non-Muslim communities and for principled engagement: expect bitterness from those steeped in hostility, find openness among the devout and humble. A historical seal: the Prophet ﷺ himself prayed the funeral prayer for the Negus, the Christian king who sheltered the believers (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3877) — the verse's affection embodied in the Prophet's own act. The next verse shows what the humble devotion produced when the Qur'an was recited.

**Extra Verified Note on the Two Findings**

Both clauses open with *la-tajidanna*, you will surely find — a prediction, not merely a description: the Prophet ﷺ and the believers would observe this pattern as their history unfolded. *Mawaddah*, affection, is distinct from *walāyah*, guardianship: the Christians can be nearest in warmth without becoming guardians (55) — kindness and loyalty are different registers. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *qiṣṣīs* as the learned narrator of scripture and *rāhib* as one so absorbed in awe of Allah he takes to worship. The structure — claim (bitterest, nearest), then double evidence (priests and monks, not arrogant) — is the sūrah's style even here: every judgment carries its reason.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:83

> When they listen to what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears because of what they recognize of the truth. They say, "Our Lord! We believe, so write us down among the witnesses"

**Eyes Overflowing With Tears for the Truth They Recognize**

*Wa idhā samiʿū mā unzila ilā ar-rasūli tarā aʿyunahum tafīḍu mina ad-damʿi mimmā ʿarafū mina al-ḥaqq* paints the scene verse 82 predicted. *Idhā samiʿū*, when they listen; *mā unzila ilā ar-rasūl*, what has been sent down to the Messenger — the Qur'an; *tarā*, you see — observable, again; *aʿyunahum tafīḍu mina ad-damʿ*, their eyes overflow with tears — *tafīḍu* from *f-y-ḍ*, to stream, overflow; *mimmā ʿarafū mina al-ḥaqq*, because of what they recognize of the truth. The tears are knowledge-driven: they hear descriptions of ʿĪsā, his mother, the miracles, the One God — truths they carried from their own books — and recognition floods out through their eyes. This is verse 82's mechanism working: priests and monks, not arrogant — knowledge meeting humility, and the meeting is weeping. Compare *those given knowledge before fall on their faces weeping when it is recited, and it increases them in humility* (17:107–109).

**"Our Lord! We Believe, So Write Us Among the Witnesses"**

*Yaqūlūna rabbanā āmannā fa-ktubnā maʿa ash-shāhidīn* — they say: Our Lord, we believe, so write us down among the witnesses. *Rabbanā*, Our Lord — the first word is supplication; *āmannā*, we believe — the declaration; *fa-ktubnā*, so write us — *kataba*, to inscribe, a request for permanent registration; *maʿa ash-shāhidīn*, among the witnesses. The *witnesses* are those who testify to the truth — the believers who witness that the Qur'an is from Allah, and in the Hereafter those who will testify that the Messenger conveyed. The same plea was made by ʿĪsā's true disciples: *our Lord, we have believed in what You revealed and followed the Messenger, so write us among the witnesses* (3:53). The Christians of verse 83 ask for exactly what the disciples asked — to be registered in the community of witnesses to truth, not hidden believers but testifiers.

**Knowledge Plus Humility Equals Tears**

The verse is the sūrah's cleanest case study of its recurring equation. Knowledge they had — *what they recognize of the truth*; humility they had — *they are not arrogant* (82); the product is *tafīḍu mina ad-damʿ*, streaming tears. Elsewhere the same two factors produce falling faces and *increased humility* (17:109), prostration and weeping (19:58), and immediate belief (28:52–53, *those to whom We gave the Book before it — they believe in it, and when it is recited to them they say: we believe in it; it is the truth from our Lord*). Conversely, the sūrah has shown knowledge plus arrogance producing distortion, concealment, mockery. The tears mark the moment humility lets knowledge become faith.

**The Ideal Response to Revelation**

This verse models complete response to revelation: listening (*samiʿū*), recognition (*ʿarafū*), emotion (*tears overflowing*), declaration (*we believe*), and commitment (*write us among the witnesses*). Nothing stays private: recognition becomes weeping, weeping becomes confession, confession becomes a plea for registration among those who will testify. The placement seals verse 82's *nearest in affection*: affection that hears the Qur'an matures into faith — and the next verse completes their plea with its rationale, *why should we not believe in Allah and what has come to us of the truth?*

**Reports About the Verse**

Reports in the tafsīr connect the verse to Christian listeners in Madinah — including delegations from Abyssinia or Najrān — who heard the Qur'an and wept at its truth; Ibn ʿAbbās is cited for such readings. The historical archetype is the Negus himself, who when the Muslims recited the Sūrah Maryam before him, wept and affirmed that what came to ʿĪsā and what came to Muḥammad ﷺ emerged from one niche of light — his belief is affirmed in the Sīrah, and the Prophet ﷺ prayed his funeral prayer in absentia (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3877). Whatever the precise occasion, the verse's description became a pattern every generation of sincere Christian listeners could recognise.

**Extra Verified Note on the Tears**

*Tafīḍu mina ad-damʿ* — the verb means a breaking-out, an overflow that cannot be contained: involuntary, which is precisely its evidentiary value. Tears at recognition are the body's testimony; *write us among the witnesses* is the soul's. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ʿarafa* as to know with acquaintance — not bare information but recognition of something known before. The next verse gives their reasoning: *why should we not believe, and we yearn that our Lord admit us with the righteous*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:84

> And why should we not believe in Allah and what has come to us of the truth? And we yearn that our Lord admit us with the righteous people

**Why Should We Not Believe?**

*Wa mā lanā lā nuʾminu bi-Allāhi wa mā jāʾanā mina al-ḥaqq* continues their speech with a rhetorical question. *Wa mā lanā*, what is with us — why should we not; *lā nuʾminu bi-Allāh*, not believe in Allah; *wa mā jāʾanā mina al-ḥaqq*, and what has come to us of the truth — the Qur'an and the Messenger it affirms. The question argues from the evidence: we recognize this as truth (verse 83's tears proved it); our own books foretold it; refusal would require arrogance we do not have (verse 82). Belief is the only rational conclusion. The question also answers the sūrah's earlier scene in reverse: verse 59 asked, *do you resent us only because we believe?* — those bitter resented belief without cause; these Christians ask, *why should we not believe?* — with every cause. The two questions frame the entire section: belief resented without reason; belief embraced for every reason.

**We Yearn That Our Lord Admit Us With the Righteous**

*Wa naṭmaʿu an yudkhilanā rabbunā maʿa al-qawmi aṣ-ṣāliḥīn* — and we yearn that our Lord admit us with the righteous people. *Naṭmaʿu* from *ṭ-m-ʿ*, to yearn, thirst for; *an yudkhilanā*, that He admit us — *d-kh-l*, to enter; *maʿa al-qawmi aṣ-ṣāliḥīn*, with the righteous people. This is the aspirational half of faith: not merely escaping punishment but longing for the company of the righteous — the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, the good, as *whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger — those will be with the ones Allah has blessed: the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous; and excellent are those as companions* (4:69) describes. The same plea was the prophets': Ibrāhīm — *my Lord, grant me authority and join me with the righteous* (26:83); Yūsuf — *cause me to die a Muslim and join me with the righteous* (12:101). New believers asking what prophets asked: the company of the righteous is the ambition of every true believer.

**Rational Belief, Aspirational Hope**

The verse joins the two engines of conversion. The rational engine: *why should we not believe... truth has come* — recognition compels assent. The aspirational engine: *we yearn... with the righteous* — assent aims at a destination, not merely a verdict. Faith that only avoids punishment is incomplete; faith that yearns for the righteous company is alive. Their sequence is instructive: recognition (83) → rational assent (84) → longing (84) → reward (85). Belief's evidence, argument, and ambition in three verses.

**A Verse That Completes the Christians' Speech**

Verses 83–84 form one continuous plea: tears, *our Lord we believe, write us among the witnesses*, *why should we not believe*, *we yearn for the righteous*. The speech is the Qur'an's portrait of a sincere convert from the People of the Book: every element — recognition, humility, urgency, ambition — present and ordered. The next verse answers the plea directly: *Allah will reward them for what they said with Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding eternally therein; that is the reward of the doers of good* (85). The plea was heard.

**Extra Verified Note on Ṣāliḥīn**

*Aṣ-ṣāliḥīn*, the righteous, from *ṣalaḥa*, to be sound and good — the Qur'an's standing name for the company of Paradise. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṭamaʿa* as to yearn for with strong desire — here made beautiful: desire not for worldly gain but for a seat among the good. The rhetorical *wa mā lanā*, why should we not, is the Arabic idiom of self-evident obligation — the answer "nothing" is built into the question. The next verse rewards the *speech* — *for what they said* — because in their case the words were faith itself made audible.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:85

> So Allah rewarded them for what they said with Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding eternally therein. And that is the reward of the doers of good

**Rewarded for What They Said**

*Fa-athābahum Allāhu bimā qālū* — so Allah rewarded them for what they said. *Athābahum* from *ʾ-th-b*, to recompense; *bimā qālū*, for what they said — the whole speech of verses 83–84: *we believe, write us among the witnesses, why should we not believe, we yearn for the righteous*. The reward is attached to *saying* because their saying was faith made audible — recognition expressed, humility confessed, ambition declared. Words backed by tears and truth are deeds. The Qur'an elsewhere rewards speech joined to sincerity identically: *we hear and we obey — forgiveness and a great reward* for those words (24:51).

**Gardens Beneath Which Rivers Flow, Abiding Eternally**

*Jannātun tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhāru khālidīna fīhā* — Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding eternally therein. The Qur'an's signature portrait of Paradise: *jannāt*, gardens; *tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār*, rivers flowing beneath them — life watered without labour or interruption; *khālidīna fīhā*, abiding eternally therein — the permanence that answers every worldly loss. The description recurs for the believers from the Makkan period onward; placing it here crowns the plea of the believing Christians: they asked to be written among the witnesses and admitted with the righteous — the answer is registration in the eternal Gardens.

**The Reward of the Doers of Good**

*Wa dhālika jazāʾu al-muḥsinīn* — and that is the reward of the *muḥsinīn*. *Jazāʾ*, recompense; *al-muḥsinīn* from *ḥ-s-n*, those who do good with excellence — *iḥsān*, which the Prophet ﷺ defined in the ḥadīth of Jibrīl (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 8): to worship Allah as though you see Him, for though you do not see Him, He sees you. The believing Christians are counted among them: their tears were sincerity, their plea humility, their longing excellence of heart. The closing generalises: this is *the* reward of *the* doers of good — the believer from any people who responds to truth this way receives this.

**A Verse That Closes the Story With a Verdict**

The unit verses 82–86 completes the arc: nearest in affection (82), tears at recognition (83), rational assent and yearning (84), and now Paradise (85). The pattern matches verse 65's promise — *had the People of the Book believed and been mindful, We would have absolved their sins and admitted them to Gardens of Bliss* — here fulfilled in specific people who did exactly that. The sūrah's fairness is complete: bitterest and nearest named (82), the near rewarded (85) — and the next verse states the other outcome for those who disbelieved and denied, closing the binary.

**Extra Verified Note on the Reward Formula**

*Athāba ʿalā/bi-*, to reward for, binds recompense to cause: *bimā qālū*, for what they said — the cause is preserved in the verse. *Khālidīna fīhā*, abiding eternally, is the same verb that sealed the punished in verse 80 — *fī al-ʿadhābi hum khālidūn* — the Qur'an using one word for two abodes: eternity is the measure of what was chosen. Reports connect the verse to the believing Christians of Madinah's era — including, in the Sīrah's wider narrative, the Negus's acceptance — the verse standing as their written verdict: rewarded for what they said, with Gardens beneath which rivers flow.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:86

> But those who disbelieved and denied Our signs — they are the companions of the Blaze

**Those Who Disbelieved and Denied Our Signs**

*Wa alladhīna kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* states the other outcome. *Alladhīna kafarū*, those who disbelieved — *kafarū* from *k-f-r*, to cover: concealing the truth they recognized; *wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā*, and denied Our signs — *kadhdhabū* from *k-dh-b*, to cry lies: the outward rejection that matches the inward cover. The two verbs name the two layers: disbelief in the heart, denial on the tongue. The *signs* — *āyāt* — include the revealed verses, the Prophet's manifest proofs, and the signs within their own scriptures that pointed to him. The same double charge — *disbelieved and denied Our signs* — opened the sūrah's verdict at verses 10–11; here it returns to close the section, the bracket complete.

**They Are the Companions of the Blaze**

*Ulāʾika aṣḥābu al-jaḥīm* — they are the companions of the Blaze. *Aṣḥāb*, companions — from *ṣ-ḥ-b*, to keep company: the abode is not a cell but a society, and its people belong to it as companions belong to each other — the permanence is in the noun itself. *Al-jaḥīm* from *jaḥama*, to blaze fiercely — the intensely burning Fire. The phrase *companions of the Blaze* recurs across the Qur'an for those who deny after clarity; verse 10 paired it with the same charges, and verse 86 completes the pairing with the believing Christians' Gardens.

**The Binary Completed**

Verses 85–86 form one sentence in two parts: rewarded for what they said... those who disbelieved and denied — companions of the Blaze. The same binary opened the sūrah at verses 9–10: *forgiveness and great reward* for believers and doers of good; *companions of the Blaze* for those who disbelieved and denied Our signs. Nothing between the two outcomes — no third category of people who recognized truth and were left neutral. Recognition obliges choice: humility converts recognition into faith (83–85); arrogance converts it into denial (86). The section on the People of the Book (verses 12–86) thus closes where verse 11 began: the same two roads, the same verdicts.

**A Verse That Closes the Unit and Turns the Page**

With this verse the long address to the People of the Book — covenant, curse, distortion, the Rabbi judgment, the Messiah's true rank, thecondemnation by Dāwūd and ʿĪsā, the alliances, the believers among the Christians — reaches its end. What follows changes register: verse 87 turns to *O you who believe* and the lawfulness of good things — the sūrah's next movement from history and theology into legislation. The verse's brevity is deliberate: after the extended mercy of verses 82–85, the other outcome needs only one sentence — mercy is elaborated, punishment summarised, exactly as verse 10 was brief after verse 9's promise.

**Extra Verified Note on Aṣḥāb al-Jaḥīm**

*Aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm* appears repeatedly in the Qur'an — 2:39, 5:10, and here — always as the fixed epithet of those who deny after the signs arrive. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *jaḥama* as to burn with a wide, fierce flame. The companionship metaphor is consistent with the Qur'an's Hereafter vocabulary — companions of the Fire, companions of the Garden (13:35) — every soul dwelling with what it chose. The next verse, *do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you*, begins the legislation that the People of the Book's distortions had made necessary: restoring *ṭayyibāt* to their place.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:87

> O you who believe! Do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love transgressors

**Do Not Forbid the Good Things Allah Made Lawful**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū lā tuḥarrimū ṭayyibāti mā aḥalla Allāhu lakum* — O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you. *Lā tuḥarrimū*, do not prohibit — from *ḥ-r-m*, to make inviolable, forbidden; *ṭayyibāt*, the good and pure things — from *ṭ-y-b*, wholesome, pleasant; *mā aḥalla Allāhu lakum*, what Allah has made lawful *for you* — the *lakum*, for you, stresses that the permission is His gift. The prohibition targets self-invented religion: making *ḥarām* what Allah made *ḥalāl* is legislating without authority, the exact mirror of the People of the Book's crimes — *do not say, falsely with your tongues, this is lawful and this is unlawful, inventing lies about Allah* (16:116). It also pairs with verse 77's warning: the People of the Book erred by excess (*lā taghlū*); the believers are now warned the same disease can grow in their own garden.

**The Occasion: Ascetic Excess Among the Companions**

Reports in the tafsīr — recorded by al-Ṭabarī and the asbāb an-nuzūl literature — connect the verse to a group of companions, including ʿUthmān ibn Maẓʿūn, who resolved to castrate themselves, abandon their wives, wear coarse clothing, and devote themselves entirely to worship; the Prophet ﷺ forbade them, and the verse came down. The verified Sunnah carries the same correction in the famous report of three men who resolved to fast every day, pray all night, and abstain from women; the Prophet ﷺ said: *I fast and break my fast, I pray and I sleep, and I marry women — so whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5063, Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1401). Islam has no monasticism: *then We sent successive messengers... and We did not enjoin monasticism upon them; they invented it* (57:27). Forbidding what Allah allowed is not extra piety — it is transgression.

**And Do Not Transgress**

*Wa lā taʿtadū* — and do not transgress. *Taʿtadū* from *ʿ-d-w*, to overstep the bounds. The verse names excess in both directions as transgression: forbidding the lawful is overstepping by adding; violating the forbidden is overstepping by removing. The seal — *inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-muʿtadīn*, indeed Allah does not love transgressors — the same seal that closed verse 2's rules of fighting: transgression is a single disease wherever it appears, in worship or in war. The middle path is the command: enjoy the *ṭayyibāt*, avoid what He forbade, add nothing, subtract nothing.

**The Sūrah's Lawful-and-Good Theme**

*Ṭayyibāt* is a keyword of this sūrah: *they ask you what is lawful; say: good things are made lawful for you* (5:4), *today the good things are made lawful for you* (5:5), and here — do not forbid them (87), eat them (88). The thread exists because this Book came as *muhaymin*, guardian, over the previous books — restoring what distortion and invented prohibitions had misplaced, including the food-laws of the Israelites *for their wrongdoing* (6:146). The verse protects the restored balance: the community must not rebuild with its own hands the wall the Qur'an just opened.

**Extra Verified Note on the Middle Path**

The Qur'an names this ummah *a middle nation* (2:143) — *wasaṭ*, the balanced center — and this verse is the balance in practice: *neither forbidding the lawful (ascetic excess) nor transgressing into the unlawful (indulgence)*. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṭayyib* as what is good in itself, agreeable to nature. The double prohibition — *lā tuḥarrimū... wa lā taʿtadū* — guards both banks of the river; the next verse shows the water flowing between them: *eat of what Allah has provided you, lawful and good*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:88

> And eat of what Allah has provided for you ˹as˺ lawful and good, and be mindful of Allah, in whom you are believers

**Eat of What Allah Has Provided — Lawful and Good**

*Wa kulū mimmā razaqakumu Allāhu ḥalālan ṭayyiban* — and eat of what Allah has provided you, lawful and good. *Kulū*, eat — the imperative that answers verse 87: after forbidding the forbidding, the command is to eat. *Mimmā razaqakumu Allāhu*, of what Allah has provided you — *razaqakum* from *r-z-q*, to provide: the food is attributed to Allah's provision, so eating it with gratitude is acknowledging the Provider. *Ḥalālan ṭayyiban* — two conditions in two words: *ḥalāl*, lawful by His permission; *ṭayyib*, good, wholesome, pure by its nature.
