Lawfulness without wholesomeness fails; wholesomeness without lawfulness fails. The pairing — *ḥalālan ṭayyiban* — is the Qur'an's standing formula for food: *eat of what is on earth, lawful and good* (2:168), *eat of what Allah has provided you, lawful and good, and be grateful* (16:114).

**The Two Conditions as a Complete Standard**

The verse sets the complete standard of the Islamic table: legality and quality. *Ḥalāl* protects the law — nothing stolen, nothing dead of itself, nothing consecrated to other than Allah; *ṭayyib* protects the body and the taste — nothing rotten, harmful, or repugnant. This sūrah has carried the standard from its opening legislation: *good things made lawful for you* (5:4), including the food of the People of the Book (5:5). Between them, the verses restore the table that invented prohibitions and unregulated appetite had both corrupted — the People of the Book erred by adding prohibitions; the verse 87 companions nearly erred the same way; and the other extreme, eating without regard to lawfulness, is the animal path the verse does not even address directly: the standard *ḥalālan ṭayyiban* quietly excludes it.

**And Be Mindful of Allah, in Whom You Are Believers**

*Wa-ttaqū Allāha alladhī antum bihi muʾminūn* — and be mindful of Allah, in whom you are believers. *Ittaqū* from *w-q-y*, to shield oneself; *alladhī antum bihi muʾminūn*, the one in whom you are believers — the relative clause binds eating to believing: your faith in Allah obligates mindfulness even at the table. The point is precise: eating is never neutral. Lawful eating with gratitude is worship; the same mouth that recites the Qur'an should not consume what betrays it. The seal connects to the sūrah's first address: *O you who believe, fulfil your contracts* (5:1) — the believer's whole day, including meals, is under the contract.

**A Verse That Balances the Two Extremes**

Verses 87–88 together draw the middle path: do not forbid the lawful (87), do eat it (88) — but the eating is bracketed by *lā taʿtadū*, do not transgress, and *ittaqū Allāh*, be mindful. Between asceticism and indulgence runs a single road: enjoy Allah's provision as His gift, within His bounds, with His remembrance. The sūrah's structure here is exact: prohibition of excess (87) → command to enjoy (88) → protection against excess (the mindfulness seal). The same balance appeared in verse 5's permission of the People of the Book's food, and verse 66's promise that observance brings provision *from above and below* — provision is Allah's gift for the obedient, not a temptation for the heedless.

**Extra Verified Note on Rizq**

*Rizq*, provision, from *razaqa*, to bestow sustenance — in the Qur'an Allah is the provider of all, and eating *of what He provided* is an act of recognition. The imperative *kulū*, eat, follows the pattern of *the gardens of Eden... eating abundantly therein with delight* — enjoyment commanded, not merely permitted. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṭāba* as pleasing and *ḥalla* as opening and untying — *ḥalāl* is the loosened, permitted thing. The next verse moves from the table to the tongue: thoughtless and deliberate oaths, and the expiation that untangles them.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:89

> Allah will not hold you accountable for your thoughtless oaths, but He will hold you accountable for the oaths you make with deliberate intent. The expiation ˹for breaking them˺ is to feed ten needy people with the average ˹quality˺ food you feed your own family, or to clothe them, or to free a bondservant. Whoever cannot afford ˹any of them˺ is to fast three days. That is the expiation for breaking your oaths. So guard your oaths. Thus does Allah make clear His verses to you, that you may be grateful

**Thoughtless Oaths Versus Deliberate Oaths**

*Lā yuʾākhidhukumu Allāhu bil-laghwi fī aymānikum wa lākin yuʾākhidhukum bimā ʿaqqadtumu al-aymān* draws the distinction. *Lā yuʾākhidhukum*, He will not take you to account — *a-kh-dh*, to take; *bil-laghwi fī aymānikum*, for the thoughtlessness in your oaths — *laghw* from *l-gh-w*, idle, unconsidered speech: the *wallāhi* that slips out, the oath whose binding force never crossed the mind, even one the speaker believed true but was not. *Wa lākin yuʾākhidhukum bimā ʿaqqadtumu al-aymān*, but He will take you to account for the oaths you knotted with determination — *ʿaqqadtum* from *ʿ-q-d*, to tie a knot: the oath made deliberately, meaning to bind. The same distinction appeared in *Allah will not impose blame upon you for thoughtlessness in your oaths, but He will impose blame upon you for what your hearts have earned* (2:225) — the deliberate oath is the heart's earning. Mercy for the accidental slip; accountability for the intentional bond.

**The Expiation: Feed, Clothe, Free, or Fast**

*Fa-kaffāratuhu iṭʿāmu ʿasharati masākīna min awsati mā tuṭʿimūna ahlīkum aw kiswatuhum aw taḥrīru raqabatin* — its expiation is feeding ten needy people with the average of what you feed your families, or clothing them, or freeing a slave. *Kaffārah* from *k-f-r*, to cover: the penalty covers the broken oath. *Iṭʿāmu ʿasharati masākīna*, feeding ten needy ones — *masākīn*, the destitute; *min awsati mā tuṭʿimūna ahlīkum*, from the middle, *awsaṭ*, of your family's food: not the cheapest leftovers nor lavish luxury — the ordinary meal of one's own household, ten portions to ten people. *Aw kiswatuhum*, or clothing them — a garment sufficient for prayer; *aw taḥrīru raqabatin*, or freeing a neck — emancipating a bondservant. The options are alternatives, any one sufficing. *Fa-man lam yajid fa-ṣiyāmu thalāthati ayyām* — whoever finds not the means fasts three days: the ladder descends from benefit to others to benefit to self, discipline as the last resort. Jurists note the expiation applies to breaking a deliberate oath concerning something lawful — an oath to do what is sinful must simply be broken, with its own separate expiation.

**Guard Your Oaths**

*Dhālika kaffāratu aymānikum idhā ḥalaftum wa-ḥfaẓū aymānakum* — that is the expiation of your oaths when you have sworn; and guard your oaths. *Iḥfaẓū* from *ḥ-f-ẓ*, to preserve, protect: do not swear lightly, do not swear excessively, and having sworn deliberately, either fulfil or expiate — never break casually. The whole apparatus — penalty, alternatives, fasting — exists to make breaking oaths costly enough that speech is weighed before it is knotted. Then the seal: *kadhālika yubayyinu Allāhu lakum āyātihi laʿallakum tashkurūn* — thus Allah makes clear His verses to you, that you may be grateful: the clarity of the law is itself a favour to thank.

**A Verse That Turns Broken Speech Into Public Good**

The wisdom of the expiation is its direction: a broken promise, a private failing, is converted into food for ten needy households, clothing for the poor, or a freed human being. Personal failure feeds public good; even the fasting option disciplines the oath-taker. The verse continues the sūrah's table-legislation (87–88): after commanding the enjoyment of lawful things, it provides the lawful exit from oaths that renounce them — the very situation of verse 87's ascetics, who had sworn off good things. They could break their self-denying oaths, expiate, and eat with gratitude.

**Extra Verified Note on the Amounts**

*Awsat* — the middle, average — established the juristic standard: each of the ten receives about half a *ṣāʿ* of staple food, or the equivalent full meal; clothing means what covers adequately for prayer; the freed *raqabah*, neck, signified emancipation of a bondservant, applied in later ages to freeing people from bondage and debt. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *laghw* as speech of no purpose and *ʿaqd* as binding a knot — the Qur'an's own metaphor for a solemn oath. The connection to the preceding verses is deliberate: the sūrah protects lawful enjoyment from both extremes — human prohibition (87) and oath-made prohibition (89) — before turning to the practices that destroy the mind and brotherhood altogether: khamr and maysir (90).
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:90

> O you who believe! Intoxicants, gambling, ˹sacrificing on˺ stone altars, and divining arrows are but defilement from the work of Satan, so avoid them that you may be successful

**Four Evils Named as Defilement**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū innamā al-khamru wa al-maysiru wa al-anṣābu wa al-azlāmu rijsun min ʿamali ash-shayṭān* names them in one breath. *Al-khamr*, intoxicants — from *kh-m-r*, to cover: whatever covers and smother the intellect; *al-maysir*, gambling — from *yusr*, ease: wealth sought without work; *al-anṣāb*, stone altars — from *n-ṣ-b*, to erect: stones set up for sacrifice to other than Allah, the same word verse 3 used for meat slaughtered on them; *al-azlām*, divining arrows — the featherless arrows the Arabs cast to decide travel, marriage, and disputes, seeking the unseen from other than Allah. *Innamā*, indeed, they are only — the restrictive particle sweeps all four into one classification: *rijsun min ʿamali ash-shayṭān*, defilement from the work of Satan. *Rijs* from *r-j-s*: ritual and moral filth, the word the Qur'an uses for the abomination that must be shunned. Pairing wine and dice with idols and divination is the point: the first two are social vices that seem worldly, the last two religious vices that seem spiritual — all four are Satan's workshop.

**So Avoid Them That You May Be Successful**

*Fa-ijtanibūhu laʿallakum tufliḥūn* — so avoid it, that you may succeed. *Ijtanibū* from *j-n-b*, to keep at a distance — stronger than *do not do*: not mere abstention but avoidance, no handling, no serving, no dalliance; the singular *hu*, it, gathers the four as one defilement. *Laʿallakum tufliḥūn*, that you may attain *falāḥ*, true success — the same *falāḥ* verse 35 summoned to: *seek the means to Him and strive, that you may succeed*. The road to success runs away from these four; they are not forbidden arbitrary but blocked exits.

**The Gradual Prohibition Completed**

The verse is the final step of a three-stage prohibition the sīrah records. First: *they ask you about khamr and maysir; say: in both is great sin and some benefit for people, but their sin is greater than their benefit* (2:219) — measured exposure. Second: *do not approach prayer while intoxicated, until you know what you are saying* (4:43) — the prayer boundary. Third, here: total prohibition as *rijs*. The gradualism was wisdom, not hesitation — habits embedded in Arab society were loosened before they were cut. When this verse came, the response became proverbial: reports say the companions cried *we have abstained, we have abstained* and poured the wine into the streets of Madinah, breaking the vessels at once. The definition remained broad: *every intoxicant is khamr, and every khamr is forbidden* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2003), and ʿUmar declared from the pulpit: *khamr is whatever covers the intellect* (recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, muʿallaq) — extending the category to every intoxicating substance, solid or liquid.

**A Verse Central to Islamic Law**

This verse is the fountainhead of the juristic prohibition of intoxicants and gambling: *khamr* is not a minor vice but *rijs* — classified with idolatrous practice, sourced to Satan's handiwork. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *khamara* as to conceal, the drunkard's intellect being the concealed thing; *maysir* as the game of chance that promises ease and delivers ruin. The next verse will explain *why* the four were classified so severely — what Satan intends through them — making the prohibition rationally transparent, not merely commanded.

**Extra Verified Note on the Four Terms**

*Al-anṣāb* were the stones around the Kaʿbah — reportedly 360 idols before its cleansing — and the altars on which Jāhilī Arabs slaughtered for their gods; *al-azlām* were the arrows of divination kept in the Kaʿbah's precinct, consulted for every major decision. The Qur'an groups the four so that no quarter of life escapes: what you drink (khamr), what you gamble (maysir), what you sacrifice (anṣāb), what you consult (azlām) — each a doorway Satan works through, each to be left at a distance for *falāḥ*, the success that ends this table-and-sanctity section as it began verse 35's summons to striving.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:91

> Satan only wants to cause between you enmity and hatred through intoxicants and gambling and to avert you from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. So will you not desist?

**Satan's Plan Through Wine and Dice**

*Innamā yurīdu ash-shayṭānu an yūqiʿa baynakumu al-ʿadāwata wa al-baghḍāʾa fī al-khamri wa al-maysiri* exposes the intent behind the prohibition. *Yurīdu*, he intends; *an yūqiʿa*, to set off — from *w-q-ʿ*, to cause to fall upon: drop enmity between you like a stone into water; *al-ʿadāwata wa al-baghḍāʾa*, enmity and hatred — the outward hostility and the inward grudge, the exact pair verse 64 said Allah cast among covenant-breakers: Satan wants the same division among believers. *Fī al-khamri wa al-maysiri*, through intoxicants and gambling — they are the *means*: the drunk loses his tongue and his temper, insults, fights, kills; the gambler who loses hates the winner, the winner fears the loser's vengeance; households break, brotherhood curdles. Reports connect the final prohibition to a drunken quarrel among companions that shed blood — the verse's cause had literally happened.

**And to Avert You From Remembrance and Prayer**

*Wa yaṣuddakum ʿan dhikri Allāhi wa ʿani aṣ-ṣalāh* — and to turn you away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. *Yaṣuddakum* from *ṣ-d-d*, to block, repel. Two targets, general and specific: *dhikr*, all remembrance of Allah, and *aṣ-ṣalāh*, the prayer itself. Intoxicants cover the intellect that remembers; gambling swallows the hours in which to remember — the earlier stage of the prohibition had already shown the collision: *do not approach prayer while intoxicated* (4:43). Satan's plan is therefore two-sided: set believers against each other, and unplug them from Allah — division among people and disconnection from heaven, the two things *falāḥ* is made of. This is why the classification as *rijs* was not harshness: the objects themselves are the delivery system for enmity and heedlessness.

**So Will You Not Desist?**

*Fa-hal antum muntahūn* — so will you not desist? *Muntahūn* from *intahā*, to stop at a boundary. The question is the Qur'an's gentlest hammer: after the classification (90) and the exposure of the scheme (91), the choice is placed before the believer's own reason — knowing all this, will you not stop? No threat opens the question; it appeals to the intellect that intoxication clouds and that this verse restores. The historical answer is the sīrah's most famous instant compliance: *we have desisted, we have desisted, our Lord!* — and the wine of Madinah flowed through its streets.

**A Verse That Explains the Law's Wisdom**

This verse supplies the *why* behind the *what*: the prohibition protects intellect, brotherhood, remembrance, and worship — the four assets Satan's handiwork attacks. Islamic legal thought later systematised this as the purpose of law — protecting religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property — and this verse is among its founding texts: the wisdom is stated inside the law itself, so abstinence is informed, not blind. The next verse will close the loop: after the prohibition, obedience — *obey Allah and obey the Messenger* — with the reminder that the Messenger's duty is clear delivery.

**Extra Verified Note on the Response**

The desistance of the companions is among the most cited events of the Madinan period: when ʿUmar called out the verse's opening, reports say wine was still foaming in the vessels as they were poured out, and the cry *fa-hal antum muntahūn — we have desisted!* answered the question before it finished. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṣadda* as to bar a road and *intahā* as to reach the limit and stop — the believer, having seen the boundary, plants himself before it. The verse's *ʿadāwah* and *baghḍāʾ* echo verse 64 verbatim, tying Satan's small scheme among the believers to the great enmity already cast among the covenant-breakers: division is always the same work, whoever the agent.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:92

> And obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware. But if you turn away — then know that upon Our Messenger is only clear notification

**Obey Allah and Obey the Messenger, and Beware**

*Wa aṭīʿū Allāha wa aṭīʿū ar-rasūla wa-ḥdharū* — and obey Allah and obey the Messenger, and beware. *Aṭīʿū* from *ṭ-w-ʿ*, to comply, obey — repeated deliberately: obey Allah in what He revealed, obey the Messenger in what he commands and explains, for the two obediences are one in practice: *whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah* (4:80). *Wa-ḥdharū*, and beware — from *ḥ-dh-r*, guarded caution. Placed immediately after the prohibition of khamr and maysir, the warning guards the prohibition's edge: beware of returning to what you shunned, beware of the scheme verse 91 exposed, beware of disobedience as such. The sequence — classification, exposure of intent, question *will you not desist?*, then obey and beware — completes the argument: prohibition appeals to reason, then claims the will.

**If You Turn Away, the Messenger's Duty Is Clear Notification**

*Fa-in tawallaytum fa-iʿlamū annamā ʿalā rasūlinā al-balāghu al-mubīn* — but if you turn away, then know that upon Our Messenger is only the clear delivery. *Tawallaytum*, you turn away — the same *w-l-y* root as the loyalty verses; *fa-iʿlamū*, then know; *annamā ʿalā rasūlinā*, only upon Our Messenger rests; *al-balāghu al-mubīn*, the clear conveyance — *balāgh*, delivery to the destination; *mubīn*, from *b-y-n*, made plain. The verse fixes the Prophet's ﷺ office: delivery, clear and complete — not compulsion, not control of hearts. Guidance of the heart belongs to Allah: *you do not guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills* (28:56). Whoever turns away after clear delivery bears his own choice; the Messenger is acquitted. The definition recurs at verse 99: *upon the Messenger is only the delivery, and Allah knows what you reveal and conceal* — delivery plus divine knowledge, the two that leave no room for excuse.

**Clear Delivery Includes Explanation**

The *balāgh mubīn* is not bare recitation: *We sent down to you the Reminder that you may make clear to the people what was sent down to them* (16:44) — the Messenger's explanation, embodied in the Sunnah, is part of the delivery. So the obedience commanded here has a full object: the Qur'an and its living explanation. This is why obeying the Messenger appears alongside obeying Allah throughout the legislation — the laws of this sūrah (foods, oaths, penalties, hunting, pilgrimage) come as revelation *and* as prophetic practice, both constituting the clear conveyance.

**A Verse That Closes the Prohibition With Authority**

The khamr prohibition, the most socially radical command yet delivered in Madinah, needed this seal: obedience is the response to revealed law; if some turn away, the law stands, the Messenger is blameless, and the turners bear it. The verse also protects the Messenger from the burden of outcomes — a theme this sūrah has traced through the People of the Book's history: revelation increases the defiant in rebellion (64, 68), yet the Messenger still delivers everything (67), and his grief is consoled (68). Duty is delivery; outcome belongs to Allah.

**Extra Verified Note on Balāgh Mubīn**

*Al-balāghu al-mubīn* — the phrase joins delivery with clarity: reaching every ear and leaving nothing obscure. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *balagha* as arrival at the utmost point. The same phrase seals several prophetic addresses (e.g., 24:54, 36:17), making it the Qur'anic job description of messengership: *our duty is only clear conveyance*. Here it guards the khamr prohibition specifically: after the clearest classification and the clearest exposure of Satan's plan, no one can claim confusion — only turning away. The next verse shows the law's other face: no blame for what was consumed before the prohibition, for those who believed and did good.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:93

> There is no blame upon those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they tasted ˹of food or drink˺, when they were mindful and believed and did righteous deeds, and then were mindful and believed, and then were mindful and excelled. And Allah loves the doers of good

**No Blame for What Was Tasted Before**

*Laysa ʿalā alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāti junāḥun fīmā ṭaʿimū* — there is no sin upon those who believed and did righteous deeds for what they tasted. *Junāḥ*, blame or sin; *fīmā ṭaʿimū*, in what they partook of — *ṭaʿimū* from *ṭ-ʿ-m*, to taste, eat: the food and drink consumed before the prohibition — khamr included — eaten and drunk while it was still lawful. Reports say the companions worried for those who had died before the final prohibition of wine, having drunk it in the lawful era: would they be blamed? The verse answered: law does not reach backwards. The same mercy governed ribā and the old practices: what was lawful when done, is not charged when later forbidden — for those who stood on faith and righteous deeds.

**The Condition: A Ladder of Faith**

*Idhā mā-ttaqaw wa āmanū wa ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāti thumma-ttaqaw wa āmanū thumma-ttaqaw wa aḥsanū* — when they were mindful and believed and did righteous deeds, then were mindful and believed, then were mindful and excelled. The verse repeats its clause three times, climbing: *ittaqaw wa āmanū wa ʿamilū aṣ-ṣāliḥāt* — mindfulness, faith, righteous deeds; *thumma-ttaqaw wa āmanū* — then deeper mindfulness and firmer faith; *thumma-ttaqaw wa aḥsanū* — then mindfulness crowned with *iḥsān*, excellence. The *thumma*, then, marks stages, not stutter: the life of faith is an ascent, each round of *taqwā* deepening belief, belief deepening deed, until deeds reach excellence — *iḥsān*, which the Prophet ﷺ defined in the ḥadīth of Jibrīl (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 8): to worship Allah as though you see Him, for though you see Him not, He sees you. The verse reads the whole life of the believers at once — what they were when they drank lawfully, and what they became: people of growing faith, whose former lawful enjoyments carry no charge.

**Allah Loves the Doers of Good**

*Wa Allāhu yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn* — and Allah loves the doers of good. The seal crowns the ladder: its top rung, *iḥsān*, is the rung of divine love. *Al-muḥsinīn*, those who do good with excellence, appear through the Qur'an as the beloved class — *Allah loves the doers of good* (2:195, 3:134). The verse's arc is thus mercy forward and mercy upward: no blame behind (for what was tasted lawfully), and love ahead (for the excellence toward which the ladder climbs).

**A Verse That Shows Law Looks Forward**

The verse states a principle the whole revelation follows: responsibility begins with revelation. *And We never punish until We send a messenger* (17:15); the People of the Book were charged for conduct against their own received warnings, never for ignorance of what had not come. Verse 95 will repeat the principle: *Allah has pardoned what is past*. Together they frame the sūrah's legislation — khamr, gambling, hunting laws, oaths — as a covenant going forward: from the moment of clarity, the boundary is live; before it, the believing dead and living are clean. The believer's past is mercy; the believer's future is the ladder.

**Extra Verified Note on the Repetition**

The threefold *ittaqaw... thumma... thumma* is unique in its rhythm; exegetes read it as the stages of a single life or the grades of a single community — always ending at *iḥsān*. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṭaʿima* as to take food into the mouth — here shorthand for all consumption, *fīmā ṭaʿimū* covering what the table had earlier supplied. The verse's tenderness should be noted alongside its rigour: the same sūrah that forbade khamr forever also cleansed its lawful era — and pointed the cleansed community at *iḥsān*, the excellence that earns love. The next verse will test precisely that community: game within reach, that Allah may mark who fears Him unseen.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:94

> O you who believe! Allah will surely test you with something of the game ˹that˺ your hands and spears ˹can˺ reach, that Allah may make known who fears Him unseen. Then whoever transgresses after that will have a painful punishment

**A Test Is Announced**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū la-yabluwannakumu Allāhu bi-shay'in mina aṣ-ṣaydi tanāluhu aydīkum wa rimāḥukum* — O you who believe, Allah will surely test you with something of the game your hands and spears reach. *La-yabluwannakum*, He will most surely test you — the doubled emphatic announces it as certain; *yabluwanna* from *b-l-w*, to try, test as gold is tried; *bi-shay'in mina aṣ-ṣayd*, with something of the game — *ṣayd*, wild game; *tanāluhu aydīkum wa rimāḥukum*, which your hands and spears attain — game close enough to grab or strike. The test's design is precise: the pilgrim in iḥrām may not hunt (verses 1–2), so the trial is game that comes *within easy reach* of the forbidden hand. Ease is the trap: what is far tests nothing; what is near tests everything.

**That Allah May Make Known Who Fears Him Unseen**

*Li-yaʿlama Allāhu man yakhāfuhu bil-ghayb* — that Allah may know who fears Him in the unseen. *Yakhāfuhu bil-ghayb*, fears Him unseen — when no watcher stands near, no companion watching, no human eye: only the Fearward of whom no one else is aware. The purpose clause *li-yaʿlama* is the Qur'an's standard formula for making manifest what already exists in Allah's knowledge — the test sorts and displays. True *taqwā* is precisely unseen-mindedness; a boundary that only matters when no one is looking is the perfect instrument to reveal who holds it from within. The same quality is named as the creed of the unknown righteous: *they fear their Lord unseen* (67:12).

**Whoever Transgresses After That**

*Fa-mani iʿtadā baʿda dhālika fa-lahu ʿadhābun alīm* — then whoever transgresses after that, for him is a painful punishment. *Iʿtadā* from *ʿ-d-w*, to overstep; *baʿda dhālika*, after that — after the clear prohibition, after the announced test, after the warning: knowledge precedes accountability. The verse and the next form a pair: the announced test (94) and the penalty for failing it (95). The Qur'an's history of such tests is long — the Sabbath fish that surfaced on the forbidden day (2:65's context), the water test of Ṭālūt's army (2:249) — tests whose ease was the point: whoever took what was easy and forbidden showed what his unseen fear was worth.

**A Verse That Returns to the Hunting Laws**

Verses 1–2 opened the sūrah's pilgrimage legislation: fulfil contracts, do not violate the rites, *nor the game of the land while you are in iḥrām*. After the long middle journey through table, oaths, khamr, and covenant-history, verse 94 returns to *ṣayd* and announces the test in advance, so that no one who falls could claim surprise. Reports place the verse around the Ḥudaybiyyah expedition (6 AH), when game came near the iḥrām-clad companions — the announced test materialised, and the penalty verse followed for those who reached. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *rimāḥ* as spears — the pilgrim's tools of opportunity.

**Extra Verified Note on the Test's Placement**

The pairing of announcement and penalty is protective: warning precedes sin's possibility, so the transgressor transgresses knowingly — *after that*. The phrase *man yakhāfuhu bil-ghayb* also appeared at verse 3's close — *that you may attain taqwā* — tying the whole lawful-and-forbidden section to one hidden trait: fear of the Unseen One when only He watches. The next verse states the penalty's three doors: equivalence judged by two just men, feeding the needy, or fasting — and pardons what is past.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:95

> O you who believe! Do not kill game while you are in the state of iḥrām. Whoever of you kills it intentionally — the compensation is the like of what he killed, judged by two just men among you, as an offering brought to the Kaʿbah, or the expiation of feeding needy people, or its equivalent in fasting, that he may taste the consequence of his deed. Allah has pardoned what is past; but whoever returns, Allah will take retribution from him. And Allah is All-Mighty, Owner of Retribution

**Do Not Kill Game While in Iḥrām**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū lā taqtulū aṣ-ṣayda wa antum ḥurum* — O you who believe, do not kill game while you are in the sacred state. *Lā taqtulū*, do not kill; *aṣ-ṣayd*, the game; *wa antum ḥurum*, while you are *ḥurum* — plural of *muḥrim*, the pilgrim wearing iḥrām, in whose presence even the land's creatures enjoy sanctuary. The prohibition repeats verses 1–2 now with teeth: the announced test (94) meets its law.

**The Penalty: Equivalence Judged Justly**

*Wa man qatalahu minkum mutaʿammidan fa-jazāʾun mithlu mā qatala mina an-naʿami yaḥkumu bihi dhawā ʿadlin minkum hadyan bāligha al-Kaʿbah* — whoever kills it intentionally among you, the compensation is the like of what he killed of livestock, judged by two just men among you, as an offering reaching the Kaʿbah. *Mutaʿammidan*, deliberately — with intent; *jazāʾun mithl*, a penalty of equivalence: for the wild creature killed, a domestic equivalent — *mina an-naʿam*, of the cattle, camels, sheep — assessed by *dhawā ʿadlin minkum*, two just men from among you: not the killer's estimate, not the ruler's whim — a just pair weighing like against like, as the Companions' rulings established: gazelle against sheep, ostrich against camel, each animal matched by the fair-minded. *Hadyan bāligha al-Kaʿbah*, as an offering reaching the Kaʿbah: the sacrifice brought to the Sacred House and its poor, so the violation repairs sanctity where it was broken.

**Or Feeding, or Fasting — to Taste the Consequence**

*Aw kaffāratun ṭaʿāmu masākīna aw ʿadlu dhālika ṣiyāman* — or the expiation of feeding needy people, or its equivalent in fasting. The second and third rungs of the ladder mirror the oath expiation of verse 89: feed the destitute by the assessed value; failing the means, fast the equivalent days. *Li-yadhūqa wabāla amrih* — that he may taste the consequence of his deed. *Wabāl*, the bitter yield of an act: the penalty is pedagogical — the violator should feel, in his own purse or stomach, the weight of what his hand did in the sanctuary.

**Allah Pardoned What Is Past; Retribution for the Returner**

*ʿAfa Allāhu ʿammā salafa wa man ʿāda fa-yantaqimu Allāhu minhu* — Allah has pardoned what is past; but whoever returns, Allah will take retribution from him. *ʿAfa*, He effaced: the violations before this legislation are wiped — the same forward-looking mercy as verse 93's *no blame for what they tasted*. *Wa man ʿāda*, whoever returns to it after the clarity: *fa-yantaqimu Allāhu minhu*, Allah will take retribution — *yantaqimu* from *n-q-m*; and the seal *wa Allāhu ʿAzīzun Dhū Intiqām* — Allah is All-Mighty, Owner of Retribution. The unique name pairing *ʿAzīz* with *Dhū Intiqām* closes the only verse in the sūrah with this double title: sanctity violated knowingly and repeatedly meets a Lord who is mighty in His reckoning. Clarity once given transforms pardon's era into accountability's era.

**A Verse That Protects the Sanctuary's Peace**

The penalty's architecture teaches the sanctity's purpose: equivalence judged by *just* men repairs justice; the offering at the Kaʿbah repairs the violated sanctuary; feeding the needy turns a hunter's sin into the poor's provision; fasting teaches the body the cost. Nothing in the scheme is revenue — everything is repair. And the two-just-men clause quietly legislates process: penalties in Islam are assessed by equity, not impulse.

**Extra Verified Note on Equivalence**

The equivalences were fixed by the Companions' practice — ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and ʿAlī judged cases of game killed in iḥrām, matching each species to its nearest livestock; the jurists systematised their rulings. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *hady* as what is driven to the House as a gift. The historical background is the Ḥudaybiyyah campaign: the test announced in verse 94 occurred, game was killed by companions in iḥrām — including the case of the wild ass hunted by Abū Quṭādah, the non-muḥrim, which the Prophet ﷺ questioned in the reported ḥadīth (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, the Book of Ḥajj) — and the legislation came down to settle every edge of it. The next verse will draw the sea's exception: its game is lawful provision for you and for travellers.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:96

> Lawful to you is the hunt of the sea and its food as provision for you and for the travellers — but forbidden to you is the hunt of the land as long as you are in the state of iḥrām. And be mindful of Allah, to whom you will be gathered

**Lawful: The Sea's Hunt and Food**

*Uḥilla lakum ṣaydu al-baḥri wa ṭaʿāmuhu matāʿan lakum wa lis-sayyārah* — made lawful for you is the hunt of the sea and its food, as provision for you and for travellers. *Uḥilla*, made lawful — the passive of permission: Allah's own opening of the door; *ṣaydu al-baḥr*, the sea's game — what is caught from the sea, alive; *wa ṭaʿāmuhu*, and its food — the sea's cast-ups and preserved catch, which Ibn ʿAbbās distinguished: *ṣayd* is what is taken alive, *ṭaʿām* what the sea has thrown out dead — both lawful. *Matāʿan lakum wa lis-sayyārah*, provision and enjoyment for you and for the travellers — the sea feeds the pilgrim who cannot touch the land's game and the wayfarer passing by. The verified Sunnah seals the breadth: *its water is a purifier and its dead are lawful* (Jāmiʿ at-Tirmidhī 69; Sunan Abī Dāwūd 83) — the sea needs neither ritual slaughter nor fear of what dies within it.

**Forbidden: The Land's Hunt, While in Iḥrām**

*Wa ḥurrima ʿalaykum ṣaydu al-barri mā dumtum ḥurumā* — but forbidden to you is the land's hunt for as long as you are in iḥrām. *Ḥurrima ʿalaykum*, made forbidden upon you; *ṣaydu al-barr*, the land's game; *mā dumtum ḥurumā*, while you remain *ḥurum* — the ban is tied to the state, not to the species: land creatures are lawful outside iḥrām (by the rules of verse 1's lawful slaughter and verse 4's trained animals), forbidden within it. The sea-land division is the verse's precise justice: the pilgrim's hunger is never trapped — the sea remains open — yet the sanctuary's land-peace stands intact. Jurists clarify the direction: even game hunted by a non-muḥrim is not lawful for the muḥrim to eat, while sea catch is lawful for him whether he hunted it or another did.

**Be Mindful of Allah, to Whom You Will Be Gathered**

*Wa-ttaqū Allāha alladhī ilayhi tuḥsharūn* — and be mindful of Allah, to whom you will be mustered. *Ilayhi tuḥsharūn* — to Him you will be gathered, *ḥ-sh-r*, assembled for the reckoning. The seal lifts the hunting rules to their true horizon: the boundary lines of sea and land are temporary; the Gathering is certain. The pilgrim who honours a boundary drawn only in iḥrām proves himself for the day when all states end and all are gathered.

**A Verse That Draws the Line With Mercy**

The verse is the resolution of the hunting section (94–96): the test announced, the penalty fixed, and now the map drawn — sea open, land closed, duration stated, gathering named. Its mercy is structural: *matāʿ*, enjoyment and provision, is the sea's stated purpose — permission for delight, not mere toleration. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *sayyārah* as those who travel the land — the provision clause embraces strangers and locals alike. The next verse will widen the lens from hunting rules to the sanctuary itself: the Kaʿbah, the sacred months, the garlanded offerings — all standing as support for people, by the knowledge of Him who knows the heavens and the earth.

**Extra Verified Note on the Distinction**

The ḥadīth *its water purifies and its dead are lawful* settled the sea's unique status among the schools; Ibn ʿAbbās's gloss — *ṣayd* taken alive, *ṭaʿām* the cast-out dead — is recorded by al-Ṭabarī. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *baḥr* as the great water and *barr* as the dry land, the pair Qur'anic for the two realms of creation. The verse's closing *tuḥsharūn* matches verse 98's accounting pair — *severe in punishment, forgiving, merciful* — the whole hunting unit framed by the Gathering it anticipates.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:97

> Allah has made the Kaʿbah, the Sacred House, a standing place for humanity, and the sacred month, and the sacrificial animals, and the garlanded ˹offerings˺. That is so you may know that Allah knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, and that Allah has full knowledge of everything

**The Kaʿbah: A Standing Place for All People**

*Jaʿala Allāhu al-Kaʿbata al-bayta al-ḥarāma qiyāman lin-nās* — Allah made the Kaʿbah, the Sacred House, a *qiyām* for humanity. *Jaʿala*, He made, appointed; *al-Kaʿbah* — named for its cubic form; *al-bayta al-ḥarām*, the Sacred House, the inviolable House, *the first House set up for humanity, in Makkah, blessed, and guidance for the worlds* (3:96), built and raised by Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl (2:127). *Qiyāman lin-nās*, a standing establishment for the people: the axis on which religious and worldly life stands — pilgrimage rising from it, security radiating from it, provision circulating to it. The word also carries security: a place where standing is safe, the House of the prayer *O Lord, make this a secure city* (2:126). All people — *lin-nās* — not one tribe: the House belongs to humanity.

**The Sacred Months, the Offerings, the Garlands**

*Wa ash-shahra al-ḥarāma wa al-hadya wa al-qalāʾid* — and the sacred month, and the sacrificial offerings, and the garlands. *Ash-shahra al-ḥarām*, the sacred months — Dhū al-Qaʿdah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram, Rajab — the quarter of the year when fighting ceases and the roads to the House stand safe, the truce even Jāhiliyyah honoured; *al-hady*, the offerings — the animals driven to the House as gifts; *al-qalāʾid*, the garlands — the collars tied on them, marking them as sacred so no hand harms them on the road. The list mirrors verse 2's *do not violate the rites of Allah, the sacred month, the offerings, the garlands* — there prohibited from violation, here declared as divine provision for human order: security in time (sacred months), security on the road (garlands), worship at the destination (offerings), sanctuary at the centre (the House).

**So You May Know That Allah Knows**

*Dhālika li-taʿlamū anna Allāha yaʿlamu mā fis-samāwāti wa mā fil-arḍi wa anna Allāha bikulli shay'in ʿAlīm* — that is so you may know that Allah knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, and that Allah has full knowledge of everything. The purpose clause ties the sanctuary-system to omniscience: this entire architecture — House, months, offerings, garlands, the hunting bounds of verses 94–96 — was designed by the One who knows every creature in heaven and earth, every pilgrim's need, every traveller's road. Legislation from such knowledge cannot err; the rites are not arbitrary relics but the product of total knowledge. The clause also answers the ancient objection: who appointed this House and these months? — the One who knows what all creation contains, and He appointed them for the good of all.

**A Verse That Crowns the Sanctuary Section**

Verses 94–97 form one arc: the test of game (94), its penalty (95), the sea's lawful exception (96), and now the meaning of it all (97) — the hunting rules are not isolated statutes but beams in the structure of the Sacred House's peace, which Allah, knowing everything, built for humanity. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *qāma* as to stand and uphold — *qiyām*, that by which things stand upright. The House stands so that human order stands. The next verse completes the section with the two Names every pilgrim must carry: severe in punishment, forgiving and merciful.

**Extra Verified Note on the Rites**

The garlands — strands of fibre or leather — are the visible sign by which Jāhiliyyah and Islam alike marked animals as protected on the road; the Qur'an inherits and sanctifies the practice (5:2). *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *qalāʾid* as the collars tied on the sacrificial animals. The four sacred months predate Islam as Arab custom and are ratified here as divine institution — the same four named in 9:36. The verse's closing *bikulli shay'in ʿAlīm* — knowing everything — is the sūrah's recurring seal of divine competence (verses 38, 40, 57): laws from the All-Knowing need no audit.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:98

> Know that Allah is severe in punishment, and that Allah is Forgiving and Merciful

**Know: Severe in Punishment, Forgiving, Merciful**

*Iʿlamū anna Allāha shadīdu al-ʿiqābi wa anna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — know that Allah is severe in punishment, and that Allah is Forgiving and Merciful. *Iʿlamū*, know — the imperative of settled knowledge, not guesswork; *shadīdu al-ʿiqāb*, severe in retribution — *ʿiqāb*, the consequence that follows; *Ghafūrun Raḥīm*, All-Forgiving, continuously Merciful. Two descriptions of one Lord, delivered in one breath: the believer must hold both together or misread Him entirely.

**Why Both Must Be Known Together**

Each Name guards against the opposite error. Severity alone breeds despair — and despair is unbelief by the back door: *do not despair of Allah's mercy* (39:53). Mercy alone breeds complacency — the criminal's miscalculation that consequences are decor. The Qur'anic method pairs them deliberately, most compactly in *the Forgiver of sin, Accepter of repentance, severe in punishment, Owner of abundance* (40:3) — forgiveness first, severity held within the same sentence. Here the pairing follows the sanctuary verses: the House stands, the months are sacred, the penalties are real (95) — *severe in punishment* — and the pardon clause stood beside them — *Allah has pardoned what is past* (95), *no blame for what they tasted* (93) — *forgiving, merciful*. The believer walks the sanctuary with both truths in hand: the boundary is serious, and the Lord of the boundary wants to forgive.

**Fear and Hope as the Pilgrim's Two Wings**

Islamic worship is balanced between *khawf*, fear, and *rajāʾ*, hope — and this verse is the balance in eleven words. The sūrah itself has modelled the pairing from its start: theft's penalty (38) followed by *whoever repents after his wrongdoing and mends — Allah accepts his repentance* (39); war-mongering's retribution (33) followed by *unless they repent before you overpower them* (34). The pilgrim circling the House should fear the Lord of its sanctity and hope in the Lord whose mercy covers what is past — both, always. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *shadīd* as intense, firm — severity of *ʿiqāb*, the reaping of what was sown.

**A Verse That Seals the Sanctuary Section**

The short verse closes the long passage from the test of game through the penalty, the sea's exception, and the House's establishment. Its final word on the section: the sanctuary's Lord is not a tame deity — His severity guards the sacred, His mercy receives the returning. *Shadīdu al-ʿiqāb* appears about fourteen times in the Qur'an, almost always paired with mercy Names — the pairing is the point, never the severity alone. The next verse returns to the theme of verses 67 and 92: the Messenger's duty is only the delivery — and Allah knows what you reveal and what you conceal.

**Extra Verified Note on the Pairing**

The exact formula *shadīdu al-ʿiqāb... Ghafūrun Raḥīm* recurs in the Qur'an (e.g., 66:8), and the theology of *khawf* and *rajāʾ* stands on these paired verses: fear without hope collapses, hope without fear drifts. *Ghafūr* — the much-forgiving — and *Raḥīm* — the ever-merciful — are the two most frequent Names after *Allah* in the Qur'an's seals. The verse's command *iʿlamū*, know, makes the knowledge of these two attributes an obligation of faith: worship without knowing *both* is worship of a half-Lord, and no such Lord exists.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:99

> The Messenger's duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah knows whatever you reveal and whatever you conceal

**The Messenger's Duty: Only Delivery**

*Mā ʿalā ar-rasūli illā al-balāgh* — upon the Messenger is nothing except the conveyance. *Mā...illā*, nothing except — the restrictive double negative defines his office to its edges: he delivers; he does not compel belief, does not control hearts, does not manage outcomes. The definition repeats verse 92's *if you turn away, know that upon Our Messenger is only clear notification*, now standing as its own sentence — and it recurs across the Qur'an (24:54, 36:17) as the standing job description of prophethood. The delivery itself is complete: the Qur'an verbatim, *and that you may make clear to the people what was sent down to them* (16:44) — the explanation that became the Sunnah. Conveyed so, the message leaves its hearers face to face with choice.

**And Allah Knows What You Reveal and Conceal**

*Wa Allāhu yaʿlamu mā tubdūna wa mā taktumūn* — and Allah knows what you reveal and what you conceal. *Tubdūna* from *b-d-w*, to make apparent; *taktumūn* from *k-t-m*, to hide. The pairing is the sūrah's recurring duet: Allah knows what is in the heavens and the earth (97), is severe in punishment and forgiving, merciful (98) — and now knows what you display and what you bury (99). The two halves of the verse lock together: since the Messenger delivers only, no one can blame him for another's hidden state; since Allah knows the hidden, no one can hide from the judgment that follows. Concealed disbelief (verse 61), concealed loyalty (52–53), concealed testimony (106's theme) — all are *mā taktumūn*, and all stand open before Him.

**A Verse That Distributes Responsibility**

The verse is a complete assignment of responsibility: the Messenger bears delivery; the people bear response; Allah bears knowledge and judgment. No one is charged with what is not theirs — not the Messenger with guidance's outcome (28:56: *you do not guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills*), not the hearers with more than choice after clarity. The placement after the sanctuary section (94–98) is deliberate: penalties prescribed, bounds set — and then the reminder that the Messenger conveyed all of it, and Allah watches the response, outward and inward.

**Extra Verified Note on the Seal**

The concealment clause also anticipates the bequest verses that follow (106–108): oaths about hidden silver bowls, testimonies about what a dying man entrusted — the law of the next verses exists because concealment is real, and this verse announces that no concealed thing escapes the One who will inform *of what you used to do* (105). *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *balagha* as reaching the utmost point — the message that arrives completely. The next verse will address the people's distorted sight: good and evil are not equal, however much the abundance of evil dazzles.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:100

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "The impure and the pure are not equal, even though the abundance of the impure amazes you. So be mindful of Allah, O people of understanding, that you may be successful"

**The Impure and the Pure Are Not Equal**

*Qul lā yastawī al-khabīthu wa aṭ-ṭayyib* — say: the impure and the pure are not equal. *Qul*, say — the Prophet ﷺ is given the words; *lā yastawī*, are not equal — *s-w-y*, level; *al-khabīth*, the impure, vile thing — from *kh-b-th*, to rot, spoil; *aṭ-ṭayyib*, the pure, wholesome thing — from *ṭ-y-b*, to be fragrant and good. The verse is the general law behind the whole table-section: *ṭayyibāt* made lawful (4–5), *ḥalālan ṭayyiban* commanded (88), khamr and maysir and idols and divination classified *rijs* (90) — and now the axiom: khabīth and ṭayyib are two species that never equate, whatever the scales say. The words extend beyond food to deeds and people — *impure things for the impure, and pure things for the pure* (24:26) — the categories are built into creation, and revelation merely names them.

**Even Though the Abundance of the Impure Amazes You**

*Wa law aʿjabaka kathratu al-khabīth* — even though the abundance of the impure may amaze you. *Aʿjabaka*, it dazzles you — *ʿ-j-b*, to astound; *kathrah*, abundance, multitude. The verse names the temptation precisely: evil impresses by mass. The Jāhiliyyah's wine-trade was vast, its idols many, its divining arrows consulted at every crossroads; the pure things stood few against them. A5 weak mind reasons from majority: so much wine, so many altars, so much business — how can it all be vile? The verse cuts the reasoning at the root: quantity is not quality. One *ṭayyib* outweighs a warehouse of *khabīth*; abundance is the impure thing's only argument, and it is no argument.

**O People of Understanding**

*Fa-ttaqū Allāha yā ulī al-albāb* — so be mindful of Allah, O possessors of *albāb*. *Ulū al-albāb*, the people of *lubb* — the kernel, the core of understanding: those who reason from essence, not surface, who can tell a rotten apple from a fragrant one no matter the pile's size. Mindfulness (*ittaqū*) and discernment are linked: the one who shields himself from Allah's displeasure is the one whose judgment the dazzle of numbers cannot capture. Then the seal — *laʿallakum tufliḥūn*, that you may succeed — the same *falāḥ* that closed verse 90's command to shun the four evils: success runs along the road of right classification, pure chosen over impure, truth over mass.

**A Verse That Summarises the Section's Principle**

The verse stands at the close of the lawful-and-unlawful block (87–99) as its distilled principle: do not forbid the pure (87), eat the pure (88), shun the impure (90), and never let the impure's abundance confuse the classification (100). The Qur'an elsewhere states the same non-equation for the day the two are separated: *We will separate the impure from the pure* (8:37) — the judgment itself is a sorting that no abundance can bribe. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *khabutha* as to become foul and *ṭāba* as to be pleasant and wholesome — two verbs of two worlds.

**Extra Verified Note on Ulū al-Albāb**

*Ulū al-albāb* is the Qur'an's standing title for its ideal reader — those who *remember Allah standing, sitting, and on their sides, and reflect on the creation* (3:191), to whom *wisdom* is given (2:269). Here the title is earned by one specific competence: refusing to equate khabīth with ṭayyib because the khabīth outnumbers. The next verse will teach those same believers a discipline of the tongue: do not ask about matters whose disclosure would distress you.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:101

> O you who believe! Do not ask about things which, if disclosed to you, would distress you. But if you ask about them while the Qur'an is being sent down, they will be disclosed to you. Allah has pardoned ˹them˺, and Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**Do Not Ask What Would Distress You**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū lā tasʾalū ʿan ashyāʾa in tubda lakum tasuʾkum* — O you who believe, do not ask about matters which, if disclosed to you, would distress you. *Lā tasʾalū*, do not ask; *in tubda lakum*, if made manifest to you; *tasuʾkum*, they would displease you — *s-w-ʾ*, to be bad, to offend. The verse legislates the etiquette of questions: not all disclosure is mercy. Some knowledge, once given, becomes binding — a question answered may convert what was tolerated into obligation, what was obscure into prohibition. Islam came to be a relief, not a burden — *Allah does not intend hardship for you* (5:6) — so the believer does not dig for details that will bury him. The prohibition protects ease, not ignorance: asking *what must I do?* is worship; asking *what more will you burden me with?* is the road the verse closes.

**But If You Ask While the Qur'an Is Being Sent Down**

*Wa in tasʾalū ʿanhā ḥīna yunazzalu al-Qurʾānu tubda lakum* — but if you ask about them while the Qur'an is being sent down, they will be disclosed to you. The exception matches the rule's purpose: questions asked of the living revelation — *O Messenger, is this lawful? what does this ruling mean?* — receive clear answers as part of the revelation's own self-explanation. Such asking is the community's education, and the verse honours it. What is discouraged is not inquiry but self-harming inquiry — matters Allah left vague in mercy, prised open into hardship. *ʿAfa Allāhu ʿanhā* — Allah has pardoned it: the questions already asked before this counsel are erased, the same forward mercy as verse 95's *Allah has pardoned what is past*. The seal — *wa Allāhu Ghafūrun Ḥalīm*, Forgiving and Forbearing — *Ḥalīm*, the One who does not hasten punishment: even presumptuous questions met forbearance, not lightning.

**The Historical Setting**

Reports connect the verse to specific questions the companions asked: a man asked whether ḥajj was obligatory every year, and the Prophet ﷺ said that if he said yes, it would become so every year — *and you would not be able to bear it*; the same report gives the Prophet's general counsel: *leave me with what I left you; those before you were destroyed by their excessive questioning and their disputes with their prophets* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1337). A related ḥadīth states the principle directly: *the greatest in sin among the Muslims is the one who asked about a thing not forbidden, and it became forbidden because of his asking* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2358). The verse gives revelation-side of the same wisdom: boundaries are mercy; questions that strip them away harm the asker first.

**A Verse That Teaches the Limits of Curiosity**

Curiosity is commanded when it seeks obligation's clarity and corrected when it seeks hardship's invention. The balance is itself a mercy of the law: enough knowledge to obey completely, not so much that obedience drowns in detail no one needs. The next verse gives the reason in history: a people before you asked such questions — and became disbelievers because of the answers.

**Extra Verified Note on the Questions**

The asking about lineage — the companion who demanded *who is my father?* — is also reported among the occasions; the answer honoured honesty but the lesson stood: some disclosures do more harm than the ignorance they replace. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *s-w-ʾ* as to sadden and offend — *tasuʾkum*, it would distress you — the verse's criterion written into its word: filter every question through one test — would the answer distress? Then the next: does it clarify obligation? The first says stop; the second says ask.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:102

> A people before you asked such questions, then became disbelievers on account of them

**A People Before You Asked — Then Disbelieved**

*Qad saʾalahā qawmun min qablikum thumma aṣbaḥū bihā kāfirīn* — a people before you asked about such things, then became disbelievers because of them. *Qad saʾalahā*, they did ask about them — *s-ʾ-l*; *thumma aṣbaḥū*, then they became — *ṣ-b-ḥ*, to wake into a state; *bihā kāfirīn*, because of them, disbelievers. The verse gives the reason behind the prohibition of verse 101: this is not hypothetical caution — it happened. Questions asked, answers disclosed, and the answerers found the truth heavier than their pride: they asked and then denied what they had begged to know.

**Who Asked, and What They Denied**

The Qur'an's own record identifies them. The Children of Israel demanded details of the cow — *call upon your Lord to make clear to us what she is... what colour... what sort* — each answer raising the price until *they almost did not do it* (2:67–71). They demanded *show us Allah outright* — and were seized by the thunderbolt (4:153). And in this very sūrah, they asked the table spread down from heaven, and were warned: *I fear for you that if you disbelieve after it, you will have a punishment like none* (5:115). The pattern is exact: excessive asking → clarified obligation or sign → denial of what was asked for. The questions became their undoing not because knowledge is dangerous, but because hearts that question for hardship's sake do not want the truth that answers them.

**The Warning Transferable**

For the believers the lesson is precise: ask what clarifies obedience (verse 101's exception), never ask what manufactures burden or tests heaven's patience (verse 101's rule) — because the previous nations' story proves the endpoint. The Prophet's ﷺ counsel matched: *leave me with what I left you; those before you were destroyed by excessive questioning* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1337). Revelation hands down enough to live rightly; the rest is either mercy or a snare, and wisdom knows which is which.

**A Verse That Completes the Argument**

Verses 101–102 form a single argument: rule (do not ask what would distress), exception (asking during revelation is answered), pardon (the past is forgiven), and now precedent (a people asked and fell into disbelief by it). The reasoning is historical induction — the Qur'an's constant method: learn from recorded ruin. The next verse will turn from questioning to its opposite disease: not asking too much, but legislating what Allah never said — the fabricated taboos of the baḥīrah, sā'ibah, waṣīlah, and ḥām.

**Extra Verified Note on "Became Disbelievers Because of Them"**

*Bihā kāfirīn* — because of them: the disbelief attaches to the questions themselves, not merely to later denial — the questions revealed the hearts that asked them. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *aṣbaḥa* as to become by morning — the state arrived as a new dawn on them: they asked as seekers and woke as deniers. The verse's brevity is its force: one sentence carries a whole psychology of faith and its opposite — the truth refuses to be treated as a burden by those who will not carry it.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:103

> Allah has not ordained any baḥīrah or sā'ibah or waṣīlah or ḥām; but those who disbelieve fabricate lies against Allah, and most of them do not understand

**Allah Has Not Ordained Them**

*Mā jaʿala Allāhu min baḥīratin wa lā sā'ibatin wa lā waṣīlatin wa lā ḥām* — Allah has not ordained any baḥīrah, sā'ibah, waṣīlah, or ḥām. *Mā jaʿala*, He has not made, instituted — legislation belongs to Allah alone; the four words are Jāhiliyyah's invented taboos, each a camel or sheep condemned by custom to live unridden, unmilked, unslaughtered, dedicated to the idols. *Al-baḥīrah*, the ear-slitted one — *baḥara*, to slit: a she-camel whose ear was split as a sign, her milk reserved for idols; *as-sā'ibah*, the set-loose one — *sāba*, to let go: a camel released to roam free for a vow, no one to ride or milk her; *al-waṣīlah*, the joined one — *waṣala*, to connect: a sheep bearing twin females, or a camel whose offspring joined, spared from use; *al-ḥām*, the spared bull — *ḥamā*, to protect: a stallion-camel retired after siring a set number, left to graze untouched. None of it came from heaven: *Allah has not ordained* any of them.

**But the Disbelievers Fabricate Lies Against Allah**

*Wa lākinna alladhīna kafarū yaftarūna ʿalā Allāhi al-kadhib* — but those who disbelieve fabricate lies against Allah. *Yaftarūna* from *f-r-y*, to invent, forge: they attributed to Allah what He never said — the exact crime verse 87 prohibited among believers (*do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful*) now exposed at its Jāhilī source. Making *ḥarām* by human invention is not excess only; it is *iftirāʾ*, a lie against Allah, because it drafts His name onto counterfeit law. The verse thus completes the sūrah's doctrine of legislation: Allah permits and forbids (verses 4–5); humans who add to either side — the People of the Book with their distortions, the idolaters with their taboos, the ascetics with their oaths — all lie against Him.

**And Most of Them Do Not Understand**

*Wa aktharuhum lā yaʿqilūn* — and most of them do not understand. *Lā yaʿqilūn* — they do not use the intellect that binds perception to truth. The phrase closed verse 58 (the mockers of the call to prayer) and here explains the taboo-makers: whoever understood that legislation belongs to Allah would never credit idols with it. Blindness to this principle is the definition of *lā yaʿqilūn* in both places — understanding, in the Qur'an's usage, is first of all knowing who is Lord of the law.

**A Verse That Abolishes the Jāhilī Taboos**

The verse simultaneously forbids the four categories and reopens every animal they condemned: the baḥīrah may be ridden, milked, slaughtered; the sā'ibah used; the waṣīlah eaten; the ḥām worked. The record of where it began: the Prophet ﷺ saw ʿAmr ibn Luḥayy — the man who first scattered idols through Arabia — *dragging his intestines in the Fire*, because *he was the first to institute the sā'ibah* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4623, Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2856). One invention, centuries of wasted wealth and worship; one verse, the whole structure demolished.

**Extra Verified Note on the Four**

*Al-baḥīrah* also appears in *it was not the cattle or the crops that Allah forbade, but the disbelievers who invented* (6:138) — the same fabrication named there. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses each term by its sign: the slit ear, the freed back, the joined young, the protected bull. The verse's place after the question-verses (101–102) is coherent: both diseases of religion are condemned together — asking what burdens, and inventing what Allah never said. The next verse shows the mask the fabricators wore: when called to revelation, they answered with their forefathers.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:104

> And when it is said to them, "Come to what Allah has sent down and to the Messenger," they say, "Enough for us is what we found our forefathers upon" — even though their forefathers knew nothing and were not guided?

**"Enough for Us Is What We Found Our Forefathers Upon"**

*Wa idhā qīla lahum taʿālaw ilā mā anzala Allāhu wa ilā ar-rasūli qālū ḥasbunā mā wajadnā ʿalayhi ābāʾanā* — when it is said to them, come to what Allah has sent down and to the Messenger, they say: enough for us is what we found our forefathers upon. *Taʿālaw*, come — the standing invitation of revelation; *ḥasbunā*, enough for us — *ḥ-s-b*, to suffice; *mā wajadnā ʿalayhi ābāʾanā*, what we found our fathers upon — *wajada*, to find by inheritance. The reply is the anthem of blind tradition: not *this is true* but *this is inherited* — quantity of generations substituted for quality of evidence. The Qur'an records the identical answer at 2:170 and 31:21: *even if their forefathers understood nothing and were not guided?* The baḥīrah-taboos of verse 103 have just been exposed as inventions; asked to exchange invention for revelation, the fabricators pledge loyalty to the inventors.

**Even Though Their Forefathers Knew Nothing and Were Not Guided?**

*A-wa law kāna ābāʾuhum lā yaʿlamūna shay'an wa lā yahtadūn* — even though their forefathers knew nothing and were not guided? *Lā yaʿlamūna shayʾan*, they knew not a thing — no knowledge of what Allah ordained; *wa lā yahtadūn*, and they were not guided — no *hudā*, no revelation-received-and-followed. The rhetorical question dismantles the claim: the forefathers' authority rests on their knowledge and guidance; strip those, and inheritance becomes an empty chain. The question also states the historical fact: those who instituted the baḥīrah and its sisters — the archetype is ʿAmr ibn Luḥayy — invented without revelation and strayed without guidance; following them is following men who knew nothing of the One who owns legislation.

**Revelation and Messenger Versus Inheritance**

The invitation names two destinations — *to what Allah has sent down, and to the Messenger* — the Book and the living explanation (as verse 92 paired obedience to both). Against these stand not rival texts but *wijdān*, inherited finding: the weakest possible ground for eternal affairs. The Qur'an never rejects all inheritance — the forefathers of the prophets were guides; it rejects inheritance *as authority against revelation*: descent does not legislate, repetition does not verify. The verse thus closes the unit begun at 101: asking too much burdens; following too little (mere inheritance) blinds — both diseases have one cure, the revelation and the Messenger.

**A Verse That Condemns Blind Taqlīd**

The target is *taqlīd* in its rawest form — acceptance without evidence — and the verse's logic transfers to every generation: any community whose answer to *come to what Allah sent down* is *enough for us what we found* stands in this verse. The test is content, not ancestry: *when those who were given the Book... they believe in it* — those who examined were saved; those who only inherited repeated the baḥīrah. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *wajada* as to find by experience — here experience one generation removed, second-hand life answering for first-hand truth.

**Extra Verified Note on the Archetype**

ʿAmr ibn Luḥayy, condemned in the Sunnah as the first to institute the sā'ibah and scatter the idols (Bukhārī 4623; Muslim 2856), is the verse's living illustration: forefathers who *knew nothing and were not guided* — yet whose practice, centuries on, was defended as *ḥasbunā*. The next verse releases the believers from the weight of such refusal: upon you is yourselves; the straying of others does not harm you, if you are guided.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:105

> O you who believe! You are responsible for your own souls. He who has strayed cannot harm you, if you are rightly guided. To Allah is the return of you all, and then He will inform you of what you used to do

**You Are Responsible for Your Own Souls**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū ʿalaykum anfusakum* — O you who believe, upon you are your own souls. *ʿAlaykum anfusakum*, upon you rests yourselves: the first duty is the soul's own guidance — reform it, guard it, keep it on the path. The command follows the verse about those who cling to forefathers against all evidence: after the call has been delivered (and the verses commanded the call — *enjoin the good*), the believer is released from the outcome. Guidance is not transmitted by force; each soul is its own steward. The verse grants the believer freedom from despair over others: your charge is your own compass, not the world's conversion.

**He Who Strays Cannot Harm You, If You Are Guided**

*Lā yaḍurrukum man ḍalla idhā ihtadaytum* — he who strays cannot harm you, if you are guided. *Lā yaḍurrukum*, does not harm you — *ḍ-r-r*; *man ḍalla*, whoever strays; *idhā ihtadaytum*, when you are on guidance. The strayers' deviation cannot corrupt the guided — misguidance is not contagious to the immune. This answered a real anxiety: surrounded by forefather-worshippers and fabricators, would the believers not be dragged down with them? The verse says no — with one condition, *idhā ihtadaytum*: the immunity belongs to the guided, and its maintenance is the *ʿalaykum anfusakum* of the first clause.

**The Verse Does Not Cancel Enjoining Good**

It must be read beside the sūrah's own commandments: the community that *did not forbid one another* (79) was cursed; the scholars who did not forbid were condemned (63). The Prophet's ﷺ interpretation settled the balance: *Abū Bakr aṣ-Ṣiddīq said: you recite this verse and misread it — I heard the Messenger of Allah ﷺ say: if a people see a wrongdoer and do not restrain him, Allah is about to envelop them all in punishment* (Sunan at-Tirmidhī 3057, graded *ḥasan*). The verse's meaning is therefore sequential: call, enjoin, restrain — and after the duty is done, the strayer's straying does not harm you. It removes anxiety, not responsibility; it ends compulsion, not counsel.

**To Allah Is the Return of You All**

*Ilā Allāhi marjiʿukum jamīʿan fa-yunabbiʾukum bimā kuntum taʿmalūn* — to Allah is the return of you all, then He will inform you of what you used to do. *Marjiʿukum jamīʿan*, the return of all of you together — guided and strayers alike; *fa-yunabbiʾukum*, then He will inform you — *n-b-ʾ*, full report; *bimā kuntum taʿmalūn*, of what you used to do. The seal closes the loop opened by verse 99: the Messenger delivered, Allah knows the revealed and concealed — and the return makes the knowledge an accounting. Each soul stands alone before the report; hence the verse's whole logic: others' straying is their file, not yours.

**A Verse That Grants Focus**

The verse is the believer's charter of priorities: guard your guidance, discharge the duty of counsel, release the outcome. It is the cure for both paralysis and arrogance — paralysis (why call, when they will not listen?) is answered by the duty to enjoin; arrogance (we are safe whatever they do?) is answered by *idhā ihtadaytum*, if you remain guided. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *rajʿa* as return to the place of origin — the soul's origin is its Lord, and the report awaits there.

**Extra Verified Note on the Occasion**

Reports say some companions took the verse to excuse withdrawal from wrong around them — the correction came from Abū Bakr's mouth, citing the Prophet ﷺ (Tirmidhī 3057), and its companion-text: *enjoin good and forbid evil until you see greed obeyed, desire followed, the world preferred, and every man impressed with his own opinion* (Sunan Abī Dāwūd 4336). The verse thus lives between two errors: thinking one's hands save the world, and thinking one's purity exempts the world. The next verse descends to a case study of concealed things and their exposure: the bequest at the edge of death.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:106

> O you who believe! Testimony ˹is required˺ between you when death approaches one of you at the time of bequest: two just men from among you, or two others from other than you, if you are travelling in the land and the calamity of death befalls you. You shall detain them after the prayer, and if you doubt, let them both swear by Allah: "We will not sell it for any price, even if he should be a near relative, nor will we conceal the testimony of Allah — for then indeed we would be among the sinners"

**Testimony at the Time of Bequest**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū shahādatu baynikum idhā ḥaḍara aḥadakumu al-mawtu ḥīna al-waṣiyyah* — O you who believe, testimony between you when death approaches one of you, at the time of bequest. *Shahādatu baynikum*, testimony between you — a formal, required witnessing; *idhā ḥaḍara aḥadakumu al-mawt*, when death comes to one of you; *ḥīna al-waṣiyyah*, at the moment of the bequest — *waṣiyyah* from *w-ṣ-y*, the will a dying person makes: debts named, trusts returned, portions assigned to those outside the heirs. The Prophet's ﷺ standard governed the bequest's size: *a third, and a third is generous* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2744) — up to one-third for non-heirs. The verse legislates the witnesses because the bequest is the most vulnerable of transactions: its author is leaving, its beneficiaries may be tempted, and its words vanish unless fixed.

**Two Just Men, or Two Others While Travelling**

*Ithnāni dhawā ʿadlin minkum aw ākharāni min ghayrikum in antum ḍarabtum fil-arḍi fa-aṣābatkum muṣībatu al-mawt* — two just men from among you, or two others from other than you, if you are journeying in the land and the calamity of death strikes you. *Dhawā ʿadl*, possessors of justice — upright men; *aw ākharāni min ghayrikum*, or two others not of your community — non-Muslims, permitted when the believer dies travelling among strangers with no Muslim witnesses near. Reports name the occasion: the bequest of a Muslim traveller witnessed by Tamīm ad-Dārī and ʿAdī ibn Baddāʾ, both Christians — a silver bowl among the effects later disputed, and the verse descended to govern exactly such a case. Necessity opens the door: rights are preserved by whatever just testimony can be had; the law bends its form to protect its substance.

**Detain Them After the Prayer; Let Them Swear**

*Taḥbisūnahumā min baʿdi aṣ-ṣalāh fa-yuqsimāni billāhi in irtabtum* — you shall detain them after the prayer, and if you doubt, they shall both swear by Allah. *Taḥbisūnahumā*, hold them back — *ḥ-b-s*, to confine; *min baʿdi aṣ-ṣalāh*, after the prayer — reports specify the ʿAṣr prayer: the day's end, the crowd assembled, minds fresh from standing before Allah. Then *fa-yuqsimāni billāhi*, they swear by Allah — the oath's wording is prescribed: *lā nashtarī bihi thamanan wa law kāna dhū qurbā*, we will not sell it for any price, though the beneficiary be a near kinsman; *wa lā naktumu shahādatu Allāh*, nor will we conceal the testimony of Allah; *innā idhan lamina al-āthimīn*, else we would be among the sinners. The oath seals the two temptations that kill testimony: bribery and concealment — and binds them with the sinner's name, so that the words themselves preach to the speaker.

**A Verse That Protects the Dying Man's Words**

The procedure assembles four safeguards: qualified witnesses (just men, Muslims at home, any just men abroad), occasion fixed (after prayer, in assembly), doubt answered (oath by Allah), temptation closed (no price, no kin, no concealment). The whole machinery exists because verse 99 declared Allah knows what is concealed — and this law drags the concealed into daylight before it can be buried. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *waṣiyyah* as the entrusting of what follows one's death.

**Extra Verified Note on the Occasion**

The report of Tamīm ad-Dārī and ʿAdī ibn Baddāʾ — the two Christian witnesses of a dying traveller's bequest, the disputed silver bowl — is recorded in the tafsīr literature as the verse's occasion; the law, as always, outgrew its occasion to stand for every deathbed testimony. The next verse will supply the remedy if these safeguards fail: two of the heirs replace them, swearing a counter-oath.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:107

> But if it is discovered that they both are guilty of sin, then two others shall stand in their place — the two nearest ˹heirs˺ from those with a claim — and they shall both swear by Allah: "Our testimony is truer than theirs, and we have not transgressed — for then indeed we would be among the wrongdoers"

**If It Is Discovered They Incurred Sin**

*Fa-in ʿuthira ʿalā annahumā istaḥaqqā ithman* — but if it is discovered that the two of them earned a sin. *ʿUthira ʿalā*, it was turned up, unearthed — the passive of discovery, whether by evidence, confession, or circumstance; *istaḥaqqā ithman*, they earned a sin — *ḥ-q-q*, to deserve: the witnesses took something, concealed something, sold something. The verse addresses the failure mode of verse 106: what if the safeguards are breached — if the men who swore *we will not sell nor conceal* did precisely that? The law's answer is not despair but replacement.

**Two Others Stand in Their Place**

*Fa-ākharāni yaqūmāni maqāmahumā mina alladhīna istaḥaqqa ʿalayhimu al-awlāyān* — then two others stand in their place, from those with the nearest claim. *Yaqūmāni maqāmahumā*, they stand in their position — the seats of false witnesses are taken; *mina alladhīna istaḥaqqa ʿalayhimu al-awlāyān*, from those most entitled — *al-awlāyān*, the two nearest, the heirs whose inheritance the bequest touched: the very people the lie injured. The choice is exact: those with the strongest motive to lie about the bequest become its final witnesses — but under the heaviest possible oath, which converts their interest into their exposure. The design is dialectic: the replacement witnesses know that the community will suspect them, and the suspicion itself disciplines the testimony.

**The Counter-Oath**

*Fa-yuqsimāni billāhi la-shahādatunā aḥaqqu min shahādatihimā wa mā iʿtadaynā* — they swear by Allah: our testimony is truer than theirs, and we have not transgressed. *La-shahādatunā aḥaqqu*, ours is the truer — *aḥaqqu*, the comparative of *ḥaqq*: closer to reality, more rightful; *wa mā iʿtadaynā*, and we did not overstep — *ʿ-d-w*; *innā idhan lamina aẓ-ẓālimīn*, else we would be among the wrongdoers. The oath mirrors the first witnesses' oath (106) clause for clause — sinners there, wrongdoers here — so the two testimonies stand weighed before Allah by identical names of guilt. Whichever side spoke falsely has sworn perjury in Allah's name with the penalty named in advance.

**A Verse That Completes the Procedure**

Verses 106–107 form the complete law of deathbed testimony: witnesses (106) → oath → failure → counter-witnesses from the heirs → counter-oath (107). The procedure's genius is that it never needs the state: the bequest's truth is defended by oath against oath, fear against fear, with Allah as the court of last resort — and the fraudulent side carries home a false oath by Allah, the heaviest luggage in creation. The report of the occasion shows it working: the heirs replaced the two witnesses, swore, and the silver bowl was restored.

**Extra Verified Note on the Design**

*Al-awlāyān*, the two nearest-entitled, from *w-l-y* — the root of *walāyah*, guardianship: the heirs are the bequest's natural guardians, and the verse arms them for exactly one duty. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *istaḥaqqa al-ithm* as to earn the sin — the language of wages, not accidents: false testimony is earned, and here it is paid back. The next verse states the whole procedure's purpose in one sentence: that testimony be given according to its true face — or that oaths fear being answered by oaths.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:108

> That is more likely that they will give testimony according to its true face, or fear that oaths will be refuted after their oaths. And be mindful of Allah and listen. Allah does not guide the rebellious people

**More Likely: True Testimony, or Fear of Refuted Oaths**

*Dhālika adnā an yaʾtū bish-shahādati ʿalā wajhihā aw yakhāfū an turadda aymānun baʿda aymānihim* — that is nearer, that they give the testimony according to its true face, or that they fear oaths answered after their oaths. *Dhālika adnā*, that is closer — the verse states the procedure's *wisdom* in the language of likelihood: *adnā*, nearer to the intended outcome. *ʿAlā wajhihā*, according to its face — testimony in its true form, undistorted, unsold, unconcealed. *Aw yakhāfū an turadda aymānun baʿda aymānihim*, or they fear oaths being thrown back after their oaths — the false witnesses' sworn words returned to them by the heirs' counter-oath, exposure facing exposure. The law of 106–107 thus aims at both: bring out truth (carrot), make falsehood dread the mirror (stick). Legislation with a stated purpose — the Qur'an never issues rulings blind.

**And Be Mindful of Allah and Listen**

*Wa-ttaqū Allāha wasmaʿū* — and be mindful of Allah and listen. *Ittaqū*, shield yourselves in mindfulness; *ismaʿū*, hear and comply — the pair completes every legislation in this sūrah: the law explained (wisdom stated), the law commanded (mindfulness), the law received (listening). After three verses of procedure, the reminder is that procedure is never self-executing: honest men, not mechanisms, give *testimony according to its face* — and the fear that disciplines them is not of procedure but of Allah, *to whom you will be gathered* (96).

**Allah Does Not Guide the Rebellious People**

*Wa Allāhu lā yahdī al-qawma al-fāsiqīn* — and Allah does not guide the rebellious people. *Al-fāsiqīn*, those who exit obedience — the seal that closed verse 51's loyalty warning returns here: the false witness, the seller of testimony, the concealer, the perjurer at the deathbed — guidance is withheld from the *fāsiq*, and his craft defeats itself. The seal also answers the whole bequest section: if the procedure still fails, the failure is not the law's but the rebellious heart's — and no refinement of oaths can guide a people Allah has left to their exit.

**A Verse That States the Law's Purpose**
