Verses 106–108 stand as a complete unit — procedure (106), remedy (107), purpose (108) — and the purpose clause is the unit's crown: the law was designed to make truth likelier and falsehood frightened. The bequest's protection is thus framed as part of the sūrah's architecture of trust: contracts (1), testimony (8), wills (106–107) — the community's dealings all held up to *its face*, *wajhihā*, the true face of things. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *danā* as to draw near — *adnā*, likelier, nearer the goal.

**Extra Verified Note on Oaths Answering Oaths**

*Turadda aymānun baʿda aymānihim* — oaths returned after oaths — is the Qur'anic name for the mutual-impeachment procedure; the jurists drew from it the rule that claim-defeating oaths can be answered by counter-oaths, each side's solemnity weighed before Allah. The seal *lā yahdī al-qawma al-fāsiqīn* is the sūrah's third use of the formula (51, 108), each time guarding a structure — loyalty, testimony — against the only thing that corrupts them: hearts that have left obedience. The next verse lifts the reader from the deathbed to the Last Day: the messengers asked what their people answered — *we have no knowledge; You are the Knower of the unseen*.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:109

> ˹Beware of˺ the Day when Allah will gather the messengers together and say, "What was the response you received?" They will say, "We have no knowledge; indeed, it is You who knows best all that is unseen"

**The Day the Messengers Are Gathered and Questioned**

*Yawma yajmaʿu Allāhu ar-rusula fa-yaqūlu mādhā ujibtum* — the Day Allah gathers the messengers and says: what response were you given? *Yawma*, the Day — the warning particle that opens judgment scenes; *yajmaʿu*, He gathers — *j-m-ʿ*, assembled from every age; *ar-rusula*, the messengers — all of them, Nūḥ to Muḥammad ﷺ, the whole commissioned line standing in one assembly; *mādhā ujibtum*, what were you answered — *j-w-b*, the response each nation gave to its messenger. The scene parallels *We will question those to whom messengers were sent, and We will question the messengers* (7:6) — both sides examined: did the message arrive? what did the people answer? The question is not for information — Allah knows all — but for proof established and response made manifest.

**"We Have No Knowledge; You Are the Knower of the Unseen"**

*Qālū lā ʿilma lanā innaka Anta ʿAllāmu al-ghuyūb* — they say: we have no knowledge; indeed You alone are the All-Knowing of the unseen. *Lā ʿilma lanā*, no knowledge is ours — the most qualified men in creation disclaim the knowledge that matters: they can report the outward reply, but the hidden states of hearts, the concealed responses, the unseen of every soul — that is Yours alone. *ʿAllām*, the intensive form — the utmost Knower; *al-ghuyūb*, the unseens — plural: everything hidden, past and future, inward and outward. The messengers' answer is prophethood's final lesson: bear witness to what you witnessed, then hand all else to the Knower of the unseen. The same words ʿĪsā will speak in verse 116 — the Qur'an teaching its prophets' speech on the Day they stand.

**A Scene That Closes the Delivery Theme**

The sūrah has hammered the theme: *the Messenger's duty is only delivery* (92, 99); *convey everything revealed* (67). This verse is the theme's horizon: the delivery is complete, the gathering comes, the question is asked — and even the messengers' knowledge ends where Allah's begins. No messenger will be charged with his people's hearts; no people will claim the message never reached them. The scene also guards the messengers from the deification their followers invented: the greatest of them, gathered before their Lord, claim no knowledge and answer as servants — the exact posture the following verses show in ʿĪsā.

**A Verse That Turns From Law to the Last Day**

The bequest verses (106–108) closed with *be mindful of Allah and listen* — and the next scene shows *why*: after death, wills, and testimony comes the assembly where every response is summoned. The sūrah's long legislative middle here bends toward the Hereafter, where its laws will be audited. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ʿAllām* as one whose knowledge is most abundant — none bears the name but Allah. The next verse begins the account of the favours upon ʿĪsā son of Maryam — the messenger whose followers' response has been the sūrah's most examined.

**Extra Verified Note on the Response**

*Ujibtum* — you were answered — is in the passive: the messengers are asked not what they said but what came back to them — belief, denial, distortion. Their *lā ʿilma lanā* is read by the exegetes as the perfect etiquette of the gathering: not ignorance of outward history, but the refusal to claim what belongs only to *ʿAllām al-ghuyūb* — the inner truths that decide each soul's file. The next verse's catalogue of favours stands under this scene: every favour will be cited before the question of response.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:110

> And ˹beware of˺ the Day when Allah will say, "O Jesus, son of Mary! Remember My favour upon you and your mother — how I supported you with the holy spirit, so you spoke to the people in the cradle and in maturity; how I taught you writing, wisdom, the Torah, and the Gospel; how you moulded from clay the likeness of a bird, by My permission, then breathed into it, and it became a bird, by My permission; and you healed the blind and the leper, by My permission; and when you brought forth the dead, by My permission; and how I restrained the Children of Israel from you when you came to them with clear proofs — and those who disbelieved among them said, 'This is nothing but evident magic.'"

**Remember My Favour Upon You and Your Mother**

*Idh qāla Allāhu yā ʿĪsā ibna Maryama-udhkur niʿmatī ʿalayka wa ʿalā wālidatika* — when Allah says: O ʿĪsā, son of Maryam, remember My favour upon you and your mother. *Udhkur niʿmatī*, remember My favour — the address to ʿĪsā on the gathering Day opens with favours to recall: support, teaching, miracles, protection — and the first pair includes his mother, the favour upon Maryam the virgin birth itself and her honour among women. The scene from verse 109 now narrows to its most scrutinised messenger: the one whose followers raised him to godhood will be made to rehearse, before all creation, that every favour was Allah's.

**Supported With the Holy Spirit; Speaking in Cradle and Maturity**

*Idh ayyadtuka bir-rūḥi al-qudusi tukallimu an-nāsa fil-mahdi wa kahlā* — I supported you with the holy spirit, so you spoke to the people in the cradle and in maturity. *Ayyadtuka*, I strengthened you — from *ʾ-y-d*; *bir-rūḥi al-qudus*, with the holy spirit — the interpretation of the early generations: Jibrīl, the spirit of holiness, ʿĪsā's constant aid (2:87, 253). *Fil-mahdi*, in the cradle: the infant's defence of his mother — *I am the servant of Allah; He gave me the Book and made me a prophet* (19:30) — the first words of the man his people would deify, declaring servanthood from the crib. *Wa kahlā*, and in maturity — the strength of his whole prophetic life. The favours begin where divinity-claims should die: his very speech was given.

**Taught Writing, Wisdom, Torah, Gospel**

*Wa idh ʿallamtuka al-kitāba wal-ḥikmata wat-Tawrāta wal-Injīl* — and I taught you the writing, the wisdom, the Torah, and the Gospel. *ʿAllamtuka*, I taught you — the Teacher is Allah: *al-kitāb*, the writing and the Book; *al-ḥikmah*, wisdom; then the two scriptures — the Torah he inherited and the Gospel given to him, *containing guidance and light, confirming what was before it of the Torah* (5:46). Every word ʿĪsā knew was taught him; the learned one was a learner.

**The Miracles — Every One by My Permission**

*Wa idh takhluqu mina aṭ-ṭīni kahayʾati aṭ-ṭayri bi-idhnī fa-tanfukhu fīhā fa-takūnu ṭayran bi-idhnī* — you created from clay the likeness of a bird by My permission, breathed into it, and it became a bird by My permission. *Wa tubriʾu al-akmaha wal-abraṣa bi-idhnī* — you healed the born-blind and the leper by My permission. *Wa idh tukhriju al-mawtā bi-idhnī* — and when you brought forth the dead by My permission. Three tiers of miracle — giving life to clay, restoring sight and skin, raising the dead — each stamped *bi-idhnī*, by My permission: the phrase repeats four times in the verse, hammering the single point. The Creator is the One who permits; the messenger is the one permitted. His own words in the Gospel-era account: *I create for you from clay... by Allah's permission; I heal... by Allah's permission; I bring the dead to life by Allah's permission* (3:49). Miracles that proved prophethood to the sincere became, for the confused, grounds of deity — and this verse strips the grounds away: every wonder flowed from permission, and the permitted is not the Permitter.

**I Restrained the Children of Israel From You**

*Wa idh kafaftu Banī Isrā'īla minka idh jiʾtahum bil-bayyināt* — and when I restrained the Children of Israel from you, when you came to them with clear proofs. *Kafaftu*, I held them back — *k-f-f*, to withhold: the plots to kill him failed under divine restraint, until *they did not kill him nor crucify him* (4:157). His answer to the proofs: *those who disbelieved among them said: this is nothing but evident magic* — the reflex of every disbelieving elite, from Pharaoh's court to Madinah's mockers: miracles named magic, prophethood named sorcery.

**A Verse That Rehearses the Case Against Deification**

The verse is the gathering-Day deposition: favour after favour, all passive of Allah's hand, all stamped with permission, ending with the witnesses' charge — *evident magic*. Placed after verse 109's messengers' disclaimers and before verse 116's direct question — *did you tell people to take you and your mother as gods?* — it builds the frame within which ʿĪsā's answer will land. The favoured, taught, wonder-working servant owes everything to his Lord; the next verse adds the favour of believing helpers.

**Extra Verified Note on the List's Order**

The favours ascend and conclude: support, speech, learning, miracles, protection — ending with the rejection *magic*, so that the verse closes on the response, tying back to verse 109's *what response were you given?* *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *akmah* as born-blind — healing him the harder wonder — and *abraṣ* as the leper whose skin the disease had mottled. *Kahl*, maturity, is the age past youth — his ministry's years, all aided by the holy spirit. The next verse: *I inspired the disciples — believe in Me and My messenger.*
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:111

> And ˹remember˺ when I inspired the disciples to believe in Me and My messenger, they said, "We believe, and bear witness that we are ones who submit ˹to Allah˺"

**I Inspired the Disciples**

*Wa idh awḥaytu ilal-ḥawāriyyīna an āminū bī wa birasūlī* — and when I inspired the disciples: believe in Me and My messenger. *Awḥaytu*, I inspired — the verb of revelation used here for guidance cast into hearts: not a book sent down, but the directing of the ḥawāriyyūn to faith through ʿĪsā's call and Allah's grace — as hearts are turned in every age. *Al-ḥawāriyyūn*, the disciples — from *ḥawar*, whiteness and sincerity: the pure-hearted, sincere companions, rendered in the Gospel tradition the twelve. Their title is their character: white of heart, devoted helpers — *who are my helpers to Allah? The disciples said: we are Allah's helpers* (61:14), *we believe in Allah, and bear witness that we are submitters* (3:52). The favour-list's next entry is companionship: the lone wonder-worker given men who believed him.

**Believe in Me and My Messenger**

*An āminū bī wa birasūlī* — believe in Me and in My messenger. The command pairs the two objects of faith exactly as this sūrah does everywhere — *whoever disbelieves in Allah and His messengers* (5:10), *those who believe in Allah and His messengers* (57:28) — the messenger's authority is not a detour around Allah but the road to Him. Inspired to believe in ʿĪsā, the disciples were inspired to believe in Allah; faith in the messenger is faith's proof, not its rival.

**"We Believe, and Bear Witness That We Are Submitters"**

*Qālū āmannā wash-had bi-annanā muslimūn* — they said: we believe, and bear witness that we are ones who submit. *Āmannā*, we believe; *wash-had*, bear witness — *sh-h-d*, the testimony that verse 83's believing Christians begged for: *write us among the witnesses*; *bi-annanā muslimūn*, that we are *muslimūn* — submitters. The word is the point: the disciples of ʿĪsā called themselves muslimūn — not by anachronism but by truth, since *islām*, submission to the one God, is the religion of every prophet and every sincere follower — *Ibrāhīm was neither a Jew nor a Christian, but one upright, submitting* (3:67). The first ʿĪsā-community was a muslim community; what later centuries built over it — the deification verse 116 will interrogate — was another religion altogether.

**A Verse That Adds the Favour of Helpers**

The favours of verse 110 were personal: support, speech, learning, miracles, protection. Verse 111 adds the communal favour: inspired helpers who believed and testified. The sequence matters for the scene's direction — the real ʿĪsā-movement declared *muslimūn* with their tongues; the deviation came later, and the interrogation of verse 116 will press exactly there: *did you tell them to take you and your mother as gods?* The disciples' own declaration is the standing evidence of what the master taught.

**Extra Verified Note on Ḥawāriyyūn**

*Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ḥawar* as the whiteness of the eye's brightness and the pure heart; the *ḥawāriyyūn* were so named for sincerity and purity — some say also for their fine clothing, as companions of a prophet. *Awḥaytu* here confirms the Qur'an's range for *waḥy*: revelation to prophets, casting into hearts (to the mother of Mūsā, 28:7), and instinct to bees (16:68) — the word denotes Allah's swift, hidden direction of His creation. The next verse brings the disciples' one request — the table spread from heaven — the episode that names this sūrah.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:112

> And ˹remember˺ when the disciples said, "O Jesus, son of Mary! Can your Lord send down to us a table ˹spread˺ with food from heaven?" He said, "Be mindful of Allah, if you are believers"

**The Disciples' Request**

*Idh qāla al-ḥawāriyyūna yā ʿĪsā ibna Maryama hal yastaṭīʿu rabbuka an yunazzila ʿalaynā mā'idatan minas-samāʾ* — when the disciples said: O ʿĪsā, son of Maryam, can your Lord send down upon us a table spread from heaven? *Hal yastaṭīʿu rabbuka*, can your Lord — the grammar has engaged the exegetes for centuries: the verbs admit a reading *can your Lord* and a reading *can you [ask] your Lord* (*hal tastaṭīʿu rabbaka*), and the Muslims among the disciples did not doubt Allah's power — they asked whether His will would open this door, or whether the prophet could obtain it. *Yunazzila*, send down — from heaven; *mā'idah*, a table spread with food — from *māda*, to move, the laden table set before guests; the word occurs only here and in the next verses, and it names the sūrah itself: *al-Mā'idah*, The Table Spread — this episode is the chapter's signature story.

**Not a Test of Power but a Request for a Sign**

The believers' request parallels Ibrāhīm's: *My Lord, show me how You give life to the dead; He said: do you not believe? He said: yes, but to reassure my heart* (2:260). Faith seeking reinforcement is not faith challenged; the disciples — just declared believers and witnesses (111) — asked for the sign that would settle their hearts and equip their testimony. The precedent stands in the wilderness: the manna and quails sent down to Banī Isrā'īl. Still, the request treads near the old Israelite habit — signs demanded, then squandered — and ʿĪsā's answer shows he heard that echo.

**"Be Mindful of Allah, If You Are Believers"**

*Qāla-ttaqū Allāha in kuntum muʾminīn* — he said: be mindful of Allah, if you are believers. *Ittaqū Allāh*, the command of every covenant's first page; *in kuntum muʾminīn*, if you are believers — a double-edged reply: if you believe, your faith should suffice without demanded signs — and remember what you ask for, for a sign accepted and then denied carries a terrible weight (as verse 115 will state). The answer is a prophet's pastoral care: before granting, he warns; before the table descends, he plants mindfulness. The measure of a true believer is not his appetite for wonders but his fear of the Lord of wonders.

**A Verse That Introduces the Named Story**

Verses 112–115 run as one narrative — request (112), justification (113), prayer (114), answer and warning (115) — and the sūrah takes its common name from the table. The story answers the sūrah's standing question — what do people do with open revelation? — in miniature: a sign sent in mercy, a warning attached to it, and history's verdict recorded at 115. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *mā'idah* as the table with its food — the movement of the laden tray giving the word its root.

**Extra Verified Note on the Two Readings**

Both canonical readings — *hal yastaṭīʿu rabbuka* (can your Lord) and *hal tastaṭīʿu rabbaka* (can you [obtain from] your Lord) — are recited; the exegetes harmonise: the disciples knew His power and asked after His willingness through His prophet. *Ittaqū Allāha in kuntum muʾminīn* also echoes verse 57's seal — the same formula closing the mockery warning — binding the table story to the sūrah's ethic of mindful belief. The next verse gives the disciples' reasons: four of them, each innocent.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:113

> They said, "We wish to eat from it and reassure our hearts, and know that you have told us the truth, and be among the witnesses over it"

**We Wish to Eat From It**

*Qālū nurīdu an naʾkula minhā* — they said: we wish to eat from it. *Nurīdu*, we wish — stated as desire, not demand; *an naʾkula minhā*, to eat from it: the table is provision, the honour of eating what heaven sent, as their fathers ate the manna. The first reason is the simplest: sustenance as blessing.

**And Reassure Our Hearts**

*Wa taṭmaʾinna qulūbunā* — and that our hearts be reassured. *Taṭmaʾinna* from *ṭ-m-ʾ-n*, to be still, tranquil: the heart's settling into certainty. This is Ibrāhīm's word: *yes, but to reassure my heart* (2:260) — faith asking for its resting place, not testing its Lord. The disciples' belief needed no proof, but hearts are creaturely: they grow calm when certainty is fed. The request is thus the opposite of the Cow-people's interrogations (2:67–71), which multiplied questions to postpone obedience; the disciples asked once, and for the sake of the faith they already held.

**And Know That You Have Told Us the Truth**

*Wa naʿlama an qad ṣadaqtanā* — and that we may know you have told us the truth. *Naʿlama*, we may know with settled recognition; *ṣadaqtanā*, you were truthful with us — the messenger's *ṣidq* confirmed: the promised sign verifies the promised man. Again the parallel holds: they do not say *we doubt you*; they say *let the truth you spoke be sealed to us* — the difference between evidence sought for confirmation and evidence sought for escape.

**And Be Among the Witnesses Over It**

*Wa nakūna ʿalayhā minash-shāhidīn* — and that we may be among the witnesses over it. *Shāhidīn*, witnesses — the word that organises this whole passage: verse 83's Christians begged to be *written among the witnesses*; verse 109's gathering asks every messenger *what response were you given*; here the disciples volunteer for the station. Witnesses to what? To the table — its descent, its reality — and through it to ʿĪsā's truth before their people and before the Day. The request converts a meal into testimony: they will eat as witnesses eat, and speak of what they saw. Verse 111's *bear witness that we are submitters* prepared this: now they ask for the content of their witness.

**Four Reasons, All of Faith**

Eating, reassurance, verification, witnessing — each reason is an act of belief, not a substitute for it. The verse is the Qur'an's portrait of the right kind of asking: desire disciplined by devotion, wonder sought in service of witness. ʿĪsā's caution answered, the request stands justified — and the next verse carries it to heaven: his prayer, *O Allah, our Lord, send down upon us a table... a feast for the first and last of us, and a sign from You.*

**Extra Verified Note on the Contrast**

The contrast with the Israelites' asking is the scene's quiet engine: they asked *show us Allah plainly* and fell thunderstruck (4:153); they asked about the cow until the doing became heavy (2:67–71). The disciples ask once, for reassurance, and accept the warning that comes with the gift (115). *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṭumaʾnīnah* as the stillness of the heart after movement — *taṭmaʾinna qulūbunā*, our hearts, moved by faith, made still by sight. The next verse: the prophet's prayer and its threefold petition.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:114

> Jesus, son of Mary, said, "O Allah, our Lord! Send down to us a table ˹spread˺ with food from heaven, to be a feast for the first and the last of us, and a sign from You. And provide for us, for You are the Best of providers"

**The Prophet's Prayer**

*Qāla ʿĪsā ibnu Maryama-llāhumma Rabbanā anzil ʿalaynā mā'idatan minas-samāʾ* — ʿĪsā, son of Maryam, said: O Allah, our Lord, send down upon us a table spread from heaven. *Allāhumma Rabbanā*, O Allah, our Lord — the double address: the proper Name and the Lordship; the prayer's first syllables confess the theology the deifiers forgot: ʿĪsā prays *to* Allah *for* the table — the worker of wonders knows where wonders come from. *Anzil ʿalaynā*, send down upon us — he grants the request not on his own authority but through supplication: the prophet is a means, the Lord the giver.

**A Feast for the First and the Last of Us**

*Takūnu ʿīdan li-awwalinā wa ākhirinā* — to be a feast for the first and the last of us. *ʿĪdan*, a feast, festival — from *ʿāda*, to return: the recurring day of celebration. The petition lifts the table beyond the moment: *for the first of us*, the disciples present, *and the last of us*, the communities to come — the event remembered and honoured down the generations, as ʿĪds are honoured. The exegetes heard in it the foretelling of a commemoration that would keep the table's memory alive among the followers of ʿĪsā — and the verse's phrasing keeps the memory in the Qur'an itself, recited wherever the Book is recited.

**A Sign From You**

*Wa āyatan minka* — and a sign from You. *Āyah*, sign — the table's official title in the prayer: not a meal, a *sign* — of ʿĪsā's truth, of heaven's care, of the Lord who feeds His servants from where they cannot reach. The disciples' four reasons (113) are gathered into the prophet's single petition: provision (eat), reassurance (hearts), verification (sign), witness (feast remembered).

**And Provide for Us; You Are the Best of Providers**

*Wa urzuqnā wa Anta khayru ar-rāziqīn* — and provide for us, for You are the Best of providers. *Urzuqnā*, provide us — the prayer ends in dependence; *khayru ar-rāziqīn*, Best of providers — the Name that also closes the sūrah's opening table-language (5:4's *fear Allah... it is Allah whom you fear*? no — of the *rāziqīn* names: 62:11's *Allah is the Best of providers*). The provider who sends from heaven cannot fail; the petition concedes that even the answered table is His gift, not a claim on Him. A prayer so framed — address, request, purpose, dependence — is itself the Qur'an's lesson in how to ask.

**A Verse That Refutes Deification by Its Grammar**

Note what the prayer does not say: no *by my authority*, no *in my name* — a prophet praying to his Lord for what only the Lord can send. The man whose followers would call him God asks God for bread from heaven. The verse sits inside the gathering-Day frame (109–116): the same ʿĪsā who prayed this prayer will answer for his message at verse 116 — *did you tell them to take you and your mother as gods?* The prayer is his precedent evidence: he could not even set a table; how could he claim a throne?

**Extra Verified Note on ʿĪd**

*Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ʿīd* as that which returns — the gathering of people at a recurring time of joy; the Qur'an uses the word only here, sealing the table as the *feast* of the ʿĪsā-community's memory. Reports describe the descent laden — fish and bread, the table's disputed contents — but the Qur'an deliberately names no menu: the *sign*, not the supper, is the story. The next verse is heaven's answer — and its warning.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:115

> Allah said, "Indeed, I am sending it down to you. But whoever disbelieves after this among you — I will punish him with a punishment by which I will not punish anyone else in the worlds"

**I Am Sending It Down to You**

*Qāla Allāhu innī munazziluhā ʿalaykum* — Allah said: indeed I am sending it down upon you. *Innī munazziluhā*, I am sending it down — the intensive form of *n-z-l*: a sending watched over, deliberate; *ʿalaykum*, upon you. The prayer is answered without hesitation — the table descends, the sign is granted, the feast for the first and last is given. Mercy answered the disciples' four faithful reasons; heaven set a table for witnesses.

**But Whoever Disbelieves After This**

*Fa-man yakfur baʿda dhālika minkum* — but whoever disbelieves after this, among you. *Yakfur*, disbelieves — the denial that can follow is now defined: after the requested sign, after the witnessed descent, *baʿda dhālika*, after that — the clearest boundary in the whole ʿĪsā-account. Before the table, denial had excuses; after the table, none. The clause's weight lies in its placement: heaven's gift and heaven's gavel arrive together.

**A Punishment I Will Not Punish Anyone Else With**

*Fa-innī uʿadhdhibuhu ʿadhāban lā uʿadhdhibuhu aḥadan minal-ʿālamīn* — I will punish him with a punishment with which I will not punish anyone among the worlds. *Uʿadhdhibuhu*, I will punish him; *lā uʿadhdhibuhu aḥadan*, I will not punish anyone else — with *it*: the sentence is not a promise to spare all others generally, but that no other in the worlds will receive *this* punishment — a torments-of-torments reserved for denying a begged-for, witnessed sign. The Qur'an's history of sign-and-punishment pairs explains the severity: the she-camel of Ṣāliḥ, demanded and then hamstrung — *the crying punishment seized them*; the Sabbath-fish, and transformation; the thunderbolt for *show us Allah plainly*. To request a miracle, receive it, and deny it is disbelief at its most wilful — it denies not what it lacks but what it holds.

**Mercy and Warning in One Breath**

The verse answers and arms itself in a single sentence: the gift (table), the gift's purpose (feast, sign), and the gift's gravity (unprecedented penalty for its denial). This is the sūrah's signature balance — *know that Allah is severe in punishment and that Allah is forgiving, merciful* (98) — compressed into a table-setting. The answer honours faith's asking; the warning honours the answer's weight. The exegetes note the pattern for all revealed bounty: what comes from heaven carries accountability in its wrapping.

**A Verse That Names the Sūrah and Seals the Story**

Verses 112–115 complete the table narrative — request, reasons, prayer, answer-with-warning — and the story gives the chapter its name, *al-Mā'idah*. The narrative also completes the arc begun at verse 110: favours upon ʿĪsā, inspiration of the disciples, the table — and next, the question that all these favours frame: *did you tell the people to take you and your mother as gods besides Allah?* Every favour rehearsed — support, teaching, miracles, helpers, the table — is a favour *from Allah*, the exact opposite of the deification charge.

**Extra Verified Note on the Unprecedented Punishment**

Reports describe the table's descent with fish and bread — details the Qur'an withholds, keeping the focus on the *sign* and the *warning*. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *munazzil* as the form of a doer engaged in the act — the sending was present, assured, *innī munazziluhā*. The precedent of the she-camel (7:77, 11:64–65) stands closest: a demanded sign denied and destroyed, and a unique punishment — the Qur'an's consistent law that the sin of rejecting a witnessed miracle is measured by the witness. The next verse returns to the gathering: the question to ʿĪsā that the favours have been preparing.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:116

> And ˹beware of˺ the Day when Allah will say, "O Jesus, son of Mary! Did you say to the people, 'Take me and my mother as gods besides Allah'?" He will say, "Glory be to You! It is not for me to say what I have no right to. If I had said it, You would have known it. You know what is within me, and I do not know what is within You. Indeed, it is You alone who knows best all that is unseen"

**The Question at the Gathering**

*Wa idh qāla Allāhu yā ʿĪsā bna Maryama a-anta qulta lin-nāsi-ttakhidhūnī wa ummiya ilāhayni min dūni Allāh* — and when Allah says: O ʿĪsā, son of Maryam, did you say to the people, take me and my mother as two gods besides Allah? *A-anta qulta*, did *you* say — the question's emphasis: you yourself, with your own tongue. *Ittakhidhūnī wa ummiya ilāhayni*, take me and my mother as two gods — *ilāhayni*, the dual: two deities set beside Allah. The address names him *son of Maryam* — the human identity itself an answer. The question interrogates the deification of both figures: the godhood of the son and, remarkably, of the mother — the veneration that swelled in some communities into worship of Maryam herself, offerings made to her as to a goddess. No prophet taught it; this scene is where the invention meets its witness.

**"Glory Be to You! It Is Not for Me to Say"**

*Qāla subḥānaka mā yakūnu lī an aqūla mā laysa lī bi-ḥaqq* — he says: Glory be to You! It is not mine to say what I have no right to. *Subḥānaka*, Glory be to You — the word of *tanzīh*, declaring Allah far above every ascription — and here the first word of ʿĪsā's defence is to exalt Allah, not to plead himself. *Mā yakūnu lī*, it is not fitting for me; *mā laysa lī bi-ḥaqq*, what is not my right — a servant has no licence to deify himself or his mother; the claim would be theft of what belongs to Allah alone. The man who spoke in the cradle (19:30 — *I am the servant of Allah*) answers in the same key at the end of days.

**"If I Had Said It, You Would Have Known It"**

*In kuntu qultuhu fa-qad ʿalimtah* — if I had said it, You would have known it. *Fa-qad ʿalimtah*, You would already have known it: nothing escapes from a tongue to the Knower. The argument is quiet and total — the question *did you say* is answered by the omnipresence of Allah's knowledge: had the words been spoken, they would have been on record before their echo faded.

**"You Know What Is Within Me, and I Know Not What Is Within You"**

*Taʿlamu mā fī nafsī wa lā aʿlamu mā fī nafsik* — You know what is within my self, and I do not know what is within Your self. *Mā fī nafsī*, what is in my inner being — intentions, secrets, the heart's rooms; *lā aʿlamu mā fī nafsik*, and I do not know what is within Your Self — the servant cannot survey his Lord's knowledge, will, or essence. The sentence states the entire creature-Creator relationship in two clauses: the Lord knows the servant exhaustively; the servant knows the Lord only as far as the Lord has taught. Then the seal — *innaka Anta ʿAllāmul-ghuyūb*, indeed You alone are the Knower of the unseen — the very words all the messengers spoke at verse 109, now on ʿĪsā's tongue: *lā ʿilma lanā* made personal.

**A Verse That Answers a Doctrine With a Deposition**

The verse is the Qur'an's conclusive counter to the deification of ʿĪsā and Maryam: not argument but testimony — under oath, before all creation, from the accused himself. The chain of evidence built from verse 110 (favours, all by permission), 111 (disciples who declared *muslimūn*), 112–115 (a prophet who prays for his own table) closes here: the same man, the same tongue, disowns the doctrine. Whoever persists in it after this scene has chosen a religion its named prophet never taught. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *subḥānaka* as the distancing of Allah from every defect — the defence's first breath.

**Extra Verified Note on "Me and My Mother"**

The pairing is deliberate: some communities exalted Maryam toward worship — the veneration the verse names *ilāhayni*, two gods — while the Qur'an honours her exactly as far as revelation does: *chosen above the women of the worlds* (3:42), *a woman of truth* (5:75) — honoured, human, no more. The next verse is ʿĪsā's account of what he *did* say: worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:117

> ˹Jesus will say,˺ "I said to them nothing except what You commanded me: 'Worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord.' And I was a witness over them as long as I remained among them; but when You took me, You were the Watcher over them, and You are a Witness over all things"

**I Said Only What You Commanded Me**

*Mā qultu lahum illā mā amartanī bihi* — I said to them nothing except what You commanded me. *Mā...illā*, nothing except — the restrictive double negative continuing the deposition: after denying the false teaching (116), ʿĪsā states the true one in full. A prophet's message is dictated by commission; there is no private theology in prophethood. The verse therefore presents the complete archive of his instruction — one command, endlessly repeated.

**Worship Allah, My Lord and Your Lord**

*Ani uʿbudū Allāha Rabbī wa Rabbakum* — worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord. The exact sentence of verse 72 — the Messiah's own words, quoted by the Qur'an twice, preached from the cradle to the gathering. *Rabbī wa Rabbakum*, my Lord and your Lord: the grammar is the creed — *my* Lord: he has a Lord, and the one with a Lord is not the Lord; *your* Lord: the same Lord, one for all. This is what he *did* say — worship, addressed to Allah, shared by all. The deification doctrine is thus refuted twice over: by what he denied (116) and by what he taught (117).

**A Witness Over Them While I Remained Among Them**

*Wa kuntu ʿalayhim shahīdan mā dumtu fīhim* — and I was a witness over them as long as I remained among them. *Shahīdan*, a witness — the messenger's office during his presence: seeing, calling, testifying; *mā dumtu fīhim*, as long as I dwelt among them — the witness's term has a boundary: presence. He does not claim watch over what came after him — an essential disavowal, for the deification he is charged with developed *after* his presence ended.

**When You Took Me, You Were the Watcher Over Them**

*Fa-lammā tawaffaytanī kunta Anta ar-raqība ʿalayhim* — but when You took me, You were the Watcher over them. *Tawaffaytanī*, You took me — the verb of full taking: the Qur'an states *I will take you and raise you to Myself* (3:55) and *Allah raised him to Himself* (4:157) — the taking of ʿĪsā was a raising, not a killing; the exegetes' majority read *tawaffā* here as the taking-up, with his death appointed for after his return. *Ar-raqīb*, the Watcher — the one who never blinks, the guardian-watchman: when the human witness's term expired, the divine Watcher's had no such term. The devotions and deviations of the centuries between — including the very deification under question — all occurred under *ar-Raqīb*'s eye.

**And You Are a Witness Over All Things**

*Wa Anta ʿalā kulli shayʾin Shahīd* — and You are a Witness over all things. The closing widens the lens from his community to everything: *Shahīd* over all — every century, every creed, every silence. The deposition ends where it must: the accused hands the case to the Witness who never left. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *raqīb* as the one who observes and preserves — the name that guarantees the question of verse 116 was asked with total information.

**A Verse That Completes the Testimony**

The unit 116–117 is a single deposition: denial (I never said it), affirmation (I said worship), term (witness while present), transfer (You watched after). Its effect is historical: the theology that grew in ʿĪsā's name grew in his absence, under a Watcher who will recount it. The next verse is his final word — not a defence of his people, but their handover: *if You punish them, they are Your servants; if You forgive them, You are the Almighty, the Wise.*
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:118

> "If You punish them, then indeed they are Your servants; but if You forgive them, then indeed it is You who are the Almighty, the All-Wise"

**If You Punish Them, They Are Your Servants**

*In tuʿadhdhibhum fa-innahum ʿibāduk* — if You punish them, then they are Your servants. *Tuʿadhdhibhum*, You punish them; *fa-innahum ʿibāduk*, they are Your slaves — all of them, even the deifiers: ownership does not wait on obedience. Every creature is Allah's *ʿabd* in the sense of possession — *all who are in the heavens and the earth come to the Most Merciful as servants* (19:93) — even when they serve another in worship. ʿĪsā's word therefore hands his people over in their true legal standing: not his followers to shield, not a cause to defend — souls owned by their Lord, whose disposition of them is His right. The great prophets of the Qur'an speak this way at the hour of intercession: Nūḥ — *my Lord, forgive me and my parents*... and for the disbelievers, no plea; Ibrāhīm's forgiveness for his father cut off at the boundary of Allah's enmity. Mercy has a creed-line, and the messengers do not cross it.

**If You Forgive Them, You Are the Almighty, the All-Wise**

*Wa in taghfir lahum fa-innaka Antal-ʿAzīzul-Ḥakīm* — but if You forgive them, then indeed You are the Almighty, the All-Wise. *Taghfir*, You forgive — covering what was done; *al-ʿAzīz*, the Almighty — might belongs to Him, so neither punishing nor sparing is beyond His power, and none can contest either; *al-Ḥakīm*, the All-Wise — every verdict placed exactly where wisdom dictates: forgiveness flowing through faith and repentance, judgment resting on the truth of hearts He alone has watched (117: *You were the Watcher over them*). The name-pair *ʿAzīz Ḥakīm* closes the deposition: power that cannot be opposed, wisdom that cannot be faulted.

**Submission, Not Abandonment**

The verse is not cold — it is exact. ʿĪsā intercedes *for* his people constantly elsewhere: *whoever disbelieves... you are over them a witness* is his only limit. Here at the trial of the deifiers, his speech models the perfect prophetic posture: state the message, hand the people over, exalt the Judge. Nothing in the verse asks for punishment — it acknowledges its rightfulness; nothing in it presumptuously grants forgiveness — it exalts the Forgiver. Between the two clauses stands the whole theology of the gathering: every soul owned, every verdict wise, no messenger overstepping his station. The Prophet's ﷺ own stance for his community mirrors it: ceaseless intercession *within* the Lord's will — *my intercession is for those of my community who committed great sins* (reported in the Sunan) — never over it.

**A Verse That Closes the Deposition**

Verses 116–118 are one speech: the denial, the message, the watching, the handover. The sūrah's long ʿĪsā-cycle — favours (110), disciples (111), table (112–115), interrogation and defence (116–117), submission (118) — ends with the name-pair of power and wisdom, the same pairing that sealed the sūrah's opening covenant-laws. The next verse lifts from ʿĪsā's community to the standing rule of all communities: *this is a Day on which the truthful benefit from their truthfulness.*

**Extra Verified Note on ʿIbāduk**

The exegetes distinguish two senses of *ʿibād*: servants by creation and ownership — all creatures — and servants by willing obedience — the believers; the verse uses the first sense, verse 79's context the second. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *taghfir* as covering with the intent of pardon. The placement of *al-ʿAzīz* before *al-Ḥakīm* reads precisely: might first — so forgiveness is generosity from strength, not necessity; wisdom second — so forgiveness lands where it truly benefits.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:119

> Allah will say, "This is the Day when the truthful will benefit from their truthfulness." For them are Gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they will abide forever. Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him. That is the great attainment

**The Day the Truthful Benefit From Their Truthfulness**

*Qāla Allāhu hādhā yawmu yanfaʿu aṣ-ṣādiqīna ṣidquhum* — Allah will say: this is the Day the truthful benefit from their truthfulness. *Yanfaʿu*, it benefits; *aṣ-ṣādiqīna*, the truthful — *ṣ-d-q*, truthfulness in belief, word, and deed; *ṣidquhum*, their truthfulness — the commodity that pays out on that Day and on no other. The declaration lands precisely where the deposition of verses 116–118 ended: ʿĪsā spoke the truth under questioning — *I said to them nothing except what You commanded me* — and this verse is the rule his truthfulness exemplifies. On the Day when every invented claim collapses — the fabricated taboos (103), the forefathers' authority (104), the deification of prophets (116–117) — one currency holds its value: *ṣidq*. The Prophet's ﷺ word seals it: *truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to Paradise; a man keeps telling the truth until he is written with Allah as truthful* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6094, Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2607).

**Gardens Beneath Which Rivers Flow, Abiding Forever**

*Lahum jannātun tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhāru khālidīna fīhā abadan* — for them are Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein forever. The standing portrait of Paradise — gardens watered without toil, *abadan*, for endless age. The formula has already crowned this sūrah's converts: the believing Christians rewarded *for what they said* (85) received exactly this. Truthfulness in word and faith is rewarded in the same coin as every excellence: permanence.

**Allah Is Pleased With Them, and They Are Pleased With Him**

*Raḍiya Allāhu ʿanhum wa raḍū ʿanhu* — Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him. *Raḍiya... raḍū*, the mutual pleasure — *riḍwān*: He is pleased with their truthfulness and obedience; they are pleased with His decree, His reward, His company — pleased with Him in what He gave and what He withheld. This is the Qur'an's highest transaction, beyond the gardens: *Allah has promised the believers... forgiveness and a great reward* — *and the pleasure of Allah is greater* (9:72). The sūrah that opened with *fulfil the contracts* closes its covenant-row with the fulfilled contract's signature: mutual pleasure, sealed forever.

**That Is the Great Attainment**

*Dhālika al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm* — that is the great attainment. *Fawz*, triumph and arrival — the success the sūrah kept promising: *that you may succeed* (35, 90, 100). Here is the *fawz* in full: gardens, permanence, mutual pleasure. The verse thus completes the two-outcome architecture of the ʿĪsā-cycle: the deifiers' doctrine collapses (116–117), its holders handed to their Owner (118), and the truthful — among them the truthful messenger — crowned (119). Between *lā yakūnu lī an aqūla* (it is not mine to say) and *yanfaʿu aṣ-ṣādiqīna ṣidquhum* (the truthful benefit) runs the whole sūrah's moral: truth is the one possession that crosses the bridge.

**Extra Verified Note on Ṣādiqīn**

*Aṣ-ṣādiqīn* spans three registers of truth: in belief (sincere faith), in speech (honest witness — the bequest-oaths of 106–108 exist to protect it), and in deed (integrity between word and act). *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *ṣadaqa* as the joining of word to fact. The verse's placement as the second-to-last of the sūrah makes it the standing verdict before the final name — as if the whole Book's accounts were settled by one sentence: the truthful profited, the falsifiers lost. The next verse, the sūrah's last, hands the entire ledger to its Owner.
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:120

> To Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth and whatever is within them, and He is over all things Competent

**To Allah Belongs the Dominion of the Heavens and the Earth**

*Lillāhi mulku as-samāwāti wal-arḍi wa mā fīhinn* — to Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth and whatever is within them. *Lillāhi*, to Allah alone; *mulku*, the dominion — *m-l-k*, ownership, sovereignty, command: the whole estate, not rented, not shared; *as-samāwāti wal-arḍ*, the heavens and the earth; *wa mā fīhinn*, and whatever is within them — the clause that leaves nothing out: every creature, angel, human, jinn, prophet, mother of a prophet, kingdom, and creed. The closing verse lands where the ʿĪsā-interrogation left off: those who took prophets as gods besides Allah are reminded that the prophets themselves are *within* the heavens and earth — owned, not owners; *within the dominion, not holders of it*. The same declaration stands at the parallel gathering-verses: *to Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth; Allah is over all things Competent* (3:189) — the Qur'an's chosen seal for scenes of final judgment.

**And He Is Over All Things Competent**

*Wa Huwa ʿalā kulli shayʾin Qadīr* — and He is over all things Competent. *Qadīr*, from *q-d-r*, the Perfect in power: everything the sūrah rehearsed is gathered under this competence — the table sent down (115), the miracles by permission (110), the gathering and questioning (109, 116), the punishment and forgiveness handed over in ʿĪsā's final words (118), the reward of the truthful (119) — all within the reach of the One who owns all and can do all. No clause of the sūrah's law outstrips the Legislator's power to uphold it; no promise exceeds His ability to pay.

**A Frame Closed**

The sūrah opened: *O you who believe, fulfil the contracts* (5:1) and *Allah judges as He wills* (5:40?) — its first verses binding believers to obligations under a sovereign Lawgiver; its last verse names the Sovereign: dominion His, power His. The frame teaches how the laws in between should be read: not as human regulations but as enactments of the Owner of the heavens and the earth — His foods, His months, His House, His penalties, His contracts. And the frame answers the sūrah's central argument at the highest level: the People of the Book claimed *we are the children of Allah and His beloved* (18) — authority by lineage; the Jāhilī Arabs legislated taboos by invention (103) — authority by fabrication. The closing verse gives the only true basis of authority in one line: *to Allah belongs the dominion* — legislation belongs to the Owner, worship to the Sovereign, and every creature, including every exalted servant, stands within *mā fīhinn*.

**The Final Word of the Chapter**

Al-Mā'idah — the sūrah of covenants fulfilled, laws restored, tables sent down, and testimony taken — ends not with a ruling but with a reality: dominion and power belong to Allah. Every reader closes the chapter where its first verse began the summons: before the Owner. *Lisān al-ʿArab* glosses *mulk* as possession and effective command — both together here, and both His alone. The sūrah is complete: *to Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth and whatever is within them, and He is over all things Competent.*
